CXL Systems Research Landscape 2024–2026: Categorized Paper Map, Gap Analysis & 4-Device Opportunity Assessment

Compiled 2026-07-08 on c28. Method: 7-stage multi-agent pipeline (≤10 concurrent agents): 10 discovery sweeps (venues 2022–2026, arXiv, 2026 accepted lists, adversarial multi-device hunt) → 97 per-paper deep-reads against primary sources with verbatim evidence quotes → 39 skeptical re-verifications (30 confirmed, 9 corrected) → map-audit intuition checks. Raw data: benchmarks/results/2026-07-08-cxl-lit-review.json. Companion: DEEP_RESEARCH_2026-06-12.html (direction ranking), Related.md (2026-05-20 scan).

Honesty notes. (1) Fields marked confidence: low or could not verify come from partially accessible sources (paywalled/embargoed) — treat as provisional. (2) The final web-wide adversarial sweeps for intuitions I1/I3 were skipped at the user's request to save budget; their verdicts below rest on the per-paper primary-source extractions, the completed map audits (I2, I4), and the independent 3-vote pass of 2026-06-12. (3) Some 2026 papers (OSDI/ISCA/FAST '26) are embargoed; entries rely on abstracts, talks, or artifact repositories where stated.

Snapshot: what 97 verified paper records say

Real-CXL statusreal ASIC: 20 · real FPGA: 13 · mixed real+emulated: 22 · NUMA-emulated only: 13 · other HW (Optane/RDMA): 9 · simulated: 8 · unknown/embargoed: 12
Device treatmentsingle device: 26 · multi-device pooled/fused: 10 · multi-device independent: 7 · multi-device mixed: 7 · simulated pool: 14 · n/a (no CXL): 24 · unknown: 9

Verdicts on the five starting intuitions

I1 — "Many CXL papers study only one CXL device": SUPPORTED (fact, from the map). Of the 73 papers whose device setup could be established, 26 evaluate on exactly one physical CXL device (TPP, Demystifying-CXL MICRO'23, CXL-Interplay DAC'25, Real-world-CXL IPDPS'25, M5, Oasis SOSP'25, SoarAlto OSDI'25, NeoMem, TraCT, IKS, Samsung CMM-H, ADMS'24/'25 bandwidth-expansion studies, Pasha CIDR'25, ...), and a further 22 use no physical CXL at all (13 NUMA-emulated incl. Pond, Colloid, MTTM, Equilibria-eval; 9 on Optane/RDMA incl. Memtis, HeMem, TMTS, ArtMem — ArtMem's "CXL" framing was corrected to Optane-PM during verification). Real multi-device work is a small minority: 10 pooled + 7 independent + 7 mixed.

I2 — "Multi-device papers fuse devices into one pooled NUMA node": SUPPORTED (map-audit agent verdict). All 10 multi-device-pooled papers fuse devices and manage the pool as a unit: Micron/Intel (8× CZ122 → one NUMA node), Memstrata/Flat-Memory-Mode (3 Astera cards, cacheline-interleaved, hardware-managed), Beluga (32 devices behind 2 XConn switches, hint-free 2 MB software interleave), Vistara ISCA'26 (2 ASICs, 256 B interleave), CXL-SpecKV (4 FPGAs pooled), Mercury, MIKU, PolarDB-CXL SIGMOD'25 (8 modules, flat devdax), Duhu, No-Atomics-RAG. None makes per-device decisions.

I3 — "Nobody measures CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference / per-device tails on one host": SUPPORTED with one nuance (fact from per-paper extraction; final adversarial web sweep skipped on request). CXL-Interplay — the self-described first real-hardware CXL interference study — has exactly one device per testbed and lists "interference between multiple CXL devices" verbatim as never tested. Melody characterizes 4 real devices individually (incl. µs-level tail latencies "even under low device bandwidth utilization") but hosts them on different servers, excludes tiering/multi-device management/QoS by design, and explicitly punts tail mitigation to "system designers". SupMario hardware-interleaves two CXL-D units for bandwidth (CXL-D*) but never studies contention between independently-managed devices. Nuance: interference between hosts sharing one pooled device is partially covered (Characterizing Multi-Host CXL 2.0, DaMoN'26 — low confidence; CSFQ-transport NSDI'26 for switched pools), which is the inverse of your setting (one host, many devices).

I4 — "Treating each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource is open": PARTIALLY SUPPORTED (map-audit agent verdict — the strongest and most nuanced result of the review). On a single host with direct-attached commodity ASIC expanders, no published system does per-device placement/QoS/tenant assignment. But four near-misses qualify the claim and must be cited: (1) CSFQ-inspired transport (NSDI'26) does per-memory-channel ("mchannel") admission/rate control — genuine per-device QoS, but at the transport layer of a switched multi-host pool, not host-side placement of direct-attached expanders; (2) Weisgut et al. (VLDB'25) place DB operators across 4 Seagate CMA FPGA blades exposed as separate NUMA nodes — real per-device placement, but static, FPGA-based, no interference/QoS/tails, database-operator-specific; (3) CXL-CCL (arXiv'26) manages 6 Micron CZ120s behind an XConn switch with per-device awareness — but for GPU collective communication scheduling, not memory placement/QoS; (4) Liaw & Chen (arXiv'25) Config B exposes 2 SMART AICs as separate NUMA nodes with a tensor-level allocator — single-tenant fine-tuning, no contention management. RamRyder (OSDI'26, embargoed) advertises "channel-level page mapping" and could not be verified. Inference: the specific combination — ≥2 direct-attached commodity ASIC expanders on one host, managed independently, driven by measured cross-device interference and per-device tails, under multi-tenancy — has no occupant in 97 verified records.

I5 — differentiation from the named systems (fact per paper, inference in the aggregate): Pond = VM-granular pooling economics, remote-socket emulation, 0 devices; TPP = kernel hotness tiering into one CXL node, emulated performance evaluation; Memtis = Optane, page classification; Nomad = migration mechanism (1 FPGA + emulation); Colloid = loaded-latency balancing across two tiers, 0 devices; Memstrata = multi-tenant isolation above a hardware-fused 3-card pool; CXL-Interplay = single-device interference characterization; Melody = multi-device characterization without management; Clio = RDMA-era hardware co-design, 0 CXL; ObjPlace (your own) = object-granular sensitivity placement onto a single CXL node. None schedules multiple real CXL devices as first-class resources; your direction composes with, rather than collides with, all of them.

Section 2 — Gap analysis (12 categories)

Format per category: why it matters → who is closest → why unsolved → does the 4× CZ120 testbed give an edge → publishability → core research question (RQ) → needed contribution. "Fact" = from verified records; "Inference" = my judgment.

1. CXL performance characterization

Crowded at single-device; open at multi-device. Single-device characterization is saturated (Demystifying-CXL MICRO'23; Melody ASPLOS'25; SupMario arXiv'24; Real-world-CXL IPDPS'25; Samsung CMM-H arXiv'25; CXL-Interplay DAC'25; ADMS'24/'25). Melody even quantifies device-population diversity and µs tails. Emulation error is itself documented (MICRO'23; Melody shows emulation misses tails). Open (fact): no characterization of several identical commodity ASIC devices on one host under concurrent load — load-induced asymmetry, per-device tail divergence, root-decoder interleave vs. independent-node tradeoffs. Edge: yes — this is exactly your hardware; nobody with 4 identical CZ120s on one Turin host has published it. Publishability (inference): characterization alone → workshop/second-tier or a strong motivation section; not a standalone OSDI paper. RQ: how do nominally identical CXL devices diverge under load, and how much performance is lost by fusing them? Contribution: the measurement study that motivates the system below.

2. Multi-device CXL behavior

The central gap. Partial: Micron 8-device (pooled only, R/W-mix bandwidth curves), Melody (4 devices, separate hosts), Weisgut VLDB'25 (4 FPGA blades, static DB placement), SupMario (2-device hardware interleave), Vistara ISCA'26 (2 ASICs fused at 256 B). Why unsolved (fact): every one either fuses devices or measures them in isolation; none manages them. Edge: yes, uniquely — 4 identical direct-attached ASICs, de-interleavable (pending the decoder-lock gate from session 10). Publishability (inference): high as the empirical core of a system paper. RQ: what does an OS/runtime gain by seeing N devices instead of one pool? Contribution: per-device placement/migration machinery + evidence that pooling wastes isolatable bandwidth.

3. CXL-to-CXL interference

Verifiably unmeasured on real hardware (fact; I3). CXL-Interplay covers CXL↔DRAM/SSD on one device and names device-level regulation as future work; MT2/Memstrata regulate tenants above one pool; multi-host DaMoN'26 covers the inverse topology. Why unsolved: requires ≥2 independently-addressable real devices on one host — rare hardware. Edge: yes — the decisive victim-on-device-A/aggressor-on-device-B experiment (disjoint cores) is runnable only on setups like yours. Publishability (inference): the headline experiment of the direction; as characterization + mitigation system it clears top-venue novelty. RQ: do co-located CXL devices interfere (shared root ports/IOMMU/host buffers), and can placement neutralize it? Contribution: interference matrix + a placement/QoS layer exploiting it.

4. CXL memory placement and scheduling

Crowded at page-hotness; open at device granularity. Kernel tiering: TPP, Memtis, Colloid, Nomad, M5, HybridTier ASPLOS'25, PACT ASPLOS'26 (criticality-first), TierBPF, ArtMem (PM). Object-level: SoarAlto OSDI'25, OBASE OSDI'26, Clove arXiv'26 (managed runtimes), X-Mem, your ObjPlace. Weighted interleave (kernel MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE, HMSDK) is static and ratio-based. Why unsolved (fact): all target which tier, none target which device; ADMS'25 shows no static interleave ratio is workload-optimal (optima shift up to 88%) and leaves the adaptive runtime unbuilt. Edge: yes — ObjPlace extends naturally from tier-choice to device-choice. Publishability (inference): strong if device-awareness demonstrably beats both pooling and best-static-interleave. RQ: is device-granular, sensitivity-aware placement a new useful control knob between "one node" and "page hotness"? Contribution: a device-aware planner + bounded migration, evaluated against TPP/Memtis/Colloid/SoarAlto-class baselines.

5. QoS and isolation

Active in 2026 but device-blind. Memstrata (page coloring + slowdown estimator above a fused pool), MTTM EuroSys'26 (fast-tier bandwidth partitioning, emulated), Equilibria arXiv'26 (Meta-scale fairness, CXL as one node), Mercury (QoS-aware tiering, pooled), CSFQ NSDI'26 (per-mchannel rate control, switched pools), CXL-Interplay's H2 proposal (device-side MBA-like regulation — proposal only). Why unsolved (fact): nobody assigns tenants to devices or isolates via device-level placement on direct-attached hardware. Edge: yes — 4 devices = a natural isolation domain per tenant; your 2.0–2.13× contention number is the motivating threat. Publishability (inference): high; "device = isolation unit" is a crisp, defensible thesis. RQ: can per-device tenant assignment beat page-coloring/throttling for CXL bandwidth isolation? Contribution: a multi-tenant scheduler with per-device SLOs + comparison against Memstrata-style coloring and MTTM-style partitioning.

6. Tail latency and predictable performance

Documented, unmitigated. Melody: some devices show µs tails even at low utilization; CPUs/MCs "need" fixes; mitigation punted. LiteSwitch OSDI'26 (embargoed, low conf): harvests sub-µs CXL stalls via context switching — tolerates, does not prevent. ADMS'25: loaded CXL latency inflates up to 5× with prefetching. Open (fact): tail-aware placement/steering across devices; per-device tail telemetry feeding policy. Edge: yes if your CZ120s exhibit tail asymmetry under load (must measure first — go/no-go). Publishability (inference): strong differentiator inside a placement/QoS paper; standalone only if tails prove dramatic. RQ: are CXL tails device-local and load-dependent, and can a runtime route latency-critical objects around them? Contribution: lightweight tail monitor + tail-aware object routing.

7. OS/runtime support

Kernel sees tiers, not devices (fact). Linux: one node per region, demotion/promotion (TPP-lineage), weighted interleave by node; TierBPF adds eBPF admission control; NEMO OSDI'26 adds observability (1 FPGA device); OBASE re-engineers address spaces for tiering. Nothing exposes per-device bandwidth/tail state or a device-placement API. Edge: partial — you can prototype in user space (ObjPlace) without kernel surgery; the famfs/devdax path is a differentiator but gated on de-interleaving. Publishability (inference): the runtime/API is the vehicle, not the headline. RQ: what minimal OS/runtime interface makes devices schedulable (per-device counters, placement hints, migration)? Contribution: the ObjPlace extension + a device-state telemetry layer.

8. Multi-tenant systems

All existing multi-tenant CXL work shares one pool (fact): Memstrata (VM-granular, fused pool), MTTM (emulated fast-tier bandwidth), Equilibria (fairness on one node, production), TMTS (Optane, fleet SLOs), vTMM (VM tiering). Open: tenant-to-device mapping, device-level fairness, harvesting idle devices. Edge: yes — with 4 devices you can run 2–4 real tenants with disjoint or shared device sets and measure isolation directly; your prior gap (synthetic-aggressor-only evidence) is fixable here. Publishability (inference): high — multi-tenant + real hardware + device granularity is an unoccupied intersection. RQ: device-partitioned vs. shared-interleaved multi-tenancy: when does each win? Contribution: tenant scheduler + real co-located workloads (not synthetic aggressors).

9. Application-level memory tiering

Object-level placement is now a crowded lane (fact): SoarAlto (profile-guided AOL), OBASE, Clove (managed runtimes), Liaw&Chen (tensor classes), CachedArrays-style hints, your ObjPlace. Open: none of them is device-aware or contention-driven; Clove/OBASE confirm the community is converging on objects — the window for "object-level" alone is closing (inference: do not lead with it). Edge: only when combined with per-device signals. RQ: what object-level signals (bandwidth-sensitivity, phase, tail-tolerance) map objects to devices, not just tiers? Contribution: ObjPlace-on-N-devices with an INTERLEAVE candidate in the planner.

10. Application domains (DB / KV / ML / graph)

DB: most active real-hardware community (Weisgut VLDB'25 — 4 independent FPGA blades; PolarDB SIGMOD'25 industrial 8-module pool; SAP-HANA VLDB'24; hash joins CIDR'26; DaMoN/ADMS series; SIDLE VLDB'26 indexes; Pasha CIDR'25; three-tier buffer manager HardBD'24). Placement is static and single-workload; no DB paper manages contention across devices. LLM/KV: pool-substrate systems (Beluga 32-dev switch pool, hint-free; TraCT Niagara FPGA; CXL-SpecKV FPGA NDP; SAC, ITME, 1M-token PNM, MoE-NDP — mostly simulated or switch-dependent); none is a commodity direct-attach serving tier, echoing your earlier KV null-result caution. RecSys/vector/graph: CARINA SIGMOD'25 (low conf, likely single-tenant), ReCXL/CLAY/PIFS-Rec/CENT (NDP silicon), Cosmos/FaTRQ/CXL-ANNS (sim/older). Edge: your GNN/embedding + membench workloads suffice; a DB co-tenant (e.g., a join-heavy analytical engine) would strengthen the multi-tenant story at low cost. Publishability (inference): domain papers are follow-ons; cite heavily, do not compete on their turf yet.

11. CXL memory pooling and disaggregation

Hot and hyperscaler-heavy (fact): OSDI'26 alone has RamRyder, Blowfish, MDK, Duhu, MEGALON, FORGE; plus Oasis SOSP'25, Pond, DirectCXL, Octopus NSDI'26, PIPM/Cxlalloc/CXL0 ASPLOS'26, Aquifer, DPC. All are switch/multi-host/pool-fabric problems needing hardware you lack (switches, multi-host). Edge: none — avoid head-on. Relevance: cite as the "other topology"; your single-host many-device setting is the complementary, unoccupied cell of the matrix.

12. Reliability, failure handling, thermal, power

Thin but present: ReScue HPCA'26 (RAS), AIORE MICRO'25 (encryption), Space-Control (security isolation, sim), Towards-CXL-Resilience arXiv'26, Where-are-the-Joules HCDS'25 (energy), GPF position paper. Melody hints thermal throttling as a tail cause (unresolved). Edge: weak — CZ120s are volatile, no persistence story (session-9 conclusion stands). One cheap novel datum (inference): per-device power/thermal vs. tail correlation on 4 identical devices would be new empirics, but a garnish, not a paper.

Section 3 — Prior-work comparison (7 categories)

(1) Single-device characterization — done: latency/bandwidth/app-impact on every commercial device class (MICRO'23, IPDPS'25, CMM-H, DAC'25, ADMS). Open: same-host multi-device dynamics. (2) Multi-device / pooling — done: fused-pool bandwidth (Micron 8-dev), switch pools (Beluga, DirectCXL, Octopus, CSFQ), hardware-fused tiering (Memstrata, Vistara). Open: host-managed independent devices. (3) Placement systems — done: page-hotness kernel tiering (TPP→PACT lineage), loaded-latency balancing (Colloid), object/profile-guided (SoarAlto, OBASE, Clove). Open: device-granular placement; adaptive interleaving (ADMS'25's unbuilt runtime). (4) QoS/interference — done: single-device interference taxonomy (CXL-Interplay), pool-level tenant isolation (Memstrata), fast-tier partitioning (MTTM), switched-pool transport QoS (CSFQ). Open: device-level isolation on direct-attach; tail-aware QoS (Melody's punt). (5) Non-CXL heterogeneous memory — Memtis/HeMem/TMTS/vTMM/Colloid/ArtMem built the tiering toolbox on Optane/NUMA; all treat the slow tier as one homogeneous sink; their page-classification machinery is reusable but their contention models do not transfer (your DRAM-vs-CXL victim asymmetry is the proof). (6) Simulation/RDMA disaggregation — LegoOS/AIFM/Clio defined APIs and latency-hiding for µs-scale far memory; CXL's ns-scale, load/store semantics invalidate their core overhead assumptions; cite as lineage, evaluate against none. Simulators (CXL-DMSim, Cohet, Cylon, Xerxes, CXLMemSim) now exist with silicon validation — useful for sensitivity studies, but the field's credibility currency is real silicon (Melody's tails are invisible to all of them). (7) DB/application CXL — the DB community has the most real-hardware deployments (incl. the only 4-independent-node testbed, Weisgut) but stops at static placement; application papers (CARINA, Beluga, IKS) each hard-code one workload's policy. Open everywhere: multi-workload contention on shared devices.

Section 4 — Preliminary opportunity assessment for the 4× CZ120 testbed

Tier 1 (strongest, do first): device-granular interference + tail characterization → per-device placement/QoS system. Fact base: I3/I4 verdicts; CXL-Interplay's explicit non-coverage; Melody's punted mitigation; Memstrata/MTTM/Equilibria all device-blind; CSFQ occupies only the switched-transport corner; Weisgut only static DB placement on FPGAs. Your edge is literal: the required hardware exists on your desk and in almost nobody else's published testbed. The 2026-06-12 staged plan (de-interleave gate → cross-device characterization → ObjPlace extension → real two-tenant evaluation vs. TPP/Memtis/Colloid/SoarAlto) remains exactly right, now backed by 97 verified records instead of 25. Risks unchanged: the decoder-lock/sudo gate (go/no-go), and the possibility that devices are perfectly isolated and tails are stable — in which case pivot to the FAST/famfs angle.

Tier 2 (supporting theses inside the same paper): (a) adaptive per-workload/per-phase interleaving — ADMS'25 proves static ratios fail by up to 88% and leaves the runtime unbuilt; an INTERLEAVE candidate in ObjPlace's planner covers your known uniform-access weakness; (b) tenant-to-device isolation vs. page coloring — a head-to-head against Memstrata-style coloring is a clean, winnable experiment; (c) tail-aware routing if (and only if) your tail measurements corroborate Melody on CZ120s.

Tier 3 (avoid): switch-pool/multi-host anything (no hardware; OSDI'26 is saturating it), KV-cache-on-CXL substrates (Beluga/TraCT own it; your layer-split null result stands), NDP/PNM (custom silicon), object-level tiering as a standalone claim (SoarAlto/OBASE/Clove close the window), crash-consistency/GPF (volatile devices), pure characterization (Melody exists).

Bottom line (inference): the review upgrades the 2026-06-12 recommendation from "verifiably open per a 25-source scan" to "verifiably open per 97 primary-source records with adversarial verification": sensitivity- and device-aware placement and movement of memory objects across DRAM and multiple real CXL devices, with per-device QoS for multi-tenant workloads — with CSFQ (NSDI'26), Weisgut (VLDB'25), CXL-CCL, Equilibria, and MTTM as the must-cite adjacent ring, and the decoder de-interleave as the sole hardware gate.

Interference, QoS & multi-tenant isolation (5 papers)

The QoS/multi-tenant frontier: everything here regulates tenants above ONE pool (Memstrata via page coloring, MTTM via emulated fast-tier bandwidth partitioning, Equilibria via fairness on a single Meta production node, Mercury via pooled tiering) or characterizes single-device interference (CXL-Interplay). The lone per-device QoS mechanism (CSFQ transport, NSDI'26) lives in the switched-pool transport layer. Device-level isolation on direct-attached expanders is unoccupied.

Building A CSFQ-Inspired Transport for Switched CXL Memory Pooling

NSDI (2026) real-asic multi-device-independent verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. unknown (memory chassis "holds up to 12 vendor-agnostic CXL DIMMs" [Micron/SMART Modular referenced]; populated count never stated; rDIMM-to-rDIMM experiments imply >=2; plus 1 XConn B2 Apollo CXL switch ASIC and ASIC host/endpoint adapters)

Problem. In switched CXL memory pooling, the host-core-to-remote-DIMM data path (cache hierarchy, host adapter, CXL switch, endpoint adapter, rDIMM) is shared by concurrent intra- and inter-host memory streams with no explicit performance control, causing severe, unpredictable interference. Characterization identifies three issues: (1) intra-host contention (FlexBus/M2PCIe queueing up to 13.4x latency increase, 50.1ns to 669.4ns; host adapter's best-effort VL FLIT scheduling cuts a victim stream's bandwidth 82.6% and inflates latency 230.2ns to 1624.2ns); (2) in-fabric congestion (credit-based hop-by-hop flow control causes HoL blocking; a 64B request interleaved with 4KB requests sees 6.9x/3.8x read/write slowdown; a 4KB stream grabs 97.8% of bandwidth vs a 64B stream); (3) unmanaged host-rDIMM interaction from hardware prefetching that transparently over-subscribes the endpoint link.

Idea. MemChannel: a transport layer over the CXL.mem fabric with an "mchannel" abstraction — a managed memory channel between a host core Ci and a specific remote CXL DIMM rDIMMj with performance attributes. A Sender-Driven Fabric-Informed protocol inspired by Core-Stateless Fair Queueing (CSFQ) admits just enough CXL loads/stores per mchannel based on estimated Core<->rDIMM bandwidth availability: time-based rate control via POSIX-timer suspension of application execution, host-side admission control, cross-host bookkeeping through shared CXL memory, fluid-model rate estimation from performance counters (OCR events, CHA occupancy, rdpmc), device-internal load as a congestion signal, and delay-based link-capacity probing. Extensions cover weighted fairness and multi-layer switching. Fair-share calculation runs once per 100us scheduling window.

Testbed. Real switched CXL pool: XConn Titan evaluation platform — ASIC host and endpoint adapters; XConn B2 Apollo CXL switch (XC50256 crossbar) with 14 upstream/downstream ports, 2 CEM slots, 128 CXL 2.0 lanes (x2/x4/x8 bifurcation); MCIO/EDSFF memory chassis holding up to 12 vendor-agnostic CXL DIMMs (E1.S/E3.S/E3.L; Micron and SMART Modular CXL modules cited); MX8 fabric-manager board. Hosts: 2U Intel Emerald Rapids servers, each 2x Xeon Gold 6530 (32 cores @2.1GHz, 160MB LLC), 1536GB DDR5, one CXL host adapter with two x8 CXL ports, Ubuntu 24.04. MLC baseline to the pool: 220.5ns latency, 47.2GB/s per server. Each rDIMM is exposed via driver as HDM / a CPUless NUMA node. DIMM capacity, vendor model, populated count, and host count not stated.

Baselines. (1) Same testbed without MemChannel (w/o MC) throughout; (2) TPP (Linux tiered-memory page promotion/demotion) under varying background traffic with local:remote capacity ratio 8:1. No comparison to other QoS/isolation systems (e.g., no Memstrata, no CXL-QoS hardware knobs).

Key findings. Characterization: switched-pool access 220.5ns/47.2GB/s per server; FlexBus congestion inflates CXL latency up to 13.4x (50.1 to 669.4ns); LLC/CHA contention up to 2.9x; host-adapter VL scheduling cuts victim bandwidth 82.6% (230.2 to 1624.2ns) at ~30% load; 64B vs 4KB interleaving gives 6.9x/3.8x slowdowns and 97.8% bandwidth capture by the large stream; prefetch-driven strided access oversubscribes the endpoint (read bandwidth 21.9 to 12.8GB/s at stride 8). MemChannel results: applications gain 2-3.5x; Silo avg CXL latency reduced 28.6-38.4% and tail 5.8-29.3% across YCSB; 225.3 MOPS / 41.0GB/s with two adapters at 64 threads; inter-host slowdown cut from 7.2x/4.2x to 3.1x/2.1x (BC/PR) at 100% contending load with 97.2% bandwidth utilization; fairness gap between co-located apps shrinks (8.9 to 1.3GB/s Bwaves+Silo, 11.5 to 1.8GB/s Bwaves+MICA, 3.1 to 0.1GB/s MICA+Silo); vs TPP: TPP 1.2x better uncontended, MemChannel 1.5x throughput and 43% latency win under heavy contention; overhead 1.7% (POSIX-timer interrupts, 100us window).

Limitations. Authors' own (Section 5.7 Discussion): evaluated only at rack scale on a single switch, not cluster-scale scale-out fabrics with PBR; relies on device-internal load + end-to-end delay rather than explicit in-fabric congestion notification; does not yet handle CXL bundled ports. Evident from methodology: the testbed table omits the populated CXL DIMM count, DIMM vendor/model/capacity, and the number of host servers used — key reproducibility gaps; rate control throttles applications by suspending execution via POSIX timers (coarse, CPU-time-costing mechanism); only TPP as an external baseline (no comparison to other bandwidth/QoS isolation systems); hosts are Intel-only (EMR counter-based estimation, CHA/OCR events, may not port to AMD like the requester's EPYC Turin); characterization single-switch, ASIC adapters proprietary (root causes established partly "by discussing with the device vendor"); no LLM/ML workloads.

Open questions. Multi-tier/PBR cluster-scale CXL fabrics; explicit congestion notification support in switches; CXL bundled ports; portability of the counter-based rate estimation to non-Intel CPUs; interaction with tiering/placement systems beyond the TPP comparison (they explicitly position mchannels as complementary to tiered/object memory systems); direct-attached (non-switched) multi-device contention is out of scope; how weights map to SLO-level tail-latency guarantees.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant to the requester's candidate direction: MemChannel's core abstraction is a per-(core, individual rDIMM) channel with per-device load signals and admission control — i.e., NSDI'26-level validation that treating each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource pays off. Its Issue #1 (intra-host contention: adapter/port FLIT scheduling agnostic to request streams, victim bandwidth -82.6%) and Issue #3 (prefetch-induced oversubscription) occur before any switch and plausibly transfer to the requester's direct-attached 4x CZ120 box, echoing their measured 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdown; the in-fabric switch congestion findings do not apply. Differentiation room for the requester: no switch needed, AMD EPYC instead of Intel EMR (their CHA/OCR-counter rate estimation is Intel-specific), object-granularity placement (ObjPlace) + per-device scheduling rather than execution-throttling admission control, and LLM workloads which MemChannel never touches. MemChannel is now the closest related work for any 'CXL bandwidth QoS/fair sharing' claim and must be cited and contrasted (transport/throttling vs placement).

Could not verify. Exact number of CXL DIMMs populated in the chassis during evaluation (only 'holds up to 12' is stated; rDIMM-to-rDIMM experiments imply at least 2), CXL DIMM vendor model and per-DIMM capacity (only generic Micron/SMART Modular product-page citations), Exact number of host servers used in the multi-host experiments, Linux kernel version (only Ubuntu 24.04 stated)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We take the recent Titan platform [18] from the XConn Tech as the evaluation target (Figure 1-b). It consists of (1) ASIC-based host and endpoint adapters; (2) an XConn B2 Apollo CXL switch with 14 upstream and downstream ports, 2 CEM [1] slots, and 128 CXL2.0 lanes, supporting ×2, ×4, and ×8 bifurcations; (3) a memory chassis using an MCIO/EDSFF backplane that holds up to 12 vendor-agnostic CXL DIMMs [8, 12] under the compact EDSFF E1.S, E3.S, and E3.L form factors [7]; and (4) an MX8 board operating as the management host and fabric manager.
  • Experimental Methodology. We use 2U Intel Emerald Rapids servers as hosts, where each contains two Xeon Gold 6530 CPUs, 1536GB DDR5 memory, and two Samsung 9MA3 960GB NVMe drives. Each processor has 32 cores running at 2.1GHz and 160MB LLC. All the hosts run Ubuntu 24.04. Each server is equipped with one CXL host adapter enclosing two ×8 CXL ports. Using Intel MLC [5], we observe that the server achieves 220.5ns and 47.2GB/s when accessing the switched CXL memory pool.
  • a device driver that makes remote memory as host-managed device memory (HDM) and exposes it as a CPUless NUMA node. The host OS fabricates an attached CXL DIMM as a memory node
  • A mchannel, established between a host core (Ci ) and a CXL DIMM (rDIMM j ), is associated
  • We intermix small and large memory requests and deploy memory streams in two directions: host→rDIMM and rDIMM→rDIMM.
  • When the adapter is nearly saturated, we observe that in the Read(T1)-Read(T2) case, the victim stream’s bandwidth is reduced by 82.6%, with latencies increased from 230.2ns to 1624.2ns.
  • Second, MemChannel is evaluated at rack scale rather than at cluster scale. It will be interesting to explore how MemChannel operates in a scale-out CXL fabric with PBR.

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/nsdi26-guo-zerui.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/nsdi26/presentation/guo-zerui https://cyrus-guo.com/publications/ https://pages.cs.wisc.edu/~mgliu/papers.html

Equilibria: Fair Multi-Tenant CXL Memory Tiering At Scale

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2602.08800); from CMU + Meta (Zhao, Gholkar, Maruf, Dhanotia, Weiner, Price, Skarlatos et al.) (2026 (v1 2026-02-09, v2 2026-04-20)) real-asic unknown verify: corrected confidence: high

CXL devices. unknown — exact count of physical expanders per server never stated; two testbeds (large: 256GB CXL, small: 64GB CXL); production servers use "a single x8 or x16 PCIe controller to connect CXL memory", suggesting one expander per server

Problem. Existing OS memory tiering for CXL (TPP/upstream Linux) is single-tenant-oriented: in multi-tenant container colocation it lacks per-container fair-share controls, causes fairness violations and performance variability (noisy neighbors thrashing pages between tiers), and offers insufficient per-container observability of tiered-memory usage for diagnosing issues at datacenter scale.

Idea. Equilibria, an OS (Linux kernel) framework for fair multi-tenant CXL tiering: per-container "lower protection" (local memory protected from demotion) and "upper bound" (max local memory before forced demotion) knobs; regulated demotion (containers under protection exempt; overage-proportional scanning d_scan = n_lru(n_cgroup - n_protection)/n_cgroup) and regulated promotion (fourth-power throttle p_scan = p_base * min(n_protection^4/n_cgroup^4, 1/16)); thrashing suppression via a hash-table sample of recently promoted pages with adaptive promotion-rate halving/doubling every 5s; new per-cgroup tier-usage and promotion/demotion counters for observability. Patches partially upstreamed to Linux; being deployed fleet-wide at Meta.

Testbed. Two Meta production-class single-socket AMD Turin-generation servers with real CXL 2.0 Type 3 memory expanders built from recycled decommissioned DDR4 DRAM. Large server: 768GB local DDR5 + 256GB DDR4-as-CXL (3:1 ratio), unloaded CXL latency 252ns. Small server: 256GB local DDR5 + 64GB DDR4-as-CXL. CXL presented as memory-only NUMA domain(s); production connects CXL via a single x8 or x16 PCIe controller. Linux kernel 6.11 base.

Evaluation. Three Meta production workloads — Cache (replayed production traces, 2 colocated instances), Web (application server, live production requests, 5 instances across two partitions), CI (build/test service, live, 4 instances/server) — plus DCPerf benchmarks SparkBench and TaoBench (3 TaoBench + 1 SparkBench colocated). Multi-tenant colocation on the small and large CXL servers; validation microbenchmarks with 3 instances per container setup.

Baselines. Upstream Linux kernel memory tiering derived from TPP (the state-of-the-art Linux solution). No direct comparison against Memtis, Pond, Memstrata, or other research tiering systems.

Key findings. Up to 52% performance improvement over Linux/TPP on production workloads and 1.7x on benchmarks (SparkBench 11.2 vs 4.2 queries/hour). For colocated Cache instances, partition B's average P99 latency drops 47% and A's 52% vs Linux; Web partition B gains 8% throughput. Fairness controls eliminate noisy-neighbor thrashing between colocated containers. Production apps use only 1-3 GB/s of CXL bandwidth, so the design targets capacity fairness, not bandwidth arbitration. Patches shared with Linux community, subset accepted upstream; fleet-wide Meta deployment underway.

Limitations. Authors admit no quantitative evaluation of the observability component, and that current CPUs/memory hardware lack support for low-overhead page migration/hotness tracking. Methodology weaknesses: number of physical CXL devices never stated and per-device behavior never examined — CXL is treated as one opaque capacity tier; bandwidth contention on the CXL link is explicitly declared out of scope ("additional memory bandwidth from CXL memory has limited practical use in current deployments") and no bandwidth-interference scenarios are evaluated; CXL media is recycled DDR4 (Meta-specific cost model, not commodity CZ120-class DDR5 expanders); max colocation tested is ~4-5 instances; only TPP/Linux as baseline.

Open questions. Bandwidth-driven inter-tenant interference on CXL (the paper measures only 1-3 GB/s CXL use and punts on bandwidth arbitration entirely); per-device or multi-device CXL management and cross-device interference; hardware-assisted hotness tracking/migration (stated as future vision); fairness for bandwidth-bound rather than capacity-bound workloads; behavior beyond ~4-5 colocated containers; VM (non-container) tenancy.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant as the closest competitor: same host CPU family (AMD Turin, like the requester's EPYC 9745) with real CXL 2.0 expanders, page-granularity kernel tiering with per-container fairness — but it treats CXL as a single opaque capacity tier, never states its device count, and explicitly dismisses CXL bandwidth as unimportant (production apps draw 1-3 GB/s). The requester's own data (per-region ~35.5 GiB/s saturating at 4 threads, 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown under a CXL co-runner) directly contradicts that dismissal for bandwidth-bound workloads, and Equilibria evaluates no bandwidth-interference or per-device scenarios. This leaves the requester's candidate direction — per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and bandwidth-aware multi-tenant QoS on 4 independent CZ120s — clearly open; ObjPlace's object-granularity, bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement is also complementary to Equilibria's capacity-fairness page tiering. Positioning must, however, contend with Equilibria's fleet-scale deployment story and its noisy-neighbor/thrashing-suppression framing for the capacity dimension.

Could not verify. Exact number of physical CXL expander devices per evaluation server (never stated in paper), Whether CXL is exposed as one or multiple NUMA nodes on the testbeds (paper says 'memory-only NUMA domains' generically), CXL link width used on the evaluation servers specifically (x8/x16 quote is about production fleet)

Verifier note. CORRECTED deviceTreatment: CXL memory is presented as a NUMA node separate from local memory (all CXL capacity appears as one memory-only CXL NUMA node vs. the local-DRAM node); per-device treatment (whether one or multiple expanders back that node) is not stated because device count is never given. Primary source read directly this session via arXiv abstract page and full v2 HTML (three extraction passes). All analyst quotes verified verbatim except one trivial discrepancy: paper reads "So, we continue to use a single x8 or x16 PCIe controller..." while the analyst wrote "Therefore, we continue...". The only substantive fix: deviceTreatment is not fully unknown — the paper states CXL memory is presented as a NUMA node separate from local memory; only the per-physical-device count/mapping is unstated. realCXL: paper never uses the word "ASIC", but devices are real production Meta CXL 2.0 Type 3 expanders (recycled DDR4), explicitly contrasted with NUMA-based emulation, so "real-asic" (in the real-vs-FPGA-vs-emulated taxonomy) is supported.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We performed experiments on two types of Meta servers equipped with CXL memory, denoted as small and large servers. Both are single-socket servers with CPUs of the AMD Turin generation. The large server features a local to CXL memory ratio of 3:1, including 768GB of local DDR5 memory and 256GB of DDR4 DRAM as CXL memory. It has an unloaded CXL memory access latency of 252ns.
  • The CXL memory expanders used in the evaluation are CXL 2.0 Type 3 devices, using recycled previously-decommissioned DDR4 memory.
  • Therefore, we continue to use a single x8 or x16 PCIe controller to connect CXL memory across several generations of servers.
  • CXL memory is attached to a host system, where it is presented as memory-only NUMA domains.
  • Equilibria improves performance over the state-of-the-art Linux solution, TPP, by up to 52% and 1.7× for production workloads and benchmarks, respectively.
  • additional memory bandwidth from CXL memory has limited practical use in current deployments
  • Although we do not provide a quantitative evaluation of the container-level observability of memory tiering provided by Equilibria, most of the evaluation and motivation studies in this paper are only possible because of it.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08800 https://arxiv.org/html/2602.08800v2

MTTM: Dynamic Fast Memory Partitioning with Bandwidth Optimization for Multi-tenant Cloud

EuroSys 2026 (21st European Conference on Computer Systems); acceptance rate 16.9%. Authors: Changjun Lee, Sangjin Choi, Youngjin Kwon (KAIST, CASYS lab). DOI 10.1145/3767295.3769371. (2026) numa-emulated n/a verify: confirmed confidence: medium

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; slow tier = remote NUMA socket DRAM with bandwidth-throttling-based latency emulation)

Problem. In multi-tenant tiered-memory clouds, prior work optimizes page movement between tiers but leaves the distribution of the fast tier's (local DRAM) capacity among competing tenants underexplored. Bandwidth utilization is critically impacted by "memory request rejection," which depends on how local DRAM is partitioned across tenants — bad partitioning wastes fast-tier bandwidth and hurts aggregate performance.

Idea. MTTM, a Linux-kernel tiered memory system (CONFIG_MTTM, Linux 5.15.145) that implements DRAM-sensitivity-based partitioning of fast memory among tenants: it allocates fast-tier DRAM capacity according to each workload's characteristics (how much its performance depends on DRAM) and dynamically adjusts each tenant's local-DRAM allocation at runtime to mitigate memory request rejection and improve fast-tier bandwidth utilization.

Testbed. Two-socket server: 2x 24-core Intel Xeon Gold 5220R @ 2.20 GHz, 6x 32 GB DRAM per socket (192 GB/socket). Fast tier = local DRAM (socket 0); slow tier = remote DRAM on socket 1 posing as CXL memory (socket-1 CPUs removed via isolcpus=24-47). Slow-memory latency emulated by combining Quartz-style uncore-register (0x190) bandwidth throttling with intel-cmt-cat membw traffic saturation, calibrated with Intel MLC to a target idle latency (example: 300 ns). Kernel: Linux 5.15.145 with CONFIG_MTTM=y, NUMA_BALANCING off. No physical CXL, Optane, or FPGA device.

Evaluation. Nine cloud workloads run as co-located tenants (cgroup-based multi-tenant mixes plus single-tenant runs): GAPBS (pre-generated -g 28 graph), XSBench, Btree (vmitosis-workloads), X-Index with YCSB-generated data (5.9 GB load / 22 GB transaction), SPEC CPU 2017, CPU DLRM inference (ISCA'23 reproduction setup), Silo, and GUPS. Experiments sweep workload mixes, memory configurations (local:remote ratios), and remote-memory access latencies (figures 8-16 in artifact scripts); metrics include performance, bandwidth, and request-rejection ratio.

Baselines. Local (all-DRAM), static partitioning, Memstrata (policy emulation on the MTTM kernel), vTMM (emulation), Memtis (its own 5.15.19-htmm kernel), TPP and Colloid (Colloid 6.3.0 kernel).

Key findings. Per the abstract, MTTM shows clear performance improvements over state-of-the-art tiered-memory baselines (Memtis, TPP, Colloid, Memstrata/vTMM emulations, static) across various workload mixes, memory configurations, and remote-memory latencies, by partitioning fast-tier DRAM by DRAM-sensitivity and dynamically adjusting allocations to reduce memory request rejection. Exact speedup numbers could not be verified (paper text paywalled); the artifact confirms the full comparison matrix (Figs 8-16) exists and that Fig 11 alone takes several days to reproduce.

Limitations. No real CXL hardware at all: the slow tier is remote-socket DRAM with uncore bandwidth throttling + traffic saturation to hit a target latency. The authors' own emulation README admits: latency and bandwidth of slow memory cannot be controlled simultaneously; the PCIe throttle register value does not survive reboots and must be recalibrated each boot; validation on real hardware was done only on Optane DC (results not public), not CXL. Single emulated slow node — no notion of multiple slow-tier devices, no CXL-to-CXL interference. Baselines Memstrata and vTMM are re-implemented as policy emulations rather than the original systems. Kernel is dated (5.15.145). Xeon Gold 5220R (Cascade Lake) has no CXL support, so device-side behaviors (CXL controller queuing, on-device bandwidth ceilings) are absent from the study.

Open questions. Whether DRAM-sensitivity-based partitioning holds on real CXL silicon, where slow-tier bandwidth is per-device-limited (~35 GB/s per CZ120 region) rather than an emulated remote socket sharing the same memory controllers. Multi-device slow tiers (partitioning tenants across several CXL devices, cross-device interference) are entirely untested. Tail latency / QoS SLOs are not the stated focus (throughput/bandwidth-utilization oriented). Interaction with huge pages, GPU/LLM workloads, and >2-tier hierarchies unaddressed.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant as the closest recent multi-tenant fast-tier-partitioning competitor (EuroSys'26, same Memstrata lineage the requester tracks), but it partitions the DRAM tier, treats the slow tier as one homogeneous emulated node, and has zero real CXL hardware — its Cascade Lake + throttled-remote-socket emulation cannot reproduce the per-device 35.5 GiB/s ceilings and 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdowns the requester measured on real CZ120s. This leaves the requester's candidate direction (per-device CXL placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, tail-aware multi-tenant isolation across 4 independent devices) untouched: MTTM answers "how much DRAM does each tenant get" while the requester can answer "which CXL device does each tenant/object get." MTTM's DRAM-sensitivity metric and request-rejection signal are directly reusable ideas to compare against or extend in ObjPlace on real hardware, and MTTM is an obvious baseline to emulate (its kernel and scripts are public).

Could not verify. Full paper text (ACM DL returned 403; no arXiv preprint or author PDF found) — abstract details ('nine cloud workloads', 'Linux v5.15', DRAM-sensitivity partitioning, request rejection) come from search-result snippets of the ACM page, not text I fetched directly, Concrete speedup numbers vs baselines (keyFindings has no verified figures), Exact emulated CXL latency values used in the paper's evaluation (artifact example shows 300 ns target but paper settings unconfirmed), Whether the paper reports any validation on real CXL/Optane hardware beyond the artifact README's Optane remark

Verifier note. Paper full text is paywalled (ACM DL 403, no arXiv, no PDF on author pages), so verification of the testbed rests on the authors' official EuroSys'26 artifact-evaluation repo (github.com/casys-kaist/MTTM_ae_EuroSys26, default branch 'master'), which documents the paper's evaluation environment. That repo describes exactly one testbed: a two-socket Xeon Gold 5220R machine where socket 1's remote DRAM is treated as emulated CXL memory (isolcpus in GRUB) with Quartz-style bandwidth throttling. No physical CXL device/ASIC/FPGA is mentioned anywhere; the emulation tool explicitly assumes a two-socket system. I attempted to refute by hunting for a second real-CXL testbed and found none. Analyst's minor URL slip: raw README is on 'master', not 'main' (their /main/ path 404s), but content matches their quotes.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)

  • From scripts/README.md (Prepare baselines): 'To emulate NUMA node 1 as CXL memory, we add "isolcpus=24-47" to GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX in /etc/default/grub file.'
  • From scripts/cxl-emulation/README.md: 'This tool lets you emulate slow memory with memory bandwidth saturation and throttling. By slowing down the memory performance of one node in a two-socket system, it emulates a heterogeneous memory system.' and 'this tool has a limitation that the latency and bandwidth of slow memory cannot be controlled simultaneously.'
  • From scripts/README.md (baselines): 'Use MTTM kernel for local, MTTM, static, Memstrata policy emulation, vTMM emulation.' ... 'To run Memtis, reboot with the Memtis kernel, 5.15.19-htmm.' ... 'To run TPP and Colloid, reboot with the Colloid kernel, 6.3.0-colloid.'

CXL-Interplay: Unraveling and Characterizing CXL Interference in Modern Computer Systems (DAC'25); arXiv preprint titled "CXL-Interference: Analysis and Characterization in Modern Computer Systems" (2411.18308) is the same paper

DAC 2025 (62nd ACM/IEEE Design Automation Conference), DOI 10.1109/DAC63849.2025.11132607; arXiv:2411.18308 v1 2024-11-27 (2025) mixed single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. System A: 1 (Montage ASIC 64GB); System B: 1 (Intel Agilex FPGA 32GB DDR4-2400)

Problem. CXL memory coexists with main memory (MMEM) and storage (SSD) in real servers, but the mutual performance interference between CXL traffic and MMEM/SSD traffic — a shared-uncore "noisy neighbor" problem — is unstudied on real CXL hardware; prior CXL characterization work profiles CXL in isolation and simulators inject fixed delays that ignore interference.

Idea. Build CXL-Interplay: a configurable microbenchmark suite (ld/st/ntst via cxlMemTest crossed with FIO randread/randwrite) plus tailored real applications, run on two real CXL testbeds (one Montage ASIC device, one Intel Agilex FPGA device) to systematically map the CXL-vs-MMEM and CXL-vs-SSD interference matrix; perform reverse-reasoning root-cause analysis with uncore PMU counters (TOR occupancy/inserts, new CXL counters) and kernel-function profiling; then evaluate software mitigations (cgroups CPU quota, frequency scaling, Intel MBA) and discuss device-side hardware regulation.

Testbed. Two Intel Sapphire Rapids testbeds, each with exactly ONE CXL 1.1 memory expander attached to socket 0 as a CPU-less NUMA node 2. System A: 4th Gen Xeon Platinum 4.0 GHz, 48 physical cores/socket, 1TB 8-channel DDR5-4800 per socket, SAS SSD, Montage ASIC CXL device 64GB, XFS, CentOS 7. System B: 4th Gen Xeon Gold 4.0 GHz, 32 physical cores/socket, 256GB 8-channel DDR5-4800 per socket, NVMe PCIe 4.0 MLC SSD, Intel Agilex FPGA CXL device 32GB DDR4-2400, XFS, Ubuntu 22.04.4 LTS. Two CPU NUMA nodes joined by UPI; figure shows CXL 1.1 off a PCIe 5.0 port; link width and kernel versions not stated. Hyper-threading disabled, CPU locked to 2GHz for microbenchmarks.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: memory ops (load, store, non-temporal store via cxlMemTest) cross-run against storage ops (FIO libaio, iodepth 16, 4KB blocks, 20s randread/randwrite), sweeping all thread counts, main and interfering processes pinned to separate cores of the same NUMA node; case studies on sequential-vs-random SSD access and MOVDIR64B. Real applications in 4 interference types (A: filesystem apps under CXL traffic; B: CXL apps under SSD traffic; C: MMEM apps under CXL traffic; D: CXL apps under MMEM traffic): Filebench fileserver/varmail, RocksDB, MLPerf Storage, GAPBS PageRank, Redis (50/50 read-update), TinyLlama LLM inference. Root-cause via uncore PMU counters (unc_cha_tor_occupancy/inserts.ia_miss, cxlcm_txc_pack_buf_inserts.mem_data) and kernel-function profiling. Mitigation experiments: cgroups CPU-quota throttling, frequency scaling, Intel MBA bandwidth restriction.

Baselines. No system baselines (characterization paper). Positioned against prior characterization work in Table I: emulation-based works (Memtis, TPP-style, CXLMemSim), Sun et al. MICRO'23, Tang et al. EuroSys'24, Liu & Wang arXiv'24 — none of which study interference or regulation on real devices. Mitigation methods compared against each other: cgroups CPU quota vs frequency scaling vs Intel MBA (plus discussed-only: Intel CAT, AMD SMBA, device-side latency injection).

Key findings. (1) CXL non-temporal stores devastate co-running MMEM/SSD bandwidth: up to 93.2% degradation; CXL ld/st alone cause >40% MMEM interference on System B. (2) Root cause: CXL traffic congests the Table of Requests (TOR) between cores and LLC — unc_cha_tor_occupancy.ia_miss rises 4090x, inserts 155x, L2-miss latency 15.2x, while L3-miss latency stays ~1.01x. (3) SSD sequential writes suffer more than random (80.9% vs 54.5% loss) due to larger LLC/DDIO occupancy; MOVDIR64B interferes less than ntst. (4) Asymmetry: MMEM/SSD have mild impact on CXL (SSD up to only ~12%), and CXL st can even be boosted by MMEM traffic; RocksDB gains up to +21.2% under CXL ld background (24% fewer memory instructions). (5) Real apps: up to ~90% decline for filesystem apps under CXL ntst; GAPBS up to 20.8x slowdown under CXL traffic. (6) Mitigations: cgroups quota restores MMEM to 190 GB/s (~92%); Intel MBA restores 99%/95%/98% across three apps by cutting CXL bandwidth just 5 GB/s (intro: "restore bandwidth of the MMEM by 204GB per second, which reaches 99% of its original level"); frequency scaling recovers only 30-52%.

Limitations. Only ONE CXL device per testbed — no CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, no interleaved multi-device regions, no pooling. Intel Sapphire Rapids only; root-cause counters (TOR/CHA) and mitigations (Intel MBA, DDIO reasoning) are Intel-uncore-specific, so transferability to AMD is unshown (authors note AMD's SMBA for CXL "has not been released"). CXL 1.1 devices of small capacity (64GB/32GB). No multi-tenant/VM scenarios — interference pairs are single co-running processes pinned to node 0. Bandwidth-focused; no tail-latency distributions. Authors admit: MBA restriction is coarse (can only cut CXL bandwidth to ~half due to I/O block configuration) and unresponsive (takes seconds); cache partitioning (CAT) ineffective against ntst; hardware device-side latency-injection solution is only discussed, not implemented, and is vendor-specific; some app results were unstable and reported as 0. Several explanations are hedged as presumptions ("presumably", "we hypothesize").

Open questions. Interference BETWEEN multiple CXL devices (never tested); behavior under CXL 2.0/3.x, switches, and pooled/multi-headed devices; AMD and ARM platforms; device-side fine-grained MBA-like regulation (takeaway H2 is a proposal only); specialized TOR for slow-tier memory (H1, hardware proposal only); responsive/fast-acting bandwidth regulation; tail-latency and QoS SLOs rather than bandwidth recovery; multi-tenant isolation policies built on their insights; root cause of the RocksDB promotion effect is only partially explained (system-specific).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant as the closest prior art on real-hardware CXL interference, but it only characterizes CXL-vs-MMEM and CXL-vs-SSD on single-device Intel testbeds — it never measures CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, so the requester's candidate direction (per-device placement, cross-device interference, multi-tenant QoS across 4 CZ120s) remains unclaimed. The requester's observed 2.0-2.13x slowdown of a CXL victim under a CXL co-runner is a different axis (contention within/across CXL devices) than this paper's headline result (CXL ntst crushing DRAM bandwidth via TOR congestion), and its reverse-reasoning methodology (uncore TOR/CHA occupancy counters) is a useful template but is Intel-Sapphire-Rapids-specific — the AMD Turin host needs a different PMU story, and the paper itself notes AMD's CXL-targeted SMBA regulator is unreleased, which is an open tooling gap on the requester's platform. Its finding that ntst/write-combining traffic is the dominant aggressor suggests a concrete aggressor dimension to add to ObjPlace's interference model and to any per-device scheduler; its cgroups/MBA mitigation results give ready software baselines to compare a QoS mechanism against.

Could not verify. Kernel versions (only OS distros given: CentOS 7, Ubuntu 22.04.4 LTS), CXL link width and PCIe lane count (Figure 1 shows 'CXL 1.1' off a 'PCIe 5.0' port but no xN width; DAC text never states it), Exact CPU SKU numbers (paper only says '4th Gen Xeon Platinum/Gold 4.0 GHz'), Word-level diff between arXiv v1 and DAC camera-ready (arXiv v1 read via summarizing fetch, DAC PDF read in full; testbeds, numbers, and abstract match, title differs)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • System A has SAS SSD and an ASIC-based CXL memory device manufactured by Montage. System B consists of NVMe SSD and an Intel FPGA-based CXL memory device.
  • TABLE II row: 'CXL memory device — Montage ASIC 64GB [System A] — Intel Agilex FPGA 32GB DDR4-2400 [System B]'; other rows: CPU '4th Gen Xeon Platinum 4.0 GHz' / '4th Gen Xeon Gold 4.0 GHz'; Memory per socket '1TB, 8 Channel, DDR5-4800' / '256GB, 8 Channel, DDR5-4800'; Storage 'SAS SSD' / 'NVMe PCIe 4.0 MLC SSD'; OS 'CentOS 7' / 'Ubuntu 22.04.4 LTS'
  • Figure 1 illustrates the topology of our hardware setup, where NUMA node 0 and NUMA node 1 each possess their own cores and DDR5 DRAM, while the CXL Memory Expander is situated on NUMA node 2 with zero processor core, directly connected to Node 0.
  • In this work, we systematically characterize and explore how CXL memory interferes with other memory-related access, specifically MMEM access, and storage-related access such as NVMe or SAS SSD, using microbenchmarks and real applications.
  • we develop a configurable microbenchmark and systematically go through the basic interference condition of CXL with MMEM and SSD system using two different real CXL hardware setups
  • To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to characterize CXL interference on real CXL hardware.
  • a) The bandwidth of MMEM/SSD suffers significant interference when CXL performs ntst, reaching up to 93.2%. b) CXL ld and st can also be disruptive to MMEM, especially on System B, where over 40% interference is observed.
  • it is feasible to restore bandwidth of the MMEM by 204GB per second, which reaches 99% of its original level, using software solutions, at the cost of a 5GB per second reduction in CXL bandwidth

https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.18308 https://www.thu-haslab.org/publication/2025-cxl-interplay/dac2025cxl-i https://arxiv.org/html/2411.18308v1 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1109/DAC63849.2025.11132607

Mercury: QoS-Aware Tiered Memory System

arXiv preprint (cs.OS), arXiv:2412.08938 (2024) mixed multi-device-pooled verify: confirmed confidence: medium

CXL devices. real-CXL testbed: 2 (Astera Labs memory expansion cards); emulation testbed: 0 (remote NUMA socket with uncore throttling)

Problem. Existing tiered memory systems (TPP, Colloid, etc.) optimize aggregate performance but lack QoS support, so when multiple applications with different priorities/SLOs share a CXL-tiered machine, memory-capacity contention on the fast tier and bandwidth interference within/across tiers cause SLO violations for latency- and bandwidth-sensitive workloads.

Idea. Mercury adds QoS to tiered memory via: (1) per-tier page reclamation enabling application-level per-tier capacity allocation (instead of global/node-level reclamation), (2) intra- and inter-tier bandwidth-interference mitigation, (3) a proactive admission control algorithm tailored for tiered memory that decides whether a new app's SLO can be satisfied before admitting it, and (4) real-time adaptation to dynamic requirement changes. Implemented in Linux kernel v6.6 plus ~3000 LoC C++ user-space components.

Testbed. Real-CXL testbed: dual-socket AMD Genoa servers, 96 physical cores and 12 DDR5 channels per socket, 768GB DDR5 per socket + 256GB CXL memory (1.8TB total) supplied by two Astera Labs memory expansion cards with 2 DDR5 channels each, each card on x16 PCIe lanes (x32 total). Emulation testbed: dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6330 (56 physical cores), 512GB DDR4 per socket, remote socket used as slow tier with uncore frequency tuned down to match measured real-CXL latency. Linux kernel v6.6.

Evaluation. Multi-tenant co-location experiments over 80 workloads across seven categories (databases, ML, KV stores/Redis, Spark, graph processing, SPEC CPU 2017, web apps, VectorDB), running applications simultaneously with assigned priorities and SLOs (latency targets for latency-sensitive apps, bandwidth targets for bandwidth-intensive ones); scenarios cover local-memory contention, bandwidth interference, combined contention+interference, and long-running dynamic adaptation.

Baselines. TPP (Transparent Page Placement) and Colloid

Key findings. Under local-memory contention Mercury cuts foreground max slowdown from 29% to 12% and VectorDB slowdown from 75% to 14%; up to 40% improvement over TPP for ML workloads under bandwidth interference; 53.4% performance improvement for VectorDB under combined contention+interference; 8.4x longer SLO-satisfaction time for Redis vs TPP; overall 14.9% and 20.3% improvement vs TPP and Colloid respectively.

Limitations. The two Astera Labs CXL cards are managed only as an abstract slow "tier" — no per-device visibility, no CXL-device-to-CXL-device interference study, and the paper never states how the two cards are exposed as NUMA nodes. Part of the evaluation runs on NUMA emulation (Intel socket with uncore throttling), not real CXL. Admission control simply rejects inadmissible apps ("the user should adjust the SLO or deploy it on another machine"), i.e., no graceful degradation. QoS mechanisms are page-granularity kernel reclamation, so per-object placement effects are out of scope. Not (yet) upstreamed; authors only plan to "share our design with the Linux community for open discussion after the paper's publication".

Open questions. How QoS/isolation should work when multiple CXL devices are managed independently rather than as one tier; cross-CXL-device bandwidth interference; scaling beyond two tiers/two devices; interaction with object-granularity placement runtimes; behavior with CXL switches/pooling across hosts; SLO negotiation instead of hard admission rejection.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant as the closest QoS-for-tiered-memory baseline: Mercury does per-tier capacity allocation, bandwidth-interference mitigation, and admission control on real CXL (2 Astera Labs cards) plus emulation — but it collapses all CXL devices into one abstract slow tier and never manages or measures devices independently, leaving the requester's candidate direction (per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, per-device tail-aware QoS on 4x CZ120) unclaimed. It validates the requester's own observation that CXL bandwidth interference (their 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown) is the key QoS problem, and its TPP/Colloid baselines and 80-workload multi-tenant methodology are a useful evaluation template. A per-device, object-granularity QoS system (ObjPlace extension) would be positioned as finer-grained than Mercury's page/tier-level kernel mechanisms; expect reviewers to demand a Mercury/Colloid comparison.

Could not verify. Whether the two Astera Labs cards are exposed as one interleaved NUMA node or two separate NUMA nodes — the paper (per fetched text) does not state this; deviceTreatment=multi-device-pooled is inferred from the paper managing CXL as a single slow tier with per-tier (not per-device) mechanisms., CXL device capacity per card and link generation (only total 256GB CXL and x16 PCIe per card stated in fetched text)., Which experiments ran on the real-CXL vs the emulated testbed (the split is stated to exist but I could not attribute every result)., Exact author-written future-work/limitations section text beyond the quotes fetched.

Verifier note. Verified directly against the arXiv HTML full text (downloaded and grepped, plus WebFetch of the abs page). All analyst quotes are verbatim except the first, which drops the word 'is' ("CXL memory is enabled via..."). One nuance for deviceTreatment: 'multi-device-pooled' is supported at the tier level (two cards -> one 256GB CXL tier), but the paper is silent on whether the two Astera cards are interleaved into a single NUMA node or exposed as multiple nodes within the tier. Also note many evaluation experiments (e.g., 5.3 'follows the same workload setup as depicted in Figure 5', the 80-workload suite) appear to run on the Xeon Gold NUMA-emulation testbed, so 'mixed' is the right realCXL label.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • CXL memory enabled via two Astera Labs memory expansion cards with 2 DDR5 channels. Each card connects via x16 PCIe lanes (x32 lanes in total).
  • The servers have a 1.8TB memory footprint with 768GB DDR5 per socket and 256GB CXL memory.
  • We run these experiments on dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6330 servers with 56 physical CPU cores where each socket contains 512GB DDR4 memory. For controlling the environmental setup, we use the remote socket as the slow tier, with uncore frequency tuned down to match the memory access latency in our real CXL hardware.
  • In a CXL-enabled tiered memory system, memory nodes appear to the system as separate NUMA nodes ... assume that each tier consists of one or multiple NUMA nodes.
  • Mercury enables per-tier page reclamation for application-level resource management ... a novel admission control algorithm tailored for tiered memory.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.08938 https://arxiv.org/html/2412.08938v1

Performance characterization & tail behavior (12 papers)

Single-device characterization is saturated and excellent; use it, don't redo it. Melody is the reference for device diversity and microsecond tails (mitigation explicitly punted); SupMario for latency modeling; the Micron study for fused-pool bandwidth; ADMS'25 for why static DRAM:CXL interleaving fails. Same-host multi-device behavior under concurrent load is the missing chapter.

Characterizing Multi-Host CXL.2.0: Latency, Bandwidth, and Synchronization Costs

DaMoN 2026 (22nd International Workshop on Data Management on New Hardware, co-located with SIGMOD 2026) (2026) unknown unknown verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. Per the title, understanding the performance behavior (latency, bandwidth) and synchronization costs of CXL 2.0 memory shared/pooled across multiple hosts. Full problem statement could not be verified — the paper text is inaccessible.

Idea. A measurement/characterization study of multi-host CXL 2.0 (inferred from title only; the group's prior DaMoN work is on CXL for distributed joins and one author advertises a thesis topic on "Benchmarking lock algorithms on CXL shared memory", suggesting real shared-memory synchronization microbenchmarks — but this is inference, not verified paper content).

Key findings. Could not be verified.
