Vistara: Making CXL Real—Full Path from ASIC Design and OS Support to Hyperscale Deployment

ISCA 2026 (Industry Session) (2026) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 2 per server (2x Meta in-house Vistara CXL 2.0 Type-3 ASICs per MemServer node, 128GB DDR4 each = 256GB CXL per server); deployed fleet-wide across millions of servers

Problem. ~40% of Meta's millions of servers are memory-capacity bound; DRAM dominates fleet embodied carbon (69%); decommissioned DDR4 DIMMs outlive servers but cannot be reused in DDR5-only platforms; and six years after CXL's introduction no large-scale production deployment had been reported, leaving CXL's real-world viability (tail latency, tiering-software overhead) contested.

Idea. Hardware-software co-design: a first-party CXL 2.0 Type-3 memory-expander ASIC (Vistara) optimized for reusing recycled DDR4 RDIMMs at low power (~9W, ~50ns idle ASIC latency), integrated 2-per-node into a memory-optimized AMD Turin "MemServer" (768GB DDR5 + 256GB CXL DDR4 = 1TB), with both devices interleaved at 256B at the CPU host-bridge and exposed as ONE CPU-less NUMA node managed by upstream-Linux tiering (TPP + TMO, ZONE_MOVABLE, weighted interleave, cgroup cpuset.mems opt-out, per-container Fair Share tier accounting). Deployed at hyperscale across diverse production services; the paper refutes CXL misconceptions (unstable tail latency, high TPP overhead).

Testbed. MemServer: single-socket AMD Turin (Zen5), 158 cores / 316 SMT threads @ ~3GHz, 300W TDP; 768GB DDR5-6400 across 12 channels (614 GB/s peak, ~130ns idle); 2x Vistara ASICs each on PCIe Gen5 x8 (chip supports x16, deployed x8), each with 2 independent 72-bit DDR4 channels @ 2400 MT/s (chip max 3200), 4x32GB reclaimed RDIMMs per ASIC = 256GB CXL total, ~76 GB/s CXL peak, ~250ns CXL idle latency, 256B host-bridge interleave across the 2 devices, ~50W total CXL power. Kernel: upstream Linux with TPP, ZONE_MOVABLE onlining via CXL driver, numa_demotion_enabled=1, numa_balancing=2, zone_reclaim_mode=7; exact kernel version not stated.

Evaluation. Production A/B testing on MemServer nodes serving live traffic: identically configured nodes differ only in CXL enabled/disabled via cpuset.mems cgroup; production load balancer splits traffic evenly; each experiment runs at least one week. Workloads: CacheA/CacheB (distributed caches), Spark, Cosco (shuffle), FtStoreX (data warehouse), CI + Devmachine (DevInfra), ML Parameter Server (5.1-20TB recommendation models), plus HHVM Web and DPP for iso-capacity local:CXL ratio sensitivity (3:1, 2:1, 1:1). Microbenchmarks: Intel MLC bandwidth/latency sweeps with threads pinned per NUMA node; latency-CDF tail study at 1-100 threads; synthetic hot-footprint knee-of-curve stress test; multi-tenant fairness test stacking two CacheA containers (~2.5M QPS, ~430GB each).

Baselines. (1) Identical MemServer with CXL disabled (cgroup opt-out) under A/B production traffic; (2) iso-capacity all-local-DRAM configuration for the ratio-sensitivity study; (3) baseline TPP vs their Fair Share per-container tier accounting for the multi-tenant study; (4) implicit comparisons against prior findings: FPGA tail-latency anomalies [Liu et al.] and reported TPP overheads [Zhong et al. OSDI'24].

Key findings. Up to 25% server-count reduction for disaggregated ML inference (parameter servers use 25% less compute capacity, 4-12% throughput gain at 5.1-20TB models); CacheB average query time -29%; CacheA +33% sustainable QPS with 5-10x longer retention age; Spark/CI/Devmachine +25-33% executors/jobs/VMs per server; OOM job failures -33% (data warehouse) and -50% for some ML workloads. Device characterization: CXL 48 GB/s all-read (62% of theoretical) vs native 497 GB/s; latency 269ns CXL vs 169ns native at 10% BW util (372 vs 234 at 60%); tail-latency CDF of CXL closely tracks local DRAM even at 100 threads, refuting FPGA-based instability claims; TPP overheads <0.5%. Knee-of-curve: performance flat until ~75% hot footprint (+22% latency at 100% hot, 34 GB/s CXL BW); production workloads draw 10-100x less CXL BW than local BW. Multi-tenant: baseline TPP lets one container hold 90% local vs neighbor's 70% (20% P99 skew; 65% QPS drop + OOM-kill under noisy neighbor); their Fair Share per-container local-DRAM bounds improve P99 by 1.6-1.8x and cap the noisy-neighbor QPS dip at 12%. 3:1 and 2:1 local:CXL ratios show <1% regression vs all-local; 1:1 degrades (Web -6% requests, +9% latency; DPP -19% throughput).

Limitations. Authors' own: DDR4-2400 + x8 links give ~10x lower bandwidth and ~60% higher latency than local; workloads at/above the 75%-hot knee need x16 links or DDR5-based CXL devices; regressions emerge at 1:1 local:CXL. Methodological: the 2 devices are always fused by 256B host-bridge interleaving into one CPU-less NUMA node — no per-device measurement, management, or cross-device interference study; results are Meta-internal production workloads on a custom ASIC, not reproducible externally; kernel version and Fair Share implementation details are not given; multi-tenant evaluation covers only two co-located instances of the same cache workload on one node; no cross-host pooling/sharing (expansion only); the paper's "CXL is fine" conclusions are conditioned on cold-page-dominated, capacity-bound workloads that keep CXL bandwidth under ~10% utilization.

Open questions. More aggressive local:CXL ratios beyond 3:1 (explicitly flagged as future opportunity); CXL memory compression as an emerging use case requiring more balanced bandwidth/capacity; higher-bandwidth (x16 / DDR5) devices for bandwidth-sensitive workloads; behavior of bandwidth-bound (not capacity-bound) workloads on CXL is deliberately out of scope (such workloads opt out); per-device scheduling, device-level QoS, and CXL-to-CXL interference between independently exposed devices are never considered; generalization of Fair Share to heterogeneous multi-tenant mixes untested.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant positioning citation: Vistara's production platform is nearly isomorphic to the requester's testbed (single-socket AMD Turin, DDR5 local + multiple real CXL 2.0 expanders interleaved at the host bridge into one CPU-less NUMA node) — but Meta permanently pools its 2 devices at 256B granularity and manages them as a single tier, never treating devices as independently schedulable resources; this leaves the requester's per-device placement / cross-device CXL-interference direction open. Vistara's Fair Share multi-tenant result is the closest overlap: Meta confirms tiered-memory noisy-neighbor problems (65% QPS drop, OOM-kill) and fixes them with per-container local-DRAM accounting, so a new system must differentiate by operating at device granularity (CXL-to-CXL contention, per-device tail QoS), not just per-container DRAM shares. Caveat for motivation: Meta argues production capacity-bound workloads draw <10% CXL bandwidth and sit below the 75%-hot knee, and that TPP overhead is <0.5% making complex OS solutions unnecessary — the requester's bandwidth-interference results (2.0-2.13x victim slowdown at ~35.5 GiB/s/region) must therefore be framed as targeting the bandwidth-sensitive / high-hot-fraction / multi-tenant regime that Vistara explicitly opts out of or identifies as its degradation regime.

Could not verify. Exact Linux kernel version used in production (not stated in the paper), Formal ISCA 2026 proceedings page numbers / DOI (read from author-hosted camera-ready PDF; ISCA program page not separately fetched)

Verifier note. Verified against actual PDF text extracted this session (pdftotext of the jovans2.github.io camera-ready, 918KB). All three analyst quotes were located verbatim in the paper (the run-together \"2.0/1.1compliant\" matches the extracted text). Minor nuances, none rising to CORRECTED: (1) chip max capacity is 256GB with 64GB DIMMs, production uses 128GB/chip with 4x32GB reused DIMMs — the analyst stated the production figure correctly; (2) analyst's \"deployed fleet-wide across millions of servers\" slightly compresses the paper's \"deployed our CXL solution in hyperscale infrastructure with millions of servers\" — the paper does not explicitly state that every one of the millions of servers has CXL, only that the fleet numbers in the millions and the solution is deployed across diverse production workloads; (3) ASIC interface is PCIe Gen5 x16 but deployed as x8 per device in MemServer (Table: \"PCIe Gen5 x16 (deployed as x8)\"). Venue confirmation is from the hosting site path (isca26) and press coverage of the ISCA 2026 Industry Session, since the camera-ready PDF has no conference banner.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • The Vistara ASIC is Meta’s first-generation, custom CXL memory expander, designed to address the growing memory capacity needs in datacenters.
  • At its core, the Vistara ASIC is designed to bridge DDR4 memory to host processors via a CXL 2.0/1.1compliant PCIe Gen5 x16 interface. Each Vistara ASIC integrates two independent 72-bit DDR4 memory channels, supporting speeds up to 3200 MT/s and up to 256GB per chip with 64GB DIMMs.
  • CXL memory expansion is enabled by two Vistara ASICs per node, each connected via a PCIe Gen5 x8 interface.
  • With two Vistara ASICs per board, this enables 256GB of CXL-attached DDR4 memory expansion in production.
  • To ensure balanced utilization of the CXL links and minimize contention, memory accesses are interleaved across Vistara devices at a 256-byte granularity at the CPU host-bridge. This means consecutive memory blocks are alternately mapped across both Vistara CXL modules.
  • CXL-attached memory is exposed to the OS as a distinct, CPU-less NUMA node, separate from the local DRAM nodes directly attached to the processor.
  • All production experiments are conducted on MemServer nodes. This is a single-socket AMD Turin machine (158 cores, 2-way SMT, ≈3 GHz) with 768 GB local DDR5-6400 and 256 GB CXL-attached DDR4-2400 (2× Vistara ASICs), totaling 1 TB per server.
  • We have deployed our CXL solution in hyperscale infrastructure with millions of servers, across a variety of production workloads
  • Under the same noisyneighbor scenario, Fair Share limits Container A’s worst-case QPS dip to 12% (recovering within seconds) and eliminates the OOM risk entirely.

https://jovans2.github.io/files/vistara_camera_ready.pdf https://aisystemcodesign.github.io/papers/isca26/vistara_camera_ready. https://www.blocksandfiles.com/architecture/2026/06/26/panmnesia-boost

Architectural and System Implications of CXL-enabled Tiered Memory (MIKU)

arXiv preprint (cs.AR, cs.ET); no conference venue stated (2025) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. Platform A: 2 (256GB Micron pre-market each), Platform B: 4 (256GB Micron pre-market each)

Problem. Tiered DDR+CXL memory suffers severe, previously uncharacterized cross-tier interference: CXL devices have limited hardware-level parallelism (few in-flight requests) and the CPU's shared on-chip request path (CHA/IRQ→ToR queuing) dispatches DDR and CXL requests at the same rate, so slow CXL requests back up shared queues and unfairly throttle fast DDR traffic — DDR bandwidth can drop by up to 81% (performance loss up to 89%) under heavy CXL load, and CXL latency rises by nearly an order of magnitude.

Idea. MIKU, a dynamic memory-request control mechanism that prioritizes DDR requests and serves CXL requests best-effort: it estimates memory-request service time / queuing pressure via hardware performance counters (Intel PMU) and dynamically throttles the CXL-request issue rate using CPU quota adjustment (Linux cgroups) or Intel RDT Memory Bandwidth Allocation (MIKU-MBA), preserving near-peak DDR throughput while keeping CXL performance high — a pure software prototype on commodity hardware, no hardware changes.

Testbed. Two real testbeds with pre-market Micron CXL ASIC memory expanders. Platform A: two sockets, processor from "vendor A" (anonymized, Intel-implied given CHA/IRQ/ToR and RDT usage), 8x 32GB DDR5-4800 DIMMs per socket, two 256GB Micron pre-market CXL devices, PCIe Gen5 x8. Platform B: single socket, processor from "vendor B" (anonymized), 12x 16GB DDR5-4800 DIMMs, four 256GB Micron pre-market CXL devices, PCIe Gen5 x8. DDR hardware-interleaved; CXL devices software-interleaved via Linux weighted interleaving with equal weights. Kernel version not stated.

Evaluation. Micro-benchmarks: lat-test (pointer chasing, load-to-use latency) and bw-test (sequential accesses, bandwidth) run as DDR/CXL co-runners to expose cross-tier queuing interference. Real applications: LLM serving (LLaMA models, prefill/decode phases), Apache Spark running TPC-H queries, and a concurrent hash table (dashmap) driven by a YCSB dataset. Workloads placed on DDR vs CXL tiers concurrently to measure interference and MIKU's mitigation.

Baselines. DataRacing (unthrottled co-running, no control); MIKU (CPU-quota-based throttling); MIKU-MBA (variant using Intel Memory Bandwidth Allocation); Opt (each workload run in isolation, no interference — upper bound).

Key findings. (1) Disparity in memory-tier parallelism lets CXL traffic reduce DDR bandwidth by up to 81%; DDR performance loss up to 89% under co-running heavy CXL load. (2) CXL latency rises nearly an order of magnitude under load because requests accumulate in shared IRQ/ToR queues — cores issue DDR and CXL requests at the same rate even though DDR completes faster, creating unfair dispatch that hurts the faster tier. (3) MIKU recovers near-peak DDR throughput while keeping CXL best-effort: achieves at least 81% of optimal performance on DDR across workloads and 89% of optimal LLM decode performance. (4) Findings hold on two vendors' CPUs and both 2-device and 4-device Micron CXL configurations.

Limitations. Authors' own: MIKU is most effective when workloads alternate between DDR and CXL phases every tens of seconds or longer; stabilization takes ~6-7 seconds, so fast-phase-changing workloads are poorly served; they punt architectural fixes (token-based differential request scheduling) to future work. Methodological: CPU vendors are anonymized ("vendor A/B") so exact CHA/queue microarchitecture is unverifiable; kernel version and CXL device model not stated; CXL devices are always pooled via equal-weight software interleaving — no per-device analysis, no CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference study, no multi-tenant isolation experiments; throttling is coarse (cgroup CPU quota / MBA acts on cores, not on CXL traffic specifically), which can collaterally slow non-memory work of throttled tasks.

Open questions. Hardware-level fixes (differential/token-based request scheduling in the CHA) are explicitly left for future investigation. Untested: per-CXL-device scheduling and interference (devices only ever used as an interleaved pool), fairness among multiple CXL tenants (only DDR-vs-CXL fairness is addressed), tail-latency SLO enforcement rather than throughput recovery, behavior on kernels with hardware tiering (e.g., TPP/weighted autotiering) migrating pages mid-run, and fast phase-alternating workloads (<10s phases).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant: Platform B is nearly the requester's exact box — a single-socket non-Intel ("vendor B") CPU with four 256GB pre-market Micron CXL devices (almost certainly CZ120s, same as the requester's EPYC 9745 + 4x CZ120). It gives an architectural mechanism (limited CXL device parallelism + unfair shared-queue dispatch) that explains the requester's own observation that a CXL co-runner slows a CXL victim 2.0-2.13x and that per-region bandwidth saturates at only 4 threads. Crucially, MIKU always pools the 4 devices via equal-weight software interleaving and never studies per-device placement or CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference — leaving the requester's candidate direction (each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource, per-device QoS, multi-tenant isolation) open; MIKU's core-level cgroup/MBA throttling is also a natural baseline for a per-device tail-aware QoS system and complements ObjPlace's object-granularity placement.

Could not verify. Exact CPU models (anonymized as 'vendor A'/'vendor B' in the paper), Linux kernel/OS version (not stated in the paper as far as fetched text shows), Exact CXL device product name (paper says only 'Micron pre-market' 256GB; CZ120 inferred, not stated), Whether each CXL device appears as its own NUMA node (implied by software weighted interleaving across devices, but not quoted verbatim), Full evaluation figures — I read the HTML via extraction prompts, not the complete PDF end-to-end

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Platform A was equipped with two sockets, each featuring a processor from vendor A, 8 DDR DIMMs (32GB each), and two 256GB Micron's (pre-market) CXL memory devices.
  • Platform B had a single socket equipped with a processor from vendor B, 12 DDR DIMMs (16GB each), and four 256GB Micron's (pre-market) CXL memory devices.
  • On both platforms, hardware interleaving was applied to all DDRs, while CXL memory devices were interleaved in software using Linux's weighted interleaving (ker, [2023]), with equal weights.
  • The disparity in handling concurrent memory requests, particularly under heavy load, between memory tiers leads to unfair memory request processing for the faster memory tier. This imbalance can significantly reduce DDR memory bandwidth, by as much as 81%.
  • CXL memory requests gradually accumulate in the IRQ because cores issue DDR and CXL requests to the IRQ at the same rate, even though DDR requests are completed more quickly at the ToR.
  • MIKU achieves 89% of the optimal decode performance.
  • MIKU achieves at least 81% of the optimal performance on DDR.
  • MIKU is most effective for workloads that alternate between DDR and CXL memory accesses every tens of seconds or longer.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.17864 https://arxiv.org/html/2503.17864v2

Demystifying CXL Memory Bandwidth Expansion for Analytical Workloads

ADMS'25 workshop @ VLDB 2025 (16th Intl. Workshop on Accelerating Analytics and Data Management Systems) (2025) real-asic single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1

Problem. Whether transparent DRAM+CXL interleaving actually delivers memory bandwidth expansion for in-memory analytical (OLAP) workloads. Prior work (Huang et al., ADMS'24) claimed a single static interleaving configuration suffices across workloads; this paper re-evaluates that claim across multiple hardware configurations (SNC modes, prefetching on/off, many interleave ratios).

Idea. Systematic measurement study (not a system): run SSB SF100 on the Proteus JIT-compiled analytical engine plus four operator-level microbenchmarks (sequential scan/write, hash join build/probe, filtering with varying selectivity, radix partitioning with varying fanout) across DRAM:CXL weighted-interleave ratios (Linux MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE, 2 MiB huge pages), three SNC configurations (full socket / SNC-2 / SNC-4), and hardware prefetching enabled/disabled; profile with VTune top-down and PCM to attribute stalls. Distills three design principles: hardware-consciousness, workload-adaptive (per-operator) interleaving, and maximizing memory-level parallelism (SIMD, coroutine software prefetching, hyper-threading when bandwidth is underutilized).

Testbed. Dual-socket 48-core Intel Xeon Platinum 8468 Sapphire Rapids (CXL 1.1 host), 210 MB LLC, 8 DDR5 channels x 32 GB RDIMM per socket; only one socket (48 physical cores) used, HT disabled except scaling experiments. One SMART Modular CXA-4F1W CXL 2.0 Type 3 memory expander populated with 4x64 GB DDR5 RDIMMs (256 GB), attached via x16 PCIe 5.0 (32 GT/s/lane), operating under CXL 1.1. Linux kernel 6.9.0 with weighted memory interleaving. Measured: DRAM peak 268.6 GB/s (MLC, full socket, prefetch on) vs CXL 43.0 GB/s; idle latency DRAM 111 ns vs CXL 229 ns (full socket).

Evaluation. Star Schema Benchmark SF100 (SF1000 spot-checked) on Proteus, a pipelined parallel in-memory JIT (LLVM) query engine, cache-resident hash joins; plus microbenchmarks: 32 GB sequential read/write, 16 GB hash-table build + probe, filtering across power-of-2 selectivities, radix partitioning across power-of-2 fanouts. Variables: DRAM:CXL weighted-interleave ratio (9:1 through CXL-only), SNC off/SNC-2/SNC-4 (8/4/2 DRAM channels), hardware prefetching on/off, scalar vs AVX-512 reads, core-count scaling, hyper-threading. Profiling via Intel MLC, sysbench, PCM, VTune top-down (memory-bound slots, bandwidth-/latency-bound stalls).

Baselines. DRAM-only placement (primary baseline for all normalized speedups); CXL-only placement; range of static DRAM:CXL weighted-interleave configurations. Positioned against Huang et al. ADMS'24 ("Bandwidth Expansion via CXL"), whose single-config claim it refutes. No tiering systems (TPP/Memtis etc.) are run as baselines.

Key findings. (1) Transparent DRAM:CXL interleaving degrades SSB average performance by 20% on full-socket and SNC-2 with prefetching enabled, even at the lightest 9:1 ratio; disabling hardware prefetching cuts degradation to at most 7%, showing prefetchers misbehave on heterogeneous interleaved memory. (2) Only SNC-4 (2 DRAM channels, DRAM bandwidth comparable to CXL) with prefetching sees gains: 2% average, 10% best single query (Q3.1) at 8:2. (3) No single interleave ratio is optimal: optimal ratios shift up to 88% with workload parameters (e.g., partitioning fanout: 9:1 to 5:5; filter selectivity: 6:4 to 4:6); microbenchmark upsides are large where DRAM is scarce (up to 72-90% speedups in SNC-4). (4) Loaded CXL latency inflates up to 5x under load with prefetching (DRAM up to 2x); loaded DRAM latency can exceed idle CXL latency. (5) Bandwidth expansion helps only when DRAM bandwidth is actually saturated; scalar reads cannot saturate full-socket DRAM (underutilization costs up to 60% of potential gains), AVX-512 SIMD or hyper-threading recovers this; SNC is not required for bandwidth expansion benefits.

Limitations. Single CXL device (one SMART CXA-4F1W expander behind one x16 link, ~43 GB/s) — no multi-device, no per-device management, no CXL-to-CXL interference. Single CPU platform (Intel SPR, CXL 1.1 host); no AMD results despite AMD trends motivating the paper. Measurement/insights paper only: the proposed adaptive per-operator interleaving system is described as implementation strategies but never built or evaluated. Single-workload-at-a-time runs; no multi-tenant or co-runner interference experiments. SSB only for full queries (SF100; SF1000 only spot-checked on full socket); hash tables cache-resident in SSB runs. Static weighted-interleave page allocation only; no migration or dynamic policies tested. No tail-latency analysis (throughput/runtime only).

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on building the workload-adaptive runtime they advocate (per-operator interleave selection, predictive data migration between hash-join build/probe phases, hysteresis against thrashing, counter-driven prefetch toggling) — all left as "implementation strategies." Untested: behavior with multiple CXL devices or higher aggregate CXL bandwidth; AMD hosts; CXL 2.0/3.x hosts and back-invalidate; concurrent queries / multi-tenant contention on the CXL link; why exactly hardware prefetchers interact pathologically with interleaved memory (mechanism not root-caused); OS/buffer-pool integration of placement hints.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Strong methodological neighbor but orthogonal to the 4-device direction: it uses exactly one CXL expander behind one x16 link, so it says nothing about per-device scheduling or CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference — the requester's testbed (4x CZ120, 2 already interleaved into node 1) can directly extend its questions. Its core findings independently corroborate the requester's own measurements: modest CXL bandwidth (~43 GB/s per link vs their ~35.5 GiB/s per region), severe loaded-latency inflation on CXL (up to 5x, matching their 2.0-2.13x co-runner victim slowdown), and that naive/transparent interleaving hurts unless DRAM bandwidth is saturated. Its explicitly unbuilt future work — per-operator, phase-aware, counter-driven adaptive placement with hysteresis — is essentially what ObjPlace already implements at object granularity, making this a citable motivation paper and a claim ObjPlace can be positioned against; the per-device/multi-tenant extension remains entirely open for the requester.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • For CXL memory expansion, we use a SMART® CXA-4F1W device [42], which is a CXL 2.0 Type 3 memory expander, populated with 4x64 GB DDR5 RDIMMs. This device connects to the utilized CPU socket via a 16-lane (x16) PCIe 5.0 interface. Consequently, the CXL link operates under the CXL 1.1 protocol, utilizing the PCIe 5.0 physical layer's data rate of 32 GT/s per lane.
  • We conduct our experiments on a server running Linux kernel version 6.9.0 with support for weighted memory interleaving [7]. The server has a dual-socket 48-core Intel® Xeon® Platinum 8468 Sapphire Rapids CPU supporting the CXL 1.1 protocol with 2 hyperthreads per physical core, a 210 MB last-level cache (LLC), and 8 DDR5 DRAM channels with 1x32 GB DDR5 RDIMMs per channel.
  • We find that interleaving DRAM and CXL memory transparently to a state-of-the-art query execution engine Proteus, when running the SSB workload, degrades its average performance by 20%.
  • However, disabling hardware prefetching dramatically reduces this penalty, limiting performance degradation to at most 7% for the lightest interleaving configurations (9:1, 8:2).
  • No single memory interleaving configuration achieves optimal performance across workload variations, with optimal ratios varying by up to 88% based on workload parameters
  • But even before the memory bandwidth is fully utilized, the loaded CXL latency increases up to 5x times when hardware prefetching is enabled.

https://www.vldb.org/2025/Workshops/VLDB-Workshops-2025/ADMS/ADMS25-02 https://adms-conf.org/adms_2025.html

Exploring and Evaluating Real-world CXL: Use Cases and System Adoption

IPDPS 2025 (arXiv:2405.14209) (2025) real-asic single-device verify: confirmed confidence: medium

CXL devices. testbed A: 1, testbed B: 1, testbed C: 1 (three different vendor devices, one per system; 3 total across three separate machines)

Problem. CXL memory performance characteristics "remain largely unclear due to the limited availability of production hardware"; unclear what the real use cases are (HPC apps, LLM training/inference), how CXL interacts with existing OS memory tiering and page-interleaving mechanisms, and how software should place data across DRAM + CXL.

Idea. Characterize three genuine (production ASIC) CXL 1.1 memory-expansion devices from three different vendors on three servers (AMD Genoa + two Intel SPR-class), evaluate HPC (NPB, XSBench) and LLM (ZeRO-Offload training, FlexGen inference) workloads, study the interplay between memory tiering (AutoNUMA/TPP) and page interleaving, and propose object-level interleaving (OLI): per-data-object placement that interleaves bandwidth-sensitive large objects across DRAM+CXL while pinning latency-sensitive objects to local DRAM.

Testbed. Three separate testbeds, each with ONE CXL device exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node over PCIe 5.0 x16 (32 GT/s), CXL 1.1. System A: 2x AMD EPYC 9354 @3.8GHz (32c, 512MB LLC each), 12x DDR5-4800 per socket (768GB/socket, 460.8 GB/s), CXL device single-channel DDR5-4800 128GB (38.4 GB/s), Ubuntu 22.04, kernel 6.2.15. System B: 2x Intel Xeon Platinum 8470 @2.0GHz (52c), 8x DDR5-4800 per socket (1TB/socket), CXL device single-channel DDR5-8000 64GB (64 GB/s), Fedora 36, kernel 6.6.0-rc5. System C: 2x Intel Xeon Gold 6438Y+ @2.0GHz (32c), 8x DDR5-4800 per socket (512GB/socket), CXL device dual-channel DDR5-6200 128GB (48.4 GB/s), Ubuntu 22.04, kernel 6.2.15. Vendor names of the three devices are not given in the testbed table (only "three CXL devices from three vendors"); Micron, Samsung, Astera Labs are mentioned elsewhere in the text.

Evaluation. Intel MLC microbenchmarks for latency/bandwidth vs local DRAM (LDRAM) and remote-socket DRAM (RDRAM); 7 HPC benchmarks (NPB BT/LU/CG/MG/SP/FT + XSBench) under different placement policies (first-touch, uniform page interleaving, OLI, LDRAM-preferred with capped fast memory 64/128GB); LLM training (BERT, GPT-2 via ZeRO-Offload) and inference (LLaMA, OPT via FlexGen); kernel tiering comparison of AutoNUMA, Tiering-0.8, TPP.

Baselines. First-touch NUMA allocation; uniform (page-level) interleaving; LDRAM-preferred placement; RDRAM placement; NVMe offload (for FlexGen); kernel tiering systems AutoNUMA, Tiering-0.8, and TPP.

Key findings. (1) CXL latency is "comparable to a two-hop-away NUMA node": +153 ns (System A) to +211 ns (System B) over local DRAM. (2) CXL bandwidth is only 17.1% (A) and 46.4% (B) of remote-socket DRAM bandwidth and saturates with >=8 threads. (3) Proposed object-level interleaving (OLI) is 65% better than uniform interleaving on average; with sufficient LDRAM it cuts fast-memory usage 32% at <1% performance loss vs LDRAM-only; with insufficient LDRAM (64GB) it beats LDRAM-preferred by 1.42x avg (up to 2.35x on BT) and uniform interleaving by 1.32x avg. (4) LLM training with CXL offload brings little benefit or up to 16% degradation; LLM inference (FlexGen) LDRAM+CXL beats LDRAM+NVMe by 24% (LLaMA) / 20% (OPT) but is only ~3% different from RDRAM. (5) No single tiering strategy (AutoNUMA/Tiering/TPP) wins across all workloads, and page migration integrates poorly with static interleaving.

Limitations. Each testbed has only ONE CXL device — no multi-device, no cross-CXL-device interference, no pooling, no multi-tenant experiments. CXL 1.1 only (no CXL 2.0 features, no P2P GPU access; authors note GPU-CXL path bottlenecked by PCIe). Vendor identities of the three devices are not disclosed in the testbed table. Could not evaluate largest LLMs (e.g., GPT-3 175B) due to GPU memory limits. OLI object selection uses a simple heuristic (>=10% footprint + access intensity) and results are single-application runs; no co-runner contention or QoS study. Tiering results show no universal winner but no fix is built.

Open questions. How page migration / dynamic tiering should coexist with (static) interleaving policies; exploiting CXL 3.0+ peer-to-peer for GPU-CXL data paths in LLM serving; automatic/online identification of bandwidth- vs latency-sensitive objects; behavior with multiple CXL devices per host, device-level interleaving, contention among co-running tenants — all untested.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant as the closest published precedent to ObjPlace: its object-level interleaving (bandwidth-sensitive objects interleaved DRAM+CXL, latency-sensitive pinned to DRAM, 65% over uniform interleaving) overlaps heavily with ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement, so it must be cited and clearly differentiated (ObjPlace adds skew/phase/pressure signals and runs on 4x CZ120). Its characterization numbers (latency ~2-hop NUMA, bandwidth saturating at ~8 threads) corroborate the requester's 1.53x latency and 4-thread saturation findings. Crucially, every testbed here has exactly ONE CXL device treated as a single CPU-less NUMA node — it says nothing about per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS, so the candidate 4-device-independent direction remains open relative to this paper.

Could not verify. Exact vendor/model names of the three CXL devices (testbed table gives only channel/capacity specs; paper says 'three CXL devices from three vendors'), Whether the devices are ASIC vs FPGA is not explicitly stated, though 'production hardware' framing and vendor mentions (Micron, Samsung, Astera Labs) strongly imply ASICs, Verbatim quotes were extracted via a fetch-summarization pipeline over the arXiv HTML full text, so minor wording drift is possible

Verifier note. Verified against arXiv HTML v3 (full text) and arXiv abs page, fetched this session. All three analyst quotes match verbatim text I saw. One nuance: the paper never uses the word "ASIC" for its own devices — it says "genuine CXL memory-expansion cards instead of using memory simulation or emulation"; "real-asic" is consistent with this (commercial vendor cards, no FPGA anywhere in the paper) but the ASIC designation itself is inferred, not quoted. Note CXL C is a dual-channel (DDR5-6200) card — still one device, so it does not change the count.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We use three CXL devices from three vendors. See Table I.
  • The CXL memory appears as a CPU-less NUMA node.
  • the CXL device is attached to Socket 1 by CXL link over PCIe 5.0 ... The system B has the same organization as A ... The system C has a different organization: the CXL device is attached to Socket 0
  • 2×AMD EPYC 9354 CPUs @3.8 GHz, 32 cores and 512 MB LLC per CPU
  • the CXL memory is comparable to a two-hop-away NUMA node, in terms of access latency
  • peak CXL memory bandwidth is lower than that of RDRAM (the CXL memory bandwidth is 17.1% and 46.4% of the RDRAM bandwidth on the systems A and B respectively)
  • OLI performance is 65% better than the uniform interleaving on average
  • LDRAM + CXL shows an improvement of 24% for LLaMA and 20% for OPT in overall throughput, compared to LDRAM + NVMe
  • However, its performance characteristics remain largely unclear due to the limited availability of production hardware.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.14209 https://arxiv.org/html/2405.14209v3

Fetch Me If You Can: Evaluating CPU Cache Prefetching and Its Reliability on High Latency Memory

DaMoN '25 (21st International Workshop on Data Management on New Hardware, co-located with SIGMOD) (2025) numa-emulated n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0

Problem. In-memory database operations (B+-tree lookups, binary search, hash probes) stall on random memory accesses; access latency grows when data sits in remote-socket memory or behind interconnects such as CXL or NVLink, and it was unclear to what extent software prefetching (coroutine/state-machine instruction-stream interleaving) can hide these increased latencies, and how CPU-specific prefetch implementations (target cache level, drop-vs-stall behavior when fill buffers are full) affect it.

Idea. Introduce open-source microbenchmarks that empirically characterize (1) the actual cache level each prefetch locality hint (NTA/T0/T1/T2, ARM KEEP/STREAM) targets and its eviction behavior, and (2) 'prefetching reliability' — whether a CPU drops prefetches when the fill buffer (FB) is full (weak) or stalls until slots free up (strong) — across seven x86/ARM CPUs; then evaluate coroutine-based prefetching on B+-tree and binary-search workloads with data placed on local vs high-latency ('remote') memory, deriving guidelines: use only T0/NTA for portability, limit speculative prefetches to FB size, sort prefetches by access probability.

Testbed. Seven systems (Table 1): EPYC-2 (2x AMD EPYC 7742, DDR4, local 153ns / remote 277ns, FB 21), EPYC-3 (2x EPYC 7413, DDR4, 83/272ns, FB 24), Xeon-E5 (2x E5-2689 v4, DDR4, 80/130ns, FB 10), Xeon-2 (2x Xeon 5220S, DDR4, 76/156ns, FB 12), Xeon-3 (2x Xeon 8352Y, DDR4, 83/134ns, FB 12), A64FX (1x Fujitsu A64FX, HBM2, 215/307ns, FB 12, configurable weak/strong reliability), Grace (NVIDIA GH200: Grace CPU LPDDR5X local 181ns + Hopper GPU HBM3 remote 760ns via NVLink-C2C, FB 16). Linux 4.18-6.2 depending on system, gcc 12, 2 MiB THP. NO CXL device anywhere: 'remote' = remote NUMA socket (x86), distant core groups (A64FX), or GPU HBM over NVLink-C2C (Grace). CXL is motivation/discussion only.

Evaluation. Microbenchmark 1: 512 MiB value array + random offset array; measure access latency of prefetched cache lines vs known-location references to identify target cache per locality hint. Microbenchmark 2: batched NTA prefetch + dependent-access phases, batch size 1-32, single-threaded, to detect drop-vs-stall (FB size) behavior. Database workloads: (a) OLC B+-tree (code from Kühn et al. DaMoN'24, extended with continuous allocator + mbind NUMA pinning), 50M uint64 KV pairs, node sizes 128B-32KiB (to 128KiB on A64FX), 5M lookups x5, single-threaded, 20 coroutines, variants Normal / Coro Half Node / Coro Full Node, local vs remote placement; (b) binary search over 100M 4B ints (~380 MiB), Zipf theta 0-1, 30 parallel streams, variants Normal / Coroutine / State Machine, T0 prefetch. Ten repetitions, median reported. Code: github.com/hpides/prefetching.

Baselines. Non-prefetching implementations of the same data structures: 'Normal' OLC B+-tree (vs Coro Half Node / Coro Full Node coroutine-prefetching variants from Kühn et al.) and 'Normal' binary search (vs Coroutine and State Machine interleaved variants based on Nishanov's public code). No cross-system tools like MLC as baselines; comparisons are prefetch-on vs prefetch-off per CPU and local vs remote placement.

Key findings. (1) Prefetching yields B+-tree speedups up to 2.6x (remote placement, small nodes; 1.1-1.9x local) and binary-search speedups 1.9-2.8x on high-latency memory vs 1.4-2.1x local — i.e., prefetching helps MORE when latency is higher, provided FB slots suffice. (2) CPUs split into weak reliability (drop prefetches when FB full: EPYC Zen2/Zen3, Grace, A64FX-weak) vs strong (stall: Intel Xeons, A64FX-strong); on 8 KiB B+-tree nodes weak reliability gives ~2x speedup while strong causes up to 2.5x slowdown (speedups 0.4-1x) because stalling to prefetch 128 cache lines defeats interleaving. (3) Microbenchmark-measured FB sizes match documented values within +/-1 (EPYC-3: 23 vs 24, Xeon-E5: 10, A64FX: 13 vs 12, Grace: 16). (4) Only 2 of 7 CPUs share locality-hint implementations; AMD ignores locality hints except NTA eviction; Intel merges T1/T2; T0 and NTA behave most consistently. (5) High Zipf skew (theta > ~0.9) makes prefetching counterproductive on local data (up to ~30% degradation on A64FX).

Limitations. No real CXL hardware at all — CXL is only invoked in abstract/intro/discussion as a motivating technology; 'high latency memory' is proxied by remote NUMA sockets (130-277ns), A64FX distant core groups, and GH200 GPU HBM3 over NVLink-C2C (760ns). Remote-socket latency (1.6-3.3x local) is a rough stand-in for CXL and lacks CXL.mem protocol effects, device-controller behavior, and bandwidth constraints. Database workloads are single-threaded microworkloads (B+-tree lookups, binary search) — no multi-threaded contention, no bandwidth saturation, no multi-tenant runs, no full DBMS. Coroutine count (20) and stream count (30) are best-effort, not tuned per system (authors admit 'more optimal setups may exist'). An anomalous EPYC-3 remote-skew slowdown is left unexplained ('likely caused by system-specific caching intricacies'). Older kernels (4.18-6.2) and DDR4-era CPUs; no Zen4/SPR CPUs that actually support CXL.

Open questions. Actual behavior of software prefetching against real CXL-attached memory (latency ~1.5-2x DRAM but also bandwidth-limited and contended) is untested — the authors explicitly punt: 'technologies like CXL will bring even more heterogeneity and higher memory access latencies... we therefore conclude that software prefetching will become even more attractive in the future.' Also open: multi-threaded prefetching under FB/bandwidth contention, interaction of software prefetch with hardware prefetchers on far memory, per-system optimal coroutine counts, whether growing FB sizes erase the weak/strong reliability distinction, and prefetching for write-heavy or scan workloads.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant as an application-level latency-hiding technique for the requester's CXL tier, but note the paper never touches real CXL silicon — its 'remote' is NUMA-socket DRAM at 130-277ns, bracketing the requester's measured 277ns CZ120 latency, so the speedup regime (1.9-2.8x on high-latency memory) plausibly transfers to their box. The AMD-specific findings matter for the EPYC 9745 (Turin): the paper's EPYC Zen2/Zen3 parts show weak prefetch reliability (drop-on-full-FB) and locality-hint-oblivious prefetches, and the FB-size microbenchmark could be rerun on Zen5 + real CZ120 memory as an easy characterization delta. It says nothing about bandwidth contention, multi-device placement, or interference — the requester's 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown regime is exactly what this single-threaded latency-focused study leaves open, so it complements rather than overlaps ObjPlace and the per-device-scheduling direction.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Memory can be located close to a CPU, at remote sockets, or on devices connected via interconnects such as CXL or NVLink.
  • We run benchmarks on seven systems, described in Table 1. For the database workloads (see Section 4), the remote setting refers to data placed in remote socket memory for the AMD EPYC and Intel Xeon systems, e.g., the access path between CPU2 and CPU1 in Figure 2.
  • A64FX's remote setting refers to data placed in memory of distant core groups [26]. Grace refers to an NVIDIA GH200 chip, where a Grace CPU and Hopper GPU are connected via NVLINK-C2C ... Here, data is placed in GPU memory in the remote setting.
  • Table 1: Specifications of analyzed systems. ... EPYC-2 2x 7742 DDR4 ... Xeon-3 2x 8352Y DDR4 ... A64FX 1x A64FX HBM2 ... Grace 1x Grace CPU LPDDR5X HBM3
  • New technologies such as CXL promise memory extension and high bandwidth but add access latency [33]. CPUs, on the other hand, are evolving too, showing increasing FB sizes over the last years [20].
  • FB sizes are increasing, and technologies like CXL will bring even more heterogeneity and higher memory access latencies to servers [20, 33]. We therefore conclude that software prefetching will become even more attractive in the future.
  • We show that prefetching can increase performance by up to 2.6x and 2.8x for B+-Tree and binary search workloads.
  • When prefetching 8 KiB B+-Tree nodes, weak reliability achieves a speedup of 2x while strong reliability degrades performance with a slowdown of 2.5x for lookup workloads.

https://hpi.de/oldsite/fileadmin/user_upload/fachgebiete/rabl/publicat https://doi.org/10.1145/3736227.3736231 https://github.com/hpides/prefetching

Performance Characterizations and Usage Guidelines of Samsung CXL Memory Module Hybrid Prototype

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2503.22017, cs.AR) (2025) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1

Problem. DRAM alone cannot economically serve data-intensive workloads that need both large capacity and persistence; Samsung's CMM-H (a CXL Type-3 hybrid device with a DRAM cache in front of NAND flash, load/store accessible without block-I/O overheads) had no public performance characterization, so users lacked guidance on which workloads it suits.

Idea. First public comprehensive characterization of the FPGA-based CMM-H prototype: microbenchmark latency/tail-latency/bandwidth studies plus application studies (SPEC CPU2017, LLaMA 3.c, XSBench, RocksDB, Redis) in three usage modes (sole memory, memory expander alongside DRAM, persistent memory with compiler-enforced idempotent processing replacing WAL), distilled into boxed usage guidelines.

Testbed. Dual-socket server, 2x AMD EPYC 9454 @2.75 GHz (48 cores/socket, 256 MB LLC per CPU), 512 GB DDR5-4800 (256 GB/socket), Ubuntu 22.04.4, Linux kernel 6.8.0-49, HT and boost disabled, GCC 11.4.0. CXL device: one Samsung CMM-H E3.L PoC built on a Xilinx/AMD Versal XCVM1802 FPGA — CXL 1.1 Type 3 (CXL.io/CXL.mem) over PCIe Gen4 x8; 1 TB Samsung TLC NAND backing store fronted by a 16 GB 8-way LRU DRAM cache with 4 KB blocks (internal cache-to-NAND link PCIe Gen4 x4, 40 W). Exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: Intel MLC v3.11b pointer-chasing (1800 MB region); custom assembly kernels of 16 64-byte accesses in temporal/non-temporal load/store variants; tail latency measured over 20M runs per region size (99.99th/99.999th/max); bandwidth via multithreaded random 64-byte reads/writes. Applications: SPEC CPU2017 (ref inputs), LLaMA 3.c running LLaMA 3.2 1B (4740 MB footprint, 1-32 threads), XSBench with 10 footprints from 80 MB to 40.9 GB, RocksDB 9.9.3 db_bench (1M KV pairs), Redis 7.4.1 redis-benchmark (200K requests, 10 KB values); placement forced with numactl page binding plus CPU pinning; persistence experiments recompile RocksDB/Redis with idempotent processing (Clang/LLVM 13.0.1) to drop WAL/logging. Memory-expander mode interleaves pages between DDR5-L and CMM-H at varying ratios.

Baselines. Local DRAM (DDR5-L) and remote-socket DRAM (DDR5-R, cross-NUMA); no Optane comparison ("not supported on our AMD CPUs-based testbed"). For persistence: conventional WAL/AOF-logging RocksDB/Redis vs. CMM-H idempotent variants.

Key findings. MLC idle latency: CMM-H 728.9 ns vs DDR5-L 122.9 ns (5.9x); fine-grained kernels 1.2x-5.9x slower, but non-temporal stores nearly match DRAM (16.0 vs 13.5 ns) and nt-st is ~7x faster than temporal st on CMM-H. Tail latency comparable to DRAM while footprint fits the 16 GB device DRAM cache, then spikes to 15-20 us at 32 GB footprints. Peak bandwidth only ~4.5 GB/s vs ~219 GB/s DDR5-L (~48x gap), saturating at 4 threads (loads) / 2 threads (stores). SPEC CPU2017: negligible impact at low memory pressure; bwaves 3.73x slowdown on CMM-H under high pressure. LLaMA 3: 4% (4 threads) to 48% (32 threads) throughput loss. XSBench: 1.58x-5.56x slowdown, DRAM-cache hit rate falling 99.97%->97.62%. As memory expander, 80% of DDR5-L performance with a 50/50 page split. As persistent memory with idempotent processing (no WAL), RocksDB gains up to 2.2x (fillseq) / 4.59x (deleteseq) and Redis 1.67x-1.71x over logged baselines.

Limitations. Authors' own: FPGA PoC, not ASIC ("planning the next generation CMM-H products based on ASIC implementation to deliver lower latency and higher bandwidth"); sequential tag lookup and unmodified PCIe Gen4 x4 NAND SSD architecture limit bandwidth; 8-way/4KB DRAM cache causes conflict misses at large footprints; no Optane baseline (AMD testbed); idempotent-persistence gains require recompilation with a special compiler. Methodological: single device only — no multi-device, no interleaving, no device-to-device or co-runner interference study; no multi-tenant or QoS experiments; CXL 1.1 only; idle-latency-centric microbenchmarks (no loaded-latency curves vs. injected bandwidth reported like MLC loaded-latency); no OS tiering system actually evaluated (only suggested).

Open questions. Automatic OS/VM memory tiering onto CMM-H is repeatedly recommended but not built or measured; software coalescing of random writes to 4 KB granularity is suggested but unimplemented; behavior of the future ASIC version, multi-device scaling, contention between co-running tenants on the device DRAM cache, and CXL 2.0+ features (pooling, hotplug) are all untested.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Different device class from the requester's testbed: CMM-H is a flash-backed hybrid FPGA prototype (16 GB DRAM cache + 1 TB NAND, ~4.5 GB/s, 729 ns idle latency) versus the requester's 4x all-DRAM Micron CZ120 ASICs (~277 ns, ~35.5 GiB/s per region), so its absolute numbers do not transfer. Its value is methodological: a template for characterization structure (idle vs tail latency across footprint, nt-load/store variants, thread-scaling bandwidth saturation, per-app footprint sweeps with numactl binding) that the requester could replicate per-device and cross-device. It is single-device and CXL 1.1 with zero interference, multi-tenant, or multi-device content, so it leaves the requester's candidate direction (per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, tail-aware QoS) entirely open — useful as related work showing the gap. The extreme thread-saturation behavior (BW saturates at 2-4 threads) echoes and corroborates the requester's own 4-thread saturation observation on CZ120 regions.

Could not verify. The paper never states an explicit device count sentence like 'one CMM-H device'; single-device is inferred from consistent singular usage ('the CMM-H device', one PoC unit described) throughout the testbed section., Author affiliations (Samsung + Purdue) not listed on the arXiv abstract page; inferred from the URL hint, not verified in fetched text., Verbatim quotes were extracted via an automated fetch of the arXiv HTML full text; minor formatting (e.g., multiplication signs) may differ from the PDF.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • E3.L form-factor PoC device which is based on Versal XCVM1802 FPGA
  • v1.1, CXL.io/CXL.mem (Type 3)
  • CMM-H is exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node to the upper-level software stack
  • 16 GB 8-way associative DRAM cache, which uses an LRU replacement policy
  • 2x AMD EPYC 9454 CPUs @2.75 GHz, 48 cores
  • Ubuntu 22.04.4 LTS with Linux kernel 6.8.0-49
  • PCIe Gen 4 x8 interface
  • Intel Memory Latency Checker (MLC) v3.11b, which performs pointer-chasing in a (1800 MB) memory region
  • bwaves suffers a 1.37x and 3.73x slowdown on DDR5-R and CMM-H, respectively
  • we are planning the next generation CMM-H products based on ASIC implementation to deliver lower latency and higher bandwidth
  • we do not conduct experiments on Intel Optane memory as it is not supported on our AMD CPUs-based testbed
