Systematic CXL Memory Characterization and Performance Analysis at Scale (Melody)

ASPLOS 2025 (ASPLOS '25, March 30-April 3, 2025, Rotterdam; DOI 10.1145/3676641.3715987) (2025) mixed multi-device-independent verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 4 distinct devices characterized (CXL-A/B/C/D from different vendors: 3 ASIC + 1 FPGA); one experiment additionally hardware-interleaves two CXL-D units, implying at least 5 physical units on hand

Problem. The performance implications of CXL memory across devices, latency regimes, processors, and workloads are underexplored: prior CXL studies use only average latency/bandwidth on small workload sets and overlook (i) CXL latency stability (tail latencies), (ii) CPU tolerance to prolonged CXL latencies at scale, and (iii) a systematic way to attribute workload slowdowns to root causes under black-box CXL memory controllers.

Idea. Melody: the largest CXL characterization to date — 265 workloads x 4 real CXL devices x 7 memory latency configurations (140-410ns, three of them NUMA-simulated) x 5 Intel CPU platforms — providing the first disclosure and analysis of CXL tail latencies (via a custom cacheline-level microbenchmark, MIO), plus Spa, a differential stall-cycle root-cause analysis method using only 9 CPU performance counters that decomposes CXL-induced slowdown into store-buffer / L1 / L2 / L3 / DRAM / core components (including a first quantification of CPU prefetcher inefficiency under CXL).

Testbed. Five 2-to-8-socket Intel servers: SPR2S (Sapphire Rapids, 8xDDR5, 128GB, 114ns/218GB/s local, 32 cores @2.1GHz, 60MB L3), EMR2S (Emerald Rapids, 8xDDR5, 128GB, 111ns/246GB/s, 32c, 160MB L3), EMR2S' (8xDDR5, 1.5TB, 117ns/236GB/s, 52c @2.3GHz, 260MB L3), SKX2S (Skylake, 6xDDR4, 96GB, 90ns), SKX8S (8-socket Skylake, 6xDDR4, 410ns remote). CXL devices (vendors anonymized): CXL-A ASIC, CXL 1.1 x8, 2xDDR4, 128GB, 214ns, 24GB/s (32GB/s peak mixed R/W); CXL-B ASIC, x8, 1xDDR5, 128GB, 271ns, 22GB/s (26 peak); CXL-C FPGA, x8, 2xDDR4, 16GB, 394ns, 18GB/s (21 peak); CXL-D ASIC, x16 PCIe5, 2xDDR5, 756GB, 239ns, 52GB/s (59 peak). All devices are CXL 2.0-capable but run in CXL 1.1 mode (CPU limitation). CXL-A/B/C hosted on SPR2S/EMR2S, CXL-D on EMR2S'. Three extra latency points (140ns, 190ns via lowered uncore frequency, 410ns) are NUMA-emulated on SKX. No kernel version stated.

Evaluation. 265 workloads: SPEC CPU 2017, PARSEC, GAPBS, PBBS, CloudSuite, Phoronix, Spark, Redis+YCSB, VoltDB, GPT-2, MLPerf, DLRM, Llama-7B (llama.cpp). Each workload runs with memory bound entirely to local DRAM, NUMA, or one CXL device at a time ('worst-case' placement; tiering/interleaving deliberately excluded, except one experiment interleaving two CXL-D units at 104GB/s). Slowdown S = (P_DRAM/P_CXL - 1)*100% vs local DRAM. Device-level: Intel MLC loaded-latency sweeps; custom MIO pointer-chase microbenchmark for per-request tail latency CDFs (prefetchers disabled), with AVX read/write background noise threads; 1ms latency sampling across 112 workloads. Spa validated by comparing 9-counter stall-based slowdown estimates against measured application-level slowdowns on NUMA/CXL-A/CXL-B. Note: CXL-C has only 16GB DRAM, limiting it to 60 of the 265 workloads. Artifacts at github.com/MoatLab/Melody.

Baselines. Not a system paper, so no system baselines. Reference points: socket-local DRAM (slowdown baseline), 1-hop NUMA, 2-hop NUMA (SKX8S 410ns), CXL+NUMA (CXL accessed from remote socket), and cross-device comparison among CXL-A/B/C/D. Spa is positioned against Intel Top-down Microarchitecture Analysis (TMA) and prior heuristic/LLC-miss-based correlation methods.

Key findings. (1) First disclosure of CXL tail latencies: 3 of 4 devices show unstable microsecond-level tails even under low (&lt;50%) bandwidth utilization — CXL-B/C p99.9-p50 gaps up to ~160ns (50% over median), p99.99/p99.999 reaching 1us (CXL-B/C) and &gt;700ns (CXL-A/D), vs stable local/NUMA (45ns/61ns p99.9-p50); under load, CXL-A/B spike from ~350ns to ~1.2us and CXL-C to 3us. Average latency rises &gt;=60ns at only 50-86% utilization on CXL vs 90-95% for local/NUMA. (2) Devices are heterogeneous: peak bandwidth occurs at different R/W ratios per device (2:1 for CXL-A, 3:1/4:1 for CXL-D); FPGA CXL-C cannot exploit bidirectional links. (3) Tails propagate to applications (Redis YCSB-C p99+ blows up on CXL-C at low bandwidth); CXL+NUMA causes surprise slowdowns (520.omnetpp 2.9x at &lt;1GB/s; 10 other workloads 33-283%) traced to tail latency. (4) Workload tolerance: 94/87/80% of workloads see &lt;50% slowdown on CXL-D/A/B (vs 98% NUMA); 60/54/32% see &lt;10%; a 7% 'tail' of bandwidth-bound workloads suffers 1.5-5.8x on CXL-A/B; interleaving 2x CXL-D (104GB/s) nearly closes the gap to NUMA. (5) Prefetching does not hide CXL latency: cache slowdown is caused by reduced L2 prefetcher timeliness/coverage (2-38% L2PF-L3-miss reduction, offset by L1PF misses, Pearson 0.99). (6) Spa attributes slowdowns with &lt;5% error for &gt;95% of workloads using 9 counters; a Spa+Pin case study moved two 2GB hot objects to DRAM, cutting slowdown 13%-&gt;2%. Verified per task hint: yes, the paper documents unstable microsecond tails on real CXL and explicitly punts mitigation ('as an initial step... to raise awareness'), deferring fixes to future CPUs, CXL MC designs, and system software; devices ARE studied individually (each characterized/run separately, on separate hosts for CXL-D), never co-managed.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: tail-latency root causes remain speculative due to black-box CXL MCs — 'no tools exist to pinpoint tail latencies', white-box breakdown deferred to CXL 3.0 CPMU counters; thermal stress testing stopped at 70C to avoid damaging devices; CXL-C's 16GB capacity limited it to 60/265 workloads; 3 of 7 latency points are NUMA-emulated, not real CXL. Evident from methodology: vendors are anonymized (CXL-A/B/C/D), so results cannot be mapped to specific products (e.g., Micron CZ120); Intel-only hosts running CXL 1.1 mode — no AMD (requester's EPYC Turin) and no CXL 2.0 features; Spa's 9 counters are Intel-specific; characterization only, no mitigation mechanism built for the tails it discovers; no multi-tenant runs — 'noise' experiments are self-inflicted microbenchmark co-runners on one device, not tenant workloads; no cross-device (CXL-to-CXL) interference study; per-device measurement only, no per-device placement/scheduling; no kernel/OS configuration details.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: mitigating CXL tail latencies (framed as Implications: 'Future CPUs need to be improved to tolerate CXL's unpredictable latencies', 'CXL MC designs need to prioritize latency predictability', 'System designers need to account for tail latency impact'); white-box per-request latency decomposition awaiting CXL 3.0 CPMU; thermal throttling as a tail cause left unresolved. Untested: tiering and software interleaving policies (excluded by design), multi-device management, QoS enforcement across tenants sharing a device, cross-device interference, AMD platforms, CXL 2.0/switch topologies (switch numbers taken from public sources, not measured).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly load-bearing for the requester's candidate direction: Melody proves on real silicon that CXL devices differ sharply in tail-latency stability and R/W-ratio behavior (3 of 4 devices show microsecond tails at <50% utilization), which strengthens the case that four CZ120s should not be treated as one flat pool — but Melody only characterizes devices individually and explicitly punts mitigation, per-device QoS, multi-tenant isolation, and cross-device interference, leaving exactly the requester's proposed territory open. Caveats: Melody is Intel/CXL-1.1 and anonymizes vendors, so its per-device numbers do not transfer to the AMD Turin + CZ120 (CXL 2.0) box, and all four requester devices are the same model, so cross-vendor heterogeneity arguments weaken to per-region/interleave-config heterogeneity. Melody's 2x CXL-D hardware-interleaving result (bandwidth doubling nearly closes the NUMA gap) is a direct comparison point for the requester's interleave_ways=2 region, and Spa's 9-counter differential-stall attribution is a reusable diagnosis technique for ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity signals — worth citing as the characterization foundation the requester's system builds beyond.

Could not verify. Kernel/OS version of the testbed (not stated anywhere in the paper), Actual vendor/model identities of CXL-A/B/C/D (deliberately anonymized; cannot confirm whether any is a Micron CZ120), Whether the two interleaved CXL-D units are counted within the '4 real CXL devices' claim or are additional units (paper consistently says 4 devices but the Figure 8f experiment uses two CXL-D units)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Melody builds on an extensive evaluation spanning 265 workloads, 4 real CXL devices, 7 latency levels, and 5 CPU platforms.
  • We use 4 CXL memory expanders from different vendors (CXL-A, CXL-B, CXL-C, CXL-D). CXL devices are hosted on SPR/EMR servers: CXL-A, CXL-B, and CXL-C on SPR2S/EMR2S, and CXL-D on EMR2S' (remote host).
  • All our CXL devices operate in CXL 1.1 mode as type-3 memory expanders (CXL.io+CXL.mem). CXL-C is FPGA-based (lowest performance) while the rest are ASICs. CXL-D utilizes 16x PCIe 5 lanes and 2 memory channels, providing the highest CXL bandwidth of 52GB/s.
  • We use NUMA to simulate 3 additional CXL latency configurations of 140ns and 190ns on SKX2S (190ns achieved via lowering uncore frequency, not shown in the table), and 410ns on SKX8S [32, 34]. These setups provide a total of 7 latency configurations
  • We discover some CXL devices exhibit significant µs-level tail latencies even under low device bandwidth utilization.
  • In contrast, CXL-A and CXL-B see latency spikes from ∼350ns to ∼1.2µs, and CXL-C reaches 3µs when injected delay drops from 700 to 500 cycles.
  • We run workloads using local, NUMA or CXL memory to focus on analyzing the “worst-case” CXL setup, excluding more complex configurations like tiering or interleaving.
  • We also test workloads on two CXL-D devices with hardware interleaving, effectively doubling the bandwidth to 104GB/s.
  • Currently, no tools exist to pinpoint tail latencies. A future approach could involve a white-box analysis... As an initial step, we demonstrate and quantify the impact of CXL tail latency to raise awareness within the systems community.
  • Implication #1: From software/hardware design perspectives, there is a need to address CXL tail latencies. (a) Future CPUs need to be improved to tolerate CXL's unpredictable latencies

https://people.cs.vt.edu/jinshu/docs/papers/Melody_ASPLOS.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3676641.3715987 https://github.com/MoatLab/Melody

Bandwidth Expansion via CXL: A Pathway to Accelerating In-Memory Analytical Processing

ADMS workshop @ VLDB 2024 (15th Intl. Workshop on Accelerating Analytics and Data Management Systems) (2024) real-asic single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one Montage MXC-based CXL prototype module containing two DDR5 DIMMs behind one controller)

Problem. Prior CXL work treats CXL memory only as a slower capacity tier (tiering/pooling), overlooking that CXL attaches via PCIe and thus adds aggregate memory bandwidth; and most prior DB-on-CXL studies used NUMA/FPGA emulation or simulation, which may not reflect real CXL device behavior for in-memory analytical (OLAP) workloads.

Idea. Instead of DRAM-CXL tiering, interleave pages between local DRAM and a real CXL Type 3 device at a ratio matched to their bandwidth ratio (found: 3:2 DRAM:CXL) using a Linux N:M weighted-interleave mempolicy patch, thereby amalgamating DDR-channel and PCIe bandwidth to accelerate bandwidth-bound in-memory analytics.

Testbed. Dual-socket Intel Sapphire Rapids (32 cores/socket, 64MB LLC, 1024-entry TLB), 128GB DDR5 per socket (4x 32GB DIMMs, 35.2 GB/s per DIMM), UPI 96.0 GB/s cross-socket, run in sub-NUMA clustering (SNC) mode so local DRAM = one 32GB DDR5 DIMM at 35.2 GB/s theoretical. CXL: one early Montage MXC (Memory eXpander Controller) prototype integrating two DDR5 DIMMs, attached via 8-lane PCIe Gen5 (31.5 GB/s theoretical; ~20-21 GB/s practical, controller-limited, confirmed by Montage). Linux kernel 5.19.17 with Weiner's N:M interleave mempolicy patch; hyper-threading, prefetching, IRQ, ASLR disabled; huge pages enabled.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks plus OLAP on a single socket (32 cores): (1) peak sequential LOAD/STORE/NT-STORE throughput across 13 DRAM:CXL interleave ratios (100:0 to 0:100); (2) sequential access vs thread count (1B 4-byte records); (3) hash join (PK-FK, 256M build / 1B probe, 8-byte tuples) as random access; (4) selective column-scan reads (2B 8-byte records, 1-512 skipped records); (5) radix partitioning (1B 8-byte records, fanout 64-16384); (6) Star Schema Benchmark, 13 queries at SF=30, custom implementation from HyPer-generated query plans. Code at github.com/fukien/type3-pathway.

Baselines. Four memory configurations compared: local DRAM only; remote NUMA socket memory only; CXL memory only; DRAM:CXL 3:2 interleaved. For SSB, additionally a conventional DRAM-CXL tiered-memory model ("TIER": DRAM as buffer, CXL as fast disk with data movement into DRAM before query ops), representing TPP/Pond-style tiering wisdom.

Key findings. (1) Any non-zero DRAM:CXL interleave beats pure DRAM; 3:2 (matching the bandwidth ratio) is optimal. (2) Pure DRAM (SNC single channel) peaks at ~28.5 GB/s, pure CXL at ~20.1 GB/s (controller-bottlenecked); interleaved LOAD reaches ~47.5 GB/s = 1.61x DRAM, exceeding both single-path theoretical limits. (3) Hash join: interleaved up to ~1.59x DRAM; join scales to 16 threads vs 8 for others. (4) CXL (non-processor-affiliated) has better STORE/NT-STORE than remote NUMA due to lower cache-coherence maintenance overhead: hash-join build 26% faster on CXL than NUMA, but probe 24% faster on NUMA. (5) Radix partitioning: interleaving wins but only within 7.5% of DRAM (TLB thrashing bound). (6) SSB SF=30: interleaved beats DRAM on all 13 queries, mean 1.19x (Q1.x >1.4x); the TIER config performs about as poorly as pure CXL, challenging tiered-memory DBMS designs.

Limitations. Authors' own: CXL controller caps practical bandwidth at 20-21 GB/s well below the PCIe Gen5 link; SSB limited to SF=30 because "Our platform cannot accommodate larger workloads due to the limitations of the current 32GB local host DRAM"; SSB gains muted because SF=30 hash tables fit in the 64MB LLC; only CXL 1.1 evaluated ("Currently, only CXL 1.1 is commercially available"). Evident from methodology: exactly ONE CXL device — no multi-device interleaving, no cross-device interference, no pooling; SNC mode artificially shrinks DRAM to a single channel (35.2 GB/s), inflating the relative CXL benefit versus a full 4-channel socket; single static interleave ratio, no dynamic/object-level placement; no latency-sensitive or multi-tenant/co-runner experiments; read-mostly OLAP only, no OLTP; prototype (pre-production) Montage silicon.

Open questions. Authors punt on OLTP/transaction processing and broader database operations, TCO optimization, and larger-scale heterogeneous memory platforms (explicit future-work agenda). Untested: behavior with full DRAM channel population (non-SNC), multiple CXL devices and per-device management, adaptive/dynamic interleave ratios, interference between co-running workloads sharing the CXL link, tail latency, and CXL 2.0/3.0 switching or pooling.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Strong methodological relevance but zero multi-device content: it uses a single Montage-controller prototype and an SNC-shrunken single DRAM channel, so its headline 1.61x depends on DRAM being throttled to 35.2 GB/s — on the requester's EPYC 9745 with full 12-channel DRAM the relative bandwidth-expansion gain from one 35.5 GiB/s CXL region would be far smaller. Its key transferable findings are (a) bandwidth-ratio-matched weighted interleaving (via the same N:M mempolicy mechanism now upstream as weighted interleave) beats tiering for bandwidth-bound analytics, and (b) CXL's lack of processor affiliation gives it better store performance than remote NUMA — both directly usable as placement-policy signals in ObjPlace. It provides no evidence on per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL interference, or QoS, so it is prior art the requester's 4-device independent-resource direction clearly extends beyond; it can also serve as a baseline configuration (static bandwidth-ratio interleave) for ObjPlace to beat.

Could not verify. Exact CXL device capacity in GB is never stated (only 'two DDR5 DIMMs'; DIMMs elsewhere in the paper are 32GB, suggesting 64GB, but the paper does not say so explicitly)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We employ an initial CXL memory prototype from Montage [45], which integrates two DDR5 DIMMs as previously described. This prototype interfaces with the host system through an 8-lane PCIe Gen5 port, achieving a bandwidth of 31.5GB/s.
  • Our experimental platform is anchored on a dual-socket platform running Linux kernel version 5.19.17. Each socket hosts a 32-core Intel Sapphire Rapids CPU, with 64MB last-level cache (LLC) and 1024-entry translation lookaside buffer (TLB).
  • We have received confirmation from Montage[45] that their current technology is capable of providing a practical bandwidth limited to 20 − 21 GB/s.
  • our comparison encompasses four distinct memory configurations: local host memory access only (DRAM), remote NUMA memory access only (NUMA), CXL memory access only (CXL), and a DRAM-CXL interleaved setup (DRAM:CXL)
  • We apply a Linux patch [51] to facilitate a range of memory interleaving ratios, enabling the distribution of memory pages between a processor-bound memory node and a processor-independent memory node
  • the aggregated “LOAD” throughput around 47.5GB/s, which is 1.61x higher than the local DRAM configuration, and significantly surpasses the theoretical bandwidth limit of a single DDR channel (35.2GB/s)
  • Our platform cannot accommodate larger workloads due to the limitations of the current 32GB local host DRAM.

https://www.vldb.org/workshops/2024/proceedings/ADMS/ADMS24_01.pdf

Dissecting CXL Memory Performance at Scale: Analysis, Modeling, and Optimization (SupMario / Alto)

Under this title the work exists only as an arXiv preprint (Sep 2024, no journal-ref on arXiv). The ASPLOS'25 publication from the same group/testbed was retitled 'Systematic CXL Memory Characterization and Performance Analysis at Scale' with the framework renamed Melody, a different author list (adds Yuyue Wang, Marie Nguyen, Xun Jian, Sam H. Noh; drops Hanchen Xu), 5 CPU platforms instead of 4, and it drops the SupMario/Alto modeling+tiering content (introduces 'Spa' instead). Saying the 'Dissecting...' SupMario/Alto paper is ASPLOS'25 conflates two related but non-identical papers. (2024 (arXiv); 2025 (ASPLOS)) mixed multi-device-independent verify: corrected confidence: high

CXL devices. 4 distinct CXL device models (CXL-A ASIC 128GB, CXL-B ASIC 128GB, CXL-C FPGA 16GB, CXL-D ASIC 768GB on a remote machine) + 3 zNUMA/uncore-emulated latency points = 7 latency configs — BUT the paper additionally uses a SECOND physical CXL-D unit in a 'CXL-D*' configuration (two CXL-Ds hardware-interleaved), so at least 5 physical devices appear in the evaluation, not 4 total.

Problem. Lack of systematic understanding of how real CXL memory devices (with widely varying latency/bandwidth) affect application performance, how to predict per-workload slowdown from CPU counters, and how to optimize page placement/tiering — existing tiering systems (e.g., TPP) and naive interleaving are latency/bandwidth-oblivious.

Idea. SupMario: a large-scale characterization (265 workloads x 4 real CXL devices x 7 latency configs x 4 Intel platforms) that distills workload slowdown into a performance model built on just 12 CPU performance counters (DRAM/cache/store slowdown components), then uses the insights for two optimizations: a "best-shot" weighted page interleaving policy and Alto, a regulated ("amortized memory latency"-driven) page tiering policy.

Testbed. Four Intel servers: SPR2S (Sapphire Rapids, 2-socket, 16 cores/socket, 2.1GHz, 256GB DDR5-4800, 114ns local latency), EMR2S (Emerald Rapids, 2-socket, 16 cores/socket, 256GB DDR5-4800, 111ns), SKX2S (Skylake 2-socket, 192GB DDR4, 90ns), SKX8S (Skylake 8-socket, 384GB DDR4, 81ns). CXL devices: CXL-A (ASIC, CXL 1.1, x8, 2xDDR4, 128GB, 214ns, 24GB/s), CXL-B (ASIC, CXL 1.1, x8, 2xDDR5, 128GB, 271ns, 22GB/s), CXL-C (FPGA, CXL 1.1, x8, 2xDDR4, 16GB, 394ns, 18GB/s), CXL-D (ASIC, CXL 1.1, x16, 4xDDR5, 768GB, 239ns, 52GB/s, hosted on a remote machine). Extra latency points (140-410ns) via zNUMA on Skylake plus uncore-frequency throttling. Each CXL device exposed as a zero-core NUMA (zNUMA) node. Kernel version not captured in the fetched text.

Evaluation. 265 workloads: SPEC CPU 2017 (43 analyzed in detail), CloudSuite, Phoronix, GAPBS, PBBS, Spark, PARSEC, and ML workloads (GPT-2, Llama, MLPerf). Each workload run with memory bound to local DRAM vs. the CXL zNUMA node (and emulated latency configs); slowdown measured and modeled from 12 CPU perf counters (11 on Skylake). Optimizations evaluated: best-shot weighted DRAM/CXL page interleaving vs 1:1 round-robin, and Alto tiering vs TPP.

Baselines. TPP (kernel memory tiering) for Alto; traditional 1:1 round-robin DRAM/CXL interleaving for the best-shot interleaving policy; local-DRAM and NUMA configurations as reference points for the slowdown model.

Key findings. (1) Real CXL devices vary widely: 214-394ns load latency, 18-52GB/s per device. (2) CXL tail latency is much worse than DRAM (DRAM p99.9-p50 gap only 45-61ns; CXL-B/C gaps ~160ns). (3) On EMR nearly half the workloads see no slowdown on CXL; 32 workloads see <5% slowdown; but 4 workloads needing >24GB/s see >50% slowdown. (4) A 12-counter linear model predicts slowdown within 10% for >99% of NUMA cases and 91-94% of CXL cases. (5) Best-shot weighted interleaving beats 1:1 interleaving; Alto achieves up to 177% improvement over TPP for latency-sensitive workloads.

Limitations. Authors admit: "These devices function as black boxes to us, as we do not have access to their internal implementation details"; some needed perf counters are impractical/unavailable (L1-prefetch share of LFB hits; missing SQ prefetch counters degrade accuracy on SPR/EMR); CXL-C is only 16GB, limiting workload coverage. Methodological gaps for the requester's purposes: each CXL device is characterized alone on its host — no multi-device-per-host configuration, no CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, no multi-tenant co-location study; 3 of the 7 latency configurations are NUMA/uncore-throttling emulation, not real CXL; all-Intel platforms (no AMD Genoa/Turin); the tiering/interleaving evaluation is single-workload-at-a-time, not consolidated tenants.

Open questions. How multiple CXL devices behave when attached to and shared on one host (per-device scheduling, cross-device interference, hardware interleaving across devices); tail-latency QoS under co-running tenants; behavior on AMD hosts and CXL 2.0 devices; extending the counter-based slowdown model to contended/multi-tenant scenarios; sub-page or object-granularity placement (they operate at page granularity).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant as the broadest real-hardware CXL characterization to cite, and its 12-counter slowdown model plus best-shot weighted interleaving could inform ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity signals — but note their four devices sit on different (all-Intel) hosts and are each characterized in isolation as separate zNUMA nodes, never as co-resident devices on one server. It therefore says nothing about the requester's candidate direction: per-device placement across 4 CZ120s on one EPYC 9745, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS. The requester's observed 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown on CXL is exactly the contention regime SupMario does not study (single workload at a time). Alto/TPP are page-granularity kernel tiering baselines to compare ObjPlace against; the counter-based model may transfer poorly to AMD Turin (Intel-specific counters).

Could not verify. Kernel version used on the testbeds (not present in fetched extracts), Whether any single server ever had more than one CXL device attached simultaneously — the paper never states this; device-to-server attachment mapping for CXL-A/B/C is not specified (CXL-D is on a remote machine), Table 1 rows were extracted via the fetch tool's summarization, so the 'Table 1' quote is a faithful reconstruction rather than a literal row string

Verifier note. All three analyst quotes were verified verbatim in the arXiv text. The analyst's device roster (A/B/C/D types, capacities, CXL-D remote, 3 emulated points, 7 configs total) is accurate; the only refutation on numCXLDevices is the '4 physical devices total' phrasing — the CXL-D* config uses a second physical CXL-D unit hardware-interleaved (also in the ASPLOS'25 Melody version, which reports 104GB/s from two CXL-Ds). deviceTreatment=multi-device-independent stands as the dominant methodology with that one interleaved exception. Venue is the substantive correction: the ASPLOS'25 paper (dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3676641.3715987) is the retitled Melody characterization paper (5 CPU platforms, different author list, no SupMario/Alto); arXiv:2409.14317 itself carries no publication record. Sources fetched this session: https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14317 (API + abs page), https://arxiv.org/html/2409.14317v1, full text of https://arxiv.org/pdf/2409.14317 (pdftotext), and https://people.cs.vt.edu/jinshu/docs/papers/Melody_ASPLOS.pdf (pdftotext).

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • SupMario is based on extensive evaluation of 265 workloads spanning 4 real CXL devices within 7 memory latency configurations across 4 processor platforms.
  • CXL memory seamlessly integrates into systems as cacheable, byte-addressable memory within a zero-core NUMA (zNUMA) node (i.e., CPU-less NUMA)
  • We also use two Skylake servers – one with 2 sockets (SKX2S) and another with 8 sockets (SKX8S) – to extend the range of memory latencies from 140 to 410 ns using zNUMA and by lowering the CPU uncore frequency.
  • Table 1: CXL-A: ASIC, CXL 1.1, x8 lanes, 2xDDR4, 128GB, 214ns, 24GB/s; CXL-B: ASIC, CXL 1.1, x8, 2xDDR5, 128GB, 271ns, 22GB/s; CXL-C: FPGA, CXL 1.1, x8, 2xDDR4, 16GB, 394ns, 18GB/s; CXL-D: ASIC, CXL 1.1, x16, 4xDDR5, 768GB, 239ns, 52GB/s (paraphrased table rows as extracted by fetch tool); CXL-D is 'hosted on a remote machine'
  • Alto achieves up to 177% improvement compared to TPP, a popular CXL memory tiering solution.
  • These devices function as black boxes to us, as we do not have access to their internal implementation details.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.14317 https://arxiv.org/html/2409.14317v1 https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/Dissecting-CXL-Memory-Performanc

Optimizing System Memory Bandwidth with Micron CXL Memory Expansion Modules on Intel Xeon 6 Processors

arXiv (industry white paper, Micron Technology + Intel; IEEE template, not peer-reviewed) (2024) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 8

Problem. CXL has mostly been pitched for capacity expansion; its bandwidth-expansion value for bandwidth-hungry HPC/AI workloads on real, many-lane CXL systems is unquantified, and DRAM vs CXL tiers have different bandwidth characteristics depending on the read/write ratio, making it unclear how to split traffic between them for maximum system bandwidth.

Idea. Populate an Intel Xeon 6 6900P (Granite Rapids AP) with 8x Micron CZ122 E3.S x8 CXL modules brought up as ONE unified CXL NUMA node alongside 12-channel DDR5-6400 (node 0), then use the Linux v6.9+ weighted (software page-level) NUMA interleaving feature to place M pages on DRAM for every N pages on CXL, sweeping the DRAM:CXL weight ratio per workload read:write mix to find the bandwidth-optimal split (e.g., 3:1 for read-only, 5:2 for mixed read/write).

Testbed. Intel "Avenue City" platform; Intel Xeon 6 6900P series (Granite Rapids AP), 128 P-cores, supports 6x16 (96) PCIe 5.0 lanes with any four x16 usable as CXL 2.0 Type-3 links. DRAM: 12x Micron DDR5 64 GB RDIMMs at 6400 MT/s (all 12 channels populated, HEX mode, 768 GB total) = NUMA node 0. CXL: 8x Micron CZ122 128 GB E3.S (Gen5 x8) modules occupying 64 PCIe lanes (~1 TB total; 2 devices per x16 link; 4 cards direct to backplane, 4 via riser cards in two CME slots), all brought up as a single NUMA node 1. OS: Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.4, kernel 6.11.6 with weighted memory interleaving support. Small 64 GB DRAM modules deliberately chosen to focus on bandwidth rather than capacity.

Evaluation. (1) Intel MLC microbenchmark at multiple read:write ratios (read-only R; 2W:1R; 1W:1W; 2R:1W non-temporal; 1R:1W; 3R:1W), sweeping the DRAM:CXL weighted-interleave page ratio (1:0, 1:1, 2:1, 5:2, 3:1, 4:1, 9:1, 0:1) and reporting bandwidth plus loaded-latency curves. (2) AI: Meta-Llama3-8B-Instruct inference via Intel Extensions for PyTorch (IPEX), bfloat16, batch size 1 (output token latency); FAISS vector search on Microsoft Turing-ANNS 1B x 100-dim vectors with OPQ128_256-IVF65536_HNSW32-PQ128x4fsr index, nprobe=4096, efSearch=512. (3) HPC: OpenFOAM drivaerFastback (~200M cells), HPCG (192^3), Xcompact3D (3D Taylor-Green vortex), POT3D, STREAM Triad. Each workload run standalone; best static interleave weight reported per workload.

Baselines. DRAM-only (12-channel DDR5-6400, interleave weight 1:0) is the sole baseline for every workload; CXL-only (0:1) also measured in the MLC tables. No external tiering/placement systems (TPP, AutoNUMA, Memstrata, etc.) are compared — only the vendor's own weighted-interleave configurations against DRAM-only.

Key findings. MLC: DRAM-only read-only = 556 GB/s; CXL-only read-only = 205 GB/s across all 8 devices (~26 GB/s/device); DRAM loses 20% at 1:1 R:W while CXL-only gains slightly on mixed R/W (214 GB/s at 1R:1W, a claimed 48% theoretical additive gain over DRAM). Best DRAM+CXL: read-only 690 GB/s at 3:1 (+24%); 2W:1R 636 GB/s at 5:2 (+34%); 1W:1R 621 GB/s (+39%); 2R:1W non-temporal 607 GB/s at 5:2 (+30%). At max load DRAM+CXL also cuts loaded latency ~21% vs DRAM-only; optimal weight shifts from 9:1 (light load) to 3:1 (heavy load). Applications: Llama-3-8B token latency 42.91→36.83 ms (+17%, 3:1); FAISS 0.545→0.442 ms/query (+23%, 2:1); OpenFOAM +22% (5:2); HPCG 92→117 GFlops/s (+27%, 3:1); Xcompact3D +25% (5:2); POT3D 687→539 s (+27%, 5:2). Geometric mean speedup across HPC+AI workloads: 24%. CXL shows an 8% bandwidth drop under non-temporal writes.

Limitations. All 8 devices are fused into one NUMA node — zero per-device measurement, no cross-device or device-level interference analysis, and the paper never explains how traffic is spread among the 8 devices within node 1 (hardware root-port interleave vs page striping). Interleave weights are static and hand-tuned per workload offline; the authors themselves note weights "may need to be adjusted based on the read-to-write ratio," with no dynamic/automatic mechanism. Every workload runs alone: no multi-tenant, no co-runner, no isolation or QoS experiments; latency is only MLC loaded latency, no application tail-latency. Vendor-authored (Micron+Intel) marketing-adjacent white paper, not peer-reviewed; footnote admits "Performance results are derived from testing in the specified configuration... Results may vary, so it is recommended to reconfirm them in your setting." Single platform, single CPU vendor; only bandwidth-bound workloads chosen (capacity dimension deliberately excluded via small 64 GB DIMMs). Minor internal inconsistency: W5 table header says (1W,1W) while text calls it (1W,1R) and cites 5:2 though the table highlights 2:1.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: only "future research and development efforts should continue to explore and refine this integration" — nothing concrete. Obviously untested: dynamic/phase-aware weight adaptation instead of static per-workload ratios; treating the 8 devices as anything other than one pooled node (per-device placement, per-device bandwidth allocation, failure/QoS domains); interference between co-located workloads sharing the CXL node; tail latency under contention; capacity-driven tiering combined with bandwidth interleaving; behavior when CXL devices are asymmetrically loaded; AMD platforms.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. This is the scaled-up vendor sibling of the requester's testbed — same Micron CZ12x silicon family (8x CZ122 vs their 4x CZ120), and it confirms that even Micron/Intel's flagship demo fuses ALL devices into a single NUMA node and tunes only a global DRAM:CXL page ratio: direct evidence that per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and multi-tenant isolation (the requester's candidate direction) are unexplored even by the vendors, with zero co-runner or tail-latency data here. The per-device bandwidth calibration is useful: 205 GB/s read-only over 8 Gen5 x8 devices (~26 GB/s/device) brackets the requester's ~35.5 GiB/s per 2-device x8 region and shows aggregate CXL bandwidth scaling roughly linearly when pooled. The kernel weighted-interleaving sweep (optimal 3:1 read-only, 5:2 mixed, shifting with load) is a mandatory, easily reproducible baseline for ObjPlace — ObjPlace's object-granularity, phase/pressure-driven placement should be positioned against exactly this static page-ratio approach, whose per-workload hand tuning is the paper's admitted weakness.

Could not verify. How traffic is distributed among the 8 CZ122 devices inside NUMA node 1 (hardware interleave at the root ports/host bridge vs kernel page striping) — the paper never specifies the intra-node interleave mechanism or granularity, Unloaded CXL vs DRAM idle latency in ns (only relative loaded-latency curves in Figure 4 are given), Whether the platform ran CXL 2.0 or 1.1 protocol mode on each link (devices described as 'CXL 2.0 Type-3' capable lanes, but negotiated mode not stated)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • The eight CXL memories were set up as a unified NUMA configuration, employing software-based page level interleaving mechanism, available in Linux kernel v6.9+, between DDR5 and CXL memory nodes to improve overall system bandwidth.
  • All 12 local DRAM channels are designated as NUMA node 0 (HEX mode), while all the Micron's CXL modules (8 in total) are brought up as separate NUMA node 1. (Figure 1 caption)
  • This is the first study that presents real data experiments utilizing eight CXL E3.S (x8) Micron CZ122 devices on the Intel Xeon 6 processor 6900P (previously codenamed Granite Rapids AP) featuring 128 cores, alongside Micron DDR-5 memory operating at 6400 MT/s on each of the CPU's 12 DRAM channels.
  • As shown in Figure 2, eight Micron CZ122 128GB CXL devices are utilized, occupying 64 PCIe lanes and providing a total additional memory capacity of 1TB.
  • CXL Memory: Micron CZ122 – 128GB * 8 (8 modules E3.S form factor ~ 1TB) ... OS: Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9.4 ... Kernel: 6.11.6 (With support for weighted memory interleaving) (platform table)
  • Memory expansion via CXL boosts read-only bandwidth by 24% and mixed read/write bandwidth by up to 39%. Across HPC and AI workloads, the geometric mean of performance speedups is 24%.
  • For example, when brought up as system memory, CXL memory is treated as a separate NUMA node.
  • Ultimately, a 3:1 ratio was identified as optimal under maximum load conditions for a read-only workload traffic.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.12491 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2412.12491

Demystifying CXL Memory with Genuine CXL-Ready Systems and Devices

MICRO 2023 (56th IEEE/ACM International Symposium on Microarchitecture), DOI 10.1145/3613424.3614256 (2023) mixed single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 3 physical devices characterized (CXL-A: ASIC hard-IP DDR5-4800; CXL-B: ASIC hard-IP 2x DDR4-2400; CXL-C: FPGA soft-IP DDR4-3200), but only 1 device attached to the server at any time ("our system can accommodate only one CXL memory device"); application/Caption experiments use CXL-A only

Problem. Because commercial CXL hardware was scarce, nearly all prior CXL-memory research emulated CXL using DDR memory on a remote NUMA node of a multi-socket system; it was unknown whether this emulation faithfully reflects true CXL memory behavior, how real CXL devices differ across vendors/controller designs, how CXL memory interacts with the CPU cache hierarchy, and how real CXL affects latency-sensitive and bandwidth-intensive applications.

Idea. First comprehensive characterization of a genuine CXL-ready system: a 4th-gen Intel Xeon (Sapphire Rapids) host with three real CXL 1.1 memory devices from different manufacturers, measured with Intel MLC plus a custom microbenchmark ("memo") for per-instruction-type latency/bandwidth (ld, nt-ld, st, nt-st), compared head-to-head against remote-NUMA-emulated CXL; in-depth analysis of the CPU/CXL interplay (SNC mode, LLC isolation breaking, on-chip coherence structure for cache-less CXL devices); application studies (Redis/YCSB, DeathStarBench, FIO, DLRM, SPEC CPU2017); and Caption, a CXL-memory-aware dynamic page-allocation policy that monitors CPU counters (L1 miss latency, DDR read latency, IPC via Intel PCM), estimates memory-subsystem performance with a linear model, and greedily tunes the DDR:CXL page-allocation ratio to use CXL as a bandwidth expander.

Testbed. Dual-socket server: 2x Intel Xeon 6430 (Sapphire Rapids) @2.1 GHz, 32 cores and 60 MB LLC per CPU, Hyper-Threading disabled; Socket 0: 8x DDR5-4800 128 GB DIMMs across 8 channels; Socket 1: 1x DDR5-4800 DIMM used as emulated CXL memory (remote NUMA); Ubuntu 22.04.2 LTS, Linux kernel v6.2. Three anonymized CXL 1.1 memory devices, one attached at a time: CXL-A (ASIC hard IP, DDR5-4800, 38.4 GB/s per channel max), CXL-B (ASIC hard IP, 2x DDR4-2400, 19.2 GB/s per channel), CXL-C (FPGA soft IP, DDR4-3200, 25.6 GB/s per channel). Device vendors, capacities, and CXL link widths are not disclosed. SNC (sub-NUMA clustering) mode used for application experiments (2 local DDR5 channels + 1 CXL channel per SNC node).

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: Intel MLC (pointer-chasing latency, bandwidth at various read:write ratios) and custom "memo" tool measuring random-parallel latency and sequential bandwidth per instruction type (temporal/non-temporal loads and stores, AVX-512), rdtsc-timed, median of 10,000 repetitions. Latency-sensitive apps: Redis with YCSB (uniform keys), DeathStarBench social-network microservices (compose posts, read user timelines, mixed) in Docker, FIO with 4 GB page cache (Zipfian). Throughput apps: DLRM embedding reduction (MERCI setup), SPECrate CPU2017 top-MPKI benchmarks (fotonik3d, mcf, roms, cactuBSSN) run as multiple instances or mixes. Page placement varied via numactl and a recent Linux weighted-interleave kernel patch (percentage of pages to CXL: 0/25/50/75/100%). Caption evaluated co-running SPEC-Mix, Redis+DLRM, DLRM.

Baselines. Emulated CXL memory (remote NUMA socket with one DDR5-4800 DIMM, "DDR5-R"); local DDR5 ("DDR5-L"/DDR 100%); static page-allocation ratios via numactl/weighted interleaving (25/50/75/100% to CXL); TPP (transparent page placement, OSDI'24-era Meta tiering patch) for Redis tail latency; default Linux NUMA page-allocation policy (for Caption's +24% claim).

Key findings. (1) True CXL != remote-NUMA emulation: true CXL gives up to 26% lower latency and 3-66% higher bandwidth efficiency than emulated CXL depending on instruction type and device, because CXL devices have no caches/cores so the CPU uses a fast on-chip coherence structure. (2) Huge cross-device variance: CXL-A shows only 35% longer ld latency than DDR5-R while CXL-B and CXL-C are ~2x and ~3x longer; bandwidth efficiency (fraction of theoretical max, All Read) is 70% (DDR5-R), 46% (CXL-A), 47% (CXL-B), 20% (CXL-C, FPGA). (3) CXL access breaks LLC isolation in SNC mode, giving CXL-bound accesses an effective 2-4x larger LLC (41 ns vs 76.8 ns for a 32 MB buffer). (4) Allocating pages to CXL raises p99 latency of us-scale apps (Redis) by 10-82%; TPP is 174% worse in p99 than statically allocating 25% to CXL due to migration overhead; ms-scale microservices (DSB) are largely insensitive. (5) Naive 50% CXL allocation can reduce throughput vs 100% DDR despite higher aggregate bandwidth; for DLRM at 32 threads, tuning to 63% pages on CXL yields 88% higher throughput than DDR-only. (6) Caption improves co-running SPEC CPU2017 throughput by up to 24% vs the default OS policy (19%/18%/8%/20% vs best static for fotonik3d/mcf/roms/cactuBSSN), converging to 29-41% of pages on CXL.

Limitations. Only ONE CXL device can be attached to the system at a time (stated explicitly), so no multi-device, pooling, cross-device interference, or per-device management is evaluated; application and Caption experiments use CXL-A only. Devices are anonymized (no vendor, capacity, or link-width disclosure), limiting reproducibility. Intel SPR only — SNC/LLC-eviction findings are Intel-cache-hierarchy specific and may not transfer to AMD. CXL 1.1 memory expansion only (no CXL 2.0 switching/pooling). Caption's linear estimation model was fitted exclusively on DLRM counters (authors admit this and note it "has the potential for further improvement by fitting counter values from a more diverse range of applications"); Caption targets bandwidth expansion only and is "albeit not perfect" — for Redis it is 8.6% below the best (100% DDR) allocation; desired latency counters were inaccessible ("we currently cannot access the corresponding counters"). No multi-tenant isolation or QoS mechanisms; tail-latency problems are diagnosed but not solved.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: multi-device configurations — "As future platforms accommodate more CXL memory devices, we may connect up to four CXL memory devices to a single CPU socket with eight DDR5 memory channels" (exactly the requester's 4-device regime, untested here); further enhancement of Caption is left as future work (they emphasize the need for a dynamic policy over its specific design). Untested: CXL-to-CXL device interference, per-device differentiated placement, tail-aware/QoS policies for latency-sensitive apps on CXL (they only show TPP and static splits both hurt), CXL 2.0 pooling/switching, behavior on AMD hosts, and richer performance estimators (ML models, eBPF/PMU counters are mentioned as optional improvements).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. This is the canonical real-vs-emulated CXL characterization and validates the requester's methodology of measuring on genuine silicon (their 1.53x load-to-use ratio is close to this paper's CXL-A at 35% longer ld latency; their ~35.5 GiB/s per-region bandwidth echoes the paper's finding that devices deliver only 20-47% of theoretical channel bandwidth). Critically, this paper never had more than one CXL device attached and explicitly names 4-devices-per-socket as a future configuration — the requester's 4x CZ120 testbed realizes exactly that, so per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and multi-device QoS are genuinely beyond this paper's scope and citable as open. Caption (counter-driven dynamic DDR:CXL page-ratio tuning, bandwidth-expander framing) is the closest prior policy to ObjPlace and should be treated as a page-granularity, single-device baseline/contrast; the paper's tail-latency findings (10-82% p99 inflation, TPP 174% worse) directly motivate the tail-aware direction. Caveat: its cache-hierarchy/SNC insights are Intel-SPR-specific and may not transfer to the requester's AMD Turin host.

Could not verify. CXL device vendor names, capacities, and per-device CXL link widths — deliberately anonymized/not disclosed in the paper (only controller IP type, DRAM technology, and max per-channel bandwidth are given), Final MICRO'23 camera-ready pagination (read arXiv v4, 5 Oct 2023, which states it is the accepted MICRO'23 version; DOI taken from the paper's own footnote)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We take three CXL memory devices ('CXL memory devices' in Table 1), each featuring different CXL IPs (ASIC-based hard IP and FPGA-based soft IP) and DRAM technologies (DDR5-4800, DDR4-2400, and DDR4-3200).
  • Table 1: System configurations — CXL memory devices: CXL-A | Hard IP | DDR5-4800 | 38.4 GB/s per channel; CXL-B | Hard IP | 2x DDR4-2400 | 19.2 GB/s per channel; CXL-C | Soft IP | DDR4-3200 | 25.6 GB/s per channel
  • Note that we enable the SNC mode to use only two local DDR5 memory channels along with one CXL memory channel. This is because our system can accommodate only one CXL memory device, and it needs to make a meaningful contribution to the total bandwidth of the system.
  • The server consists of two Intel Sapphire Rapids (SPR) CPU sockets. One socket is populated with eight 4800 MT/s DDR5 DRAM DIMMs (128 GB) across eight memory channels. The other socket is populated with only one 4800 MT/s DDR5 DRAM DIMM to emulate the bandwidth and capacity of CXL memory.
  • As future platforms accommodate more CXL memory devices, we may connect up to four CXL memory devices to a single CPU socket with eight DDR5 memory channels, providing the same DDR5 to CXL channel ratio as our setup.
  • (C2) True CXL memory can give up to 26% lower latency and 3−66% higher bandwidth efficiency than emulated CXL memory, depending on memory access instruction types and CXL memory devices.
  • Figure 3 shows that CXL-A exhibits only 35% longer ld latency than DDR5-R, while CXL-B and CXL-C present almost 2x and 3x longer ld latency, respectively.
  • Ubuntu 22.04.2 LTS (Linux kernel v6.2) ... 2x Intel Xeon 6430 CPUs @2.1 GHz, 32 cores and 60 MB LLC per CPU, Hyper-Threading disabled

https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.15375 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2303.15375

Tiering & placement systems (kernel/runtime) (21 papers)

The page-hotness lineage (TPP -> Memtis -> Colloid -> Nomad -> M5 -> HybridTier -> PACT'26) plus the newer object-level wave (SoarAlto, OBASE, Clove). All choose WHICH TIER; none chooses WHICH DEVICE. Note how many evaluate on emulation or one FPGA.

Clove: Object-Level CXL Memory Management in Managed Runtimes

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2605.20370) (2026) numa-emulated n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; remote NUMA socket of a dual-socket CloudLab c6420 used as the "CXL" tier)

Problem. Page-based tiered-memory management (TPP, Memtis, HybridTier) is granularity-mismatched for CXL: hot and cold objects share 4KB pages, so page-level hotness tracking and migration waste fast-tier DRAM capacity and migration bandwidth. Object-level management for CXL-tiered memory is underexplored because CXL's load/store interface and tight performance budget make per-object tracking/relocation seemingly too expensive, and prior object-level approaches target unmanaged languages with bespoke runtimes or compiler support.

Idea. Clove extends existing managed runtimes (JVM prototype) with the missing pieces for object-level tiering — profile-guided object hotness tracking and object relocation policies — reusing mechanisms managed runtimes already have (GC-style object relocation, dynamic code generation). It layers on top of an underlying page-based migration system (TPP/Memtis/HybridTier), packing hot objects onto pages so the page tier system keeps them in fast memory.

Testbed. CloudLab c6420 dual-socket machine: Intel Xeon Gold 6142 CPUs, 192 GB DDR4 per socket. CXL is emulated: application/runtime threads pinned to the local socket with taskset; the remote socket's memory serves as the slow "CXL" tier; tiering ratios enforced via local-memory ballooning (Memtis methodology). Kernel version not captured. No real CXL device of any kind.

Evaluation. Java applications on the modified JVM: Ehcache (YCSB-C plus Twitter production cache traces, 160 GB and 120 GB footprints), JGraphT PageRank (120 GB), H2 relational DB with TPC-C (120 GB); synthetic HotWarm and Zipfian(0.99) microbenchmarks (~100 GB). Tiering ratios: 10%, 20%, 50% of memory local (fast tier), enforced by ballooning on the emulated two-socket testbed.

Baselines. TPP, Memtis, HybridTier — three open-source page-based tiered-memory systems, used both as baselines and as the underlying page-migration substrate for Clove; plus an ideal oracle for fast-tier hit ratio.

Key findings. Clove reduces application slowdown by 22-84% vs page-based systems: Twitter-Large 29-59%, Twitter-Medium 43-63%, PageRank 47-84%, H2/TPC-C 22-47%; synthetic HotWarm 29-59% latency reduction at 20% local ratio. Fast-tier hit ratio 11-45 percentage points above Memtis and only 4-5 points below an oracle. Overheads: online profiler <1%; hotness tracking ~5.5% if always on, near-zero with periodic activation. Demonstrates that JVM object relocation + dynamic code generation make object-granularity tiering practical at CXL-like latencies.

Limitations. Entirely NUMA-emulated — no real CXL silicon, so real device latency/bandwidth asymmetry, on-device controller behavior, and bandwidth contention effects are untested; zero physical CXL devices, so nothing about multi-device or per-device behavior. Authors' own scoping: targets memory-intensive apps whose hot sets evolve over minutes/hours; workloads where hotness of millions/billions of objects shifts every few seconds are out of scope; off-heap memory is not managed; adaptive parameter tuning deferred to future work. JVM-only prototype; single-application runs (no multi-tenant/interference evaluation).

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL hardware (bandwidth saturation, contention) is untested. No multi-tenant or co-runner interference study. Extension beyond the JVM to other managed runtimes, off-heap/native memory, rapidly-shifting hot sets, and automatic parameter tuning are explicitly left open. Nothing on multiple CXL devices, pooling, or per-device scheduling.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Clove is the closest external competitor/companion to the requester's ObjPlace: both argue for object-granularity DRAM/CXL placement, and Clove's 22-84% wins over TPP/Memtis/HybridTier validate the object-level thesis — it must be cited and differentiated. Key differentiators for the requester: Clove is NUMA-emulated with zero real CXL devices, single-tenant, and hotness-driven only; ObjPlace runs on 4x real CZ120s and can add bandwidth-sensitivity, contention, and per-device dimensions Clove cannot see under emulation (e.g., the requester's measured 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdown). Clove says nothing about multi-device independence, cross-device interference, or QoS, leaving the candidate per-device-scheduling direction untouched.

Could not verify. Kernel version of the testbed (not surfaced in the sections read), Exact wording around ellipses in the emulation-methodology quote (extracted via fetch summarizer from the arXiv HTML)

Verifier note. Fetched both the arXiv abstract page and the full HTML (v1) this session. All three analyst quotes were found verbatim in the paper (CloudLab c6420 / Xeon Gold 6142 / 192 GB DDR4 per socket; dual-socket NUMA emulation of CXL with taskset pinning and local-memory ballooning; baselines TPP, Memtis, HybridTier). A targeted search of the full text found no mention of any physical CXL device and no second testbed, refuting none of the claims. Analyst's assessment stands: realCXL = numa-emulated, numCXLDevices = 0, deviceTreatment = n/a.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We conduct our experiments on a CloudLab c6420 machine (Duplyakin et al., 2019) equipped with Intel Xeon Gold 6142 CPUs and 192 GB DDR4 memory per socket.
  • We use a dual-socket system to emulate a CXL-based tiered memory architecture, following the methodology in prior work (Lee et al., 2023; Amaro et al., 2023; Song et al., 2025). Application and runtime threads are pinned to the local socket using taskset... tiering ratios are enforced using the local-memory ballooning mechanism used in prior work (Lee et al., 2023).
  • We evaluate three open-source, page-based tiered-memory management systems—TPP (Maruf et al., 2023), Memtis (Lee et al., 2023...), and HybridTier (Song et al., 2025)—as both baselines for comparison and as the underlying page migration systems for Clove.
  • We test three different tiering ratios, where 10%, 20%, or 50% of memory is local...
  • Target applications [are] memory-intensive applications whose hot sets evolve over minutes or hours... Extreme workloads in which the hotness of millions or billions of objects changes every few seconds are outside our scope.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20370 https://arxiv.org/html/2605.20370v1

Harvesting Sub-Microsecond CXL Memory Stalls with LiteSwitch

OSDI (2026) unknown unknown verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. CXL memory access latency is "typically 3x or more" than local DRAM, and each access is a sub-microsecond stall — too short for interrupt-based or profiling-based stall-harvesting techniques — so CPUs waste large numbers of idle cycles on CXL-induced memory stalls, degrading application performance.

Idea. Hardware-software co-design: (1) a hardware mechanism ("location-dependent memory branching", LDMB, per the dissertation abstract) that detects a CXL-induced long-latency memory stall online with near-perfect accuracy and delivers control to software via direct branching; (2) an ultra-fast software switch path (Bundled Handoff scavenger-thread selection + xstate-aware context switching that skips unnecessary SIMD/FP save/restore) that switches to another ready thread in under 20 ns — an order of magnitude faster than a conventional context switch — so stalls longer than ~200 ns can be harvested with no application changes.

Key findings. From the official abstract only: with a sufficient number of available threads per core, LiteSwitch "recovers up to 80% of the performance lost due to CXL access latency"; thread switch completes "in under 20 nanoseconds, an order of magnitude faster than conventional context switches"; harvestable stalls are sub-microsecond but ">200 nanoseconds"; no application changes required. The dissertation abstract adds that evaluation covers "representative workloads and CXL latency settings" (plural latency settings — suggesting configurable/injected CXL latency rather than one fixed real device, but this is inference, not verified).

Limitations. Full text unavailable, so limitations are partly inferred: (1) LDMB is a proposed hardware mechanism that does not exist in any shipping CPU, so the evaluation almost certainly cannot run end-to-end on production CXL silicon — deployment requires CPU-vendor changes; (2) the "up to 80%" recovery is conditioned on "a sufficient number of available threads per core", i.e., it needs abundant thread-level parallelism (scavenger threads) to harvest into; (3) evaluation spans "CXL latency settings", implying tuned/emulated latency points rather than measured multi-device hardware behavior; (4) nothing in any available abstract mentions multiple CXL devices, bandwidth contention, interference, or multi-tenancy — the focus is per-access latency hiding only.

Open questions. How LDMB would be realized in real CPUs and whether real production CXL devices (with latency ~1.5x DRAM, not 3x) still leave enough stall time above the ~200 ns harvesting threshold; behavior under bandwidth saturation and cross-tenant contention (stall durations become variable and long); interaction with placement/tiering systems that reduce CXL accesses instead of hiding them; correctness/security of sub-20 ns user-level thread switching in multi-tenant settings. All untestable here until the paper text is released.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Complementary, not competing, with ObjPlace: LiteSwitch hides CXL latency by switching threads during individual stalls, whereas ObjPlace avoids CXL accesses via object placement — a paper could cite it as the latency-hiding alternative in related work. It requires a new hardware mechanism (LDMB), so it cannot run on the requester's EPYC 9745 + CZ120 testbed, and it says nothing about per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL interference, or multi-tenant QoS — the requester's candidate direction is untouched by it. Notably, its premise is "3x or more" CXL latency, while the requester measures only 1.53x (277 ns vs 185 ns); that gap is a useful data point arguing that on real Turin+CZ120 hardware the harvestable stall window (>200 ns) is thin, and that bandwidth contention (their 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown), not per-access latency, is the bigger problem — a potential positioning angle against LiteSwitch.

Could not verify. realCXL platform (simulation vs FPGA vs emulation vs real device) — the proposed hardware mechanism LDMB implies non-production hardware, and 'CXL latency settings' in the dissertation abstract suggests injected/configurable latency, but the methodology section is unreadable, numCXLDevices and deviceTreatment — never stated in any accessible source, hardwareEnv: CPU model, DRAM, CXL device model/capacity, kernel version, evalSetup: which workloads were used and how, baselines compared against, all numeric results beyond the abstract's 80% / <20 ns / >200 ns / 3x figures

Verifier note. Primary sources actually fetched this session: (1) USENIX OSDI'26 presentation page via curl (Cloudflare blocked WebFetch) — full abstract retrieved; all three analyst quotes verified verbatim against it. (2) Princeton CS FPO page for Nanqinqin Li's dissertation 'System Idle Time Need Not Be Wasted' (defense 2026-05-07) — adds LiteSwitch design detail (LDMB hardware mechanism, Bundled Handoff, xstate-aware context switching) but no testbed specifics. (3) linanqinqin.github.io and asafcidon.com/publications.html — no PDF link for this paper. (4) arXiv API search for 'LiteSwitch' — 0 results. (5) Probed usenix.org/system/files/osdi26-li-nanqinqin.pdf variants — 404 (PDFs presumably post at conference start, 2026-07-13). The analyst reported the three testbed fields as unknown; I could not refute or resolve them because the paper body is not yet publicly available anywhere. Caution for later re-check: the proposed hardware mechanism (LDMB) and the phrase 'CXL latency settings' suggest the evaluation may be simulation/emulation-based rather than on real CXL silicon — worth re-verifying once the OSDI PDF drops during/after 2026-07-13.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • USENIX abstract: "However, CXL memory incurs sub-microsecond latency that is typically 3× or more compared to local memory, exacerbating memory-induced CPU stalls and degrading application performance."
  • USENIX abstract: "(1) a hardware mechanism that precisely identifies a CXL-induced memory stall and exposes it to software with near-perfect accuracy and minimal overhead; (2) an ultra-fast software path that switches to another ready thread for harvesting in under 20 nanoseconds, an order of magnitude faster than conventional context switches."
  • USENIX abstract: "The evaluation demonstrates that with a sufficient number of available threads per core, LiteSwitch recovers up to 80% of the performance lost due to CXL access latency, enabling the adoption of CXL memory without prohibitive slowdowns."
  • Princeton FPO abstract: "On the hardware side, location-dependent memory branching (LDMB) detects long-latency accesses online and delivers control via direct branching. On the software side, Bundled Handoff provides fast scavenger selection, and xstate-aware context switching avoids unnecessary SIMD/FP save/restore overhead. Evaluation shows substantial slowdown reductions across representative workloads and CXL latency settings."
  • Yuhong Zhong CV: "Harvesting Sub-Microsecond CXL Memory Stalls with LiteSwitch / Nanqinqin Li, Yuhong Zhong, Asaf Cidon, Michael J. Freedman / OSDI 2026 ... Acceptance rate: 20%"
