MDK: Rethinking the Data Center Memory Reclamation Problem

OSDI '26 (USENIX), session "Memory Tiering and CXL" (2026) unknown n/a verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. Traditional memory-management tooling (OPT/Belady, Miss Ratio Curves, efficient MRC generation) optimizes application performance under a fixed memory budget. Data centers face the inverted problem: maximize the number of co-located jobs per server without violating performance SLOs by proactively reclaiming memory from running jobs; the traditional tools do not apply to this flipped optimization problem.

Idea. MDK (Memory Designer's Kit): a framework for designing and evaluating data-center memory reclamation policies, comprising (1) an offline provably optimal reclamation policy (a bound analogous to OPT), (2) Memory Performance Curves (MPCs) that show how memory savings vary when constrained by a performance target (the dual of MRCs), and (3) an efficient MPC-generation technique up to 208x faster than simulation. Used to design three new data-center reclamation policies.

Testbed. Not verifiable — full paper embargoed until OSDI '26 (July 13-15, 2026). Abstract gives no testbed; the "208x faster than simulation" phrasing suggests a largely trace-/simulation-driven methodology, but this is inference, not verified fact. No CXL hardware is mentioned anywhere in the abstract.

Evaluation. Not verifiable from available sources. Abstract states only that they "demonstrate MDK's utility by developing three data center policies" evaluated for average memory savings against a state-of-the-art policy; workloads, traces, and infrastructure are not disclosed in the abstract.

Baselines. Abstract mentions comparison against "a state-of-the-art policy" (unnamed in abstract; plausibly Google's proactive-reclamation/zswap lineage given the author list, but unverified) and against simulation for MPC-generation speed.

Key findings. From abstract only: MDK's MPC-generation technique is up to 208x faster than simulation; the three MDK-designed policies improve average memory savings by up to 10% relative to a state-of-the-art policy; the offline policy is provably optimal for the data-center reclamation objective (maximize savings subject to SLO), establishing an upper bound analogous to OPT/Belady in the traditional setting.

Limitations. Full text unreadable, so authors' own admissions unknown. From what is visible: the abstract never mentions CXL, tiered hardware, or any physical testbed, so this appears to be a policy/modeling framework paper (analytic + trace/simulation) rather than a CXL hardware evaluation; whether policies were validated on real machines, with real far-memory backends (zswap/CXL), or under multi-tenant interference cannot be determined.

Open questions. Unknown from abstract: whether MPCs and the optimal policy account for reclamation-target heterogeneity (compressed RAM vs disk vs CXL tier), for bandwidth interference between co-located jobs, or for tail-latency SLOs vs average performance; whether the framework extends to placement (promotion/demotion) rather than pure reclamation; online applicability of the offline optimal policy.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Conceptually relevant, not hardware-relevant: MDK gives no evidence of touching real CXL devices, so it says nothing about the requester's 4x CZ120 per-device questions. Its useful import is methodological — Memory Performance Curves (memory savings as a function of a performance constraint) and a provably optimal offline bound are exactly the kind of tooling ObjPlace lacks for justifying placement decisions; an analogous 'DRAM-savings vs slowdown' curve per object class, or an offline-optimal placement bound, would strengthen an ObjPlace-successor paper's evaluation. It also signals that OSDI reviewers value flipped-objective formulations (maximize consolidation subject to SLO), which maps directly onto the candidate multi-tenant per-CXL-device QoS direction. Re-check the full PDF after July 13, 2026 to confirm whether reclamation targets include a CXL/far tier.

Could not verify. Full paper text (embargoed until OSDI '26, July 13-15, 2026; usenix.org PDF returns 404, no arXiv preprint, not on author homepage), realCXL / numCXLDevices / deviceTreatment - no testbed information available, hardwareEnv: CPU, DRAM, kernel, any CXL hardware, evalSetup: workloads, traces, how policies were run, baselines: name of the 'state-of-the-art policy' compared against, authors' stated limitations and future work, whether reclamation destination is zswap/disk/CXL far tier

Verifier note. Confidence: high for title/venue/session (read directly from usenix.org HTML fetched via curl; WebFetch was 403-blocked by Cloudflare but curl with a browser UA succeeded). Low for the three testbed fields — couldNotVerify: realCXL, numCXLDevices, deviceTreatment. The analyst's evidence quotes match the abstract verbatim (author list, 'proactively reclaim memory... flips the optimization problem', 'Memory Designer's Kit... Memory Performance Curves (MPCs)... efficient techniqu[e]'), confirming the analyst only had abstract access, which explains unknown/unknown/n/a. The abstract frames MDK as a trace/simulation policy-design framework ('up to 208x faster than simulation', policies improving memory savings vs a state-of-the-art policy) with zero mention of CXL hardware, so 'n/a' is plausible but unproven. Sources fetched: https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/patel , https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/technical-sessions , arXiv API queries, https://shauryapatel1995.github.io/ , https://sherlockwu.github.io/ . Recommend re-checking usenix.org for the open-access PDF after the conference (OSDI '26 proceedings front-matter files exist but per-paper PDFs are not yet posted).

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • MDK: Rethinking the Data Center Memory Reclamation Problem. Shaurya Patel, Google and University of British Columbia; Suli Yang and Yawen Wang, Google; Kan Wu, xAI; Alexandra (Sasha) Fedorova, University of British Columbia and MongoDB; Margo Seltzer, University of British Columbia; Kimberly Keeton, Google
  • Since a key constraint for placing additional jobs is memory, data center systems proactively reclaim memory from running jobs to create space for new jobs. This difference fundamentally flips the optimization problem that memory management policies need to address.
  • We present the Memory Designer's Kit, MDK, a framework for designing and evaluating data center memory management policies. MDK includes an offline provably optimal policy; Memory Performance Curves (MPCs), which show how memory savings vary when constrained by performance; and an efficient technique that is up to 208× faster than simulation for producing MPCs. We demonstrate MDK's utility by developing three data center policies that improve average memory savings by up to 10% relative to a state-of-the-art policy.
  • NOTE: the word 'CXL' does not appear anywhere in the paper's official abstract; the session title 'Memory Tiering and CXL' is the only CXL association. No testbed, device count, or hardware description is available before the embargo lifts.

https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/patel https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/technical-sessions https://shauryapatel1995.github.io/

OBASE: Object-Based Address-Space Engineering to Improve Memory Tiering

OSDI 2026 (also arXiv:2603.00378) (2026) other-hw n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL hardware; slow tier is 4x 128GB Intel Optane DC Persistent Memory 100 modules exposed as a separate NUMA node)

Problem. Hotness fragmentation: memory allocators in unmanaged languages (C/C++) place objects by size class, not access pattern, so hot and cold objects are interleaved within the same 4KB pages. A single hot cache line marks a whole page active, trapping cold bytes in DRAM; the authors' analysis of six Google production workload traces shows up to 97% of bytes in active pages are cold and unreclaimable, defeating page-granularity tiering/reclamation backends (TPP, AutoNUMA, Memtis, kswapd, cgroups, TMO).

Idea. "Address-space engineering": a compiler-runtime system (OBASE) that dynamically reorganizes virtual memory so hot objects cluster into uniformly hot pages and cold objects into uniformly cold pages. Objects are accessed via 'guides' (tagged indirection pointers, 16 upper bits repurposed for hotness/lock metadata); pointer instrumentation tracks per-object access; an Object Collector scans every 120s and migrates objects between NEW/HOT/COLD virtual heaps using a lock-free two-phase CAS protocol safe under concurrency (concurrent access vetoes migration via CAS failure). OBASE acts as an object-aware frontend for unmodified page-aware OS backends; a feedback controller adapts the cold threshold to hold promotion rate at 1%.

Testbed. Intel Xeon Gold 5218 (16 cores, SMT disabled, performance governor), Ubuntu 22.04, Linux kernel 6.12. Fast tier: 2x 16GB DDR4-2400 DRAM. Slow tier: 4x 128GB Intel Optane DC Persistent Memory 100 modules at 2666 MHz, configured in Memory Mode and exposed as a separate NUMA node to emulate first-generation CXL memory (~2.5x DRAM latency). All six memory devices on distinct channels. Swap experiments use a 512GB Intel P4800X Optane SSD. No real CXL device anywhere in the testbed.

Evaluation. CrestDB, an in-memory KV store the authors built integrating ten concurrent data structures (hash tables, skiplists, B+Trees, ART, MassTree, etc., many from ASCYLIB), 6 server + 6 client threads over Unix domain sockets. Workloads: YCSB A/B/C with Zipfian keys (10M keys, 30B keys / 1024B values, 13 GiB; tiering runs use 50M keys, 67 GiB), plus four production trace replays: Meta CacheLib, Meta DBench Mixgraph, Twitter Cluster 7 and Cluster 23. Tiering experiments vary DRAM:slow-tier ratio 1:4 / 1:8 / 1:16 by shrinking DRAM budget; scalability measured 2-32 threads.

Baselines. Six OS backends run with vs. without OBASE as frontend: reclamation - Linux kswapd, cgroup memory limits, Meta TMO (PSI-based); tiering - TPP, Linux AutoNUMA, Memtis (PEBS-based). Also an uninstrumented DRAM-only baseline for overhead, and a CXL(slow-tier)-only baseline for tiering normalization. Object-level far-memory systems AIFM and MIRA are discussed but explicitly NOT compared ("they target custom runtimes").

Key findings. Page utilization improves 2-4x (baseline 18-20% to 40-80%) across all ten data structures; RSS drops 65-72% (12.4 GiB to 3.5-4.0 GiB for YCSB-B 10M keys). With OBASE all reclamation backends reach the aggressive 4 GiB RSS with no throughput loss (without it, cgroup loses 38% throughput; TMO stalls at 6.5 GiB). Tiering: hot set compacts from 16.3 GiB @21% utilization to 6.33 GiB @57%; TPP gains 16% at 1:16 (1.25x to 1.45x), AutoNUMA gains 12-29%, Memtis 3-10%; OBASE at ratio 1:X roughly matches baseline at 1:(X/2), i.e. same performance with half the DRAM. Runtime overhead 2-5% throughput (avg 2.5%), +5% p90 latency, flat from 2 to 32 threads; guide dereference RMW costs 4-5ns. Production traces: 1.8-3.4x utilization gain, 36-58% RSS reduction, +15-30% savings on top of TMO; controller converges in ~25 min and re-converges after workload shifts.

Limitations. Authors' own: requires developer annotation of relocatable pointers and applies only to pointer-based data structures; hardware-aware tuning of the promotion-rate target is left to future work; OBASE cannot determine which tier a page occupies (backend-agnostic by necessity). Methodology weaknesses: NO real CXL hardware - slow tier is Optane PMEM 100 declared "representative of emerging CXL-attached memory deployments"; single old 16-core Xeon Gold 5218 host; evaluation entirely on the authors' own CrestDB KV store (no unmodified real applications end-to-end); single-tenant runs only, no interference or bandwidth-contention study; Optane emulates latency but not CXL bandwidth characteristics; no comparison against object-level runtimes (AIFM/MIRA); 2-4x/70% headline numbers are on synthetic YCSB, production traces show smaller 36-58% reductions.

Open questions. How OBASE behaves on real CXL silicon where bandwidth (not just latency) is the constraint; hardware-aware promotion-rate tuning (explicitly punted); multi-tenant / co-runner interference; whether annotation burden is acceptable for large real codebases (only CrestDB evaluated); extension to multiple slow tiers or per-device placement (the paper treats the slow tier as one monolithic NUMA node); interaction with huge pages; applicability beyond pointer-based KV structures (arrays, ML tensors).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly validates ObjPlace's core premise from an independent OSDI'26 team: object-granularity (not page-granularity) hotness management is the right frontend for DRAM/CXL tiering, and their 97%-cold-bytes Google analysis is a strong motivation citation. However, OBASE is latency/capacity-driven and evaluated only on Optane-emulated CXL on one Xeon with one monolithic slow NUMA node - it never touches real CXL silicon, bandwidth contention, or multiple devices, exactly the gaps the requester's CZ120 testbed exposes (35.5 GiB/s per-region saturation, 2x co-runner slowdown). ObjPlace must differentiate on bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement on real hardware, and the candidate per-device/interference direction is untouched by OBASE, which explicitly cannot even tell which tier a page occupies. Its lock-free object-migration protocol and tagged-guide indirection are reusable mechanism ideas if ObjPlace needs safe concurrent object relocation.

Could not verify. Official USENIX OSDI '26 page returned HTTP 403, so session name ('Memory Tiering and CXL') and camera-ready differences vs. the arXiv version (submitted 2026-02-27) could not be checked; all evaluation details are from the arXiv full text., Author affiliations beyond footnote 'Work done at Google' (search results say UW-Madison, Google, xAI) not verified from the paper itself.

Verifier note. Primary source read directly: arXiv PDF (2603.00378v1) downloaded and text-extracted with pdftotext; Section 5.1 Experimental Setup read verbatim. All three analyst quotes match the paper text verbatim (their truncated quotes complete as in the PDF; the 97% claim appears in the abstract/intro: \"Our analysis of Google production workloads shows that up to 97% of the bytes in active pages are cold and unreclaimable.\"). One nuance: the evaluation figures/text refer to the slow tier as \"CXL\" (e.g., \"CXL-only\", \"DRAM:CXL ratio\"), which could mislead a skimmer into thinking real CXL hardware was used — it is Optane PMem in Memory Mode exposed as a separate NUMA node. No second testbed exists. Sources: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00378 , https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.00378 , https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/banakar

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • All experiments run on an Intel Xeon Gold 5218 (16 cores, SMT disabled) configured in performance governor mode with Ubuntu 22.04 and Linux kernel 6.12. The memory subsystem comprises two tiers: a fast tier of 2×16 GB DDR4 DRAM modules at 2400 MHz, and a slow tier of 4×128 GB Intel Optane DC Persistent Memory 100 modules at 2666 MHz.
  • We configure Optane PMEM in Memory Mode and expose it as a separate NUMA node, creating a two-tier hierarchy representative of emerging CXL-attached memory deployments [40, 42]. The Optane tier provides approximately 2.5× higher access latency than local DRAM [55], consistent with first-generation CXL memory characteristics [49].
  • Our analysis of Google production workloads shows that up to 97% of the bytes in active pages are cold and unreclaimable.
  • We evaluate OBASE with ten concurrent pointer-based data structures and six state-of-the-art reclamation and tiering backends [14, 38, 42, 51, 53]. Using key-value store workloads (YCSB) and production traces (Meta, Twitter), we demonstrate that OBASE improves page utilization (by 2-4x) and achieves higher memory savings (up to 70%)
  • The approach has limitations: it requires developer annotation of relocatable pointers and applies only to pointer-based structures.
  • The optimal promotion rate is hardware-dependent: faster secondary tiers can tolerate higher cold-access fractions, while much slower tiers may require more conservative thresholds; exploring such hardware-aware tuning is left to future work.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00378 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2603.00378 https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/banakar (403-blo

PACT: A Criticality-First Design for Tiered Memory

ASPLOS 2026 (31st ACM ASPLOS, Volume 2; Best Paper honorable mention per search snippet) (2026) numa-emulated n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; CXL emulated by uncore-throttling the remote NUMA socket of a dual-socket Skylake to 190ns)

Problem. Existing tiered-memory systems place/migrate pages by access frequency ("hotness"), but frequency does not predict actual performance cost: pages with identical access frequency can differ in stall contribution by up to 65x because MLP/latency-tolerance varies. Coarse per-core stall counters lack the per-page, per-tier granularity needed to place pages by true performance impact.

Idea. Introduce PAC (Page Access Criticality): estimate each page's contribution to LLC-miss-induced CPU stalls online via an MLP-aware analytical model using only 4 standard CPU performance counters (per-tier stalls modeled from LLC misses, latency, and TOR-occupancy-derived MLP; Pearson >0.98), attributing 20ms stall windows proportionally to PEBS-sampled page accesses. PACT is a Linux 5.15 tiering system where PAC drives placement: eager demotion (via MGLRU, proactively freeing fast-tier space) plus adaptive promotion using dynamic binning over a hash table + priority queues, avoiding global sorting and static hot/cold thresholds. Samples only slow-tier load accesses; ~25 bytes per tracked 4KB page (0.6% overhead).

Testbed. Dual-socket Intel Skylake server on CloudLab; each socket a 10-core Xeon at 2.2GHz with 96GB DDR4 (52GB/s, 90ns local; cross-socket NUMA 32GB/s, 140ns). CXL emulated by reducing remote-NUMA-node uncore frequency to raise latency to 190ns (2.1x DRAM). Implemented on Linux 5.15 (incorporating modules/fixes from TPP). No real CXL silicon anywhere in the paper.

Evaluation. 13 memory-intensive workloads: graph analytics (GAPBS), GPT-2 inference, in-memory databases, SPEC CPU 2017 HPC; 8 threads each, RSS 6.6-40GB, bandwidth demand 6-45GB/s. Varied 7 fast/slow tier ratios from 8:1 to 1:8, 4KB vs THP, memory pressure, plus sensitivity analyses and a controlled colocation microbenchmark (Figure 12). Metric: runtime slowdown normalized to DRAM-only ideal; also migration counts. Motivation study profiled Masim, GUPS, tc-twitter on the 190ns emulated tier; model validation over 96 workloads across three latency configs (90/140/190ns).

Baselines. Soar and Alto, Memtis, Colloid, Nomad, TPP, Linux NUMA Balancing Tiering (NBT), plus NoTier (first-touch) and DRAM-only/CXL-only reference lines.

Key findings. Frequency-identical pages differ in criticality by up to 65x. PACT achieves up to 61% improvement over the second-best system across 13 workloads and 7 tier ratios; when not best, it trails the winner by 4.1% on average (max 11.8%). Up to 50x (50.1x/40.6x cited) fewer migrations and up to 10.4x fewer promotions than second-best; outperforms Colloid by up to 33% and Nomad by over 500% across 12 workloads; average improvements 9.95% (1:2 ratio) and 10.66% (2:1) with peaks of 57%/61%. Under colocation microbenchmarks PACT still beats prior policies. Per-tier stall model reaches Pearson 0.98 vs 0.82-0.89 for raw LLC-miss counts.

Limitations. Authors admit: proportional stall attribution breaks down when heterogeneous access streams share a tier (e.g., multi-tenant CXL memory) — latency-bound and streaming accesses get frequency-diluted criticality; finer-grained stall observability is left as future work, as is latency-weighted attribution and systematic exploration of PAC cooling mechanisms. Methodology weaknesses: entirely NUMA-emulated CXL (uncore throttling to 190ns) on an old dual-socket Skylake — no real CXL device, so no CXL-specific bandwidth ceilings, controller queuing, or head-of-line effects; single slow tier only (no multi-device or per-device treatment); PEBS-dependent (Intel-only counters, e.g., TOR occupancy); demotion delegated to LRU rather than PAC-driven; single-workload runs except one colocation microbenchmark.

Open questions. PAC estimation under multi-tenant/colocated tiers (explicitly punted, needs finer stall attribution such as latency-weighted PAC or CXL 3.2 CHMU-based sampling). Behavior on real CXL ASICs where bandwidth contention (not just latency) dominates is untested. Multiple slow tiers / multiple independently managed CXL devices are never considered — the design assumes one fast and one slow tier. Portability beyond Intel PEBS/TOR counters (e.g., AMD) is unaddressed.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant as the state-of-the-art frequency-vs-criticality tiering baseline for ObjPlace: PAC's insight (bandwidth/latency-tolerance, not hotness, drives cost) aligns directly with ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity/skew/phase signals, but PACT works at page granularity in-kernel while ObjPlace works at object granularity in userspace — a clean positioning contrast. Critically, PACT is evaluated only on uncore-throttled NUMA emulation with zero real CXL devices and a single slow tier, so it never sees the requester's measured CXL bandwidth cliffs (35.5 GiB/s per region, 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown) or per-device effects. The authors themselves flag multi-tenant/colocated CXL as an open observability gap — exactly the per-device interference and QoS direction the requester is considering on 4x CZ120, so PACT strengthens rather than closes that gap. Note PACT is PEBS/TOR-counter (Intel) based; porting its stall model to the requester's AMD EPYC 9745 (IBS instead of PEBS) is nontrivial and a potential differentiator.

Verifier note. Fetched the primary-source PDF from the author's Virginia Tech page (people.cs.vt.edu/jinshu/docs/papers/PACT_ASPLOS.pdf) and extracted full text with pdftotext. The testbed section (§5.1) matches the analyst's quotes exactly. No physical CXL device anywhere in the paper — CXL is emulated by reducing remote NUMA node uncore frequency on a dual-socket Skylake CloudLab server, raising latency to 190ns; the profiling section (§2) likewise uses 'an emulated CXL device with 190ns latency'. Searched for a second testbed / FPGA / ASIC / real CXL hardware: none present. Could not independently confirm the 'Best Paper honorable mention' claim from the PDF itself (not part of the checked fields).

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Testbed. We evaluate PACT on a dual-socket Intel Skylake server on CloudLab [15]. Each socket contains a 10-core Xeon CPU (2.2GHz) with 96GB DDR4, providing 52GB/s bandwidth and 90ns access latency for local memory access. Cross-socket NUMA accesses exhibit 32GB/s bandwidth and 140ns latency. To emulate CXL memory, we reduce remote NUMA node uncore frequency, increasing access latency to 190ns (2.1× DRAM latency), consistent with measured CXL device characteristics and prior work [30, 32, 37, 51, 53].
  • We use three memory configurations: local DRAM (90ns), NUMA (140ns), and simulated CXL with CPUless NUMA (190ns).
  • we profile three applications on an emulated CXL device with 190ns latency (§5): Masim [9], GUPS [4], and tc-twitter from GAPBS [13].
  • The limitations of proportional attribution arise when multiple, heterogeneous memory access patterns are colocated within the same memory tier, such as multi-tenant CXL memory deployments.
  • We implement PACT on Linux 5.15 and incorporate several modules from TPP [37], including fixes to improve the reliability of LRU-based page demotion.
  • Across these settings, PACT achieves strong performance improvements, by up to 61% over the second-best system. In cases where PACT is not the top performer, it closely tracks the best-performing baseline, with an average gap of 4.1% and a maximum gap of 11.8%.

https://people.cs.vt.edu/jinshu/docs/papers/PACT_ASPLOS.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3779212.3790198

SIDLE: Tree-structure Aware Indexes for CXL-based Heterogeneous Memory

PVLDB Vol 19, No. 7 (VLDB 2026), pp. 1499-1515, doi:10.14778/3801059.3801065 (2026) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. primary testbed: 1 (one 32 GiB CXL 1.1 memory expansion card, Montage MXC controller [ref 14]); large-scale scalability testbed: 0 (downclocked remote NUMA emulation on a 1 TB dual-socket machine)

Problem. Tree-structure indexes placed on CXL heterogeneous memory suffer ~70% slowdowns because CXL has ~2x latency / ~60% bandwidth of DRAM, and existing page-level CXL-HM placement schemes mismatch the granularity of small tree nodes, while prior NUMA/RDMA/PM index techniques don't transfer to CXL.

Idea. SIDLE: a CXL-native, tree-structure-aware, node-grained data placement scheme/framework that matches the tree's inherent characteristics (layered structure, path-correlated hotness, node size hierarchy) to CXL-HM features. It does layer-aware allocation (upper/internal nodes preferentially in fast memory), node-granularity hotness tracking and migration that propagates along tree paths (demoting a node queues its parent; promotions follow hot paths), adaptive thresholds via a "hyper watermark" (high/low watermarks 95%/85%) that tunes fast-memory usage online, and lightweight background workers (migration trigger, cooler, watermark maintainer; 5 threads) with optimistic concurrency control for migration correctness (TLA+-verified). Developers integrate existing indexes (B+ tree, ART/PACTree-style, Masstree) with the SIDLE framework.

Testbed. Primary: Intel Xeon Platinum 8468V (48 cores, 395 MiB CPU cache), 32 GiB DRAM + 32 GiB CXL-attached DRAM behind one CXL 1.1 memory expansion card (Montage MXC CXL Memory eXpander Controller, ref [14]); single socket used (remote NUMA socket excluded as it is another slow tier). Link width and kernel version not stated in the text I read. Secondary (only for datasets > ~400M records): 1 TB dual-socket machine emulating CXL via downclocked remote NUMA tuned to comparable latency/bandwidth.

Evaluation. Workloads: YCSB (multiple mixes: Read Only, Read Mostly, Update Heavy, With Insert, workloads A-F, short/long ranges), TPC-C for database tasks, and Alibaba Block Traces as a real-world production workload. Indexes integrated: B+ tree, radix tree (S-ART) and S-Masstree. Default 20M records with fast memory capped at 20% of total footprint (following MEMTIS/HeMem/Colloid practice); sensitivity sweeps over fast-memory ratio and dataset size 10M-4B records; concurrency scaling to 48 threads; SIDLE-Prophet (oracle pre-placement of hot paths, ~10% fast memory) used as ideal reference and its footprint as the fair budget for all systems.

Baselines. Baseline = default weighted allocation; TPP; MEMTIS; Caption; Random migration; PAC-L (optimized PACTree); Mass-L (optimized Masstree); SIDLE-Prophet oracle.

Key findings. Up to 71% higher throughput and up to 81% lower P99 latency vs SOTA placement (MEMTIS) and HM-optimized indexes (PACTree) on YCSB/real workloads. TPC-C: up to 133% higher throughput and 67% lower P99 than MEMTIS. Microbenchmarks: S-Masstree beats weighted-allocation baseline by 50.4-82.2%; S-ART/S-Masstree outperform baseline by 31.7-83.6% and 30.8-56.6% across YCSB mixes; Alibaba traces: up to 90.3% over baseline throughput, P99 down up to 81.2% vs MEMTIS and 63.2% vs TPP. Layer-aware allocation alone adds 2.8-28.7% throughput. Adaptive (hyper watermark) thresholds gain 1.7-16.8% over static. Scales to 48 threads (28.6-38.1x single-thread) with negligible background-worker overhead; stable from 10M to 4B records.

Limitations. Authors' own: SIDLE targets only software-visible cache-coherent CXL memory with latency in the same order as DRAM — ill-suited to hardware-managed-caching CXL devices (e.g., Intel Flat Memory Mode) or non-CC devices; currently two-tier only (multi-layer hierarchies left as extension); mainly targets low memory-contention scenarios (defers contention handling to Colloid); software access sampling has overhead, PEBS left as future work. Methodological: only ONE small (32 GiB) CXL 1.1 device — no multi-device, no interleaving, no cross-device or multi-tenant interference study; large-scale results rely on downclocked-NUMA emulation, not real CXL; single-socket only; no bandwidth-contention/co-runner experiments; index-specific (trees only), requires index code integration rather than transparent placement.

Open questions. Behavior under memory bandwidth contention/co-located tenants (explicitly punted to Colloid); multi-layer/multi-device tiers (only sketched: rank tiers and apply pairwise); hardware sampling (PEBS) integration; CXL 2.0/3.0 switched or pooled topologies never tested despite citing 1.5-4x latency across generations; interaction of node-grained migration with page-level OS tiering running concurrently for non-index data.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. SIDLE is the strongest published validation of the requester's ObjPlace thesis from the database side: it argues explicitly that page-granularity CXL placement (TPP/MEMTIS/Caption) mismatches object (tree-node) granularity and shows large wins from object-structure-aware, node-grained placement on real CXL silicon — directly overlapping with ObjPlace's object-granularity claim, so it must be positioned against (it is index-specific and requires code integration, whereas ObjPlace is a general runtime). It offers nothing on the candidate multi-device direction: one 32 GiB CXL 1.1 card, single-device, no interleaving, and it explicitly assumes low memory contention and defers bandwidth-contention handling to Colloid — exactly the regime the requester's 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown measurements target. The requester's 4x CZ120 testbed could both stress-test SIDLE-style placement under contention and extend it to per-device scheduling, a gap SIDLE leaves open.

Could not verify. CXL link width (x8/x16) not stated in the paper text, Linux kernel version not stated in the sections I extracted, Exact vendor of the memory expansion card module (only the Montage MXC controller is cited)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Testbed. We run all experiments on Intel® Xeon® Platinum 8468V CPUs (48 cores), equipped with 395 MiB CPU cache, 32 GiB DRAM, and 32 GiB CXL-attached DRAM (connected through a CXL 1.1 memory expansion card [14]), except the large scale dataset scalability test (§6.4 part2). Since the remote NUMA-node is another tier of slow memory besides CXL, all experiments use a single socket
  • [14] 2024. CXL® Memory eXpander Controller (MXC). https://www.montagetech.com/MXC.
  • Datasets exceeding our CXL testbed’s memory limit (~400 M records) are evaluated on a 1 TB dual-socket machine, where we emulate CXL memory using downclocked remote NUMA [79] to achieve comparable performance (Figure 1(c)).
  • Evaluations show that SIDLE improves throughput by up to 71% and reduces P99 latency by up to 81% compared with state-of-the-art data placement schemes (e.g., MEMTIS) and HM-optimized tree-structure indexes (e.g., PACTree) in YCSB and real-world workloads.
  • SIDLE’s Scope and Limitations. SIDLE mainly targets CXL devices with software-visible, cache-coherent (CC) memory whose latency is within the same order of magnitude as local DRAM. SIDLE is ill-suited for CXL devices with hardware-managed caching [156] or lacking CC.
  • Note that SIDLE mainly targets scenarios with low memory contention; Colloid [130] may further enhance SIDLE when contention occurs.

TierBPF: Page Migration Admission Control for Tiered Memory via eBPF

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2604.12300) (2026) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. CXL testbed: 1 (single CXL Type-3 memory expansion device, 128 GB DDR4, PCIe 5.0; vendor/model not stated); PMEM testbed: 0 (Optane, no CXL)

Problem. Existing kernel memory-tiering systems (AutoNUMA, TPP, Colloid, MEMTIS) make page migration decisions that ignore (1) page size — migrating whole 2 MB THPs wastes scarce fast-tier capacity when intra-THP access is skewed, while splitting to 4 KB destroys TLB coverage — and (2) the underlying memory architecture — e.g., CXL's full-duplex PCIe link vs. Optane PMEM's half-duplex DDR bus means a promotion policy tuned for one substrate can hurt on another. Per-page profiling (MEMTIS-style) also has runtime/memory overhead that scales with working-set size (up to 15% runtime overhead cited).

Idea. Add three eBPF hooks on the kernel's NUMA migration path — mTHP split-order selection, subpage hot/cold classification, and duplex-aware migration admission — so users can load custom, architecture-aware migration policies without kernel modification. Profiling runs entirely in-kernel as an eBPF program attached to hardware-event sampling (no perf ring buffer / user-space round trip), using blocked counting Bloom filters and per-THP heat histograms whose overhead is independent of working-set size. Policies: split THPs to intermediate mTHP sizes and migrate only hot subfolios (entropy-based uniformity check falls back to whole-THP migration); on CXL, defer promotion of pages whose read/write class does not match the dominant direction of current CXL traffic (BalanceTraffic).

Testbed. Two servers. (1) CXL server (default): dual-socket AMD EPYC 9555 (64 cores/socket), 8x32 GB DDR5 DRAM per socket; Socket 0 connects to one CXL Type-3 Memory Expansion device (128 GB DDR4) over PCIe 5.0 (full-duplex); only Socket 0 used to isolate migration effects from cross-socket NUMA. AMD IBS for hardware event sampling at 4,000 samples/sec per CPU. (2) PMEM server: dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6252 (24 cores/socket), 6x16 GB DDR4 DRAM fast tier + 6x128 GB Intel Optane PMEM slow tier per socket. Linux kernel v6.12 (vanilla for AutoNUMA; TPP and Colloid re-implemented on v6.12 with ~270 and additional LOC).

Evaluation. 17 memory-intensive benchmarks: 12 GAPBS graph workloads (bc/bfs/cc/pr on Twitter, uniform-random, Web graphs), Silo-YCSB in-memory DB, and 4 NPB HPC kernels (LU, SP, CG, MG; working sets 12-76 GB). GAPBS/NPB run with 12 threads, Silo with 8; cgroup-based CPU/memory limits for reproducibility. Bandwidth-contention experiments run a non-temporal-store antagonist pinned to separate cores on the CXL/slow tier at ~0, 5, 10, 20 GB/s. THP enabled (always/always/inherit), numa_balancing=2, demotion enabled.

Baselines. Integrated into and compared against unmodified AutoNUMA (Linux v6.12), TPP (re-implemented on v6.12), and Colloid (TPP-based, with AMD Data Fabric counters replacing Intel CHA monitoring) on the CXL server; additionally compared against MEMTIS on the PMEM server (MEMTIS cannot run on the AMD CXL server because it needs Intel PEBS; AMD IBS lacks equivalent counters).

Key findings. On the CXL server, mTHP-splitting TierBPF improves geomean throughput across 17 workloads by 12.3% (AutoNUMA), 17.7% (TPP), 7.4% (Colloid); individual gains up to 30% (bc-twitter on AutoNUMA), 29% (CG on TPP), up to 75% cited across all experiments. Splitting benefits are contention-dependent: geomean gain is only 3.4% at 0 GB/s and -0.1% at 5 GB/s background CXL traffic, 7.0% at 10 GB/s, and clear/consistent at 20 GB/s. The duplex-aware admission policy (BalanceTraffic) gives 5.2%/4.7% geomean under moderate 10 GB/s contention but is counterproductive under 20 GB/s (Split+BalanceTraffic drops TPP's gain from 17.7% to 1.8%). On PMEM, TierBPF-enhanced AutoNUMA beats baseline by 7.1% geomean and outperforms MEMTIS on 11 of 15 workloads (up to 26%); MEMTIS's 4 KB all-or-nothing splitting degrades MG.D by 7% and LU.D by 8% via TLB-miss inflation (3.26 vs 2.90 vs 2.22 L2 DTLB misses/1K instr for MEMTIS/TierBPF/AutoNUMA on LU.D).

Limitations. Authors' own: two-tier configurations only — "more than two memory tiers or CXL-SSD ... could be implemented in the future"; BalanceTraffic admission control hurts under high (20 GB/s) contention and gives only modest standalone gains (3.2% geomean on TPP, 10 of 17 workloads degrade without Split); MEMTIS comparison only possible on the Intel/PMEM box. Evident from methodology: only ONE physical CXL device (vendor/model never named) — no multi-device, cross-device, or pooled scenarios; single-socket runs only (cross-socket NUMA deliberately excluded); no multi-tenant experiments (contention is a synthetic streaming antagonist, not co-located tenants); single-benchmark-at-a-time runs; no LLM or latency-SLO workloads; no tail-latency metrics (throughput only); Silo crashes at 12 threads (run at 8).

Open questions. Extension to more than two tiers and CXL-SSD substrates is explicitly punted to future work. Untested: per-device policies when multiple CXL devices are present; interaction of the admission hook with real multi-tenant contention rather than a synthetic stressor; how BalanceTraffic should adapt or disable itself as contention crosses the ~10-20 GB/s regime (they show it flips from helpful to harmful but leave the adaptive policy open); behavior on GPU/inference workloads; whether the duplex-aware policy holds on multi-headed or switched CXL topologies.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant as mechanism, not as multi-device precedent: TierBPF uses a single unnamed 128 GB CXL device on AMD EPYC (very close to your EPYC 9745 + CZ120 box) and never treats devices independently, so your per-device placement / CXL-to-CXL interference direction remains open. Its duplex-aware admission hook — monitoring live CXL read/write channel traffic and deferring mismatched promotions — is a policy you could generalize per-device across your 4x CZ120s, and its finding that traffic-balancing helps at 10 GB/s but backfires at 20 GB/s contention maps onto your measured 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown regime. Note it operates at page granularity inside the kernel, complementary to (and a potential competing baseline framing for) ObjPlace's object-granularity user-level placement; its eBPF-hook architecture is a plausible implementation substrate for per-device QoS policies. Their working-set-independent bCBF profiling is also relevant if ObjPlace's profiling cost scales with object count.

Could not verify. Vendor/model and CXL spec revision of the CXL Type-3 device is never stated (only '128 GB DDR4 over PCIe 5.0'), so real-asic vs real-fpga cannot be distinguished from the text; realCXL is set to 'mixed' because evaluation spans one real CXL device plus an Optane PMEM platform., The '75%' per-workload maximum is stated in abstract/intro; the largest individual number visible in the evaluation text I extracted was 36% (bc-twitter on PMEM) — the 75% case likely appears in a figure I could not read., Paper text in section 6.5 refers to 'FlexTier-enhanced AutoNUMA', apparently a leftover earlier system name for TierBPF.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • A server with CXL memory expansion with dual-socket AMD EPYC 9555s (64 cores per socket). Each socket has 8×32 GB DDR5 DRAM as local memory. Socket 0 connects to a CXL Type-3 Memory Expansion device (128 GB DDR4) over PCIe 5.0, providing a full-duplex link.
  • A server with Intel Optane PMEM with dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6252 (24 cores per socket). Each socket has 6×16 GB DDR4 DRAM as the fast tier and 6×128 GB Intel Optane PMEM as the slow tier.
  • By default, we use the CXL server. To isolate the impact of page migration from cross-socket NUMA effects, we use Socket 0 only, following prior efforts in [12, 21, 50].
  • Across all 17 workloads, TierBPF improves the geomean throughput of AutoNUMA by 12.3%, TPP by 17.7%, and Colloid by 7.4%.
  • On AutoNUMA, enabling Split alone improves geomean performance by 12.3%, while Split+BalanceTraffic reduces the gain to 6.6%. The effect is more pronounced on TPP: Split alone achieves 17.7% geomean throughput improvement, whereas Split+BalanceTraffic yields only 1.8%.
  • TierBPF enables sufficient flexibility to develop novel policies in diverse environments, such as more than two memory tiers or CXL-SSD, which could be implemented in the future.

TierScape: Harnessing Multiple Compressed Tiers to Tame Server Memory TCO (arXiv v1 title: "Taming Server Memory TCO with Multiple Software-Defined Compressed Tiers")

EuroSys 2026 (ACM DOI 10.1145/3767295.3769321); arXiv:2404.13886 preprint 2024 (2026) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL devices; far tier is Intel Optane DC PMM, 1.6 TB, flat mode)

Problem. Memory is 33-50% of server TCO; existing 2-tier cold-data solutions (DRAM + one zswap compressed tier, as in Google's production setup) force a coarse trade-off between TCO savings and performance because a single compressed tier cannot serve both warm and cold data well.

Idea. Create N software-defined compressed memory tiers by combining different compression algorithms (lz4/lzo/deflate), allocators (zbud/zsmalloc), and backing media (DRAM/Optane/potentially CXL) via a modified multi-pool zswap; a userspace TS-Daemon profiles 2MB-region hotness with Intel PEBS and places/migrates pages across tiers using either a waterfall policy or an analytical performance-vs-TCO cost model, putting warm data in low-latency compressed tiers and cold data in max-TCO-savings tiers.

Testbed. 2-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6252N (24 cores/socket, 2-way HT, 96 logical CPUs), 384 GB DRAM near tier, 1.6 TB Intel Optane DC PMM in flat (volatile) mode as far media, Fedora 30, modified Linux kernel 5.17. No CXL hardware.

Evaluation. Five workloads: Memcached (44 GB) and Redis (41 GB) driven by memtier with Gaussian key-access distributions, BFS (18 GB), PageRank (18 GB), XSBench (119 GB). 120 s profiling windows, 100 s warm-up; TS-Daemon samples hotness via Intel PEBS at 2 MB granularity. Five compressed tier configs tested: T1 zbud/lz4/DRAM, T2 zbud/lz4/Optane, T3 zsmalloc/lz4/Optane, T4 zsmalloc/lzo/DRAM, T5 zsmalloc/deflate/Optane.

Baselines. 2-Tier zswap (Google production-style: DRAM + zsmalloc/lzo/DRAM) at conservative/moderate/aggressive coldness thresholds (2T-C/2T-M/2T-A), vs TierScape 6-tier waterfall (6T-WF) and 6-tier analytical-model (6T-AM) variants.

Key findings. Overall 22-40 percentage-point higher memory TCO savings than 2-tier at performance parity or 2-10% better performance. Redis: 6T-AM-0.1 gets 64.10% TCO savings at 14.37% slowdown vs 2T-A's 34.84% savings at 18.33% slowdown. PageRank: 6T-WF-C 40.78% savings/13.09% slowdown vs 2T-C 24.86%/21.82%. BFS: 6T-AM-0.1 is 4.05 pp faster than 2T-M at ~63% TCO savings. TS-Daemon overhead 1.2-7% CPU.

Limitations. Authors admit: waterfall model needs N profiling windows for cold pages to reach the last tier; no prefetching ("pages that the prefetcher fails to identify still incur high access latency"); naive migration path (decompress then recompress); analytical model assumes stable access patterns. Methodological: no CXL hardware at all — far media is Optane PMM (discontinued product); single-server, apparently single-workload-at-a-time runs (no multi-tenant experiments); TCO savings computed via an analytical cost model rather than measured dollar costs; arXiv v1 read here may differ from the EuroSys'26 camera-ready.

Open questions. How the multi-tier design behaves when compressed pools are backed by actual CXL memory (latency/bandwidth characteristics differ from Optane); interaction with hardware tiering/interleaving; multi-tenant contention on compression CPU and far-media bandwidth; prefetching from compressed tiers; whether the PEBS-based profiler works on AMD platforms (PEBS is Intel-specific).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Marginal for the requester's 4x CZ120 per-device-scheduling direction: TierScape never touches CXL hardware (Optane stands in as far media) and manages software-defined compressed tiers, not physical devices, so it says nothing about cross-device interference, per-device bandwidth, or multi-tenant QoS on real CXL. Its useful ideas for ObjPlace are orthogonal: an analytical cost model trading performance vs TCO for placement decisions, and the observation that backing media choice per tier matters — the requester could treat each CZ120 (or famfs region) as a distinct backing medium for compressed or uncompressed tiers. It also confirms EuroSys'26 has no real-CXL multi-device competitor in this space.

Could not verify. EuroSys 2026 camera-ready text (ACM DOI returns 403); all evaluation details verified only against arXiv v1 (April 2024), which may differ from the published version, Exact CXL wording in the camera-ready; quotes are from the arXiv v1 HTML as extracted by the fetch tool

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Intel Xeon Gold 6252N with 2 sockets, 24 cores per socket, and 2-way HT for a total of 96 cores. It has a DRAM-based near-memory tier with 384 GB capacity and a far-memory tier with Intel's Optane DC PMM configured in flat mode (i.e., as volatile main memory) with 1.6 TB capacity. We run Fedora 30 and use a modified Linux kernel, 5.17.
  • Using cheaper and denser memory, such as NVMMs or CXL-attached memory, to store compressed pages increases memory TCO savings
  • storing compressed pages on DRAM offers the lowest possible media access latency and hence suits low latency tiers

https://arxiv.org/html/2404.13886v1 https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.13886 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3767295.3769321

ArtMem: Adaptive Migration in Reinforcement Learning-Enabled Tiered Memory

ISCA (52nd Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture), Tokyo — note: ISCA 2025, pp. 405-418, NOT ISCA 2026 as the tasking hint believed (2025) unknown unknown verify: corrected confidence: low

CXL devices. 0 CXL devices. The slow tier is 4x 128GB Intel persistent memory modules (512GB PM) on one socket; fast tier is 4x 16GB DDR4 (64GB DRAM).

Problem. Existing tiered-memory management systems perform inconsistently across workloads, fail to adjust migration strategies when fast-tier access rates are low, and do not dynamically tune migration scope to workload patterns.

Idea. ArtMem is an RL-driven (tabular Q-learning) tiered-memory manager: a modified Linux kernel (5.15.19, Memtis/htmm-lineage PEBS-sampling design with per-cgroup hot/cold tracking) exposes the fast-tier hit ratio via a cgroup file, and a userspace Q-learning agent observes the hit-ratio decile as state, chooses a migration-scope action (0 to 512Ki pages, i.e., quota sizes 4096..524288 pages x 4KB written back to memory.action_show every 10s), with reward = hit-ratio decile minus a threshold, thereby adaptively tuning migration aggressiveness/scope to increase fast-tier accesses while reducing unnecessary migrations.

Testbed. Could not read the paper's testbed section. From the artifact: Intel x86 server (PEBS events MEM_LOAD_L3_MISS_RETIRED.LOCAL_DRAM, MEM_INST_RETIRED.ALL_STORES/ALL_LOADS), Linux kernel 5.15.19-artmem (same base version as Memtis SOSP'23), DRAM tier ending at phys 0x207fffffff (~130 GB) and a 'PM' slow tier spanning 0x2080000000-0x11c7fffffff (~1 TB), CPUS_PER_SOCKET=8. No CXL device model, DRAM/CXL capacities, or emulation method verifiable from the paper itself.

Evaluation. Not verifiable this session. Abstract states evaluation "over diverse workloads"; a third-party citing paper (ICPP'25 'Leave No One Behind') evaluates ArtMem alongside Memtis/TPP/Nomad on ML/graph benchmarks, but ArtMem's own workload list, footprint ratios, and run methodology could not be read.

Baselines. Abstract says ArtMem "outperforms state-of-the-art tiering systems"; exact baseline names not verifiable (Memtis lineage of the code and contemporaneous papers suggest Memtis/TPP/AutoNUMA/Nomad-class baselines, but this is inference, not verified). Author README admits "A comparison with MGLRU is currently missing."

Key findings. Per the publisher abstract (via Semantic Scholar/OpenAlex): ArtMem achieves 35%-172% performance improvements over diverse workloads vs. state-of-the-art tiering systems while reducing unnecessary migrations (the author lab page states "35% to 191%" — discrepancy unresolved). Design finding: system-level feedback (access frequency, recency, migration counts) fed to an RL agent enables better page placement and fewer wasted migrations than static-heuristic tiering.

Limitations. Authors' own (GitHub README): "Several magic numbers and manually tuned parameters make configuration less convenient" and "A comparison with MGLRU is currently missing." From the artifact: tabular Q-learning with a coarse 10-bucket hit-ratio state and 10-second decision epoch; hardcoded physical address ranges for the DRAM/PM tiers (single fast + single slow tier only, no per-device awareness); single-cgroup focus with no evident multi-tenant evaluation; slow tier appears to be PM/Optane-class rather than real CXL silicon (unconfirmed). Full-text limitations section unread.

Open questions. Whether the RL policy transfers to real CXL ASIC latency/bandwidth profiles (vs. the PM-style tier in the artifact); multi-application/multi-tenant fairness (explicitly taken up by the follow-on ICPP'25 'Leave No One Behind' paper that benchmarks ArtMem); handling of more than two tiers or multiple independently addressable slow devices; MGLRU comparison; sensitivity to the many hand-tuned magic numbers (PEBS period lists, 10s epoch, reward threshold).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Moderately relevant as a policy baseline, not as CXL systems evidence: ArtMem treats memory as one fast tier plus one flat slow tier (hardcoded physical ranges, apparently PM/Optane-class, no CXL device awareness), so it says nothing about per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS — the requester's candidate direction. Its useful ideas for the c28 testbed are (a) closed-loop adaptation of migration scope/aggressiveness from an observed fast-tier hit ratio, and (b) the finding that fixed-heuristic tiering is workload-inconsistent — both could motivate an adaptive per-device controller layered on ObjPlace. Because ArtMem is page-granularity kernel tiering with a single slow tier, ObjPlace's object-granularity, bandwidth/interference-aware placement across 4 independently addressable CZ120 devices remains clearly differentiated; ArtMem (open-source, kernel 5.15) is a portable baseline candidate. Caveat: I could not read the paper's evaluation, so its testbed realism vs. this real-CXL setup is unverified.

Could not verify. Full paper text (ACM DL Cloudflare-403 on abstract, full-HTML, and gold-OA PDF endpoints; no arXiv/author-hosted/Wayback/scholar.archive copy exists), realCXL: whether the eval testbed used real CXL, Optane PMem, or NUMA emulation — artifact code points to a PM (Optane-style) slow tier but the paper's testbed table is unread, numCXLDevices and deviceTreatment, Exact testbed specs (CPU model, DRAM/slow-tier capacities, device models), Workload list and run methodology, Named baselines and per-baseline numbers, Whether headline improvement is 35%-172% (ACM abstract via S2/OpenAlex) or 35%-191% (author lab page)

Verifier note. CORRECTED realCXL: No — the evaluation testbed uses no CXL hardware; the slow tier is Intel Optane-style persistent memory (PM) on an Intel Xeon Gold 6330 host, Memtis-style. CORRECTED deviceTreatment: Two-tier DRAM + Optane-style persistent memory: kernel distinguishes tiers by hardcoded physical address ranges (DRAM below 0x2080000000, PM above), samples accesses via Intel PEBS (MEM_LOAD_L3_MISS_RETIRED.LOCAL_DRAM etc.), and enforces per-NUMA-node capacity via cgroup 'memory.max_at_node${NODE_ID}'. Not CXL devices, not CXL-interleaving; single-socket 64GB DRAM / 512GB PM evaluation. Paper PDF is paywalled (dl.acm.org 403 even with browser UA; no arXiv preprint; author pages say "Paper download: N/A"). Evidence basis: (1) artifact repo github.com/Yitrus/ArtMem cloned and read directly — kernel/include/linux/artmem.h and kernel/README.md confirm a two-tier DRAM + 'PM' (persistent memory) physical-address-range design with Intel PEBS events, matching the analyst's verbatim code quotes exactly; (2) two independent web searches whose top hit was the ACM full-text record consistently extracted the same testbed: Intel Xeon Gold 6330 (28 cores/socket), per socket 4x 16GB DDR4 + 4x 128GB Intel persistent memory modules, evaluation on one socket with 64GB DRAM fast tier and 512GB PM slow tier. No source anywhere mentions CXL hardware in ArtMem's evaluation. Because the settling testbed sentences come from search-engine extraction of the indexed ACM page rather than text I read verbat

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • From author lab page (chaselab.wiki/publication/2025-artmem): ArtMem "outperforms state-of-the-art tiering systems, achieving performance improvements ranging from 35% to 191%, over diverse workloads."
  • From Semantic Scholar record of the ACM abstract: ArtMem is "an RL-driven framework" that "dynamically manages tiered memory systems and adapts to workload evolution," achieving "35% - 172% performance improvements over diverse workloads."
  • From artifact kernel/include/linux/artmem.h (verbatim code): "#define DRAM_ADDR_END 0x207fffffff" / "#define PM_ADDR_START 0x2080000000" / "#define PM_ADDR_END 0x11c7fffffff" / "#define DRAM_LLC_LOAD_MISS 0x1d3 // MEM_LOAD_L3_MISS_RETIRED.LOCAL_DRAM" — a two-tier DRAM + 'PM' physical layout on an Intel PEBS machine; no CXL identifiers anywhere in the artifact.
  • From artifact kernel/Makefile (verbatim): "VERSION = 5 / PATCHLEVEL = 15 / SUBLEVEL = 19 / EXTRAVERSION = -artmem" — same 5.15.19 base kernel as Memtis (SOSP'23, Optane testbed).
  • From artifact Q-Learning/env.py (verbatim): "self.action_space = [0, 4096, 8192, 16384, 32768, 65536, 131072, 262144, 524288]" and "cat /sys/fs/cgroup/artmem/memory.hit_ratio_show" — userspace Q-learning agent picks migration-scope actions from kernel-exported hit ratio.
  • From GitHub README (verbatim, authors' own limitations): "1. Several magic numbers and manually tuned parameters make configuration less convenient; 2. A comparison with MGLRU is currently missing."

Exploiting Locality in Flat Memory with CXL for In-Memory Database Management Systems

DaMoN 2025 (21st International Workshop on Data Management on New Hardware, co-located with SIGMOD) (2025) real-asic multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. flat-memory testbed: 1 (256 GB Samsung CMM-D); background CXL-NUMA testbed (prior DaMoN'23/VLDB'24 setup re-shown): 3 (3x 256 GB); mixed/consolidated-mode testbed: 256 GB CXL total, exact device count not stated in slides (diagrams draw two CMM-D boxes)

Problem. Software-managed tiered CXL memory (memory-only NUMA node or heterogeneous interleaving) causes workload/access-pattern-dependent performance loss for in-memory DBMSs (SAP HANA); the paper asks whether hardware-managed, cache-line-granular tiering (Intel Flat Memory Mode) can exploit locality to hide CXL latency/bandwidth limits without DBMS-side configuration, and how to relax its rigid 1:1 DRAM:CXL sizing constraint.

Idea. Evaluate Intel Flat Memory Mode ("first cache-line granular, hardware-managed memory tiering system for CXL": DRAM+CXL fused 1:1 into one NUMA node, direct-mapped exclusive placement, 64B promotion/eviction with no OS/SW involvement) under SAP HANA TPC-C/TPC-DS; then compare Mixed Mode (dedicated local DRAM node 0 + flat-memory node 1, allowing more native DRAM than 1:1) against a newly proposed Consolidated Mode that merges the two Mixed-Mode NUMA nodes into a single node by modifying Linux ACPI tables, so HANA needs no placement configuration at all.

Testbed. 1 CPU Intel Xeon 6 (Granite Rapids) with 80 physical cores, hyper-threading enabled; local DRAM DDR5-4800, 32 GB x 8 channels = 256 GB; CXL memory: 256 GB Samsung CMM-D at 5000 MT/s (locally attached, PCIe Gen5), giving a 512 GB flat-memory node. Mixed/Consolidated experiments: 1024 GB DRAM host, node 1 = 256 GB DRAM + 256 GB CXL flat memory (Consolidated: single 1280 GB node with variable pre-reserved DRAM area). Background experiments used 256 GB local + 3x 256 GB CXL as a memory-only NUMA node. Kernel version and CXL link width not stated in the slides.

Evaluation. SAP HANA running TPC-C (OLTP) and TPC-DS SF500/SF800 (OLAP) with 0-14 parallel client streams; throughput normalized to DRAM-only baseline; CXL/DRAM bandwidth traffic and flat-memory hit rates measured. HANA data placement varied: main store (columnar, sequential-dominant) vs HEX heap (execution engine, random-dominant) placed in CXL/flat memory; Consolidated Mode swept effective size via pre-reserving 0-512 GB of DRAM (1280 GB down to 768 GB).

Baselines. DRAM-only SAP HANA (baseline); direct-attached CXL as memory-only NUMA node with 1, 2, or 3 interleaved devices (Main-in-CXL and HEX-in-CXL variants, from the authors' DaMoN'23/VLDB'24 setup); Mixed Mode (Main-in-Flat, HEX-in-Flat) vs proposed Consolidated Mode at several capacities.

Key findings. Flat Memory Mode: no performance impact on TPC-C, and only 3.23-5.96% TPC-DS throughput loss vs DRAM-only even with a single CXL device, with 92+% local-DRAM hit rate. At 8 streams SF500 it shows 5.3% degradation using only 11.2 GB/s CXL bandwidth, vs 6.5% at 25.0 GB/s for Main-in-CXL-x3 and 16.2% at 49.4 GB/s for HEX-in-CXL-x3 — locality exploitation gives better performance with far less CXL bandwidth, enabling cheaper/lower-BW CXL devices (TCO argument). Mixed Mode: Main-in-Flat ~no impact (~99% hit rate); HEX-in-Flat 5-11% degradation (~92% hit rate), still better than HEX-in-CXL. Consolidated Mode: 0-2.8% degradation even at 768 GB (most constrained), CXL traffic ~9.0 GB/s (below the ~20+ GB/s offered), with zero HANA configuration needed.

Limitations. Authors' own: Flat Memory Mode supports only a 1:1 DRAM:CXL capacity ratio (motivating Mixed/Consolidated); Consolidated Mode is an ACPI-table hack in the Linux kernel, "not officially supported product feature"; Mixed Mode requires extra HANA configuration. Methodological: requires Intel Xeon 6 (Granite Rapids) hardware feature, so results do not transfer to AMD or older Intel hosts; single-socket, single-CXL-device main evaluation (one 256 GB CMM-D); direct-mapped exclusive HW cache — no study of pathological conflict-miss workloads; SAP HANA + TPC-C/TPC-DS only; no tail-latency, no multi-tenant/interference runs; no kernel version or link details in the slides; per-device behavior never examined.

Open questions. How HW-managed cacheline tiering behaves under adversarial/low-locality access patterns or cache-conflict-heavy workloads; multi-device and multi-socket scaling of Flat Memory Mode; interaction/co-existence of HW tiering with SW page- or object-level tiering; QoS and multi-tenant isolation on a flat-memory node; whether Consolidated Mode's random OS allocation degrades under memory pressure beyond the tested 768 GB point; ratios other than 1:1 in hardware.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant as the strongest published counterpoint to software object placement: Intel's HW cacheline tiering achieves <6% OLAP loss at 92-99% hit rates while consuming only ~11 GB/s of CXL bandwidth — versus the requester's finding that CXL bandwidth contention causes 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown, this shows locality-driven placement can shrink CXL traffic below the interference regime, an argument ObjPlace can make in software on AMD Turin where Flat Memory Mode does not exist (Granite Rapids only). The paper treats multiple CXL devices only as interleaved capacity (x1/x2/x3 into one NUMA node) and its main evaluation uses a single CMM-D; it never studies per-device scheduling, cross-device interference, or multi-tenancy, so the requester's per-device direction is untouched by it. The Consolidated-Mode ACPI trick (merging tiered nodes into one) is also a useful transparency technique to cite when arguing why applications should not need per-node configuration.

Could not verify. Camera-ready ACM paper text (dl.acm.org returned 403); analysis is based on the authors' full DaMoN'25 slide deck, Kernel version and CXL link width/speed of the testbed, Exact number of physical CMM-D devices in the Mixed/Consolidated-Mode testbed (256 GB CXL total; diagrams depict two CMM-D boxes but count not stated), CMM-D vendor stated only as the product name CMM-D (Samsung's product line); slides do not spell out 'Samsung'

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • "System Configuration ... 1 CPU (Intel® Granite Rapids) w/ 80 physical cores – Hyper-thread enabled ... Local DRAM – DDR5 4800 MT/s 32 GB * 8 channels (256GB) ... CXL memory (local) – 256 GB CMM-D (5000 MT/s)"
  • "OLAP shows only 3.23% ~ 5.96% perf impact – Even with a single CXL device"
  • "Intel Flat Memory Mode: First cache-line granular, hardware-managed Memory tiering system for CXL memory"
  • "Easily configured in BIOS as a single NUMA node – Combines local & CXL memory as a single NUMA node ... Ex) 256G DRAM + 256G CXL memory => 512GB – Supporting only 1:1 ratio of DRAMs and CXL memory"
