HybridTier: an Adaptive and Lightweight CXL-Memory Tiering System

ASPLOS 2025 (Proceedings of the 30th ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, Volume 3, ASPLOS '25), DOI 10.1145/3676642.3736119 (2025) numa-emulated n/a verify: corrected confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL devices; CXL emulated with the remote socket's DDR4 of a two-socket system)

Problem. High-performance memory tiering between fast DRAM and slower CXL memory requires (1) adapting to skewed but dynamically shifting data-hotness distributions and (2) keeping the tiering system's own metadata memory and CPU-cache overhead low; prior systems (TPP, AutoNUMA, Memtis, classic caching policies ARC/TwoQ) fail on one or both, adapting slowly to hotness shifts and paying large per-page metadata/cache-miss costs.

Idea. A userspace (LD_PRELOAD-injected) per-process tiering runtime that samples accesses via Intel PEBS and tracks hotness probabilistically with two blocked Counting Bloom Filters (4-bit counters capped at 15): a long-term "frequency" tracker (high cooling period) and a short-term "momentum" tracker (low cooling period). Pages with high frequency OR high momentum are promoted, letting the system both capture stable hot sets and quickly promote newly-hot pages; probabilistic tracking trades small inaccuracy for 2.0-7.8x less metadata memory and 1.7-3.5x fewer cache misses. Supports 4KB pages and 2MB THP.

Testbed. Two-socket server; each socket a 16-core Intel Xeon 4314 (Ice Lake) with 512GB DDR4. CXL emulated as the remote NUMA node: emulated idle latency 124ns, bandwidth 34 GB/s. Linux kernel v6.2 for AutoNUMA and HybridTier. No CXL device model/link width - no real CXL hardware present.

Evaluation. Workloads: CacheLib (CDN and social-graph traces), GAP graph kernels (BFS, CC, PR), SPEC CPU 2017 (603.bwaves, 654.roms), Silo in-memory database, XGBoost. All experiments use 16 threads pinned to the 16 physical cores of the local socket; application memory split between local DRAM (fast tier) and remote-socket DRAM emulating CXL (slow tier), with varying fast-tier size ratios.

Baselines. TPP, AutoNUMA (Linux kernel tiering), Memtis, and caching policies ARC and TwoQ.

Key findings. On average HybridTier outperforms TPP, AutoNUMA, Memtis, ARC, and TwoQ by 32%, 11%, 29%, 50%, and 40% respectively; achieves 2.0-7.8x less metadata memory overhead and 1.7-3.5x fewer cache misses than prior tiering systems; adapts to hotness-distribution shifts 3.2x faster (3.2x less time to adapt).

Limitations. Evaluation is entirely on NUMA emulation (remote-socket DDR4, 124ns idle latency) - no production CXL silicon, so real CXL controller effects (higher load-to-use latency ratios like the requester's 1.53x, bandwidth saturation at low thread counts, contention behavior) are untested; authors justify this by noting many CXL devices "are not commercially available on the market" (paper predates wide CZ120 availability). Single emulated slow tier - no multiple CXL devices, no per-device management, no bandwidth-interference or co-runner analysis, no multi-tenant evaluation (global tiering via a "central HybridTier controller" is only sketched in Discussion). Per-process userspace design requires PEBS (Intel-specific; requester's box is AMD EPYC, which would need IBS instead). One-time-only access patterns (scans, pointer chasing) require manual tuning of the momentum threshold. No dedicated limitations section.

Open questions. How the CBF frequency/momentum approach behaves on real CXL ASICs where latency and especially bandwidth contention differ from a remote NUMA socket; global/multi-tenant tiering (explicitly punted to a hypothetical central controller); multiple independent CXL devices and inter-device placement; bandwidth-aware (not just latency/hotness-aware) placement; AMD/IBS-based access sampling; interaction with famfs or shared CXL memory.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Direct competitor in hotness-driven DRAM/CXL tiering, but evaluated purely on remote-NUMA emulation (124ns, 34 GB/s remote DDR4) - it never sees real CXL contention effects like the requester's measured 2.0-2.13x slowdown of a bandwidth-bound victim under a CXL co-runner, nor the 1.53x load-to-use latency gap on CZ120s. It manages one flat slow tier per process and explicitly does not address multiple CXL devices, per-device placement, bandwidth interference, or multi-tenancy - exactly the gap the requester's candidate direction (per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL interference, multi-tenant QoS on 4x CZ120) targets. Its PEBS-based probabilistic (CBF frequency+momentum) tracking is a useful lightweight-telemetry baseline for ObjPlace, though on the AMD EPYC 9745 it would need IBS rather than PEBS. Strong differentiation angle: real-ASIC multi-device results vs HybridTier's single emulated tier.

Could not verify. Exact ASPLOS presentation year: arXiv v2 (3 Jul 2025) citation block says ASPLOS '25 Rotterdam, but the ACM DOI is in ASPLOS Vol. 3 and the requester's fetch of the official ASPLOS 2026 program lists it in Session 1D - Vol. 3/late-cycle papers are presented at ASPLOS 2026, Whether the camera-ready differs from arXiv v2 (dl.acm.org full text not fetched; read arXiv v2 HTML instead)

Verifier note. Primary source read this session: arXiv HTML full text (2312.04789v2, revised 2025-07-03) plus the arXiv abstract page. Section 5.1 confirms remote-NUMA-node CXL emulation on a two-socket, 16-core Xeon 4314, 512GB-DDR4-per-socket system; analyst's first two evidence quotes verified verbatim (modulo inline citation markers in quote 1). Caveat on the analyst's third evidence quote (CBF sentence): it does NOT appear verbatim in the paper — the paper says HybridTier tracks accesses probabilistically with CBFs, \"obtaining higher memory efficiency by trading off a small amount of tracking inaccuracy\", and separately defines a CBF as k hash functions plus an array supporting GET and INCREMENT. This does not affect the three field verdicts. dl.acm.org was not needed (DOI redirect noted but arXiv sufficed). Emulated CXL characteristics per the analyst (124ns idle latency, 34 GB/s bandwidth) were also reported from the same testbed section.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Similar to recent works, we use a remote NUMA node on a two-socket system to emulate CXL. The emulated CXL memory has idle latency of 124ns and bandwidth of 34 GB/s
  • Each socket has a 16-core Intel Xeon 4314 processor and 512GB of DDR4 memory.
  • HybridTier tracks memory accesses using counting bloom filters (CBFs), a probabilistically data structure that trades higher memory efficiency for lower tracking accuracy.
  • HybridTier utilizes Intel Processor Event-Based Sampling (PEBS), where each access sample contains the virtual memory address being accessed.
  • On average, HybridTier outperforms TPP, AutoNUMA, Memtis, ARC, and TwoQ by 32%, 11%, 29%, 50%, and 40%
  • For AutoNUMA, HybridTier, we use Linux kernel v6.2.
  • To support global memory tiering (e.g., multi-tenant VM, co-located applications), one could use a central HybridTier controller.

https://arxiv.org/html/2312.04789v2 https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.04789 https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1145/3676642.3736119

M5: Mastering Page Migration and Memory Management for CXL-based Tiered Memory Systems

ASPLOS 2025 (30th ACM ASPLOS, Volume 2, Rotterdam, pp. 604-621, DOI 10.1145/3676641.3711999) (2025) real-fpga single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one Intel Agilex-7 I-Series development kit with 8GB on-board DDR4 DRAM)

Problem. CXL DRAM has 2-3x longer access latency than DDR DRAM, so tiered-memory systems need page migration; but CPU-driven hot-page identification (Autonuma/ANB hint faults, DAMON PTE scanning, PEBS sampling) is imprecise (identifies warm pages as hot) and costly enough that migration can degrade application performance. There was also no ground-truth way to count every page/word access in CXL DRAM to even evaluate these solutions.

Idea. Exploit the CXL device controller itself (device-driven / near-memory profiling): implement Page Access Counter (PAC) and Word Access Counter (WAC) in an FPGA CXL controller to count every access to every 4KB page and 64B word in CXL DRAM (ground truth); use PAC/WAC to show ANB/DAMON find pages with only 21%/29% of the access counts of true hot pages and that many hot pages are sparse (86% of Redis pages have <=16/64 words accessed). Then build M5: hardware CM-Sketch/Space-Saving-based top-K Hot Page Tracker (HPT) and Hot Word Tracker (HWT) in the CXL controller, plus an M5-manager kernel-side interface (Nominator/Elector/Promoter) for building migration policies that avoid migrating sparse pages.

Testbed. Dual-socket server: 2x Intel Xeon 6430 (4th-gen Xeon Scalable / Sapphire Rapids) @2.1 GHz, 32 cores + 60 MB LLC per CPU, hyper-threading disabled; 4x DDR5-4800 channels per socket. CXL device: one Intel Agilex-7 I-Series FPGA development kit with hardened CXL x16 endpoint IP and a DDR4-2666 controller with 8GB on-board DRAM. Kernels: M5 in Linux 6.5; ANB baseline on Linux 5.19; DAMON baseline on Linux 6.11. ASIC projections of the trackers synthesized in 7nm logic; larger-N tracker preciseness evaluated in a trace-driven simulator (FPGA limited to N<=50 at 400MHz).

Evaluation. 12 memory-intensive benchmarks sized to ~8GB footprint (matching CXL capacity): 4 SPECrate CPU2017 (mcf_r, cactuBSSN_r, fotonik3d_r, roms_r; 8 instances each), 6 GAP graph kernels (BFS, SSSP, PR, CC, BC, TC on Twitter/Google graphs), Liblinear (KDD2012), Redis 6.0.16 with YCSB-A. All pages initially allocated on CXL DRAM; DDR DRAM capped at 3GB (~50% of pages migratable); CPU cores hot-unplugged to match benchmark threads and LLC partitioned proportionally via Intel CAT so migration daemons contend with the workload. Metrics: access-count ratio of identified hot pages vs PAC ground truth, kernel CPU cycles, and end-to-end performance (p99 latency inverse for Redis).

Baselines. ANB (Autonuma Balancing, Linux 5.19), DAMON (Linux 6.11), and no-page-migration; discussion-level comparisons to Memtis, TPP, Intel Flat Memory Mode, and concurrent work NeoMem.

Key findings. (1) ANB and DAMON identify hot pages with only 21% and 29% of the access counts of the true top-K hot pages determined by PAC. (2) Hot pages are often sparse: 86% of Redis pages have <=16 of 64 words accessed. (3) Hot-page identification overhead is large: kernel CPU cycles increase up to 487%; DAMON degrades Redis by 16% by continuing PTE scans at equilibrium. (4) M5 identifies on average 47% hotter pages and gives 20%/14% higher performance than ANB/DAMON (106% over no migration) even with a simple y=x^n policy; Redis gains 10%/43% over ANB/DAMON with the HWT-driven policy at virtually no profiling cost. (5) CM-Sketch top-K tracker in 7nm ASIC costs ~0.01% of the chip area of an 8GB DRAM module's dies.

Limitations. Single FPGA CXL device with only 8GB DDR4-2666 on-board DRAM — much smaller capacity, older DRAM, and different latency/bandwidth profile than production CXL ASICs; no multi-device, no interleaving, no pooling. Requires custom hardware in the CXL controller (PAC/WAC/HPT/HWT) that no shipping ASIC device provides; ASIC results are synthesis projections and large-N tracker precision is simulator-based (FPGA capped at N=50 @400MHz). Authors admit: benchmarks do not use huge pages (2MB tracking is future work); M5-manager policy is a deliberately simple sample (best n chosen by hand-tuning per benchmark, adaptive f_default "out of intended scope"); ANB/DAMON/M5 run on three different kernel versions (5.19/6.11/6.5). Single-workload runs only — no multi-tenant or co-location experiments; no bandwidth-interference study.

Open questions. Adaptive migration policies on top of HPT/HWT (they only demo a hand-tuned sample policy); huge-page (2MB) hot tracking; scaling trackers to larger capacities (>128K CM-Sketch entries via per-rank tracker instances is only sketched); word-level migration/caching synergy with Intel Flat Memory Mode; whether commodity CXL ASIC vendors will expose such device-side profiling counters; behavior under multi-tenant contention and across multiple CXL devices is untested.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. M5 is device-side profiling on a single 8GB FPGA prototype, so nothing in its evaluation speaks to multi-device questions — no per-device placement, interleaving, or CXL-to-CXL interference. Its relevance to the CZ120 testbed is conceptual and as a foil: it argues host-side hotness tracking (ANB/DAMON/PEBS) is imprecise and expensive, which supports ObjPlace-style application/object-level signals over kernel page-fault/PTE-scan tiering, but its proposed fix (custom counters in the CXL controller) is unavailable on Micron CZ120 silicon. For the per-device-scheduling direction, M5 is useful related work to cite for "hot-page identification cost/precision" and NeoMem as concurrent device-side tracking, and its finding that migration overhead can outweigh benefit strengthens a case for coarser object-granularity, bandwidth-aware placement on real multi-device hardware.

Verifier note. Verified against the actual PDF from jiyuan.is (ACM DL stamped copy, downloaded and text-extracted this session). All three analyst claims and the analyst's evidence quotes are accurate; one trivial truncation in the analyst's first quote ("harden" should read "hardened CXL ×16 endpoint IP"). The paper additionally uses an in-house simulator only for HPT/HWT tracker design-space exploration (Section on Space-Saving vs CM-Sketch), not as a CXL memory testbed — no second testbed, no NUMA emulation, no multi-device interleaving.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • System. We use a dual-socket server based on the Intel 4th generation Xeon Scalable Processor. We connect the server to the latest Intel Agilex-7 I-Series development kit [22] (or simply Agilex7 henceforth), which serves as a CXL development platform in this work. Agilex7 is integrated with a hardened CXL ×16 endpoint IP and a DDR4-2666 DRAM controller connected to 8GB of on-board DRAM.
  • Table 2. System configuration of a dual-socket server. CPU: 2× Intel® Xeon 6430 CPUs @2.1 GHz [21], 32 cores and 60 MB LLC per CPU, Hyper-Threading disabled. Memory: Socket 0: 4× DDR5-4800 channels; Socket 1: 4× DDR5-4800 channels
  • we present Page Access Counter (PAC) and Word Access Counter (WAC), implemented in an FPGA-based CXL device. They exploit the near-memory processing capability of the CXL controller to count the number of accesses to every 4KB page and 64B word, respectively, in CXL DRAM.
  • which collectively have a memory footprint close to the capacity of our 8GB CXL DRAM
  • the number of accesses to hot pages identified by ANB and DAMON is only 21% and 29%, respectively, of the number of accesses to the same number of hot pages determined by PAC
  • On average, M5 can identify 47% hotter pages and offer 20% and 14% higher performance than ANB and DAMON
  • For ANB, we use Linux version 5.19 ... For DAMON we use Linux kernel version 6.11 ... M5 is implemented in Linux kernel version 6.5.

https://jiyuan.is/papers/asplos25-m5.pdf https://github.com/ece-fast-lab/ASPLOS-2025-M5 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3676641.3711999

Tiered Memory Management Beyond Hotness

OSDI (19th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2025) mixed single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. testbed SKX (CloudLab Skylake): 0 (NUMA/uncore-throttling emulation); testbed SPR (Sapphire Rapids): 1 (one ASIC-based 128 GB CXL expander, PCIe 5 x8)

Problem. Existing tiered-memory systems (TPP, NBT, Nomad, Colloid, HeMem...) use access frequency ("hotness") to decide DRAM vs CXL placement, but hot data is not always performance-critical: memory-level parallelism (MLP) masks slow-tier latency, so per-access performance impact varies up to 4x across workloads. Hotness-based policies also mis-place data at allocation time and generate excessive, sometimes harmful page migrations (TPP up to 482% slowdown, worse than running entirely on CXL).

Idea. Introduce Amortized Offcore Latency (AOL) = memory access latency / MLP combined with CPU stall cycles, a metric validated across 56 workloads that captures true performance impact of memory accesses. Two AOL-driven mechanisms: SOAR, an offline profile-guided static object allocation policy that ranks objects by accumulated AOL-based performance contribution and places top-ranked objects in the fast tier (no runtime migration); and ALTO, a lightweight kernel-side page-migration regulation layer (~30 LOC kernel-side per system) that throttles promotions when AOL is low (high MLP means latency is masked), integrated on top of TPP, NBT, Nomad, and Colloid.

Testbed. Two platforms. (1) "SKX": CloudLab dual-socket Intel Skylake, two 10-core CPUs, 96 GB DDR4 per socket; CXL emulated by lowering uncore frequency and disabling cores on one NUMA node, yielding 90/190 ns (2.1x) latency and 49/17 GB/s bandwidth. (2) "SPR": local Intel Sapphire Rapids server, 32-core CPU per socket, 192 GB DDR5 DRAM, one ASIC-based 128 GB CXL memory expander over PCIe 5 x8; 114/271 ns (2.4x) latency, 218/26 GB/s bandwidth. CXL vendor/model and kernel version not stated in the text I read.

Evaluation. Workloads span graph analytics (GAPBS: bc-urand, bc-kron, bc-twitter, sssp-kron, tc-twitter), machine learning (GPT-2 inference), caching (Redis), and HPC (bwaves); 8 threads by default, RSS 8-35 GB, bandwidth usage 2.3-21 GB/s, fast/slow-tier ratios swept 10-90% of RSS. Performance reported as slowdown relative to fast-tier(DRAM)-only. Bandwidth-contention experiments use Intel MLC co-runners (0-9 threads, ~8 GB/s each, up to 98% of fast-tier bandwidth, 2x inflated latency) on SKX. Sensitivity studies over AOL thresholds and sampling intervals (100ms-1s).

Baselines. TPP, Linux NUMA Balancing Tiering (NBT), Nomad, Colloid (TPP-based implementation), and NoTier (first-touch-only); DRAM-only and CXL-only bounds.

Key findings. (1) SOAR+ALTO outperform four state-of-the-art tiering designs by up to 12.4x, underperforming in a few cases by no more than 3%. (2) On real CXL (SPR), SOAR worst-case slowdown is 42% for bc-urand vs Nomad up to 588% and Colloid up to 92%; SOAR keeps <20% slowdown with up to 90% slow-tier memory on SKX. (3) ALTO+TPP outperforms TPP by 2-471% (SKX) and 2-178% (CXL); ALTO+NBT 1-23%, ALTO+Nomad 0-35%, ALTO+Colloid 0-18% on CXL. (4) ALTO cuts page promotions by up to 127.4x (e.g., 1.6M to 190K pages for TPP). (5) Under bandwidth contention, all hotness-based tiering underperforms NoTier; SOAR beats second-best by 4-41%, but gains shrink from 33% to 4% at 9 MLC threads; ALTO+Colloid loses 3% under extreme contention unless AOL thresholds are re-tuned (33% to 23% slowdown after tuning).

Limitations. Authors admit: ALTO's default AOL thresholds degrade under extreme bandwidth contention (Alto+Colloid -3% vs Colloid at 9 MLC threads) and need contention-dependent tuning ("Developing an auto-tuning mechanism for AOL thresholds ... is an interesting direction for future work"). Methodology weaknesses: SOAR requires offline profiling of each workload (profile-guided, static placement — no adaptation to unseen inputs at runtime discussed as a deployment mode); only ONE physical CXL device on the real testbed, half the experiments on uncore-throttled NUMA emulation; bandwidth-contention experiments run only on the emulated SKX platform with synthetic MLC traffic, not real CXL; no multi-tenant experiments (single foreground workload plus MLC noise); workloads deliberately do not saturate fast-tier bandwidth ("in our setup, none of the workloads saturate fast-tier bandwidth"); no CXL device model/vendor or kernel version disclosed.

Open questions. Auto-tuning AOL thresholds under varying bandwidth contention (explicitly punted). Untested: multiple CXL devices, per-device placement or cross-CXL-device interference, multi-tenant isolation/QoS, tail latency (all results are runtime slowdown), online (non-profile-guided) object placement, virtualized environments, larger-than-128GB CXL capacities, AMD platforms (AOL is built on Intel offcore latency/occupancy PMU events — portability to AMD EPYC counters is not addressed).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly competing prior work for ObjPlace: SOAR is also object-granularity DRAM/CXL placement, but driven by an MLP-aware latency metric (AOL) rather than bandwidth-sensitivity/skew/phase/pressure — the requester must differentiate against it and ideally compare or incorporate AOL/MLP-awareness. Its bandwidth-contention results (hotness-based tiering underperforms NoTier under contention; AOL thresholds break at 2x inflated latency) corroborate the requester's 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdown finding, but SoarAlto only injects synthetic MLC noise on an emulated NUMA platform and never runs contention on real CXL. Its real testbed has exactly one 128 GB CXL device, so per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and multi-tenant tail QoS on 4x CZ120 remain completely open — the requester's candidate direction is not covered here. Caveat: AOL relies on Intel offcore PMU events; reproducing it on the AMD EPYC 9745 testbed would require mapping to AMD equivalents.

Could not verify. CXL device vendor/model on the SPR testbed (paper only says 'ASIC-based 128 GB CXL memory expander (PCIe 5 x8)'), Linux kernel version used (not stated in the extracted text)

Verifier note. WebFetch to usenix.org returned 403, but curl with a browser user-agent retrieved the open-access PDF (https://www.usenix.org/system/files/osdi25-liu.pdf), which I converted with pdftotext and read directly. All three analyst quotes match the paper verbatim (the first quote was merely truncated at '90/19'; full text: '90/190 ns (2.1x) and bandwidths of 49/17 GB/s'). No corrections needed. Authors: Jinshu Liu, Hamid Hadian, Hanchen Xu, Huaicheng Li (Virginia Tech); pages 731-747 per ACM DL listing (https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.5555/3767901.3767941).
