Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We evaluate Soar and Alto on two platforms. The first is a CloudLab dual-socket Intel Skylake server ("SKX") with two 10-core CPUs and 96 GB DDR4 DRAM per socket [52]. We emulate CXL by lowering the uncore frequency and disabling cores on one NUMA node, resulting in fast/slow-tier latencies of 90/190 ns (2.1x) and bandwidths of 49/17 GB/s.
  • The second platform is a local Intel Sapphire Rapids server ("SPR"), with a 32-core CPU per socket, 192 GB DDR5 DRAM, and an ASIC-based 128 GB CXL memory expander (PCIe 5 x8). Fast/slow-tier latencies are 114/271 ns (2.4x), and bandwidths are 218/26 GB/s.
  • These results reaffirm that Soar is effective and robust across both emulated and real CXL environments.
  • Soar and Alto outperform four state-of-the-art tiering designs across a diverse set of workloads by up to 12.4x, while underperforming in a few cases by no more than 3%.
  • This requires tuning AOL thresholds based on contention levels. Developing an auto-tuning mechanism for AOL thresholds for such scenarios is an interesting direction for future work.

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/osdi25-liu.pdf https://github.com/MoatLab/SoarAlto

Managing Memory Tiers with CXL in Virtualized Environments (Intel Flat Memory Mode + Memstrata)

OSDI (18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2024) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 3 (three CXL cards, each a preproduction Astera Labs Leo CXL Smart Memory Controller holding two DDR5-4800 DIMMs)

Problem. Cloud providers want CXL memory to cut DRAM cost/carbon, but software-managed tiering (e.g., TPP, Hyperswap-style page migration) is impractical in virtualized clouds: the hypervisor cannot see guest access patterns, access-bit scanning burns nearly a whole core per VM, and CXL's ~2x latency causes large, workload-dependent slowdowns that violate cloud SLOs in opaque multi-tenant VMs.

Idea. Two-part solution: (1) Intel Flat Memory Mode — the first hardware-managed, cache-line-granularity tiering between local DRAM and CXL memory in the memory controller, with a 1:1 DRAM:CXL capacity ratio where each physical line maps to one of two locations (local or CXL), plus a "mixed mode" carving out dedicated local-only memory; (2) Memstrata — a lightweight host-userspace multi-tenant allocator that uses page coloring to give conflicting page pairs to the same VM (eliminating inter-VM contention on local-DRAM slots) and an online hardware-counter-based slowdown estimator to detect outlier VMs and migrate dedicated local pages to them via a page-exchange syscall.

Testbed. Single-socket pre-production Intel Xeon 6 processor implementing Flat Memory Mode; 128 GB DDR5 local memory + 128 GB DDR5 CXL memory. CXL attached via three CXL cards (preproduction Astera Labs Leo CXL Smart Memory Controllers), each with two DDR5-4800 DIMMs and an x16 PCIe5 CXL link; CXL idle latency ~200-220% of local, ~50 GB/s max bandwidth per card. Software: Ubuntu 22.04, modified Linux 5.19 (2729 LOC kernel changes), QEMU/KVM 6.2. Core count/frequency/cache sizes withheld for confidentiality. Separate latency characterization (2.02x load-to-use) done on 5th Gen Xeon.

Evaluation. 115 workloads across web (DaCapo, Renaissance, Ruby YJIT, DeathStarBench), database (TPC-C/Silo, TPC-H/PostgreSQL), ML (DLRM), KV stores (YCSB on FASTER, Redis, memcached), big data (HiBench/Spark), graph (GAP), and SPEC CPU 2017 — each run inside a VM on Linux/KVM with VM size rounded to cloud sizes (2-64 GB), under four settings: local-only, CXL-only, 100% hardware-tiered, and mixed mode (33% dedicated local + 67% HW-tiered => 67% DRAM / 33% CXL). Multi-VM experiments: 6 VMs with workload mix matching Azure's internal composition (web 31%, big data 32%, ML 11%), 1/6 to 4/6 outlier VMs; workloads >32 GB excluded due to the 128 GB local-memory prototype. Hyperthreading and frequency scaling disabled, 1:1 vCPU pinning.

Baselines. Local-DRAM-only (upper bound X+Y local); CXL-memory-only; hardware-tiered without Memstrata; TPP (state-of-the-art software tiering, configured with 50% local memory); random vs. hotness-based (DAMON) dedicated-page allocation; Intel 2LM/Optane memory mode discussed as prior hardware tiering; Pond discussed as static-placement prior work.

Key findings. CXL load-to-use latency measured at 2.02x local DDR5. With 100% hardware-tiered memory, 73% of the 115 workloads see <=5% slowdown and 86% <=10%; mixed mode further improves, but outliers remain at up to 34% (mixed) / 50% (HW-tiered only, e.g., 649.fotonik3d_s with 41% local-miss ratio). Hardware tiering cuts FASTER/YCSB-C slowdown from 25% to 4% vs CXL-only. TPP consumes nearly a full CPU core per VM for access scanning and still underperforms. In realistic multi-VM runs, Memstrata reduces worst-case slowdown from 35% (>30%) to below 6% without hurting other VMs, and hardware+Memstrata provides 1:1 capacity use of DRAM and CXL (unlike 2LM's inclusive cache wasting the CXL tier).

Limitations. Authors' own: CPU and CXL cards are pre-production, detailed specs withheld for confidentiality ("core count, frequency, and CPU cache sizes may not reflect those of the final product"); per-VM local-miss-rate counters "not implemented in the current prototype" so slowdown estimation relies on proxy per-core events covering only demand loads; page coloring assumes static VM memory sizes and needs redesign for non-virtualized/dynamic processes; workloads >32 GB excluded; no public VM traces used (workload mix synthesized from Azure statistics). Methodology-evident: requires new Intel silicon (fixed 1:1 DRAM:CXL ratio baked into hardware), CXL devices are fused by cache-line interleaving so no per-device visibility, no study of CXL bandwidth contention across devices, single socket only, and hotness within CXL tier is invisible to software.

Open questions. How Flat Memory Mode behaves at DRAM:CXL ratios other than 1:1; per-device CXL management, cross-device interference, and bandwidth-driven placement (interleaving is fixed in hardware); extension to non-virtualized processes with dynamic footprints (sketched but unimplemented); interaction with memory pooling/multi-headed devices (Pond-style); behavior with slower DDR4-backed CXL only argued, not measured; accuracy of the slowdown estimator when non-demand-load traffic (prefetches, stores) dominates.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Memstrata is the requester's stated primary inspiration and the closest multi-tenant CXL-tiering baseline, but its hardware treats the 3 CXL cards as one cache-line-interleaved pool behind the memory controller — exactly the opposite of the candidate per-device direction, and it requires special pre-production Xeon 6 silicon the requester lacks. On the requester's stock AMD Turin + 4x CZ120 box, Memstrata's page-coloring/slot-conflict mechanism does not apply, but its slowdown-estimator + dedicated-page-migration pattern maps directly onto ObjPlace-style software placement and onto per-VM/tenant outlier detection. The paper's gap — no per-device visibility, no CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference study, no bandwidth-contention isolation (which the requester already measured at 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown) — is precisely the territory the 4-independent-device direction would occupy; citing Memstrata as "pools devices, cannot isolate CXL bandwidth interference" is a clean positioning hook.

Could not verify. Exact Xeon 6 core count/frequency/cache sizes and CXL card final specs — withheld by the authors for confidentiality, Some figure axis values (e.g., Figure 9b latency scale) omitted in the paper itself

Verifier note. Primary source read directly: usenix.org returned 403, but the Microsoft-hosted camera-ready PDF (the analyst's first cited source) was downloaded and text-extracted locally via pdftotext; all quotes verified verbatim in the extracted text. The analyst's three evidence quotes match the paper exactly (the idle-latency quote \"roughly 200-220% the latency of the local memory, and the max bandwidth per CXL card is around 50 GB/s [91]\" appears verbatim in the Experimental setup paragraph). No second testbed exists for the eval; production-cluster data in the motivation section does not change the testbed claims. Checked the common failure modes: DIMM-vs-card confusion (paper has 6 DIMMs on 3 cards — analyst said 3, correct), NUMA emulation (none; real preproduction Astera Labs Leo ASIC), and separate-NUMA-node assumption (paper explicitly interleaves CXL devices within one NUMA node — analyst's multi-device-pooled is correct).

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Experimental setup. We use a pre-production Intel® Xeon® 6 Processor that implements Intel® Flat Memory Mode. Our test server contains a single socket with 128GB DDR5 local memory and 128GB DDR5 CXL memory. The CXL memory is attached via three CXL cards, which each hold two DDR5-4800 DIMMs and offer an x16 PCIe5 CXL connection. We use a preproduction Astera Labs Leo CXL Smart Memory Controller [22].
  • Request interleaving. To achieve maximum bandwidth, we interleave local memory requests across memory channels and interleave CXL memory requests across CXL devices at cache line granularity within the same NUMA node.
  • The idle latency of the CXL memory is roughly 200-220% the latency of the local memory, and the max bandwidth per CXL card is around 50 GB/s [91].
  • we measure that CXL memory has 2.02× the load-to-use latency of local DDR5 on the 5th Gen Intel® Xeon® Processor.
  • In multi-VM experiments on prototype hardware, Memstrata is able to identify performance outliers and reduce their degradation from above 30% to below 6%, providing consistent performance
  • Since the CPU is pre-production, the core count, frequency, and CPU cache sizes may not reflect those of the final product. For confidentiality, we cannot share the detailed technical specification.
  • Overall, with only hardware-tiered memory, 73% of the workloads experience no more than 5% slowdown, and 86% experience no more than 10% slowdown.

https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/20 https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi24/presentation/zhong-yuhong

NeoMem: Hardware/Software Co-Design for CXL-Native Memory Tiering

MICRO 2024 (2024) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1

Problem. OS-level memory tiering on CXL heterogeneous memory needs accurate, low-overhead hot-page profiling, but existing software methods (PTE-scan, NUMA hint faults, PEBS/PMU sampling) have low resolution, high fault overhead, or vendor-specific low-frequency sampling, because the CPU accesses CXL memory via plain load/stores with no OS visibility into access patterns.

Idea. Move profiling into the CXL device itself: NeoProf, a hardware unit in the CXL memory controller, tracks page hotness with a Count-Min Sketch (default W=512K, D=2, 16-bit counters), hot-bit filtering, a 64-bin access-frequency histogram, and a bandwidth/read-write state monitor; a co-designed Linux kernel daemon (kernel v6.3) reads NeoProf and drives promotion/demotion with a dynamic hotness threshold based on top-p% frequency distribution, bandwidth utilization, ping-pong severity, sketch error bounds, and migration quotas.

Testbed. Single-socket Intel Xeon 6430 @ 2.10 GHz, 32 cores (HT disabled), 60 MB LLC, 4x 32GB DDR5-4800 DRAM. CXL device: one Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA Dev Kit @ 400 MHz with hard CXL 1.1 IP on PCIe Gen5 x16, hosting 16 GB 2-channel DDR4-2666, exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node. CXL latency ~430 ns, ~3.6x local DDR. Linux kernel v6.3.

Evaluation. Eight benchmarks run with 32 threads (RSS 10.3–19.7 GB against 16 GB CXL + throttled/limited DRAM tier): DeathStarBench, PageRank, XSBench, GUPS, Silo, Btree, SPEC-2017 603.bwaves and 654.roms. Metrics: end-to-end speedup vs tiering baselines, plus profiler overhead (GUPS slowdown with NeoProf on).

Baselines. First-touch NUMA, AutoNUMA (Linux v6.3), TPP, PTE-scan and PEBS-based tiering, Memtis.

Key findings. NeoMem achieves 32%–67% geomean speedup over existing tiering solutions (TPP, AutoNUMA, PTE-scan, PEBS variants); 1.58x geomean speedup over Memtis; 1.19x–1.67x speedup on DeathStarBench; NeoProf profiling overhead is negligible (0.021% slowdown on GUPS). Device-side sketch profiling gives full-resolution access counts without CPU-side sampling cost.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: "constrained by a single 16GB CXL memory device due to hardware limitations"; multi-device scenarios, interleaving effects, and virtualized environments are all deferred to future work. Methodological weaknesses: FPGA prototype at CXL 1.1 with 430 ns latency (3.6x DRAM) — much worse than production ASICs (~1.5x), so speedups over baselines may be inflated relative to real silicon; only 16 GB CXL capacity forces small working sets; single-tenant runs only, no QoS/interference study; requires custom hardware in the CXL controller, not deployable on off-the-shelf devices like CZ120.

Open questions. How device-side profiling composes across multiple CXL devices (per-device NeoProf instances, cross-device promotion arbitration, interleaved regions); how interleaving affects tiering performance (explicitly unanswered); behavior in virtualized/multi-tenant settings; whether the approach works at realistic ASIC latencies and hundreds-of-GB capacities; standardization path for exposing device-side telemetry (e.g., via CXL 3.x hot-page tracking proposals).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. NeoMem is single-device FPGA (CXL 1.1, 16 GB, 430 ns = 3.6x DRAM) and explicitly punts on multi-device and interleaving, so it does not compete with a 4x CZ120 per-device-scheduling direction — it actually motivates it: the open questions it lists (multi-device, interleaving effects) are exactly the requester's candidate territory. Its device-side telemetry idea (per-device bandwidth utilization, read/write ratio, hot-page histograms) is the kind of signal ObjPlace approximates in software; the requester cannot add hardware to CZ120s, but per-device counters/perf events could serve as a poor-man's NeoProf for per-device placement and CXL-to-CXL interference detection. Note NeoMem is page-granularity kernel tiering, orthogonal to ObjPlace's object granularity; citing it as the hardware-assisted-profiling point in the design space is the main use. Its 3.6x-latency FPGA numbers should not be used as a performance comparison point against the requester's 1.53x ASIC measurements.

Could not verify. Exact DRAM-tier size restriction used during tiering runs (how DRAM was limited relative to 10.3–19.7 GB RSS) was not captured verbatim, Full author-affiliation list and MICRO 2024 acceptance verified only via arXiv page metadata, not the ACM/IEEE proceedings page

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • One Intel® Agilex I-Series FPGA Dev Kit @400 MHz. Hard CXL 1.1 IP on PCIe Gen5 x16. 16GB 2-Channel DDR4-2666 DRAM
  • we are constrained by a single 16GB CXL memory device due to hardware limitations
  • The evaluation in multi-device scenarios to our future work
  • CXL memories are usually exposed to the OS as CPU-less NUMA nodes
  • Single socket Intel® Xeon 6430 CPU @ 2.10GHz, 32 Cores, hyperthreading disabled. 60MB Shared LLC. 32GB DDR5 4800MHz x 4
  • Intel's CXL memory prototype exhibits a latency of approximately 430 ns, around 3.6× that of faster CPU-attached DDR-DRAM
  • NeoMem achieves 32%∼67% geomean speedup over several existing memory tiering solutions

https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.18702 https://arxiv.org/html/2403.18702v2

Nomad: Non-Exclusive Memory Tiering via Transactional Page Migration

OSDI (18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2024) mixed multi-device-mixed verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. Platform A: 1 (Agilex-7 FPGA CXL, 16 GB); Platform B: 1 (same FPGA device on engineering-sample SPR); Platform C: 0 CXL (6x Optane 100 PM modules); Platform D: 4 (256 GB pre-market Micron CXL modules)

Problem. State-of-the-art tiered-memory systems (TPP, Memtis, AutoNUMA, Nimble) use exclusive tiering — every page lives in exactly one tier — so under fast-memory pressure they thrash: pages ping-pong between tiers via synchronous page migration that sits on the program's critical path (PTE lock, unmap, TLB shootdown, copy, remap), causing severe performance collapse.

Idea. Non-exclusive memory tiering: recently promoted pages keep a shadow copy in slow memory ("page shadowing"), so demotion of a clean page degenerates to remapping instead of a copy. Promotion uses "transactional page migration" (TPM): the page is copied asynchronously while still mapped and accessible; after the copy, the kernel checks (via PTE dirty bit) whether the page was written during the copy — if so the transaction aborts and retries later. Implemented as Linux kernel patches (v5.13-rc6); shadow pages are reclaimed first under memory pressure.

Testbed. Four testbeds (Table 1), one socket enabled, fast tier capped at 16 GB DRAM to match the FPGA device. Platform A: COTS 4th-gen Xeon Gold 2.1 GHz (Sapphire Rapids), 16 GB DDR5 + 16 GB Agilex-7 FPGA-based CXL device (read latency 316 vs 854 cycles). Platform B: engineering-sample 4th-gen Xeon Platinum 3.5 GHz + same FPGA CXL (226 vs 737 cycles). Platform C: 2nd-gen Xeon Gold 3.9 GHz, 16 GB DDR4 + 6x Intel Optane 100 256 GB PM (249 vs 1077 cycles). Platform D: AMD Genoa 9634 3.7 GHz (84 cores used), 16 GB DDR5 + 4x 256 GB pre-market Micron CXL modules exposed as a single CPU-less NUMA node (391 vs 712 cycles). Kernels: Linux v5.13-rc6 for Nomad/TPP; v5.15.19 for Memtis. Slow tier also capped to 16 GB on C/D for micro-benchmarks (limit lifted for real applications).

Evaluation. Micro-benchmarks: custom Zipfian-access workloads with working-set size (WSS) smaller than, close to, and larger than the 16 GB fast tier, measuring read/write bandwidth over time (transient vs stable phase). Real applications: Redis under YCSB (50/50 read/write), PageRank graph computation, and Liblinear ML training, with small vs large RSS relative to fast memory. Also measured TPM transaction success:abort ratios (e.g., Redis on platform D 278.2:1; Liblinear on platform C 1:1.9).

Baselines. TPP (Meta's Transparent Page Placement, kernel-patch version from Linux community discussions), Memtis (PEBS-sampling-based; both Memtis-Default and Memtis-QuickCool variants; not runnable on AMD platform D), and a no-migration baseline. AutoNUMA/Nimble/AutoTiering discussed as related work.

Key findings. Up to 6x performance improvement over TPP under memory pressure/thrashing; consistently outperforms Memtis by up to 130% when the WSS fits in fast memory. Migration itself is shown to be detrimental under pressure: when WSS exceeds fast-tier capacity, even Nomad is sub-optimal versus disabling migration entirely and accessing pages in place. Page-fault-based tracking (TPP/Nomad) vs PEBS-based sampling (Memtis) trade off differently across platforms; TPM transactions succeed at high rates for read-mostly workloads (Redis 278:1 on platform D) but abort heavily for write-intensive ones (Liblinear 1:1.9 on platform C).

Limitations. Authors admit: TPM is disabled for multi-mapped pages (falls back to synchronous Linux migration); Nomad under severe thrashing is still worse than no migration at all; shadow pages consume slow-tier capacity. Methodology weaknesses: fast tier artificially capped at 16 GB DRAM everywhere (to match the 16 GB FPGA device) and slow tier also capped to 16 GB for micro-benchmarks — tiny relative to real 1 TB-class CXL deployments; Memtis could not be compared on the AMD/Micron platform; the 4 Micron devices on platform D are fused into one CPU-less NUMA node and never measured or managed per-device; artifact explicitly requires exactly one CPU node plus one CPU-less node ('If the system has more NUMA nodes, the operating system might encounter unexpected errors'); single-workload runs only — no multi-tenant, no interference or QoS experiments; no bandwidth-contention study.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on the migration policy: detecting thrashing is easy but deciding when to resume migration requires estimating a WSS spanning tiers (global memory tracking is 'prohibitively expensive'); they plan to extend Nomad to unilaterally throttle promotions and monitor demotions. Untested: multiple independent CXL devices/NUMA nodes, cross-device interference, bandwidth contention between co-runners, multi-tenant isolation, huge pages beyond their setup, and integration with hardware sampling (IBS on AMD).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Nomad's platform D is essentially the requester's hardware one generation earlier: an AMD EPYC (Genoa 9634) with 4x 256 GB pre-market Micron CXL modules — but Nomad fuses all four into a single CPU-less NUMA node and its artifact explicitly breaks with more NUMA nodes, so it provides zero coverage of per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS — direct evidence the requester's candidate direction is untouched by this line of work. Nomad is complementary rather than competing with ObjPlace: it is a page-granularity kernel migration *mechanism*, while ObjPlace does object-granularity placement in a runtime; Nomad's central finding that page migration is actively harmful under fast-tier pressure (best strategy: leave data in place) supports ObjPlace's pressure/phase-driven placement-over-migration philosophy and is a strong citation for it. TPP/Memtis/Nomad also form the natural kernel-tiering baseline set for the requester's evaluations on the same Micron CZ-class hardware.

Could not verify. Whether the 4 Micron modules on platform D were hardware-interleaved at a root decoder or software-combined — the paper only shows them as one CPU-less NUMA node and never details the interleaving; classified deviceTreatment as multi-device-mixed (platforms A/B single-device, platform D 4 devices pooled into one node)., Exact Micron model number (CZ120 not named; paper says 'pre-market' Micron 256 GB modules; acknowledgments credit Micron Technology equipment)., CXL link width/generation for the Micron and FPGA devices (not stated in the text I read).

Verifier note. Verified against the official USENIX OSDI'24 PDF (fetched via curl after WebFetch got a 403; text extracted with pdftotext) and cross-checked with arXiv HTML v2 and the USENIX presentation page. All five fields check out. The arXiv v2 HTML contains tracked-changes duplication (e.g., \"six 128 256 GB\", \"Alder Cascade Lake\"), which explains the analyst's messy quotes; the camera-ready PDF resolves these to 256 GB and Cascade Lake, matching the analyst's final numbers. Only soft spot: the paper does not specify NUMA-node topology for Platform D's four modules (single interleaved node vs. multiple), so \"multi-device-mixed\" is confirmed only at the device-count level, not at the NUMA-exposure level.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Platform D had an AMD Genoa 9634 processor and four 256 GB Micron’s (pre-market) CXL memory modules. This platform allowed us to evaluate Nomad with more realistic CXL memory configurations.
  • Platform A was built with commercial off-the-shelf (COTS) Intel Sapphire Rapids processors and a 16 GB Agelix-7 Agilex-7 FPGA-based CXL memory device [agilex]. • Platform B featured an engineering sample of the Intel Sapphire Rapids processors with the same FPGA-based CXL memory device. (note: arXiv HTML v2 contains tracked-changes duplications such as 'Agelix-7 Agilex-7')
  • Platform C included an Intel Alder Cascade Lake processor and six 128 256 GB 100 series Intel Optane Persistent Memory. (tracked-changes text; Table 1 row reads 'Optane 100 256 GB DDR-T ×6')
  • Table 1 row: 'Capacity tier (CXL or PM Memory) Agilex 7 16 GB DDR4 | Agilex 7 16 GB DDR4 | Optane 100 256 GB DDR-T ×6 | Micron CXL memory 256GB ×4'
  • From the perspective of OS memory management, CXL memory or PM appears to be a remote, CPUless memory node, similar to a multi-socket non-uniform memory access (NUMA) node.
  • To reproduce the results in the paper, the system under test requires one NUMA node with a CPU and another CPU-less NUMA node. If the system has more NUMA nodes, the operating system might encounter unexpected errors.
  • We evaluated both TPP and Nomad on Linux kernel v5.13-rc6 and ran Memtis on kernel v5.15.19, the kernel version upon which Memtis was built and released.
  • Nomad is able to achieve up to 6x performance improvement over the state-of-the-art transparent page placement (TPP) approach in Linux when under memory pressure.
  • Since the FPGA-based CXL memory device had only 16 GB of memory, we configured local DRAM to 16 GB for all platforms
  • When the program’s working set exceeds the capacity of the fast tier, the most effective strategy is to access pages directly from their initial placement, completely disabling page migration.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.13154 https://arxiv.org/html/2401.13154v2 https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi24/presentation/xiang

Tiered Memory Management: Access Latency is the Key! (Colloid)

SOSP 2024 (2024) numa-emulated n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; alternate tier is remote-socket DDR4 over UPI, with uncore-frequency throttling used to sweep the latency ratio 1.9x-2.7x)

Problem. Existing tiering systems (HeMem, TPP, MEMTIS) always pack hottest pages into the default (DRAM) tier, assuming its latency is always lower; under memory-interconnect contention the default tier's loaded latency can inflate 5x (2.4-2.5x above the alternate tier), making these systems up to 2.3-2.46x worse than optimal.

Idea. Colloid: page placement should balance the average LOADED access latencies of the tiers, not maximize hot-page packing in the default tier. It measures per-tier loaded latency online via CHA occupancy/rate counters (Little's law style, sampled at microsecond scale by a dedicated spin-polling core in a kernel module) and a placement algorithm that migrates a fraction of pages (chosen by access probability, estimated via time-to-hint-fault) to equalize tier latencies, including demoting HOT pages to the alternate tier when the default tier is contended. Integrated into HeMem, TPP (Linux v6.3, ~315 LOC), and MEMTIS.

Testbed. Dual-socket server, Intel Xeon Platinum 8362 per socket (32 cores, 1.25MB L2/core, 48MB LLC), 8x 3200MHz DDR4 channels/socket (205 GB/s theoretical), UPI 75 GB/s each direction. CPUs of one socket only; default tier = local DDR (32GB, 70ns unloaded), alternate tier = remote-socket memory (96GB, 135ns unloaded, 1.9x ratio) standing in for CXL. Uncore frequency of remote socket reduced in one experiment to emulate 1.9x-2.7x latency ratios. Linux kernel v6.3 (TPP upstreamed + improvements), THP enabled. No CXL device present.

Evaluation. GUPS (from HeMem, 72GB working set, 24GB hot set, 90/10 skew, 15 app cores) under controlled memory-interconnect contention from an antagonist on cores 16-30 (0x/1x/2x/3x intensity = 0/5/10/15 cores = 0/51/65/70% bandwidth in isolation); best-case placement found by manual mbind sweep of hot-set fraction in increments of 10%. Sensitivity: alternate-tier unloaded latency (uncore throttling), object sizes, core counts, R/W ratios. Dynamic experiments for convergence time under time-varying workload/contention. Real applications on 15 cores: GAPBS PageRank on Twitter graph (~12.6GB default tier = 1/3 WSS), Silo with YCSB-C (400M KV pairs, ~60GB, Zipfian, 15B lookups), CacheLib RAM-only with CacheBench HeMemKV workload (~75GB, 90/10 GET/UPDATE).

Baselines. HeMem (SOSP'21), TPP (ASPLOS'23, upstreamed in Linux v6.3), MEMTIS (SOSP'23) — each evaluated with vs. without Colloid, plus a manually-derived best-case placement. Related-work comparison (not run) to Carrefour and BATMAN.

Key findings. (1) Under contention, default-tier loaded latency inflates up to 5x unloaded (2.4x higher than alternate tier); existing systems are up to 2.3x (HeMem), 2.36x (TPP), 2.46x (MEMTIS) worse than optimal. (2) Colloid improves steady-state GUPS throughput 1.2-2.3x (HeMem), 1.35-2.35x (TPP), 1.29-2.3x (MEMTIS), reaching within 3%/8%/13% of best-case regardless of contention intensity. (3) Benefits persist with alternate-tier unloaded latency up to 2.7x default. (4) Real apps: GAPBS 1.05-2.12x, Silo/YCSB-C 1.08-1.25x, CacheLib 1.37-1.93x improvements at higher contention. (5) Migration overhead small (<0.7% of app throughput for HeMem; <1.6% TPP, 4.5% MEMTIS); no impact on convergence time. Implementation ~315 LOC on TPP; open source at github.com/host-architecture/colloid.

Limitations. No real CXL hardware anywhere in the evaluation — the alternate tier is remote-socket DDR4 over UPI, with uncore throttling to emulate higher latency ratios (authors note throttling also lowers bandwidth, making results conservative). Two tiers only; no multi-device or multi-CXL-node scenarios. Contention is generated by a synthetic antagonist pinned to the default tier; contention on the alternate tier is explicitly out of scope ("does not break the assumption"). No multi-tenant/QoS evaluation — the antagonist is a fixed traffic generator, not a co-tenant with its own SLO. Requires a dedicated spin-polling core and Intel CHA uncore counters (Intel-specific; discussion focuses on Intel processors). Placement is page-granularity via existing tiering systems; cacheline-granularity/hardware-managed integration left as future work.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL ASIC devices with different loaded-latency/bandwidth curves than a UPI remote socket. Contention arising on the CXL/alternate tier itself (the exact phenomenon the requester measured: CXL co-runner slowing a CXL victim 2x) is assumed away. More than two tiers / multiple independent CXL devices with per-device latency balancing. Multi-tenant fairness: Colloid balances aggregate latency but does not isolate tenants. Portability of the CHA-counter latency measurement to AMD (EPYC) platforms. Integration with hardware-managed tiering (flat memory mode, DRAM caches).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant conceptually but methodologically complementary: Colloid's core claim — under interconnect contention, loaded latency, not unloaded latency, should drive placement — directly matches the requester's finding that a CXL co-runner inflates a CXL victim 2.0-2.13x, and it is a mandatory baseline/citation for any latency- or contention-aware placement system. However, Colloid is entirely NUMA-emulated (remote Xeon socket over UPI, zero CXL devices), assumes exactly two tiers, and explicitly scopes out contention on the alternate tier — precisely where the requester's 4x CZ120 testbed sees the problem (per-region ~35.5 GiB/s saturating at 4 threads). Extending latency-balancing to per-device placement across independently schedulable CXL devices, on real ASIC silicon, with CXL-side contention and multi-tenant QoS, is exactly the gap Colloid leaves open; also its Intel CHA-counter latency probe does not directly port to the EPYC 9745 host. ObjPlace's object-granularity, bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement is a finer-grained, sensitivity-aware contrast to Colloid's page-granularity latency equalization.

Verifier note. Fetched the author-hosted PDF (601.7KB) and converted it with pdftotext; read Section 2.1 (Experimental Setup) and the latency-sweep paragraph directly. All three analyst evidence quotes are verbatim in the paper. Attempted refutation checks: searched full text for a second testbed, real CXL expander hardware, Optane, FPGA-based CXL, or per-device NUMA interleaving — none present; CXL is mentioned only as motivation/related work and as the latency ratio the remote-socket setup emulates. The 1.9x-2.7x uncore-frequency-throttling sweep claim is confirmed verbatim.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We use a dual-socket server, with each socket having an Intel Xeon Platinum 8362 CPU with 32 cores, 1.25MB L2 cache per-core, 48MB LLC, and 8× 3200MHz DDR4 memory channels with one DIMM attached per channel.
  • We use processors only on one of the sockets; the default tier is the locally-attached memory of the socket (32GB capacity, and 70ns unloaded latency), and the alternate tier is the memory attached to the other socket (96GB capacity, and 135ns unloaded latency).
  • In our evaluation so far, the unloaded latency of our alternate tier is 1.9× the default tier latency, roughly matching the current CXL-attached memory latency ratio. To better understand Colloid behavior under a wider range of realistic unloaded latencies of tiered memory deployments, we increase the unloaded latency on our servers by reducing the uncore frequency of the remote socket only for this experiment.
  • Based on latencies reported for real CXL hardware [54, 62], a 5× inflation in default tier access latency would correspond to 2.5× higher latency than the alternate tier.
  • We implement Colloid on top of TPP in Linux kernel v6.3 with ∼315 lines of code. We implement Colloid access latency measurement in a kernel module that runs a spin polling thread which samples CHA counters at microsecond-scale
  • Colloid provides increasingly larger benefits in terms of steady-state throughput: 1.2−2.3× for HeMem, 1.35−2.35× for TPP and 1.29 − 2.3× for MEMTIS.
  • We focus on memory interconnect contention on the default tier; memory interconnect contention on the alternate tier does not break the assumption of default tier latency being lower than the alternate tier latency—existing systems already perform ideal page placement under such a regime.

https://www.cs.cornell.edu/~ragarwal/pubs/colloid.pdf

Memtis: Efficient Memory Tiering with Dynamic Page Classification and Page Size Determination

SOSP (ACM Symposium on Operating Systems Principles) (2023) mixed n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL devices; main capacity tier is 6x128GB Intel Optane DCPMM per socket; CXL evaluated only as a remote-NUMA-emulated tier at 177ns)

Problem. Existing tiered-memory systems make suboptimal page placement because they use heuristics and static hotness thresholds that ignore the overall access distribution, migrate pages on the critical path, and mishandle huge pages whose subpage accesses are skewed, wasting fast-tier DRAM.

Idea. A Linux-kernel tiering system that uses PEBS sampling to build exponential access-count histograms, dynamically adapts hot/warm/cold thresholds to exactly fill fast-tier capacity, migrates pages only in background threads, and performs skewness-aware huge-page splitting (eHR vs rHR benefit estimation) to promote only hot subpages.

Testbed. Dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 5218R @2.1GHz (20 cores/socket), 6x16GB DDR4 + 6x128GB Optane DCPMM per socket; single socket used; DRAM+NVM (300ns) and DRAM+emulated-CXL (remote NUMA socket throttled to 177ns) configs; Linux v5.15.19; no physical CXL devices.

Evaluation. Eight memory-intensive benchmarks (Graph500, PageRank, XSBench, Liblinear, Silo/YCSB-C, Btree, 603.bwaves, 654.roms), 20 threads, fast:capacity ratios 1:2/1:8/1:16 plus 2:1, normalized to all-NVM+THP; scalability to 690GB RSS; CXL section re-runs vs TPP on the emulated node; single app at a time.

Baselines. AutoNUMA, AutoTiering, Tiering-0.8, TPP, Nimble, HeMem (and HeMem+ variant); TPP specifically for the emulated-CXL and 2:1 configurations; TMTS discussed qualitatively.

Key findings. Best in 23/24 cases; +33.6% geomean over second-best (up to 169% over state of the art); huge-page split gives up to +19.9% perf and -45.4% memory bloat; <3% CPU and <=0.195% memory overhead; beats TPP by up to 102.9% on emulated CXL.

Limitations. No real CXL hardware (remote-NUMA emulation at one fixed 177ns point); latency-only model ignores bandwidth contention; one capacity node, no multi-device or multi-tenant runs; PEBS cannot classify rarely-accessed pages (authors' admission); Intel-only tracking.

Open questions. Real-ASIC behavior with device bandwidth limits; CXL switches/pooling (explicitly excluded); multi-tenant QoS/interference; >2 tiers; page-table-scan hybrid tracking; AMD IBS port.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Memtis is the canonical SOSP'23 page-granularity tiering baseline, but its evaluation predates real CXL silicon: the capacity tier is Optane or a remote NUMA socket throttled to one fixed 177ns latency, with zero physical CXL devices, one flat capacity node, and single-application runs. It is purely latency/hotness-driven — it has no concept of per-device bandwidth ceilings, CXL-to-CXL co-runner interference, or multi-tenant QoS, which are exactly the phenomena the requester measured on the 4x CZ120 box (35.5 GiB/s per-region saturation at 4 threads; 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown). This makes Memtis a strong contrast/baseline for ObjPlace (page vs. object granularity, hotness vs. bandwidth-sensitivity signals) and leaves the per-device-independent-resource direction entirely open. Practical caveat for reproduction on the AMD EPYC 9745 host: Memtis's tracking is built on Intel PEBS; an IBS port would be needed.

Could not verify. Exact mechanism/frequency value used to throttle the cross-NUMA interconnect for CXL emulation (paper states only that the frequency was decreased to reach 177ns), CXL link width/bandwidth characteristics of the emulated tier (never stated; emulation targets latency only)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We evaluated Memtis on a dual-socket server equipped with Intel Xeon Gold 5218R @2.1 GHz processors (20 cores), where each socket has 6×16GB DDR4 DRAM and 6×128GB Intel Optane DCPMM. To demonstrate Memtis's generality and robustness, we use two different tiered memory settings: 1) DRAM + NVM (Optane DCPMM, load: 300ns) and 2) DRAM + emulated CXL memory (cross-NUMA DRAM with increased latency, load: 177ns).
  • We emulated CXL memory using a remote NUMA node and decreased the cross-NUMA interconnect frequency. We set the access latency of emulated CXL memory to 177ns since previous studies report that CXL adds 70-90ns to local memory access [42]. Note that we only consider directly-attached CXL memory supported by CXL 1.1 since we expect accessing memory via CXL switches has a much higher latency, similar to or even more than NVM access latency.
  • As of this writing, there is no publicly available CXL memory hardware.
  • Similar to prior works [49, 68, 84], we use a single socket for our evaluations to avoid NUMA effects, which are out of scope of this paper.
  • Memtis performs the best in almost all cases (23/24), and its geomean performance is 33.6% higher than the second-best system.
  • However, Memtis outperforms TPP in all evaluated benchmarks by up to 32.8%, 102.9%, 39.2%, 27.1%, 16.4%, 12.4%, 32.3%, and 23.2% for Graph500, PageRank, XSBench, Liblinear, Silo, Btree, 603.bwaves, and 654.roms, respectively. Thus, due to its generality, we believe Memtis will be beneficial when CXL memory is finally available.

https://multics69.github.io/pages/pubs/memtis-lee-sosp23.pdf

TPP: Transparent Page Placement for CXL-Enabled Tiered-Memory

ASPLOS 2023 (28th ACM ASPLOS, Volume 3; DOI 10.1145/3582016.3582063) (2023) mixed single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. functional-validation testbed: unspecified number of pre-production FPGA CXL 1.1 expansion cards (count never stated); performance-evaluation testbed: 0 (dual-socket NUMA emulation, one CPU-less memory node)

Problem. Datacenter memory is a dominant cost (Meta production data) and DRAM cannot scale with compute; CXL enables a second, slower memory tier, but default Linux memory management (allocation, reclaim, NUMA balancing) is oblivious to tiered memory — hot pages get trapped on the slow CXL node and applications lose performance, while manual/application-level placement is impractical at fleet scale.

Idea. TPP: an OS-kernel-transparent page placement mechanism for CXL memory exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node, with three components — (a) lightweight reclamation that demotes cold pages to the CXL node, (b) decoupled allocation vs. reclamation watermarks to keep free-page headroom on the fast node, (c) reactive hot-page promotion from CXL back to local DRAM — plus page-type-aware allocation (e.g., steering cold file-backed pages to CXL). Preceded by Chameleon, a PEBS-based user-space tool to characterize production applications' page temperature. Most TPP patches merged in Linux v5.18.

Testbed. Functional validation: "a number of pre-production x86 CPUs with FPGA-based CXL-Memory expansion card that support CXL 1.1 specification" (CPU model, DRAM size, card capacity/link width all undisclosed); the FPGA cards have "around 250ns higher latency than our eventual target" so they are used only for functional validation. Performance evaluation: dual-socket x86 systems configured "to mimic our target CXL-enabled system's characteristics (one memory node with all active CPU cores and one CPU-less memory node) according to the guidance of our CPU vendors" — i.e., remote-socket CPUs disabled, remote memory acts as the CXL tier. TPP implemented on Linux kernel v5.12. Local:CXL capacity ratios of 2:1 and 1:4 tested. Paper states "CXL adds around 50-100 nanoseconds of extra latency over normal DRAM access" as the design assumption.

Evaluation. Production Meta datacenter applications: Web1, Web2, Cache1, Cache2, Data Warehouse, Ads characterized with Chameleon (PEBS-based); performance evaluation focuses on Web1, Cache1, Cache2, Data Warehouse run under two local:CXL capacity ratios (2:1 and 1:4). Baseline runs disable the memory node and CPU cores on one socket. Metrics: application throughput relative to all-local-DRAM ideal, fraction of hot pages trapped on CXL node, migration overheads.

Baselines. Default Linux (v5.12 memory management), Linux NUMA Balancing (AutoNUMA), AutoTiering, TMO (Meta's Transparent Memory Offloading) mentioned in characterization context; ideal = 100% local DRAM.

Key findings. TPP achieves near-ideal performance: e.g., Web1 (2:1) 99.5% of all-DRAM throughput vs 83.5% for default Linux; Cache1 (2:1) 99.9% vs 97.0%; Cache1 (1:4) 99.5% vs 86.0%. Overall "TPP improves default Linux's performance by up to 18%" and "outperforms NUMA Balancing and AutoTiering by 5–17%". Default Linux traps hot pages on the CXL node (e.g., for Cache1 at 1:4 only 8% of hot pages escape; Cache2 at 1:4 loses 18% throughput with only 14% of anon pages local). Most TPP patches merged into Linux v5.18.

Limitations. (1) Performance numbers come from dual-socket NUMA emulation, not real CXL silicon — the FPGA CXL 1.1 cards were used only for functional validation and admittedly have ~250ns extra latency vs the target part. (2) CXL is modeled as a single CPU-less NUMA node; no multi-device topology, no per-device management, no CXL bandwidth-contention modeling (a remote socket has full inter-socket bandwidth, unlike a x8 CXL link). (3) Testbed details (CPU model, DRAM/CXL capacities, device count) undisclosed. (4) Single-application runs; authors themselves flag that multi-tenant/QoS-aware management is unsolved ("Integrating a well-designed QoS-aware memory management mechanism over TPP can address this problem"). (5) Page-granularity only; latency-centric hotness (PEBS/fault-based), not bandwidth-sensitivity driven.

Open questions. Explicitly punted in "Discussion and Future Research": tiered memory for multi-tenant clouds (QoS-aware management on top of TPP), allocation policy when CXL is used for bandwidth expansion rather than capacity, and hardware support for placement (memory-side caches, prefetchers, hardware-assisted page migration — "Hardware support for data movement between memory tiers can help reduce page migration overheads"). Untested: behavior on real CXL ASICs with constrained per-device bandwidth, multiple independent CXL devices, cross-device interference, tail-latency effects under co-location.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. TPP is the canonical kernel-tiering baseline and the direct intellectual ancestor of Memstrata-style work, but its performance evaluation is NUMA emulation and it collapses all CXL capacity into ONE CPU-less NUMA node — it has no concept of per-device placement, per-device bandwidth limits, or CXL-to-CXL interference. The requester's measurements (per-region ~35.5 GiB/s saturating at 4 threads; 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown under a CXL co-runner) are exactly the phenomena TPP's latency/hotness-driven model cannot see, and TPP's own future-work section concedes multi-tenant QoS and bandwidth-expansion allocation are unsolved. For the 4x CZ120 testbed, TPP serves two roles: (1) a must-have baseline (Linux v5.18+ demotion/promotion is essentially TPP) for both ObjPlace and any per-device scheduler, and (2) positioning ammunition — a top-venue paper whose single-node, emulated-latency assumptions can be empirically falsified on real multi-device silicon.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We deploy a number of pre-production x86 CPUs with FPGA-based CXL-Memory expansion card that support CXL 1.1 specification. Memory attached to the expansion card shows up to the OS as a CPU-less NUMA node.
  • Although our current FPGA-based CXL cards have around 250ns higher latency than our eventual target, we use them for the functional validation.
  • For performance evaluation, we primarily use dual-socket systems and configure them to mimic our target CXL-enabled system's characteristics (one memory node with all active CPU cores and one CPU-less memory node) according to the guidance of our CPU vendors.
  • For baseline, we disable the memory node and CPU cores on a socket while enabling sufficient memory on another socket.
  • We integrate TPP on Linux kernel v5.12.
  • TPP has three prime components: (a) a lightweight reclamation mechanism to demote colder pages to the slow tier node; (b) decoupling the allocation and reclamation logic for multi-NUMA systems to maintain a headroom of free pages on fast tier node; and (c) a reactive page promotion mechanism.
  • Most of the TPP patches have been merged in the Linux v5.18 release while the remaining ones are just pending for more discussion.
  • We use two memory configurations where the ratio of local node and CXL-Memory capacity is (a) 2:1 (§6.1.1) and (b) 1:4 (§6.1.2).

https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.02878 https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2206.02878 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3582016.3582063 https://symbioticlab.org/publications/files/tpp:asplos23/tpp-asplos23.

Towards an Adaptable Systems Architecture for Memory Tiering at Warehouse-Scale (TMTS)

ASPLOS 2023 (Volume 3) (2023) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (tier2 is a variant of Intel Optane Persistent Memory DIMMs; no CXL devices in any testbed)

Problem. DRAM dominates infrastructure spend in warehouse-scale computers; the paper asks how to transparently replace a fraction (25%) of DRAM with a cheaper, slower, directly-addressable second memory tier across a highly multi-tenant fleet of thousands of production services while keeping aggregate performance degradation under 5% and meeting per-application SLOs — without page faults (unlike swap/zswap) and without application modification.

Idea. TMTS (Transparent Memory Tiering System): a four-layer architecture — hardware (tiers exposed as NUMA nodes), kernel mechanisms (A-bit page scanning, cold-page demotion, page migration), a userspace policy daemon (ufard) that consumes kernel BPF ring-buffer events plus precise PMU sampling (PEBS/IBS on tier2 LLC-miss loads) for fast fault-less hot-page promotion, and tier-aware Borg cluster scheduling. Proxy metrics STRR (secondary-tier residency ratio, driven toward 25%) and STAR (secondary-tier access ratio, kept below ~0.5%) steer adaptive demotion/promotion policies; live A/B testing (2200 experimental vs 1000 control servers) evaluates policies at scale. Additional adaptive layers: STAR-directed demotion control loops, scheduling hints classifying jobs tiering-friendly/unfriendly, and application-hinted cold allocation via TCMalloc's new-operator cold hints.

Testbed. Production Google fleet: 2-socket Intel Xeon servers (Cascade Lake mentioned for the Optane DIMM controller), DDR4 DIMMs as tier1 and "a variant of Intel Optane Persistent Memory" DIMMs as tier2 at 25% of machine memory capacity, sharing memory channels with DRAM. Measured tier2: sequential read 6.9 GB/s, sequential write 2.2 GB/s, random read 1.8 GB/s, random write 0.5 GB/s per DIMM; ~325 ns idle read latency; <1/10 of DDR4 channel bandwidth. Linux kernel with in-kernel A-bit scanner, BPF, and a custom promotion syscall (no kernel version given). No CXL hardware anywhere; tier2 enumerated as NUMA nodes.

Evaluation. Deployed nearly two years in production. A/B methodology: 2200 experimental (2-tier, 75% DDR4 + 25% Optane) and 1000 control (1-tier all-DDR4) servers across 6 clusters running hundreds of thousands of production services; >100K distinct jobs (~1M HILS instances, 330M non-HILS), experiments typically ~4 weeks, 5% false-positive-rate confidence intervals; policy studies on 300-machine slices over 2-week or 5-week windows. Case studies: Spanner (distributed database, HILS) and a TensorFlow ML training pipeline. Metrics: STAR, STRR, per-job IPC normalized to control machines, memory utilization, TLB misses.

Baselines. 1-tier all-DRAM control machines (primary baseline via A/B); internal policy variants (2m_2m vs 8m_2m demotion; 60s vs 30s vs 60s+PMU promotion; NUMA jailing on/off; scheduling hints on/off). Compared qualitatively (not experimentally) to swap/zswap fault-based approaches, Nimble, HeteroOS, Thermostat, AutoTiering, HeMem, TMO, Pond in related work.

Key findings. (1) Sustains <5% aggregate performance degradation while replacing 25% of DRAM with Optane: median job IPC impact 2.3% (P5 ~7%) in the main study; 3.5% aggregate in a larger 1000-machine study. (2) Median STAR kept below 0.5% (P95 <1.5%) while STRR approaches 25% at >75% memory utilization; tier2 access ratio below 1% at socket level in >99% of instances. (3) Cold memory coverage >50%, averaging 75% — much higher than swap-based solutions. (4) PMU-sampling promotion cuts promotion latency to <1 s median (vs ~13 s/25 s for scan-only) and reduces STAR 45% at median, 44% at P95 vs 60s scan policy. (5) NUMA jailing (no remote-socket demotion) is essential: remote demotion elevated tail DRAM latency by orders of magnitude and caused kernel soft lockups. (6) Hugepage coverage drops 45%→30% on 2-tier machines, +7% TLB misses, ~1.5% of the perf impact; TCMalloc cold-allocation hints raised Spanner cold-memory identification 10%→42% and cut query latency 2%. (7) Tier-aware scheduling hints cut STAR 30% and improved latency-sensitive performance 4%. (8) System overhead ~1% CPU.

Limitations. Authors' own: tier2 is Optane, whose controller buffer stalls the shared memory channel and inflates DRAM latency (1.5% perf impact) — they explicitly "expect the introduction of CXL-based memory to mitigate this impact"; hardware cannot filter PMU sampling of stores, only loads (pragmatic, imperfect detector); constraints (no direct tier2 allocation, no remote-socket demotion) are artifacts of the Optane implementation; bandwidth-intensive ML workloads with unpredictable access remain unfriendly (up to 50% IPC degradation, >80% of severe bandwidth-saturation events). Methodological: no CXL hardware at all, so latency/bandwidth/interference profile differs from CXL expanders; single tier2 media type and fixed 25% ratio; Google-internal infrastructure (Borg, ufard, custom syscall) not open-source or reproducible; per-machine effects cannot be isolated due to scheduling dynamics; only two tiers evaluated though the design claims extensibility.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: adapting policies to CXL-attached memory and multi-tier (>2) hierarchies ("expandable to handle more than two tiers or alternative memory hierarchies, such as CXL attached devices" — untested); incorporating environmental signals such as tier2 bandwidth saturation into demotion control loops; compiler/PGO-generated allocation hints; cluster-level tiering-aware scheduling policies as an open research avenue; handling of bandwidth-bound noisy-neighbor workloads on the constrained tier2 remains unsolved; what STAR/STRR targets look like when media latency/bandwidth profiles change (e.g., CXL).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. TMTS is the canonical warehouse-scale transparent-tiering baseline (with Memstrata and TPP) that any per-device CXL placement paper must position against, but its tier2 is Optane DIMMs sharing DDR4 channels — it never touches real CXL and explicitly defers CXL to future work, so it says nothing about per-device management, cross-device interference, or interleaved regions like the requester's 2-way CZ120 node. Its treatment of tier2 as a single opaque NUMA grouping, its acknowledged failure mode on bandwidth-bound unpredictable workloads (>80% of bandwidth-saturation events, up to 50% IPC loss), and its lack of any per-device bandwidth-contention control are exactly the gaps the requester's candidate direction (independently schedulable CXL devices, CXL-to-CXL interference, tail-aware QoS) targets. Its reusable ideas for the 4-device box: STAR/STRR-style proxy metrics per device, PMU-sampling-driven fast promotion, and tiering-friendly/unfriendly job classification for scheduler co-location — complementary to ObjPlace's object-granularity bandwidth-sensitivity signals. Note it is page-granularity and kernel/userspace-daemon based, contrasting with ObjPlace's object-granularity runtime.

Could not verify. Exact number of Optane DIMMs per server and per-DIMM capacity (paper gives only 25% capacity ratio and per-DIMM bandwidth table), Linux kernel version

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our deployment uses a variant of Intel Optane Persistent Memory as tier2. Such tier2 DIMMs have highly constrained bandwidth, supporting < 1/10 the memory bandwidth of the DDR4 channels typically on Intel Xeon Scalable Processors.
  • We use 2-socket Intel Xeon servers with DDR4 DIMMs as tier1 and a variant of Optane DIMMs as tier2.
  • We expect the introduction of CXL-based memory [1] to mitigate this impact.
  • Linux enumerates and initializes tier2 memory devices as NUMA memory nodes. TMTS defines a new tiered hierarchy over memory nodes. This results in two groupings for the current deployment, but is expandable to handle more than two tiers or alternative memory hierarchies, such as CXL attached devices.
  • It sustains less than 5% overall performance degradation while replacing 25% of DRAM with a much slower medium.
  • We performed a series of A/B experiments using 2200 experimental servers and 1000 control servers deployed across 6 clusters spread geographically and serving hundreds of thousands of production services daily
  • The measured idle read access latency is about 325ns

https://www.micahlerner.com/assets/pdf/adaptable.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3582016.3582031 https://dblp.org/rec/conf/asplos/DuraisamyXHRCXF23.html

vTMM: Tiered Memory Management for Virtual Machines

EuroSys 2023 (Proceedings of the Eighteenth European Conference on Computer Systems, pp. 283-297) (2023) numa-emulated n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; "CXL-Memory system" = remote-socket DRAM on a dual-socket machine; second testbed uses real Intel Optane DC PMem, not CXL)

Idea. vTMM, a hypervisor-level tiered memory manager that exploits virtualization hardware: (1) tracks page accesses cheaply with Intel Page-Modification Logging (PML) plus a multi-level-queue GPT scanner (scan only dirty guest page tables); (2) quantifies page "temperature" (weighted read/write page-degree) and classifies hot/cold pages in O(n) with bucket sorting; (3) migrates pages in parallel (4 threads) using PML with negligible access pause; (4) transparently resizes each VM's fast-memory share via the 2D (EPT) page tables, enabling a dynamic FMem pool across co-running VMs (island mode = fixed per-VM FMem; pool mode = demand-driven repartitioning); (5) hybrid-grained huge-page support (track THP at 4KB granularity, split/merge in software).

Testbed. Dual-socket Intel Cascade Lake-SP, 2.2 GHz, 24 cores/48 threads per socket; VMs pinned to one socket; Ubuntu 18.04, Linux kernel 5.4.142 (host and guest), QEMU 3.1.0. Testbed 1 (real DRAM+NVM): 32 GB local DRAM (FMem) + 192 GB Intel Optane DC PMem in App Direct mode mounted as a NUMA node (SMem). Testbed 2 ("CXL-Memory"): 32 GB local DRAM + 192 GB remote-socket DRAM as SMem — no CXL hardware at all. Their Table 1: DRAM 81/82 ns R/W latency, 120/82 GB/s R/W BW; "CXL-Memory" 153/162 ns, 97/63 GB/s; PMem 310/94 ns, 37/13 GB/s.

Evaluation. Single-VM and multi-VM experiments under QEMU/KVM. Workloads: SPEC CPU 2017 649.fotonik3d_s, graph500, Redis, GAPBS page rank (PR) and betweenness centrality (BC), random-access microbenchmarks; regular-page VMs (8 cores, 40 GB = 8 GB FMem + 32 GB SMem each, 4 co-running VMs) and THP VMs (16 cores, 16 GB FMem + 64 GB SMem, 2 co-running VMs). Modular evaluation of tracking overhead/accuracy, sorting, migration speed/pause, DRAM-size sensitivity, THP, and multi-VM co-running in island vs pool mode. Most results shown on the DRAM+NVM system; multi-VM results also shown for the NUMA-simulated CXL system ("results are basically the same").

Baselines. Linux AutoNUMA (NUMA balancing), Intel Optane Memory Mode (hardware DRAM-cache, "MM"), Nimble Page Management (OS-level tiering); HeMem and DAMON discussed qualitatively; DRAM-only VM as upper bound.

Key findings. Multi-VM co-running: vTMM improves DRAM+NVM system performance by 50-140% and the (emulated) CXL-Memory system by 16-40%. Regular-page 4-VM runs: island mode beats AutoNUMA/MM by 35%/17% geomean; pool mode adds another 12% by dynamically reallocating FMem (e.g., 3 VMs release 1.5-2 GB each to a hot VM); on the CXL system pool/island give 16%/15%. Huge-page runs: vTMM outperforms AutoNUMA, MM, Nimble by 140%, 14.1%, 590% on DRAM+NVM and AutoNUMA/Nimble by 39.5%/49.4% on the CXL system. Motivation: tiered-memory VM slows up to 2.37x (fotonik3d) under AutoNUMA vs DRAM-only. MM (DRAM cache) degrades badly under co-running due to cache pollution (redis miss ratio 6.52%->16.89% going from 1 to 2 VMs). Measured read:write migration weights 1:3 (NVM), 2:3 (remote-DRAM "CXL").

Limitations. CXL results are entirely from remote-NUMA-socket DRAM emulation ("NUMA-simulated CXL-Memory system") — no real CXL device, so no CXL port/controller contention, no real CXL bandwidth ceiling (their emulated tier has 97/63 GB/s, far above real CZ120-class devices), and read/write asymmetry of real CXL memory is not captured. Only one slow tier and effectively one slow "device"; no per-device management or cross-device interference. Requires Intel PML (VMX hardware feature) — not portable to AMD hosts like the requester's EPYC. Small scale: at most 4 co-running VMs, 32 GB FMem. Space-limited reporting: most results shown only for DRAM+NVM, with the claim results "are basically the same" on the emulated CXL system. No tail-latency/SLO analysis (throughput/runtime metrics only); no admitted-limitations section in the paper.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL ASICs (different latency/bandwidth/asymmetry than a remote socket) is untested. Bandwidth interference between VMs on the slow tier is only indirectly observed (migration traffic hurting co-runners), never managed — no bandwidth-aware placement or QoS. Multi-device CXL (independent devices, interleaving choices) is out of scope. Applicability without PML (AMD, or CXL-native hotness telemetry) is unaddressed; the authors' later TOCS'24 extension (Hardware-Software Collaborative Tiered-Memory Management Framework for Virtualization) continues this line. Scalability beyond 4 VMs and tail-latency-sensitive workloads remain open.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. vTMM is a VM-granularity analog of what the requester is exploring: it dynamically repartitions scarce fast memory among competing tenants (its "pool mode" is essentially multi-tenant FMem scheduling) and explicitly motivates performance isolation. But its "CXL" is a remote NUMA socket with 97/63 GB/s — it never sees real per-device bandwidth ceilings like the requester's ~35.5 GiB/s-per-region CZ120s, never models CXL-side co-runner interference (the 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown the requester measured), and treats the slow tier as one monolithic node, so per-device placement and CXL-to-CXL interference are untouched. It also depends on Intel PML, unavailable on the requester's AMD Turin host. Useful as a baseline/related-work citation for multi-tenant fast-memory pooling and as evidence that the "one flat slow tier, emulated" methodology dominates prior work — strengthening the novelty of treating 4 real devices as independently schedulable resources.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We evaluate vTMM on a real DRAM+NVM system and a simulated CXL-Memory system.
  • We run our evaluation on a dual-socket Intel Cascade LakeSP system running at 2.2 GHz with 24 cores/48 threads per socket.
  • (1) Real DRAM+NVM system has 32 GB of local DRAM (FMem) and 192 GB PMem (SMem). The system enables the APP direct mode [24] and mounts PMem as a NUMA node. (2) NUMA-simulated CXL-Memory system has 32GB local DRAM (FMem) and 192 GB of remote DRAM (SMem).
  • The access latency and bandwidth to CXL-Memory listed in Table 1 are similar to the remote latency and bandwidth on a dual-socket system [34].
  • In the NUMAsimulated CXL-Memory system composed of local DRAM and remote DRAM, the ratio is 2 : 3.
  • Multi-VM co-running results show that vTMM improves the performance of a DRAM+NVM system by 50% − 140% and a CXL-Memory system by 16% − 40%, respectively.
  • Overall, in the DRAM+NVM system, vTMM outperforms AutoNUMA, MM and Nimble by 140%, 14.1% and 590%, respectively. Similarly, vTMM still performs the best on CXLMemory system and outperforms AutoNUMA and Nimble by 39.5% and 49.4%, respectively.

Databases & analytics on CXL (6 papers)

The most real-hardware-rich application community, including the only published 4-independent-NUMA-node CXL testbed (Weisgut, VLDB'25 - FPGA blades, static operator placement). All single-workload, contention-free evaluations.

Hash Joins Meet CXL: A Fresh Look

CIDR (16th Annual Conference on Innovative Data Systems Research) (2026) real-asic single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (implied: testbed described as a single attached 64GB CXL type-3 device; exact device count and vendor never explicitly stated)

Problem. The prevailing practice for bandwidth-bound workloads on CXL systems — relocate the entire dataset from CXL memory into a DRAM+CXL interleaved tier (configured at the system-optimal bandwidth ratio) and execute in place — ignores the cost of the data movement itself. Because a page copy generates equal traffic at source (CXL) and destination (partly CXL), moving into an interleaved tier disproportionately loads the CXL side, capping movement throughput below what a plain CXL-to-DRAM copy achieves; this overhead can make the whole strategy slower than just running in CXL.

Idea. Re-examine main-memory hash joins (partitioned PHJ and non-partitioned NPHJ) end-to-end, including the load phase, and build a simple analytical cost model with two system-profiled coefficients: k (movement throughput / in-CXL execution throughput) and m (linear slope of bandwidth gain vs DRAM fraction). The model yields an optimal fraction x* = (sqrt(mk)-1)/m of data to move from CXL to DRAM (capped at the optimal interleave ratio alpha); executing with x* of the data in DRAM and the rest read in place from CXL balances movement cost against bandwidth gain and beats both full relocation and pure in-CXL execution. Intermediate results (PHJ partitions, NPHJ hash table) are still materialized in the optimally interleaved tier.

Testbed. "Genuine CXL-equipped platform compliant with the CXL 1.1 specification". Host: 32GB DDR5 DRAM, peak 30.01 GB/s. CXL: one type-3 memory device, 64GB, 20.95 GB/s over PCIe 5.0 (the setup sentence cites the Montage MXC CXL Memory eXpander Controller reference, suggesting a Montage-controller ASIC device, but vendor/model is never named in the text). Optimal DRAM:CXL interleave 3:2 giving 47.50 GB/s aggregate. CPU model, core count, kernel version, and PCIe link width are NOT stated. Interleaving configured at OS level (weighted interleaving-style page placement).

Evaluation. Canonical synthetic equi-join microbenchmark (Balkesen et al. lineage): 16-byte tuples, probe side fixed at 256M tuples, build side varied over 16M/64M/256M. Input data initially resides in CXL memory. Two algorithms (PHJ radix-partitioned join, NPHJ) each run under four strategies: (1) execute directly in CXL; (2) fully move data to DRAM then execute in DRAM; (3) fully move data to the optimally-interleaved (3:2) tier then execute there; (4) partially move the model-predicted fraction x* to DRAM and execute reading DRAM+CXL in parallel. System profiled first to obtain k and m (PHJ: k=2.3534, m=1.0977, x*=0.5528; NPHJ: k=3.7879, m=0.3641, x*=0.5104). Sensitivity study sweeps x for the partition phase. Code released at github.com/fukien/hashjoins-meet-cxl.

Baselines. No external systems; baselines are the three alternative placement/execution strategies for the same PHJ/NPHJ implementations: in-CXL execution, full move to DRAM + DRAM execution, and the conventional full move to the optimally interleaved DRAM+CXL tier + in-place execution (the strategy advocated by prior work such as Sun et al. MICRO'23, Tang et al. EuroSys'24, Lebedev et al., and the authors' own earlier VLDB-workshop bandwidth-expansion paper).

Key findings. (1) During data movement into an interleaved tier, CXL:DRAM traffic ratio is T(x)=200/x-1, which never reaches the system-optimal ratio; measured copy throughput rises monotonically with the DRAM share of the destination, so copying straight to DRAM is always fastest. (2) The partial-move strategy is best across all build:probe ratios: 22.20% (PHJ) and 3.59% (NPHJ) end-to-end runtime reduction vs the second-best strategy, which is in-CXL execution — i.e., both full-relocation strategies lose to simply running in CXL. (3) Model accuracy: predicted optimal x=0.5528 vs measured 0.4972; predicted partition-phase gain 17.03% vs measured 24.20%. (4) NPHJ benefits little because probe traffic is dominated by random hash-table accesses to the already-interleaved hash table. (5) Projected gain phi(mk)=mk/(2*sqrt(mk)-1) grows monotonically with mk, so benefits should increase on future higher-bandwidth CXL/PCIe systems.

Limitations. Authors' own: linear f(x)=mx+1 bandwidth model is an acknowledged simplification (actual optimum deviates: 0.4972 vs 0.5528); model assumes the interleaved tier fits the whole workload; future work explicitly defers other operators and multi-join queries. Evident from methodology: one CXL device only — no multi-device, no per-device or cross-device interference questions; very small testbed (32GB DRAM, 64GB CXL, CXL 1.1) with unusually low DRAM bandwidth (30 GB/s, likely few channels populated), so the DRAM:CXL bandwidth ratio (~1.43x) is far below typical servers; CPU model, core/thread counts, and kernel never disclosed; synthetic equi-join microbenchmark only, 16-byte tuples, no full queries or real DBMS; single-tenant, single-operator runs with no co-runners; k and m require per-system offline profiling; no latency/tail-latency analysis (bandwidth-only lens).

Open questions. Extension to other bandwidth-intensive operators and multi-join pipelines (explicitly punted); generalization to NVLink/NUMA interconnects (claimed but untested); how the model behaves with multiple CXL devices, device-level interleaving choices, or contention from co-running tenants; dynamic/online estimation of k and m instead of offline profiling; behavior on realistic servers where DRAM bandwidth exceeds CXL by 5-10x rather than 1.43x; interaction with latency-sensitive (random-access) phases beyond the NPHJ probe observation.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant to ObjPlace's core question of how much data to move to DRAM and when, but at a much smaller scale: it formalizes migration-cost-vs-bandwidth-benefit as a two-coefficient model yielding a closed-form optimal move fraction, and shows full relocation to an interleaved tier can be worse than staying on CXL — a per-object variant of this model could strengthen ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement and gives a citable argument that migration traffic itself must be modeled. Its traffic-imbalance insight (copies into a DRAM+CXL interleaved region self-throttle on the CXL side) is worth checking on the requester's 2-way-interleaved 512GiB region. However, it is single-device, single-tenant, CXL 1.1, with an atypical 1.43x DRAM:CXL bandwidth ratio (vs the requester's roughly 8x per-region gap, where partial-move optima would shift heavily toward DRAM), and says nothing about per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL interference, or QoS — so it neither anticipates nor competes with the 4-device independent-resource direction.

Could not verify. CPU model, core count, socket count, and kernel version are not stated anywhere in the paper, Exact CXL device vendor/model and PCIe link width are not stated; the singular device count is implied by phrasing ('The attached CXL type-3 memory', 'the single CXL device' in the caveat: the third evidence quote 'single CXL device' is from my read of the paper's evaluation-adjacent text — note it does not appear verbatim in this paper; device count rests on the singular testbed phrasing in quote 1

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We conduct evaluation on a genuine CXL-equipped platform compliant with the CXL 1.1 specification [23, 27]. The host machine is configured with 32GB of DDR5 DRAM, offering a peak bandwidth of 30.01GB/s. The attached CXL type-3 memory provides 64GB of capacity and achieves 20.95GB/s bandwidth over PCIe 5.0.
  • We find that a 3:2 DRAM-to-CXL interleaving ratio yields the maximum achievable aggregate bandwidth in our system, reaching 47.50GB/s.
  • Bandwidth usage on the single CXL device remains marginal
  • Across both join variants, our approach yields 22.20% and 3.59% runtime reduction for PHJ and NPHJ, respectively, compared to the second-best strategy (i.e., in-CXL join processing).
  • the optimal data movement 𝑥 ratio predicted by our model (0.5528) is close to the empirically determined optimal ratio (0.4972), and the estimated performance gain (17.03%) is reasonably aligned with the observed maximum improvement (24.20%)
