CXL Memory Performance for In-Memory Data Processing

PVLDB (VLDB 2025), Vol. 18, No. 9, pp. 3119-3133 (2025) real-fpga multi-device-independent verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 4 (four Seagate Composable Memory Appliance (CMA) FPGA-based blades, each treated as one CXL device; each blade hosts 8x 128 GiB DDR4-1866)

Problem. Efficiently using CXL-attached memory in in-memory database systems requires knowing how DB operations (scans, B+tree index ops, full analytical workloads) behave on real CXL memory, and specifically how placing/interleaving data across MULTIPLE real CXL devices affects performance - prior studies used a single device or emulation.

Idea. An in-depth experimental study on a server with four real x16 PCIe Gen5 CXL 1.1 memory expansion devices, each exposed as a separate NUMA node: microbenchmarks (CXL-Bench, successor of PerMA-Bench) for throughput/latency scaling over 1-4 devices via round-robin page interleaving with mbind, TMA bottleneck analysis of vectorized column scans and BTreeOLC, and TPC-H on Hyrise with page-interleaving and access-frequency-based column placement strategies, plus an economic (TCO) analysis.

Testbed. Supermicro SYS-741GE-TNRT server; 2x Intel 5th Gen Xeon Scalable Gold 6542Y (24 cores each); 8x 32 GB DDR5-4800 per CPU; Ubuntu 24.04, Kernel 6.13. CXL: four Seagate Composable Memory Appliance (CMA) Blade prototypes, FPGA-based Type 3 memory expansion, PCIe Gen5 x16 CXL 1.1, each blade with four DDR4 channels x 2 DIMMs = 8x 128 GiB DDR4-1866 (1 TiB/blade). Each blade configured as a separate memory-only NUMA node. Interconnect table: DDR 64-bit 5.2 GT/s 41.6 GB/s per channel; CXL x16 32 GT/s 64 GB/s per link.

Evaluation. (1) CXL-Bench microbenchmarks: sequential/random reads/writes, 8 B-8 KiB access sizes, 1-48 threads, 7 GiB regions pinned via mbind to CPU / remote-CPU / 1-4 CXL NUMA nodes (round-robin page interleaving for >=2 devices); latency percentiles via dependent-chain 8-B accesses. (2) AVX-512 filtering column scan (512 MiB columns, selectivity 0.1%/100%) with placements CPU / CXL-1 / CXL-4 / ColumnsCXL (columns in CXL, TID writes to CPU), plus hybrid share-of-columns sweeps; Intel VTune TMA bottleneck breakdown. (3) BTreeOLC with YCSB read-heavy (95/5) and write-heavy (5/95) Zipfian workloads, 100 M ops on 100 M records, node sizes 256 B-4 KiB. (4) TPC-H SF 100 on Hyrise, 10 clients running all 22 queries in random order for 20 minutes; placement via polymorphic memory resources with linear allocators: round-robin/weighted page interleaving and top-n access-frequency column placement.

Baselines. All-in-CPU-memory (local DDR5) placement is the primary baseline throughout; remote-CPU (cross-socket UPI) memory as a second reference in latency/throughput microbenchmarks; single-CXL-device placement as baseline for 2/3/4-device scaling. Systems used: CXL-Bench (their tool), BTreeOLC (Muhlig et al. implementation), Hyrise DBMS. No external competing systems compared.

Key findings. One CXL device sustains ~38-40 GB/s reads (vs ~260 GB/s CPU memory sequential reads) and ~13.5-18 GB/s writes. Round-robin page interleaving across devices scales bandwidth: sequential 4 KiB reads at 24 threads reach 1.9x/2.3x/2.5x with 2/3/4 devices; writes 2x/3x/3.7x. Latency: average random-read latency 520 ns on CXL (vs ~200-ish CPU; sequential reads average 65 ns CXL vs 45 ns CPU because the hardware prefetcher hides latency); 99.9th percentile random reads ~785 ns above CPU median. Scans: 100%-selectivity scan all-in-CXL is 10 GB/s on one device; writing TIDs to CPU memory instead gives 4x (40 GB/s); four devices reach 42 GB/s (4x one device) and 92 GB/s at 0.1% selectivity (2.3x). B+tree: all-CXL achieves 38%/40% (read-heavy) and 25%/39% (write-heavy) of CPU baseline with 1/4 devices - multiple devices help write-heavy (+57%) far more than read-heavy (+6%). TPC-H on Hyrise: all-in-CXL round-robin across 1-4 devices gives 71-73% of baseline, and multiple devices give <2% gain because the workload is latency-bound; a 2:1 CPU:CXL page ratio gives 87%; keeping the 16 most-accessed columns in CPU memory (over 80% of table data in CXL) gives 94% (abstract states >80% data in CXL at 85% performance). Economics: one CXL device cuts cost up to 61% for 4-5 TiB demands; 46-64% for 4-8 TiB.

Limitations. Authors admit: the FPGA-based CMA blade design "is not performance-optimized for production" (so absolute CXL bandwidth/latency understate ASIC devices); "finding an optimal placement strategy is not the focus of this work"; TPC-H has less access skew than real workloads. Evident from methodology: CXL 1.1 devices only (no CXL 2.0 features, no hardware interleaving studied); single host, single DBMS process - no multi-tenant runs, no interference or QoS study between co-running workloads on different devices, no measurement of CXL-to-CXL cross-device contention (devices are only used cooperatively via interleaving); DDR4-1866 backing memory makes per-device bandwidth low relative to the x16 link; placement strategies are static/offline (access counters collected in a prior run); no OS/kernel tiering (e.g., TPP/AutoNUMA) comparison.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on: developing lightweight online approaches for identifying hot data and optimal placement granularities (table/column/page) as future work; co-design between database systems and operating systems; software prefetching for random CXL accesses; shared CXL memory across multiple compute nodes (spilling large intermediate results) as a new use case. Untested: per-device scheduling/isolation, interference between independent tenants on separate devices, dynamic/phase-aware migration, behavior with production ASIC devices, transactional workloads.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. This is the closest published testbed to the requester's: 4 real CXL devices, each a separate NUMA node on one host, with software round-robin/weighted page interleaving across them - directly validating that 4-device bandwidth aggregation (2.5x reads, 3.7x writes) is real, but also showing latency-bound workloads (TPC-H/Hyrise) gain <2% from extra devices, a useful decision criterion for ObjPlace-style placement. Their devices are FPGA blades with DDR4 (per-device ~38 GB/s reads), so per-device numbers roughly match the requester's CZ120 regions but latency (520 ns avg random read) is worse than the requester's 277 ns ASIC measurement. Critically, they only use the 4 devices cooperatively (interleave to add bandwidth for one workload) and never study cross-device interference, per-device QoS, or multi-tenant isolation - the requester's candidate direction of treating each device as an independently schedulable resource remains unclaimed by this paper and can cite it as motivation that multi-device CXL testbeds exist but are only exploited for bandwidth pooling.

Could not verify. Pages 3131-3133 (tail of related work/references) were not rendered, but methodology, all evaluation sections, discussion, and conclusion were read in full.

Verifier note. Fetched the official PVLDB PDF (vldb.org/pvldb/vol18/p3119-weisgut.pdf) and extracted its full text with pdftotext this session. All three analyst quotes are verbatim-accurate, including Table 2 (Supermicro SYS-741GE-TNRT, 2x Intel Xeon Gold 6542Y 24-core, 8x 32 GB DDR5-4800, Ubuntu 24.04 Kernel 6.13). No second testbed and no NUMA-emulation setup found; single evaluation server attached to four Seagate CMA FPGA blade prototypes, each treated as one CXL device with 8x 128 GiB DDR4-1866, each configured as a separate NUMA node, with software round-robin page interleaving when 2+ devices are used. Note: the shared scratchpad file paper.txt was overwritten by a sibling agent mid-check (it contained a Memstrata-like paper); I re-extracted to a distinct file (weisgut.txt, md5 582ac5329feba916a19506461ba0a2d4) and verified its header matches the Weisgut paper before quoting.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • The server is attached to four Seagate Composable Memory Appliance (CMA) Blade prototypes [23, 55]. Each CMA blade is an FPGA-based memory expansion solution. The FPGA-based design is not performance-optimized for production. A CMA blade supports PCIe Gen5 x16 CXL 1.1 specification connectivity. Each blade has four DDR4 channels with two DIMMs connected per channel. We refer to a blade as a CXL device.
  • Table 2: Specifications of the evaluation server. ... CPUs: 2x Intel 5th Gen Xeon Scalable Gold 6542Y with 24 cores ... Memory: 8x 32 GB DDR5 with a speed of 4800 MT/s ... OS: Ubuntu 24.04, Kernel 6.13
  • We use CXL-Bench with a memory region of 7 GiB pinned to a set of the CXL devices' NUMA nodes. This results in a round-robin interleaving of pages with two or more devices.
  • When using multiple CXL devices, this allows interleaving pages across all devices, assuming that each device is configured as a separate NUMA node.
  • In this paper, we experimentally evaluate and analyze CXL memory performance with four x16 PCIe/CXL memory expansion devices directly attached to a CPU.
  • For sequential 4 KiB reads with 24 threads (matching the core count), the throughput achieves 1.9x, 2.3x, and 2.5x with two, three, and four devices compared to one device (with 38 GB/s). For sequential 4 KiB writes with 24 threads, the throughput achieves 2x, 3x, and 3.7x compared to one device (with 13.5 GB/s).
  • Our TPC-H evaluation shows that placing table columns based on access frequencies allows storing over 80% of the table data in CXL memory with a performance of 85% of a local-memory-only solution.
  • Placing all segments in CXL memory with a round-robin page interleaving across one to four devices yields 71% to 73% (i.e., 27.5 Q/h/c to 28 Q/h/c) of the baseline throughput. Using multiple devices does not show significant throughput increases (< 2%) over one CXL device.

https://www.vldb.org/pvldb/vol18/p3119-weisgut.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.14778/3746405.3746432

Pasha: An Efficient, Scalable Database Architecture for CXL Pods

CIDR 2025 (15th Annual Conference on Innovative Data Systems Research, Amsterdam) (2025) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (a single 128 GB CXL 1.1 memory device, "hardware prototype CXL module," vendor anonymized)

Problem. Scaling a single-node OLTP database across multiple hosts without the throughput collapse caused by multi-host transactions and two-phase commit; existing partition-based shared-nothing databases degrade sharply as multi-partition transaction fraction rises, and RDMA-based shared-memory designs pay 1-2 orders of magnitude higher memory latency plus software coherence cost.

Idea. A "partitioned + shared" database architecture for a CXL pod (a few hosts sharing a CXL memory module, of which only a small region, ~hundreds of MB, is hardware cache-coherent across hosts). Each host keeps its own partition in local DRAM; data touched by multiple hosts lives in a shared region in CXL memory, with the hardware cache-coherent region used for synchronization primitives (latches/locks) and a software coherence protocol for the rest. Tuples move dynamically between partitions and the shared region so every transaction is a single-host transaction executed with a single-node concurrency-control protocol, eliminating 2PC. The paper is a vision/architecture paper laying out 7 research challenges (data movement, software cache coherence, MVCC across tiers, partitioning, high concurrency, partial-failure tolerance, auto-scaling); precursor to the group's OSDI'25 Tigon system.

Testbed. One physical machine: two Intel Xeon 8460H CPUs, 1 TB local DRAM (DDR5 4800), and one 128 GB CXL 1.1 memory device (also DDR5 4800, single memory channel, PCIe 5.0 x8 link) — a "hardware prototype CXL module" whose manufacturer required all measurements to be reported normalized only. The multi-host CXL pod is emulated: 8 VMs (4 vCPUs, 8 GB DRAM each) on this one machine share the CXL device; inter-VM coherence is the single host's ordinary hardware coherence, so it is faster than a real cross-host CXL 3.x pod would be. No kernel version given.

Evaluation. Microbenchmark: Intel MLC latency/bandwidth of local DRAM vs CXL memory at 3:1 read:write ratio (normalized results only). Main experiment: TPC-C with only NewOrder and Payment procedures (50/50 mix; the other three procedures are unsupported by Sundial), 8 warehouses, one warehouse per emulated host (8 VMs), 2 worker threads per approach, varying the multi-warehouse transaction percentage from 0/0 to 60/90 (NewOrder/Payment). Pasha prototype built inside the Sundial distributed in-memory OLTP DBMS; no dynamic data movement — shared tables (CUSTOMER, STOCK, 58% of TPC-C data) are placed in CXL memory before the run.

Baselines. All Sundial variants: Sundial-NET (default partition-based shared-nothing over network), Sundial-CXL (partition-based but message queues in CXL memory instead of network), Sundial-SHM (fully shared-memory: all data in cache-coherent CXL memory), vs Sundial-Pasha.

Key findings. (1) Their CXL 1.1 device has ~2.3x higher latency than local DRAM and 58% of DRAM single-channel bandwidth. (2) TPC-C: with 0% multi-warehouse transactions Sundial-Pasha matches Sundial-NET/CXL and is 1.4x over Sundial-SHM; at 60/90% multi-warehouse transactions Pasha is up to 5.9x over Sundial-NET, 1.6x over Sundial-CXL, and 1.1x over Sundial-SHM (Sundial-NET needs ~3.6 network messages/transaction there). (3) Pasha only places 58% of TPC-C data in shared CXL memory vs 100% for SHM, reducing cross-host synchronization; dynamic data movement would shrink this further.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: no hardware exists for cross-host CXL 3.x cache coherence, so the pod is emulated with 8 VMs on one machine and "inter-host coherence actions in our testbed are faster than they would be on a CXL pod"; cost modeling of coherence atomics is acknowledged as a challenge; the prototype does not support dynamic data movement (shared tables are pre-placed); measurements are normalized (NDA). Methodological weaknesses: single physical host, single CXL device, only 2 of 5 TPC-C procedures, only 2 worker threads per approach, 8 warehouses, no latency/tail results (throughput only), no multi-tenant or interference experiments, vision paper with preliminary results only.

Open questions. The 7 explicitly-punted research challenges: safe data access during partition<->shared-region movement; a database-tailored software cache-coherence protocol layered on a small (~256 MB) hardware-coherent region; MVCC with versions split across DRAM and CXL; new partitioning algorithms minimizing shared-region operations; conflict-avoiding transaction scheduling at ~4,608-core concurrency; partial-failure tolerance (illegal in-memory states, allocator recovery); CXL-based auto-scaling/live migration and cost-optimal scaling across DRAM/CXL/CPU dimensions. Also untested: behavior on real multi-host CXL 3.x hardware, real back-invalidation costs, bandwidth contention on the shared device.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's single-host 4x CZ120 testbed: Pasha targets multi-host CXL pods with (hypothetical) cross-host hardware cache coherence, uses one CXL device, and never treats devices as independently schedulable resources or studies CXL-to-CXL interference. Its useful overlap is the DRAM-vs-CXL placement philosophy — hot/private data in DRAM, shared/cold data in CXL — which is conceptually adjacent to ObjPlace's object-granularity placement, and its 2.3x latency ratio corroborates the requester's 1.53x-2x range measurements. It also illustrates the field's evaluation gap the requester can exploit: even at CIDR the multi-device/multi-host story is VM-emulated on one device, so real per-device measurements on 4 physical CZ120s (cross-device interference, per-device bandwidth) would be novel. Cite as motivation/related work for CXL-aware data placement, not as a competing system.

Could not verify. Whether the prototype CXL module is ASIC or FPGA based — the paper anonymizes the manufacturer, hence realCXL=mixed (real CXL 1.1 device for memory characteristics + VM-based emulation of the multi-host pod and its cross-host coherence), Kernel version and absolute latency/bandwidth numbers (withheld at manufacturer's request)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our evaluation (§4) uses a hardware prototype CXL module. The evaluation machine includes two Intel Xeon 8460H CPUs, 1 TB local DRAM, and a 128 GB CXL 1.1 memory device. Both local DRAM and CXL memory use DDR5 4800 DRAM.
  • The CXL memory module has a single memory channel and is connected to the CPU via a PCIe 5.0 x8 link.
  • We emulate a CXL pod on the machine described earlier (§2.2) because there are no commercially available (or hardware prototype) CXL devices that support fine-grained memory sharing with hardware cache coherence. We run 8 virtual machines (VMs) on this machine, each with 4 vCPUs and 8 GB local DRAM, and configure the VMs to share the CXL memory device to emulate a CXL pod that includes 8 machines sharing cache-coherent CXL memory.
  • That means that inter-host coherence actions in our testbed are faster than they would be on a CXL pod.
  • We find that the latency of the tested CXL memory is around 2.3× higher than local DRAM and its bandwidth is 58% of the single-channel bandwidth of local DRAM. Note that at the request of the hardware manufacturer, we only provide normalized performance, not absolute measurements.
  • Sundial-Pasha has up to 5.9× and 1.6× higher throughput than Sundial-NET and Sundial-CXL, respectively

https://vldb.org/cidrdb/papers/2025/p8-huang.pdf

A Three-Tier Buffer Manager Integrating CXL Device Memory for Database Systems

HardBD & Active workshop (co-located with ICDE) (2024) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1

Problem. Three-tier buffer managers exist for DRAM/PMem/SSD (HyMem, Spitfire) but rely on invasive pointer swizzling and none integrate byte-addressable CXL device memory as a middle tier between local DRAM and SSD in a DBMS buffer pool.

Idea. A three-tier (local DRAM / CXL device memory / SSD) buffer manager that combines vmcache-style virtual-memory-based page-identifier translation (mmap + madvise(MADV_DONTNEED), mbind/move_pages across NUMA nodes) with Zhou et al.'s probabilistic page-migration policy (probabilities Lr, Lw, Cr, Cw) and variable power-of-two page sizes (4 KiB-2 MiB), integrated into the in-memory DBMS Hyrise without pointer swizzling.

Testbed. Dual-socket 4th Gen Intel Xeon (Sapphire Rapids), 48 cores/socket; 256 GiB (8x32 GiB) DDR5 on socket 0 as local memory; one Seagate Technology CXL 1.1 Type 3 device prototype attached via PCIe/CXL x8 Gen4 to node 0, FPGA-based (Bittware Xilinx VU13P) memory controller with 64 GiB (4x16 GiB) DDR4-2400; Micron Crucial CT500P1SSD8 500 GB NVMe SSD; pre-released Linux kernel 6.3.0-060300rc1-generic. MLC: local 125.5 ns idle latency / 224.3 GB/s; CXL device 557 ns / 9.4 GB/s.

Evaluation. YCSB-based microbenchmarks (Update Heavy ~ YCSB A: 50/50 lookup/update; Read Mostly ~ YCSB B: 95/5; Scan ~ YCSB E: full page scans + 5% updates) with zipfian skew, 64 B tuples accessed via AVX-512 load/store (_mm512_load_si512, _mm512_stream_si512). Scalability: fixed 2 GiB database, 1-48 threads, buffer pool entirely on local vs. entirely on CXL memory, pinned to NUMA node 0, hyper-threading and NUMA balancing off. Migration-policy study: 2 GiB local + 4 GiB CXL buffer pools, database 1-8 GiB, 48 threads, Eager (probability 1.0) vs Lazy (0.2) three-tier policies vs two-tier setups.

Baselines. Two-tier configurations of their own buffer manager: Local Memory-SSD and CXL Device Memory-SSD; eager vs lazy probabilistic migration policies. No external systems (HyMem/Spitfire/vmcache discussed as related work only, not run).

Key findings. Point-lookup-dominated workloads (Update Heavy, Read Mostly) show similar throughput on local and CXL device memory despite 557 ns vs 125.5 ns latency; Scan on local memory is up to 2x faster at low thread counts but converges to CXL throughput as threads increase; latencies ~300 ns at low threads to 28 us at 48 threads for lookups. Three-tier (2 GiB local + 4 GiB CXL = 6 GiB pool) sustains high throughput for databases up to combined capacity, while two-tier throughput collapses once the pool spills to SSD; lazy policy generally beats eager (except a tipping point at 6 GiB DB for Update Heavy). move_pages between volatile tiers is a bottleneck; mbind with MPOL_MF_MOVE doubles page-movement throughput. Scalability is limited by their implementation (false sharing on frame arrays, tbb::concurrent_queue contention).

Limitations. Authors admit: results are bound to the prototype's characteristics (9.4 GB/s, 557 ns - far below production CXL devices); "our work is limited to a single device prototype"; achieved performance is limited by their buffer manager implementation (false sharing, queue contention); optimal migration probabilities require per-hardware tuning; OS page-movement syscalls are serialized/single-threaded. Methodological weaknesses: only one CXL device, only YCSB-style microbenchmarks (no TPC-C/TPC-H or full Hyrise query workloads evaluated), no multi-tenant or interference experiments, buffer pools artificially small (2/4 GiB), workshop-length evaluation.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: improving buffer manager scalability; evaluating impact of storing data on CXL memory in a three-tier vs two-tier storage hierarchy; how different throughput/latency characteristics of (production) CXL Type 3 devices influence three-tier buffer manager design; multi-threaded page migration without kernel modifications (thread-local buffer copy scheme is proposed but not evaluated). Untested: multiple CXL devices, device-level bandwidth contention, multi-tenant isolation, real query workloads.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Marginal to the requester's multi-device direction: this is a single FPGA-prototype device (9.4 GB/s, 557 ns) treated as one NUMA node, so it says nothing about per-device scheduling or CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference on the 4x CZ120 testbed. It is useful as a related-work datapoint for object/page placement across DRAM/CXL (probabilistic migration probabilities as an alternative policy to ObjPlace's sensitivity/skew/phase signals) and for the practical finding that move_pages is a migration bottleneck while mbind(MPOL_MF_MOVE) doubles movement throughput - directly relevant to ObjPlace's migration path. Its observation that latency-insensitive point-lookup workloads run equally well on CXL echoes the requester's bandwidth-vs-latency sensitivity distinction, but its prototype bandwidth (9.4 GB/s) is far below the CZ120s' 35.5 GiB/s/region, limiting quantitative transfer. The paper explicitly names multi-device CXL characteristics as future work, supporting the novelty of a per-device treatment.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • A CXL 1.1 Type 3 device prototype provided by Seagate Technology LLC is attached via a PCIe/CXL x8 Gen4 connection to node 0.
  • The device is an FPGA-based (Bittware Xilinx VU13P) memory controller containing 64 GiB (4 x 16 GiB) DDR4 DIMMs with a speed of 2400 MT/s.
  • While our work is limited to a single device prototype, future work needs to evaluate how different throughput and latency characteristics of CXL Type 3 devices influence the quality of three-tier buffer manager designs.
  • We conduct the experiments on a dual-socket platform with 4th Generation Intel Xeon CPUs (Sapphire Rapids), each having 48 cores. The server has 256 GiB (8 x 32 GiB) DDR5 DIMMs on the first socket (NUMA node 0), which we use as local memory in the experiments.
  • For CXL device memory, we observe 557 ns and 9.4 GB/s. Note that we use a preliminary CXL device prototype.
  • The OS is a pre-released Linux kernel version 6.3.0-060300rc1-generic to support CXL memory.
  • Experiments suggest that the performance of move_pages between volatile tiers is a limitation and that using mbind with the MPOL_MF_MOVE flag doubles the page movement throughput.

https://hardbd-active.github.io/2024/papers/HardBDActive24-Riekenbrauc

An Examination of CXL Memory Use Cases for In-Memory Database Management Systems using SAP HANA

PVLDB Vol. 17 No. 12 (VLDB 2024, industrial track) (2024) mixed multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. memory-expansion testbed: 2 Samsung CMM-D 256GB ASIC devices (experiments run with 1 device and with 2 devices interleaved); failover testbed: 1 Altera Agilex AGI027 FPGA CXL board shared between 2 servers

Problem. IMDBMSs like SAP HANA face two constraints: (1) limited memory capacity per server (limited DIMM slots, overprovisioning/OOM risk) and (2) long restart/failover times because table data must be reloaded into memory. Whether commercial CXL memory can practically alleviate these in an enterprise-scale IMDBMS was unvalidated.

Idea. Two use cases integrated into a real SAP HANA prototype: (a) dynamic memory expansion — selectively place three HANA data-structure classes (main storage via fsdax path, HEX operational heap memory via mbind() to the memory-only CXL NUMA node, and temporary tables) on real Samsung CMM-D CXL 2.0 ASIC devices, analyzing performance impact by access pattern (sequential vs random); (b) failover with fast restart — an FPGA prototype emulating CXL 3.0 shared memory lets a standby server attach the CXL region holding main-storage tables and restart without reloading data.

Testbed. Expansion testbed: 1-socket Intel 5th-gen Xeon (Emerald Rapids), 56 physical cores + HT, 8x 128GB DDR5-4800 (1024 GB DRAM); CXL = engineering samples of Samsung CMM-D, each a 256GB DDR5-5200 DIMM behind an ASIC CXL-2.0-to-DDR5 controller, PCIe Gen5 x8, ~2.3x local NUMA latency, CXL type-3, exposed as memory-only NUMA node, Linux kernel 6.4.0. Single-CXL config = 1 device (256GB NUMA node); double-CXL config = 2 devices with CXL homogeneous interleaving exposed as one 512GB NUMA node 1. Failover testbed: two 2-socket Intel Xeon Platinum 8468H (Sapphire Rapids, 48 cores each, 128GB DDR5-4800 per processor) servers sharing one Altera Agilex AGI027 FPGA board (PCIe Gen5 x16, CXL 1.1 connectivity, 2x 8GB DDR4-1333 on board; ~4x local latency per their prior work), connected to server 1 via PCIe edge and server 2 via two MCIO cables.

Evaluation. SAP HANA Cloud prototype with modified memory allocator (mbind() with CXL NUMA node; fsdax path for main storage). Expansion: TPC-C 100 warehouses (OLTP, scaling client threads to 256), TPC-DS SF100 (OLAP, 1-8 parallel streams), configurations Baseline / Main FAR / Main FAR x2 / HEX FAR / HEX FAR x2 (FAR = data on CXL; x2 = two interleaved devices); CXL traffic measured with Intel PCM, stall breakdown with VTune top-down analysis. Temporary tables: SAP PaPM (real-world financial workload, up to 20 processes) and ProcBench SF100 (Temp FAR vs Baseline). Failover: two-host HA setup, TPC-H SF10 (limited by 16GB FPGA capacity), main storage moved into fsdax on CXL shared memory, restart timestamps (Restart/Ready/Completed) collected across failover/failback with consistency checks.

Baselines. All-DRAM baseline (main storage + HEX heap + temp tables in native host DRAM); comparisons among CXL placement configs (Main FAR, Main FAR x2, HEX FAR, HEX FAR x2, Temp FAR); failover baselines: no-preload restart and preload-all-without-fast-restart (reload from disk to DRAM); qualitative comparison against their earlier FPGA CXL 1.1 prototype (DaMoN'23) with ~4x latency.

Key findings. TPC-C (OLTP): zero performance degradation with main storage or HEX heap on CXL (workload is lock/synchronization bound, low CXL traffic). TPC-DS SF100 (OLAP): Main FAR 27% degradation at ~19 GB/s CXL traffic (bandwidth-limited); interleaving 2 devices (Main FAR x2) cuts it to 10% at ~26 GB/s; HEX FAR 78% degradation at ~27 GB/s (latency-bound random accesses), HEX FAR x2 still 65% at ~45 GB/s — doubling bandwidth is not enough for random-access operational memory. Temp tables: PaPM 4-13% degradation saturating at ~8 GB/s CXL traffic; ProcBench ~none (~0.11 GB/s traffic). Failover: CXL shared memory saves 85% of data-preloading time and 40% of total restart time for TPC-H SF10; estimated 95% preload / 84% restart savings at SF100. The ASIC device (2.3x DRAM latency) vs their earlier FPGA (4x): 1.6x lower latency, 2.6x total bandwidth (1.3x per device). Sequential-access data (main storage) tolerates CXL via prefetching; random-access data does not.

Limitations. Authors' own: FPGA shared-memory prototype only emulates selected CXL 3.0 functionality at CXL 1.1 speeds with 16GB capacity (forcing TPC-H SF10) and no full hardware coherency (software-managed, memory mounted on one server at a time, no simultaneous writes); TPC-C x2 runs excluded as "not bound by CXL memory bandwidth"; SF100 failover savings are estimated, not measured; gigabit Ethernet between failover servers. Methodological: single-socket single-host expansion testbed; max 2 ASIC devices, always fused via interleaving into one NUMA node — devices are never measured or managed independently, and no CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, per-device placement, multi-tenant, or tail-latency analysis; placement decided per data-structure class offline, no dynamic/automatic tiering; engineering-sample hardware; delta storage explicitly excluded from CXL placement.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on: finer-grained placement of data objects (dictionaries, value arrays, per-column) on CXL; additional CXL shared-memory use cases (result buffers, file caches across instances); performance impact of CXL switches in flexible disaggregated topologies; hardware-managed tiering (Intel Flat Memory Mode) for temperature-based automatic placement; fully hardware-coherent failover/failback without human involvement. Untested: >2 devices, per-device scheduling, interference between co-running workloads on CXL, QoS/tail latency.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant validation of the requester's bandwidth-first findings on nearly identical hardware philosophy: the paper shows OLAP degradation on one ASIC device (27%) is largely a bandwidth ceiling that 2-way device interleaving (the requester's exact interleave_ways=2 setup) cuts to 10%, while random-access (latency-bound) data barely improves (78%→65%) — a clean sequential-vs-random placement rule ObjPlace can cite and extend. Critically for the 4-device direction, this SAP/Intel/Samsung study only ever fuses its 2 devices into one interleaved NUMA node and never treats devices independently, measures cross-device interference, or runs multi-tenant/tail-latency experiments — leaving the requester's per-device scheduling and CXL-to-CXL interference angle open even in the strongest industrial CXL-DBMS paper. Its data-structure-class placement (main storage / heap / temp tables) is coarser than ObjPlace's object granularity, and its authors explicitly list finer-grained object placement and hardware-managed tiering as future work.

Could not verify. Exact BIOS/firmware and CXL driver configuration details beyond kernel 6.4.0 are not stated, Absolute (non-normalized) throughput numbers are not reported (all figures normalized)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • During our experiments, we employ engineering samples of Samsung CMM-D (CXL Memory Module-DRAM), cutting-edge commercial CXL memory devices supporting CXL 2.0 [32]. Each CXL memory device consists of a 256GB DDR5 DIMM supporting 5200 MT/s, an internal memory controller, an ASIC logic converting CXL protocols to DDR5, and a PCIe Gen5x8 interface. The overall access latency is about 2.3x of the local NUMA latency, while the FPGA implementation in our previous work [22] has about 4x latency.
  • These devices are connected as CXL type 3 devices in the test machine. Each memory device is exposed as a memory-only NUMA node by default in the Linux Kernel 6.4.0.
  • To see the performance effect on CXL bandwidth, we use two different CXL memory setups, (1) single CXL with one CXL device and (2) double CXL with two CXL devices. First, in the configurations with a single CXL memory device, we install one 256 GB CXL device. The CXL memory is exposed as a single NUMA node with 256 GB at NUMA node 1. Second, in the configurations with two CXL memory devices, we install two CXL devices (256 GB per device), and activate CXL homogeneous interleaving upon them to double up the CXL bandwidth. Then, the CXL memory is exposed as a single NUMA node with 512 GB at NUMA node 1.
  • We set up the system with Intel 5th generation Xeon® processor code-named Emerald Rapids. The system is equipped with one processor with 56 physical cores using Hyper-Threading. The processor has one 128 GB DDR5 4800 MT/s DIMM per channel, totally 1024 GB in 8 channels.
  • The CXL memory pool is realized using an Altera® Agilex™ AGI027 FPGA development board which supports PCIe Gen5 x16 CXL spec 1.1 connectivity. ... Our memory prototype implemented on the FPGA acts as a software development vehicle (SDV) for the emulating selected functionality of CXL 3.0.
  • We observe that Main FAR has 27% performance degradation with around 19 GB/s of CXL traffic due to the CXL bandwidth limitation. After doubling the CXL bandwidth by CXL interleaving, the performance degradation is reduced to 10% in Main FAR x2 with around 26 GB/s of CXL traffic
  • Unlike Main FAR, HEX FAR has 78% performance degradation with around 27 GB/s of CXL traffic. ... After doubling up the CXL bandwidth by CXL interleaving, the performance degradation in HEX FAR x2 is reduced to 65% with around 45 GB/s of CXL traffic.
  • It saves 85% of data preloading time, totally reducing 40% of the restart time, compared to the second configuration
  • The FPGA board hosts 2x 8GB on-board DDR4 1333 MHz memory, which is exposed to the servers as memory. It is connected to the first server by directly inserting it in a PCIe slot of the primary server with 16 PCIe lanes and connected to the standby server through two MCIO cables.

So Far and yet so Near - Accelerating Distributed Joins with CXL

DaMoN '24 (20th International Workshop on Data Management on New Hardware, co-located with SIGMOD) (2024) numa-emulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; a hypothetical CXL 3.0 GFAM shared-memory device is emulated via QEMU instances backed by local-NUMA / remote-NUMA / Optane PMem memory on one 4-socket host)

Problem. Distributed partitioned joins in MPP DBMSs are bottlenecked by network data-exchange cost; even RDMA has much higher latency than local DRAM and lacks hardware cache coherence, requiring software coherence protocols and extra round trips.

Idea. Use CXL 3.0 shared memory (GFAM through a CXL switch) as the data-exchange medium for engine-internal communication in a distributed DBMS: map MPI-style sender/receiver communication onto shared CXL memory via three strategies (polling on read/write pointers, condition variables, hybrid MPI/CXL pointer-passing), organize CXL memory with a header + per-operator data-access tables and spinlock/barrier synchronization via CXL-visible atomics, and adapt three distributed hash joins (Repartition, Broadcast, Shared-Hash-Table) with direct-write (CXL-D) vs local-buffer-then-copy (CXL-C) variants, integrated into the commercial Actian Vector (X100) DBMS.

Testbed. Single supervisor host: 4x Intel Xeon Platinum 8376H (56 threads each, 4 NUMA nodes), 768 GB DDR4 (24 DIMMs), 1537 GB Intel Optane PMem 200 Series (24x 128 GB NV-DIMMs), 6x Intel NVMe SSDs (2.5 TB each), Linux 5.4 / Ubuntu 20.04, KVM. Multiple QEMU instances emulate hosts; the "CXL device" is a file-backed memory region exposed to guests as a remote NUMA node, placed on same NUMA node (optimistic, <100ns), remote NUMA node (realistic, <500ns), or PMem (pessimistic, <2000ns); emulated bandwidth ~29 GB/s in all settings; ~50ns CXL protocol latency and explicit cache-line flushes additionally modeled. No physical CXL silicon or FPGA.

Baselines. Equivalent MPI-based network implementations of the same distributed hash joins in Actian Vector (Intel MPI 2019 over the hypervisor network stack, i.e., an idealized high-bandwidth low-latency network); also MPI configured with shared-memory transport (I_MPI_SHM) for the intra-host comparison. No RDMA hardware baseline — RDMA is discussed only in related work.

Key findings. (1) Polling on CXL read/write pointers gives the fastest RTT in all latency settings; CXL-based methods cut system calls by up to 50% vs MPI. (2) In synthetic 4-node joins, every CXL variant beats its MPI equivalent; MPI degrades with growing |S| while CXL runtime grows only marginally. (3) TPC-H SF500: CXL data exchange reduces runtime by up to 25% for data-transfer-bound queries. (4) CXL-D (direct writes) wins for fine-grained access (SHT-HJ) and low latency; CXL-C (local buffer + copy) hides high latency better for R-HJ/BC-HJ. (5) CXL approaches beat even intra-host shared-memory MPI, suggesting NUMA-like data-exchange performance; adding ~50ns CXL protocol latency per access changes runtime only marginally.

Limitations. Entirely emulation-based: authors state CXL 3.0 hardware is unavailable, so all "CXL" numbers come from QEMU + NUMA/PMem-backed memory with hardware cache coherence provided implicitly by the single hypervisor (premise P2 assumes coherence that CXL 2.0 hardware does not provide without software protocols). Bandwidth is fixed at ~29 GB/s in all latency settings, so latency and bandwidth are not swept independently. Authors admit future CXL 3.0 hardware may add coherency/protocol latencies and bandwidth limits not captured. The network baseline runs over the hypervisor's internal network (favorable to MPI in bandwidth but not a real RDMA/InfiniBand comparison — RDMA is never actually measured). Single emulated switch/device topology; no multi-device, no multi-tenant, no interference experiments; small synthetic join sizes (up to 100k tuples) for the microbenchmark; only 5 TPC-H queries.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL 3.0 GFAM/switched hardware (coherence traffic cost, back-invalidation, real protocol latency); inter-rack scaling where CXL must be combined with RDMA (authors explicitly note racks still need RDMA); CXL memory management/allocation below the application layer (they manage HDM at application level because drivers are immature); optimizer policies for choosing CXL-D vs CXL-C and join variant; contention/interference when many hosts hammer one shared CXL device; failure handling of shared CXL memory.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's setup: this is a multi-HOST CXL 3.0 shared-memory (GFAM) emulation study, not a study of multiple real CXL expansion devices under one host, and it contains zero real-hardware measurements — its latency points (100/500/2000 ns) bracket the requester's measured 277 ns but its flat 29 GB/s emulated bandwidth ignores exactly the per-device bandwidth saturation and co-runner interference the requester measured. Its useful takeaways are workload-side: the CXL-D vs CXL-C (direct access vs staged copy) tradeoff as a function of access granularity and latency is a placement-policy insight ObjPlace-style runtimes could adopt, and distributed/parallel hash joins are a concrete bandwidth-bound tenant workload for per-device scheduling experiments. It says nothing about treating devices as independently schedulable resources, cross-device interference, or QoS.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Emulation. We use QEMU and NUMA-based emulation of CXL shared memory due to the unavailability of CXL 3.0 hardware [8]. We emulate multiple instances on a single hypervisor.
  • Our experiments were performed on a system equipped with 4 Intel Xeon 8376H CPUs (56 threads each, across 4 NUMA nodes), 768 GB DDR4 RAM (24 DIMMs), 1537 GB Intel Optane Persistent Memory 200 Series (24 NV-DIMMs of 128 GB), and 6 Intel NVMe SSDs (2.5 TB each), running on Linux 5.4 and Ubuntu 20.04.
  • We use three hardware latencies to evaluate different latency configurations: optimistic (local NUMA), realistic (remote NUMA), and pessimistic (PMem-like) (cf. Table 2). ... In the emulated environment, the CXL device is exposed as a remote NUMA node, allowing the OS to access it as detailed in Section 2.2.
  • We integrated the distributed join algorithms into the commercial Actian Vector DBMS [10], built on the X100 [7] analytical database engine. For MPI, we use Intel MPI 2019.
  • The higher bandwidth of a CXL can decrease run time by up to 25% for queries bottlenecked by data exchange.
  • Using strace, we found CXL-based methods cut system calls by up to 50% and decreased time per call by reducing network stack calls, thus lowering communication costs.

LLM & ML systems on CXL (7 papers)

LLM/KV work treats CXL as a pooled substrate (Beluga: 32 devices, hint-free interleave; TraCT: one Niagara FPGA) or needs custom NDP silicon. No commodity direct-attach serving tier with contention management.

CXL-CCL: Inter-Node Collective GPU-Communication Using a CXL Shared Memory Pool (v1 title: CCCL: Node-Spanning GPU Collectives with CXL Memory Pooling)

ICS '26 (40th ACM International Conference on Supercomputing); arXiv:2602.22457 (v1 2026-02-25, v3 2026-05-06) (2026) real-asic multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 6 (six Micron CZ120 128GB cards behind a TITAN-II CXL switch, shared by 3 host nodes); scalability beyond 3 nodes via an analytical bandwidth-sharing emulator

Problem. Cross-node GPU collective communication (AllReduce, AllGather, Broadcast, etc.) today requires expensive RDMA NICs/switches (InfiniBand); the paper asks whether a switch-attached CXL shared-memory pool can replace RDMA for inter-node collectives, despite CXL 2.0's lack of cross-host hardware cache coherence, higher latency, and per-device bandwidth limits.

Idea. CXL-CCL, an NCCL-compatible collective library that stages data through a multi-host CXL 2.0 shared memory pool: (1) doorbell/semaphore synchronization built on explicit cache-line flush + re-read to get read-after-write visibility across hosts without hardware coherence; (2) software round-robin interleaving of data blocks across the six individual CXL devices (with exclusive per-rank device ranges for N-to-N collectives) to aggregate per-device x8 bandwidth and avoid contention; (3) parallelized CPU-GPU DMA pipelines (DAX mmap + cudaHostRegister + cudaMemcpyAsync) to overlap GPU-CXL transfers.

Testbed. Three nodes, each: Intel Xeon 6960P (72 physical cores), 256 GB DRAM, 1x NVIDIA H100 80GB on PCIe Gen5 x16; nodes attach via PCIe/CXL Gen5 x16 to an XConn TITAN-II CXL switch; behind the switch a memory box with six Micron CZ120 CXL Type-3 cards, 128 GB each (PCIe/CXL Gen5 x8), 768 GB total pool; Linux 6.2.6, CUDA 12.8. Pool exposed as devdax, sequentially stacked address space (device i at [i*128GB,(i+1)*128GB)), mmap'ed by all hosts.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks of 8 collectives (AllReduce, Broadcast, Reduce, AllGather, Gather, ReduceScatter, Scatter, AllToAll) with message sizes 1MB-4GB across the 3 real nodes; pool characterization (latency/bandwidth vs DRAM); LLM training case study: Llama-3-8B on Wikipedia with PyTorch FSDP; scalability study 3-12 nodes via an emulator that assumes concurrent requests share device bandwidth uniformly.

Baselines. NCCL 2.28.3 over 200 Gb/s InfiniBand (RDMA). No Gloo/MPI baselines.

Key findings. On real 3-node CXL-pool hardware, CXL-CCL beats NCCL-over-200Gbps-IB by 1.34x (AllGather), 1.84x (Broadcast), 1.94x (Gather); Llama-3-8B FSDP training gets 1.11x speedup while cutting interconnect hardware cost 2.75x. Characterization: CXL pool load latency 658ns vs 214ns local DRAM (3.1x); GPU-to-CXL transfer bandwidth ~20 GB/s (bounded by per-device Gen5 x8 and one GPU DMA engine per direction). Software per-device interleaving is essential: without it concurrent accesses converge on one device and serialize; emulated scaling holds to ~12 nodes before device contention degrades performance.

Limitations. Only 3 real nodes; all scaling beyond that is an analytical emulator with a uniform bandwidth-sharing assumption (no queuing/tail effects). Single baseline (NCCL/IB at 200 Gbps; no 400G NDR comparison). Small messages are software-overhead dominated. CXL 2.0 pool has no hardware cross-host coherence, so correctness rests on flush/re-read discipline; pool exposed only via devdax (no NUMA integration). Latency 3.1x DRAM. Authors note large multi-host CXL pool platforms 'remain difficult to access commercially' and that CXL-CCL is research-only, not in ByteDance production. No multi-tenant or interference experiments; one GPU per node only.

Open questions. No explicit future-work section. Untested: >3 real nodes, multiple GPUs per node, contention between collective traffic and other tenants sharing the pool, tail latency/QoS under mixed workloads, comparison against 400Gbps+ RDMA, CXL 3.x hardware coherence replacing the software doorbell scheme, and per-device scheduling policies beyond static round-robin interleaving.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant hardware precedent: same Micron CZ120 silicon as the requester's c28, but six cards behind an XConn TITAN-II switch shared by three hosts, versus the requester's four host-attached cards. Its key design lesson maps exactly onto the requester's candidate direction: hardware exposes the devices as one flat stacked devdax address space, yet performance depends on software treating each device independently — static round-robin interleaving plus exclusive per-rank device ranges to prevent concurrent requests converging on one device. That validates 'per-device placement / cross-device contention matters' but only with a static policy for collectives; dynamic, workload-aware per-device scheduling, tail-aware QoS, and multi-tenant isolation (ObjPlace's territory) are untouched. Also gives comparable CZ120 numbers on a different platform: 658ns vs 214ns DRAM (3.1x, worse than c28's 1.53x because of the switch hop) and ~20 GB/s per-device Gen5 x8 GPU transfer bandwidth.

Could not verify. Exact author list and affiliations beyond 'UC Merced, Zhejiang University, ByteDance, Xconn-tech' (abstract page summarized 'and 9 others'), Whether the six devices ever appear as separate OS-visible dax devices per host vs one aggregated region (paper describes stacked address ranges per device via DAX; exact daxctl topology not quoted), DRAM configuration details (channel count/speed) and NIC model of the InfiniBand baseline

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Three nodes, each equipped with an Intel(R) Xeon(R) 6960P CPU (72 physical cores), 256 GB of DRAM, and one NVIDIA H100 GPU with 80 GB device memory, connected through a PCIe Gen5×16 link.
  • TITAN-II CXL switch using PCIe/CXL Gen5×16 links. Behind the switch, a memory box provides a CXL shared memory pool using six Micron CZ120 CXL Type-3 memory cards, each with 128 GB capacity and a PCIe/CXL Gen5×8 interface, for a total pool size of 768 GB.
  • addresses [0, 128 GB) map to device 0, [128 GB, 256 GB) to device 1
  • CXL-CCL employs a round-robin strategy to interleave data block across all CXL devices based on the identifier data_id.
  • the CXL memory pool shows 3.1× higher latency than local DRAM
  • we develop an emulator that reproduces system performance as the number of nodes is scaled. Specifically...we assume that concurrent read or write requests...share the available bandwidth uniformly.
  • a rank cannot reliably determine when another rank's write has completed and become globally visible. Without a RAW guarantee, cross-rank synchronization is difficult.
  • All nodes run Linux 6.2.6 with CUDA 12.8.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22457 https://arxiv.org/html/2602.22457v3 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3797905.3807846

CXL-SpecKV: A Disaggregated FPGA Speculative KV-Cache for Datacenter LLM Serving

FPGA 2026 (ACM/SIGDA Int'l Symposium on FPGAs; also arXiv:2512.11920) (2026) real-fpga multi-device-pooled verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 4 (4x Intel Agilex-7 FPGAs, each with a CXL 2.0 controller / attached Type-3 expander memory)

Problem. KV-caches for autoregressive LLM decoding consume huge GPU HBM, capping batch sizes and throughput; existing offload paths (CPU DRAM, NVMe) add too much latency to be practical for datacenter serving.

Idea. Disaggregate the KV-cache onto CXL 2.0-attached FPGA memory: a CXL Memory Manager transparently offloads KV blocks to FPGA-attached memory (64-256GB/device), a lightweight speculative prefetcher predicts which KV blocks future tokens will need (95% top-4 accuracy) and prefetches them ahead of the GPU, and an FPGA Cache Engine does inline compression/decompression (~3.2x) and address translation, hiding CXL latency behind speculation.

Testbed. Claimed testbed: 8x NVIDIA A100 80GB GPUs (NVLink), 4x Intel Agilex-7 FPGAs (933K ALMs, 64GB HBM each) with CXL 2.0 controllers (64GB/s per x16 link, T_CXL<400ns), dual-socket Intel Sapphire Rapids CPUs (96 cores, 1TB DDR5), Ubuntu 22.04; GPU-FPGA link is PCIe Gen4 x16; FPGA connects to a CXL 2.0 Type-3 memory expander; Intel CXL IP v1.2 in cache mode. No kernel version given. Note: the paper never explicitly states results were measured on this hardware vs modeled.

Evaluation. LLM serving workloads across LLaMA-2/3 (7B-70B), Qwen (1.8B-72B), Mistral/Mixtral, Gemma, CodeLLaMA; workload classes: chatbot (128/256 tokens), summarization (1024-2048 in / 128-256 out), code generation, QA. Metrics: throughput, TTFT, per-token latency, P99, memory expansion, cost-performance, J/token, multi-GPU scaling.

Baselines. GPU-Only (vLLM), CPU Offload (FlexGen), NVMe Offload, Compression-Only (INT8), CXL-NoSpec (ablation without speculative prefetch)

Key findings. Up to 3.2x throughput vs GPU-only (2.4x average) via 4-8x larger batches; +8.2% per-token decode latency and +4.2% TTFT overhead (P99 +12.3% from prefetch misses); 8x KV capacity expansion (24x with compression); 95% top-4 prefetch accuracy (87.2% precision); 3.21x average compression; 2.8x memory cost reduction, 1.75x cost-performance, 1.90x better J/token; 87% parallel efficiency at 8 GPUs. Code at github.com/FastLM/CXL-SpecKV (kernel driver, C++ host stack, Verilog RTL).

Limitations. Authors: P99 latency +12.3% from prefetch misses; 12.8% over-prefetch bandwidth waste; no dedicated limitations section. Methodological weaknesses: the paper NEVER explicitly states that the evaluation ran on the described physical testbed (no statement distinguishing measured hardware results from projection/modeling); no per-CXL-device breakdown or cross-device interference analysis despite 4 devices; no multi-tenant experiments; CXL memory is FPGA-attached prototype memory, not production CXL ASIC expanders, so latency/bandwidth characteristics differ from commodity devices; the two-author paper claims an unusually large testbed (8xA100 + 4x Agilex-7), and authors (Dong Liu, Yanxuan Yu / FastLM) have a very high-volume arXiv output pattern, warranting caution about result reproducibility.

Open questions. Authors punt on: fine-tuning/training workloads, CXL 3.0 (128GB/s, memory-side caching), heterogeneous memory tiers (HBM/DDR/NVM), and security/isolation for shared CXL memory. Untested: contention between co-located tenants on the CXL pool, per-device scheduling, behavior on production CXL ASIC expanders, tail-latency QoS under interference.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low-to-moderate relevance. CXL-SpecKV's "CXL" is FPGA-attached prototype memory behind Agilex-7 CXL 2.0 controllers, pooled as one cache-coherent KV store — a very different substrate from the requester's 4x Micron CZ120 ASIC expanders, and it never studies per-device behavior, cross-device interference, or multi-tenant isolation, leaving the requester's candidate direction (each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource) untouched. It is useful mainly as related work showing (a) KV-cache-on-CXL for LLM serving is an active claim space and (b) latency-hiding via application-semantic prefetch is the standard counter to CXL's ~1.5x latency penalty, which ObjPlace's bandwidth/phase-driven placement could contrast with. Treat its headline numbers cautiously: the paper never explicitly confirms the results were measured on the described 8xA100/4xFPGA testbed, so it is a weak empirical anchor for comparisons on real CZ120 hardware.

Could not verify. Whether the evaluation numbers were actually measured on the described physical testbed vs. modeled/projected - the paper contains no explicit statement either way, Whether the 4 FPGA-attached CXL memories are truly fused into one flat address space or accessed per-device (paper says both 'per device' capacities and 'cache-coherent shared resource'; classified as pooled based on the 'CXL Memory Pool' description), Kernel version and exact CXL memory device (expander) part number/capacity actually populated, ACM DL proceedings page content (not fetched; venue confirmed via search result listing FPGA 2026 proceedings), Verbatim quotes were extracted via a fetch-and-summarize tool over the arXiv HTML; the testbed quote was returned identically on two independent fetches

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our platform consists of 8× NVIDIA A100 80GB GPUs (1.6TB/s HBM, 312 TFLOPS FP16) with NVLink, 4× Intel Agilex-7 FPGAs (933K ALMs, 64GB HBM per device, 1.6TB/s bandwidth) with CXL 2.0 controllers (64GB/s per x16 link, T_CXL<400ns latency), and dual-socket Sapphire Rapids CPUs (96 cores, 1TB DDR5) running Ubuntu 22.04.
  • The A100 GPU connects to the Agilex-7 FPGA via PCIe Gen4×16, and the FPGA connects to a CXL 2.0 Type-3 memory expander.
  • CXL Memory Pool: Large-capacity FPGA-attached memory (64-256GB per device) connected via CXL 2.0 interface provides the main KV-cache storage. This memory is organized as a cache-coherent shared resource accessible to both GPU and FPGA.
  • Our code implementation has been open-sourced at https://github.com/FastLM/CXL-SpecKV
