ITME: Inference Tiered Memory Expansion with Disaggregated CXL-Hybrid Memories

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2606.12556) (2026) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. performance testbed: 1 SK hynix CMM (CXL DRAM+NAND hybrid, 32 GB used as DRAM cache) plus 2 KIOXIA PCIe Gen5 NVMe SSDs; functional testbed: 1 Intel Agilex 7 FPGA CXL prototype (32 GB DDR4 cache) plus 2 SK hynix Platinum P51 Gen5 SSDs

Problem. Agentic and long-context LLM inference produces TB-scale context state (model weights + prefix/KV caches) that exceeds GPU HBM and host DRAM; conventional disaggregated NVMe-oF storage tiers impose block-I/O software overheads and stall inference, while pure DRAM expansion is too costly at TB scale.

Idea. ITME builds a remote "shared context tier" (T3.5) out of CXL-hybrid memory devices — NAND flash fronted by a hardware-managed CXL-DRAM cache — exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node on a memory server and reached by GPU servers as byte-addressable memory over standard RDMA. Because weights and prefix caches have deterministic access patterns, a user-level prefetch API (chm_prefetch / chm_prefetch_object / chm_prefetch_size) lets the inference framework proactively stage data from NAND into the CXL-DRAM cache, driving a pipelined multi-tier DMA path (remote CXL-hybrid -> host memory -> GPU). Integrated into vLLM v0.17.0.

Testbed. GPU/host server: dual Intel Xeon 6730, 256 GB DDR5 (128 GB/socket, tier T2), NVIDIA A100 80 GB HBM (T1); servers interconnected via dual Mellanox ConnectX-6 100 Gbps NICs (RDMA). Remote memory server: production-grade platform with one SK hynix CMM (32 GB of its capacity used as DRAM cache, exposed as CPU-less NUMA node over PCIe/CXL) + two KIOXIA PCIe Gen5 NVMe SSDs (8 PCIe lanes total, ~22 GB/s peak read; CMM host path ~18 GB/s, 4-channel DRAM / 2-channel SSD config). Separate functional prototype: Intel Agilex 7 I-Series FPGA with 32 GB DDR4 hardware-managed cache + two SK hynix Platinum P51 Gen5 NVMe SSDs. Kernel version not stated in what I read.

Evaluation. Multi-turn LLM inference in a modified vLLM v0.17.0 with Llama-3.1 8B and 70B; ShareGPT and Mooncake datasets; e.g. 128 concurrent conversations up to 5 turns with >=2000 tokens, extended 35-turn / 256-conversation benchmarks. Performance runs on the SK hynix CMM empirical platform; the FPGA prototype validates hardware control logic and the software prefetcher functionally.

Baselines. GPU-memory-only with recomputation (T1); CPU-offload baseline (128 GB host memory, T2); recompute-only; local NVMe-oF disaggregated-storage baseline (local to eliminate network overhead); ideal 80 GB GPU-memory configuration.

Key findings. Up to 35.7% throughput improvement over the CPU-offload baseline in extended multi-turn runs; 1.81x speedup over recomputation by turn 5; 1.80x throughput improvement over the NVMe-oF disaggregated-storage baseline; internal hardware prefetcher + user-level prefetch API hides NAND latency behind the CXL-DRAM cache for deterministic weight/prefix-cache streams.

Limitations. Authors admit the FPGA prototype's bandwidth collapses below 1 GB/s on cache misses (theoretical max 23.3 GB/s, still being optimized) and that ITME shows performance fluctuations from unpredictable I/O stalls under intense contention. Methodological weaknesses: single CMM device and single memory server — no multi-device or scale-out evaluation; performance results come from the CMM platform while the actual ITME hardware logic is only functionally validated on the FPGA (performance and function are validated on different platforms); no multi-tenant runs; no CXL-vs-DRAM interference study; industry preprint (SK hynix Memory Systems Research), not peer-reviewed.

Open questions. Scaling to multiple CXL-hybrid devices or multiple memory servers (pooling, cross-device scheduling) is not addressed; write-heavy KV-cache paths and cache-miss behavior at full bandwidth remain open; multi-tenant sharing of the "shared context tier" and QoS/isolation are untested; interaction of the internal hardware prefetcher with irregular (non-deterministic) access patterns is unexplored.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. ITME is single-CXL-device and remote/RDMA-disaggregated: one SK hynix CMM (DRAM-cache-over-NAND hybrid) on a memory server, not multiple locally-attached expanders like the requester's 4x CZ120. It offers no evidence on per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant isolation — the requester's candidate direction remains unclaimed by this work. Its useful overlap is the exploitation of deterministic LLM access patterns (weights, prefix caches) via an explicit application-level prefetch API, which is conceptually adjacent to ObjPlace's phase/skew-driven object placement; ITME's 35.7%/1.80x numbers also confirm that byte-addressable CXL-backed tiers beat block-I/O NVMe-oF for KV-cache overflow. Its admitted single-device, no-contention-handling gaps strengthen the motivation for treating each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource.

Could not verify. Exact total capacity of the SK hynix CMM device (only the 32 GB DRAM-cache portion is stated in text I retrieved), Kernel version of the testbed, CXL link width/generation of the CMM (paper says PCIe physical layer, CPU-less NUMA node; lane count not quoted), Whether more than one CMM could be present on the memory server — all retrieved text refers to a single CMM

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • we developed a production-grade empirical platform integrating an SK Hynix CMM (SK hynix, [2024]) and two KIOXIA PCIe Gen5 NVMe SSDs (KIOXIA, [2024]). To maintain architectural consistency with the FPGA prototype, we utilized 32 GB of the CMM capacity as the DRAM cache.
  • A functional prototype, implemented on an Intel Agilex 7 I-Series FPGA (Altera, [2026]), validates the hardware-level control logic and the software prefetcher. This platform integrates 32 GB of DDR4 DRAM as a hardware-managed cache for two SK Hynix Platinum P51 PCIe Gen5 NVMe SSDs.
  • By operating over the PCIe physical layer and exposing itself to the host operating system as a CPU-less NUMA node, the device successfully bypasses the physical pin-count limitations of traditional DDR channels.
  • The remote region functions as a massive, byte-addressable memory, providing GPU servers with a scalable extension of their physical memory via standard RDMA protocols.
  • The host features dual Intel Xeon 6730 CPUs and 256 GB of DDR5 DRAM (128 GB per socket), representing the host memory tier (T2). It is equipped with an NVIDIA A100 (80 GB HBM) GPU (T1).
  • Overall, ITME enhances conventional CPU-offloading by providing additional remote memory expansion to accommodate large KV cache footprints beyond host memory limits, achieving up to a 35.7% throughput improvement.
  • The current FPGA implementation's bandwidth degrades below 1 GB/s upon cache misses. We are actively optimizing the hardware logic to close this gap and reach the theoretical maximum of 23.3 GB/s.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12556 https://arxiv.org/html/2606.12556v2

Analysis and Optimized CXL-Attached Memory Allocation for Long-Context LLM Fine-Tuning

arXiv preprint (cs.DC), no conference venue indicated (2025) real-asic multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. Config A: 1 (CXA-8F2W 512GB AIC); Config B: 2 (CXA-4F1W 256GB AICs) — SMART Modular CMM CXL 2.0 add-in cards

Problem. Long-context LLM fine-tuning with optimizer/parameter offloading exhausts host DRAM; CXL AIC memory could extend capacity but PyTorch lacks per-tensor NUMA allocation control and naive placement of latency-critical optimizer data on higher-latency CXL memory severely degrades training throughput.

Idea. Characterize CXL AIC memory for fine-tuning offload workloads, then build (1) a PyTorch extension enabling tensor-level system-memory/NUMA placement control and (2) a CXL-aware memory allocator (latency-first greedy algorithm producing an allocation table with per-data-item placement policy and interleave ratio) that pins latency-critical tensors (optimizer states used in the CPU optimizer STEP) in local DRAM while interleaving latency-tolerant, bandwidth-consuming data across one or more CXL devices.

Testbed. 1x Intel Xeon 6780E (Sierra Forest E-core), 512 GB DDR5-6400 (4x128GB) local DRAM, 2x NVIDIA H100 80GB PCIe GPUs, PCIe 5.0 x16 for GPUs and AICs; CXL: Config A = 1x SMART Modular CXA-8F2W 512GB CXL 2.0 AIC, Config B = 2x CXA-4F1W 256GB CXL 2.0 AICs; CXL exposed as CPU-less NUMA nodes; Ubuntu 24.04 LTS, Linux kernel v6.9; PyTorch 2.5.1, DeepSpeed ZeRO-Offload, Flash-Attention, Liger-Kernel.

Evaluation. Causal-LM fine-tuning of Qwen2.5-7B and Mistral NeMo 12B with 4K-32K context lengths, batch sizes 1-48, bf16 mixed precision with fp32 master params/optimizer states offloaded to CPU memory via DeepSpeed ZeRO-Offload; activation checkpointing; single- and dual-GPU runs; measured end-to-end training throughput and per-phase (e.g., optimizer STEP) latency under DRAM-only vs naive CXL interleaving vs proposed allocator, on single-AIC and dual-AIC configs.

Baselines. DRAM-only (all offload data in local DRAM); naive CXL placement via numactl --interleave=all; comparison discussion vs OS-level tiering systems TPP and NOMAD (argued suboptimal, workload-agnostic).

Key findings. Naive interleaving across DRAM+CXL yields 76-94% (7B) and 72-93% (12B) of DRAM-only throughput; the CXL-aware allocator recovers 97-99% (7B, up to +21% over naive) and 88-96% (12B, up to +16%) with a single AIC, and 100-101% of DRAM-only with dual AICs. Naive CXL placement inflates CPU optimizer STEP latency to ~4x baseline for >20M-element tensors, motivating DRAM pinning of optimizer data. GPU<->host transfer bandwidth from CXL is comparable to DRAM over PCIe; latency differences are negligible for asynchronous transfers. PyTorch lacks tensor-level NUMA control, requiring their extension.

Limitations. Authors admit limited CXL device availability constrains testbed scope (claiming observations are "representative and transferable"); single-AIC with 12B or dual-GPU setups shows residual bandwidth contention that only dual AICs fully resolve. Methodological weaknesses: max 2 CXL devices; single workload class (offload-based fine-tuning, one framework stack); no multi-tenant or co-runner interference experiments; E-core Xeon 6780E host may not generalize to P-core/AMD platforms; allocation table appears offline/profile-driven for a predictable workload, not dynamic; no comparison against actual runs of TPP/NOMAD kernels (discussed, not measured as far as reported).

Open questions. Scaling beyond 2 AICs and cross-device interference when multiple independently managed CXL devices serve different tenants/streams; dynamic/online placement for less predictable workloads; interaction with OS tiering (TPP/NOMAD) rather than replacing it; multi-GPU larger-model training; QoS between latency-critical and bandwidth-hungry tenants sharing the same AIC. Authors explicitly leave "future exploration of advanced memory management strategies in heterogeneous systems" enabled by their PyTorch extension.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant precedent: a real CXL-2.0-AIC testbed (up to 2 devices as separate CPU-less NUMA nodes) showing that per-tensor/per-object placement with latency-critical data pinned to DRAM and bandwidth-tolerant data interleaved per-device recovers 97-101% of DRAM throughput — the same latency-vs-bandwidth-sensitivity logic as ObjPlace, applied to LLM fine-tuning offload. Its dual-AIC results (per-device interleave ratios in an allocation table) are the closest published instance of treating multiple CXL devices as separately targetable resources, but it stops at 2 devices, one cooperative workload, and no cross-device interference or multi-tenant/QoS study — leaving the requester's 4x-CZ120 per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL interference, and tail-aware isolation direction open. Also useful as a citable motivation that frameworks lack NUMA-granular allocation control, and as a contrast point: their placement is workload-specific and offline, whereas ObjPlace is a general phase/pressure-driven runtime.

Could not verify. Exact per-device NUMA node numbering and whether Config B devices were hardware-interleaved at any level (paper text as read indicates software interleaving across separate NUMA nodes), Whether TPP/NOMAD were experimentally run or only discussed, Explicit future-work section wording (summarized from fetched text)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Config. A (Single AIC): 1 × CXA-8F2W (512 GB AIC)
  • Config. B (Dual AIC): 2 × CXA-4F1W (256 GB AIC)
  • 1 × Intel(R) Xeon(R) 6780E ... 512 GB (4 × 128 GB DDR5-6400) ... 2 × NVIDIA H100 80GB PCIe ... PCIe 5.0 (x16 links for GPUs and AICs) ... Ubuntu 24.04 LTS ... Linux Kernel v6.9
  • CXL-attached memory is integrated as CPU-less Non-Uniform Memory Access (NUMA) nodes
  • interleaves latency-tolerant data across one or more CXL devices ... allocation table that maps each data item to a specific placement policy and its corresponding interleave ratio across memory tiers
  • up to 21% improvement over naive interleaving
  • establishes the foundational mechanism required for the CXL-aware memory allocator and enables future exploration of advanced memory management strategies in heterogeneous systems

https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.03305 https://arxiv.org/html/2507.03305v2

TRACE: Unlocking Effective CXL Bandwidth via Lossless Compression and Precision Scaling

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2509.03377) (2025 (v1 2025-09-03; v3 2026-01-30)) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; one hypothetical CXL Type-3 device is modeled analytically)

Problem. LLM inference with weights/KV cache offloaded to CXL memory is bottlenecked by CXL link and device-side DDR bandwidth; raw CXL bandwidth is far below what long-context decoding and large-model weight streaming need.

Idea. TRACE puts transparent lossless compression inside the CXL Type-3 device controller, behind an unmodified CXL.mem interface. It stores tensors in a channel-major, disaggregated bit-plane layout (so low-entropy exponent/sign bit-planes compress well and precision can be scaled by reading fewer bit-planes), applies a KV-specific transform before compression, and uses an inline generic codec on 4 KB blocks staged in device SRAM — amplifying effective link and DDR bandwidth without host software or protocol changes.

Testbed. No physical testbed. Analytical/first-order bandwidth model in Python of a "single-GPU + CXL Type-3 system with a 512 GB/s per-direction CXL link and 256 GB/s device DDR bandwidth" (PCIe 7.0 x16-class link; DDR5-6400 / DDR6-12800 device channels; 76 GB usable HBM modeled on GPU side). Controller logic implemented in SystemVerilog and synthesized in ASAP7 7 nm at 2 GHz / 0.7 V; DRAM energy via DRAMSim3. No CPU model, kernel, or real CXL device — none exists in the evaluation.

Evaluation. Trace-driven/analytical evaluation: real tensor data from LLaMA 3.1 8B/70B, Gemma 2 2B, Mistral 7B, OPT 13B/30B, Mixtral 8x7B, GPT-OSS-120B-MXFP4 over WikiText, BookSum (LongBench), C4, UltraChat, contexts 4K–256K tokens. Compression ratios measured on real weights/KV tensors; decoding throughput computed by first-order bandwidth accounting (per-token bytes over CXL link and device DDR channels, bottleneck taken); hardware cost/energy via 7nm synthesis and DRAMSim3.

Baselines. CXL-Plain (word-major layout, no compression) and CXL-GComp (word-major layout + inline generic lossless compression); comparisons across link/DDR bandwidth settings.

Key findings. Bit-plane layout + KV transform enables 25.2% lossless footprint reduction for BF16 weights and 46.9% for BF16 KV cache (per-layer peaks 2.69x). Modeled decode throughput up to 4.24x for GPT-OSS-120B-MXFP4 at 128k tokens (16.28 -> 68.99 tok/s). Up to 40.3% lower DRAM access energy (DRAMSim3, per-head/neuron granularity on OPT-30B), 29.9% at per-expert granularity; model load latency reduced up to 30.0%. 7 nm SystemVerilog implementation sustains 256 GB/s device bandwidth.

Limitations. Entirely simulated/modeled: no real CXL silicon, no FPGA prototype, no end-to-end system run — throughput numbers come from first-order bandwidth-ceiling accounting, ignoring latency effects, queuing, interference, and host software stack. Assumes futuristic link speeds (512 GB/s per-direction, PCIe 7.0-class) far beyond shipping CXL 2.0 devices. Single hypothetical device, single GPU, single tenant; no multi-device or contention study. Authors acknowledge combined policies with lossy techniques (quantization, KV eviction) are unevaluated future work.

Open questions. How the design behaves on real CXL hardware at realistic (tens of GB/s) bandwidth and ~250-300ns latency; interaction with OS-level page placement and multi-tenant contention; a physical FPGA/ASIC prototype behind an actual CXL.mem endpoint; combining device-internal lossless compression with lossy quantization/eviction policies (explicitly punted); variable-size compressed block management and address indirection overheads at scale.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x CZ120 testbed: TRACE is a device-architecture proposal evaluated purely in simulation at hypothetical 512 GB/s links, whereas the requester's measured per-region bandwidth is ~35.5 GiB/s on real CXL 2.0 silicon — nothing in TRACE can be reproduced or deployed on CZ120s. It is useful as related work for the "effective bandwidth is the scarce resource" motivation and as a contrast point: TRACE amplifies bandwidth inside one device, while the requester's candidate direction (per-device placement, cross-device interference, tail-aware QoS) manages bandwidth across devices in software. If pursuing the multi-device direction, cite TRACE as an orthogonal hardware-side approach that a placement runtime like ObjPlace could sit above.

Could not verify. Exact per-model per-dataset compression-ratio table entries beyond the headline numbers, Whether v3 added any hardware validation absent from the sections extracted (none was found in methodology/evaluation), Author affiliations (not extracted)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We model decoding throughput with first-order bandwidth accounting in Python. For each setting, we compute per-token traffic on the CXL link and on the device-side DDR channels, then convert each to a tok/s ceiling by dividing the corresponding bandwidth by bytes-per-token and taking the bottleneck.
  • single-GPU + CXL Type-3 system with a 512 GB/s per-direction CXL link and 256 GB/s device DDR bandwidth
  • All designs are synthesized in ASAP7 7 nm at 2 GHz and 0.7 V.
  • TRACE performs a device-internal layout transformation...On the KV write path, incoming cache-line stores are first coalesced into 4 KB blocks in the SRAM staging buffer, then transformed...and finally compressed by a generic inline codec before DRAM commit.
  • Evaluating such combined policies is a natural future direction

https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03377 https://arxiv.org/html/2509.03377v3

TraCT: Disaggregated LLM Serving with CXL Shared Memory KV Cache at Rack-Scale

arXiv (cs.DC preprint) (2025) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one SK hynix Niagara 2.0 multi-headed pooled-memory device shared between 2 servers)

Problem. In disaggregated (prefill/decode-split) LLM serving, transferring KV-cache tensors between prefill and decode nodes over RDMA/NIC (e.g., Dynamo's NIXL/UCX) is a bandwidth and latency bottleneck, and DRAM-resident prefix caches (LMCache) are capacity-limited and node-local. Additionally, CXL Type-3 devices lack cross-host atomics/coherence, so conventional synchronization cannot be extended across hosts sharing a CXL memory pool.

Idea. Place the KV cache in a rack-scale CXL shared-memory pool that multiple serving nodes attach to: prefill workers DMA KV blocks GPU-to-CXL and decode workers fetch CXL-to-GPU directly (the CXL DAX region is pinned via CUDA host-memory registration so GPU DMA engines bypass intermediate buffers and the network entirely). Software solves the missing cross-host coherence/atomics with a two-tier inter-node synchronization mechanism: per-node pthread-style local locks in DRAM ensure only one process per node contends for a global lock array in CXL memory, managed by a dedicated lock manager marking slots IDLE/WAITING/LOCKED. A shared prefix cache in CXL (LRU eviction) serves both nodes.

Testbed. 2 servers, each with an NVIDIA A6000 GPU (48 GB GDDR) and 512 GB host DRAM; baseline interconnect 100 Gbps Mellanox MT2892 NIC. CXL: one SK hynix Niagara 2.0, a second-generation CXL Type-3 multi-headed pooled-memory device (FPGA-based prototype per SK hynix public materials; the paper itself does not state FPGA/ASIC), exposed as a DAX region; measured 640 ns access latency and 10.1 GB/s bandwidth via Intel MLC; 64 GB configured as shared memory between workers. CPU models, kernel version, and CXL link width not stated in the paper.

Evaluation. Two-server disaggregated serving: Server 1 runs the benchmark client, decode, and all runtime components; Server 2 runs only prefill. Model: DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B. Workloads: (a) static workloads with fixed output length (3 tokens) and input lengths swept over 1500/3000/4500/6000 tokens to control KV transfer volume; (b) synthetic workloads: three request sets from Dynamo's built-in data generator with varying unique prefix lengths.

Baselines. (1) NVIDIA Dynamo runtime with NIXL/UCX KV transfer over 100 Gbps RDMA NIC (no prefix caching); (2) LMCache with a 48 GB DRAM-resident prefix cache; TraCT uses a 48 GB CXL-resident shared cache.

Key findings. Verbatim headline: "TraCT reduces average TTFT by up to 9.8x, lowers P99 latency by up to 6.2x, and improves peak throughput by up to 1.6x" versus the RDMA/LMCache baselines. For pure KV transfer without caching, TraCT is comparable to NIXL despite the Niagara device's modest 10.1 GB/s bandwidth, because GPU-CXL DMA avoids network stack overheads; the big wins come from the shared CXL prefix cache eliminating both re-prefill and network transfer.

Limitations. Authors admit only that the LRU eviction policy is naive ("exploring more advanced policies is left to future work"). Evident from methodology: single FPGA-prototype pooled device (640 ns / 10.1 GB/s — far slower than production ASIC expanders), only 2 servers despite the "rack-scale" title, one small 8B model on a single A6000 per node, no multi-tenant or interference experiments, CPU/kernel/CXL-spec details unreported, 64 GB of pool used. No per-device placement question arises since there is one device.

Open questions. Scaling beyond 2 hosts (Niagara 2.0 supports up to 8) untested; behavior with production-grade ASIC CXL bandwidth/latency unknown; contention on the shared global lock array and on device bandwidth when many nodes serve concurrently; smarter-than-LRU shared-cache eviction; multi-tenant isolation and QoS on the shared pool; larger models / multi-GPU nodes.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. TraCT targets multi-host sharing of one pooled CXL device (SK hynix Niagara 2.0 FPGA prototype, 640 ns / 10.1 GB/s) for KV-cache exchange, whereas the requester's testbed is single-host with 4 production Micron CZ120 ASICs (277 ns, ~35.5 GiB/s per region) — the hardware regimes and problems are nearly orthogonal. It offers no per-device placement, cross-device interference, or QoS treatment: one device, two hosts, no multi-tenant runs, so it does not preempt the candidate direction of treating each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource. It is useful as related work showing (a) KV cache is a natural CXL-resident object class for an ObjPlace-style placement runtime, and (b) GPU-CXL direct DMA via CUDA host registration works, which the requester could reuse on c28's 4060 Ti for KV-cache baselines. Its software cross-host locking is irrelevant to a single-host multi-device setup.

Could not verify. CPU model, kernel version, CXL spec/link width of the testbed (not stated in the paper), Whether Niagara 2.0 is FPGA-based is not stated in the paper itself; classified real-fpga based on SK hynix's public description of Niagara as a 4U FPGA-based multi-port CXL prototype, Whether the Niagara device is physically attached to both servers simultaneously is not explicitly stated in the evaluation section, though the shared-memory design implies it, Exact arXiv submission date (page did not show it; arXiv ID 2512.x implies December 2025)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • For the CXL experiments, we employ a Niagara 2.0 device, a second-generation CXL Type-3 memory expander that provides byte-addressable, load/store–accessible memory through the CXL.mem interface.
  • Niagara exposes its capacity to the host as a DAX memory region and functions as a high-capacity, moderate-latency memory tier. In our setup, it delivers an access latency of 640 ns and a bandwidth of 10.1 GB/s, measured using Intel's Memory Latency Checker (MLC). We configured 64 GB of space of Niagara for shared memory between workers.
  • Each server is equipped with an NVIDIA A6000 GPU (48 GB GDDR) and 512 GB of host DRAM. For the baseline (NIXL/UCX) configuration, the servers are interconnected using a 100 Gbps Mellanox MT2892 NIC.
  • Our evaluation environment is configured with 2 servers. Server 1 executes the benchmark client and all runtime components aside from prefill worker. Server 2 is provisioned to perform only prefill tasks.
  • TraCT reduces average TTFT by up to 9.8x, lowers P99 latency by up to 6.2x, and improves peak throughput by up to 1.6x
  • CXL Type-3 devices do not expose cross-node atomic operations, so traditional synchronization primitives cannot be directly extended across hosts
  • exploring more advanced policies is left to future work

https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18194 https://arxiv.org/html/2512.18194v1 https://news.skhynix.com/sk-hynix-presents-innovative-ai-and-hpc-solut

Exploring CXL-based KV Cache Storage for LLM Serving

Machine Learning for Systems Workshop at NeurIPS 2024 (2024) real-asic single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1

Problem. KV cache for long-context, high-throughput LLM serving overwhelms GPU HBM and even host CPU memory; full KV re-computation wastes GPU FLOPs and hurts TTFT SLO compliance, so a larger, cheap, low-latency storage tier for prefix KV cache is needed.

Idea. Store prefix KV cache on ASIC CXL memory expansion and load it directly to the GPU over the CXL-GPU path (cudaMemcpyAsync from buffers allocated on the CPU-less CXL NUMA node), via a save/load token-chunk hash interface integrated into gpt-fast; first platform integrating a real ASIC CXL device and a GPU in one inference server, plus a production ROI model.

Testbed. Single-socket Supermicro server: Intel Xeon Platinum CPU (exact SKU not given), 1 TB DDR5-4800 host DRAM, NVIDIA H100 GPU with 96 GB HBM, and one ASIC-based CXL memory expansion card with 256 GB DDR5-4800 (device model/vendor and link width not stated; ref [13] is the authors' ByteDance ASIC-CXL EuroSys'24 paper). CXL exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node. Kernel version not stated.

Evaluation. (1) Microbenchmark: latency/bandwidth of copying data between CXL NUMA-node host buffers and GPU buffers via cudaMemcpyAsync, access sizes 1KB-256MB, vs CPU-GPU copies. (2) TTFT on LLaMA-2-13B in modified gpt-fast (batched inference), conversation requests of 256-2048 input tokens from ShareGPT-Vicuna-Unfiltered. (3) Max batch size under a 400ms TTFT SLO. (4) Analytical ROI model (100 TFLOP/s GPU, 25 TFLOP prefill, 0.1s KV load, 90% prefix ratio).

Baselines. Full KV re-compute on GPU; prefix caching with GPU memory (PC-GPU, optimal baseline); CPU-GPU interconnect transfers (for the microbenchmark).

Key findings. CXL-GPU transfer latency/bandwidth is on par with CPU-GPU (data path bypasses host DRAM, direct CXL-to-GPU over PCIe; bandwidth saturates around 4MB transfers, latency grows past 64KB). PC-CXL TTFT 55-336ms across 256-2048 token inputs, at least as good as full re-compute; PC-GPU is 0.44-0.56ms. Under a 400ms TTFT SLO, max batch size rises from 44 (re-compute) to 57 with CXL (+30%). ROI model: 7.5x more prefill requests per GPU, up to 87% fewer GPUs vs discarding KV.

Limitations. Workshop-length preliminary study: one CXL card, one GPU, one server — no multi-device, multi-GPU, or multi-tenant experiments. TTFT/batch-size results only on LLaMA-2-13B with gpt-fast (not vLLM/production stack) and one dataset; save/load only at request end / prefill start, no eviction or cache-management policy evaluated. No comparison against prefix caching in host DRAM (only CXL vs GPU vs re-compute) despite DRAM being the obvious intermediate tier. Cost claims (87% GPU reduction, 7.5x) come from an analytical ROI model with assumed parameters (90% prefix ratio, 0.1s load), not measured end-to-end. No interference or bandwidth-contention study; device model/link width/kernel unspecified.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt to future work: integrating CXL memory with multi-GPU systems and maintaining cache coherence across GPUs. Untested: multiple CXL devices, pooled/shared CXL across servers, eviction/placement policies between DRAM and CXL tiers, contention between KV-cache traffic and other CXL users, decode-stage offloading, larger models, real production traces.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Relevant as motivating literature but orthogonal in axis: it uses a single 256GB ASIC CXL card and studies the CXL-to-GPU transfer path, whereas the requester's testbed has 4x CZ120 and cares about CPU-side per-device placement and cross-device interference. Its key measured fact — CXL-GPU cudaMemcpyAsync bypasses host DRAM and matches CPU-GPU bandwidth — is worth replicating on c28 (though c28's GPU is only a 4060 Ti; the H100 nodes lack CXL), and its finding that KV loads saturate around 4MB transfers suggests KV-cache traffic is a bandwidth-bound tenant that would trigger exactly the 2x co-runner slowdown the requester measured. The paper does not consider multiple devices, interference, QoS, or placement policy, so a per-device-scheduling ObjPlace extension with LLM KV cache as a workload would be complementary, not overlapping.

Could not verify. Exact CXL device vendor/model and PCIe/CXL link width (paper only cites ref [13], the authors' ByteDance ASIC-CXL EuroSys'24 paper), Exact Xeon Platinum SKU and kernel version, Whether exactly one card was installed is inferred from the singular 'a CXL memory expansion card'; no testbed table gives a device count

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our single socket server is equipped with Intel Xeon Platinum processors [20], 1TB of 4800 MHz DDR5 memory, an NVIDIA H100 GPU with 96GB HBM, and a CXL memory expansion card with 256 GB of DDR5 memory at 4800 MHz [13].
  • to our knowledge, we are the first to integrate a real ASIC-CXL device and a GPU within a single inference server and evaluate it for KV cache storage.
  • Since CXL memory devices are exposed to the system as NUMA nodes without CPUs by default [13], we allocate a set of host buffers on the CXL NUMA node and use cudaMemcpyAsync to copy data between the host buffers and GPU device buffers allocated via the CUDA API [26].
  • when leveraging CXL for storing KV cache, the system can handle a maximum batch size of 57, which is a 30% increase compared to KV re-compute.
  • From the estimate, we can reduce the number of GPUs needed to reach the same throughput while adhering SLO on TTFT by 87% when compared to KV re-compute.

https://mlforsystems.org/assets/papers/neurips2024/paper17.pdf

Vector search, recommendation & graph workloads (4 papers)

RecSys/vector/graph on CXL is mostly NDP proposals (ReCXL, CLAY, PIFS-Rec) or simulation (Cosmos, FaTRQ); CARINA (SIGMOD'25) is the exception to watch (access was limited - low confidence).

FaTRQ: Tiered Residual Quantization for LLM Vector Search in Far-Memory-Aware ANNS Systems

arXiv preprint (cs.LG, cs.AR, cs.IR) (2026) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 0 (one hypothetical CXL Type-2 device simulated via Ramulator extension; no physical CXL hardware)

Problem. In ANNS for RAG, the second-pass refinement stage that fetches full-precision vectors from SSD dominates query latency for modern high-dimensional text/multimodal embeddings; storing full vectors in fast memory is too costly.

Idea. FaTRQ replaces SSD full-vector refinement with a progressive distance estimator over compact ternary-encoded residuals ("tiered residual quantization") streamed from CXL far memory, with early termination once a candidate is provably outside top-k; a custom accelerator inside a CXL Type-2 device performs the refinement locally (near-memory), so only 4-byte coarse distances cross the link from the GPU.

Testbed. Host for software experiments/baselines: 40-thread Intel Xeon Gold 6230 CPU, 128 GB DRAM, 1 TB SSD, NVIDIA A10 GPU (24 GB). CXL device: NOT real — Ramulator extended to simulate far-memory access of a CXL Type-2 device (8Gb x16 DDR5-4800, timings 34-34-34; assumed CXL latency/throughput 271 ns / 22 GB/s; SSD 45 us / 1200K IOPS). Accelerator synthesized in Verilog at 1 GHz in ASAP7 (0.729 mm^2). No kernel version relevant; no physical CXL device.

Evaluation. Datasets: Wiki (88M SBERT 768-D embeddings, 251 GB) and LAION (100M CLIP embeddings, 286 GB). Two variants evaluated: FaTRQ-SW (residual codes in CXL memory, filtering on CPU) and FaTRQ-HW (filtering offloaded to simulated CXL Type-2 accelerator). GPU does coarse index traversal; refinement latency modeled with the Ramulator-based CXL simulation; accelerator area/power via ASAP7 synthesis. Metrics: throughput vs recall, distance-estimation MSE, storage efficiency.

Baselines. IVF-FAISS (CPU), CAGRA-cuVS (GPU graph ANNS); scalar-quantization residual schemes for accuracy comparison.

Key findings. FaTRQ-HW delivers 3.1x-9.4x speedup over IVF-FAISS and 2.6x-4.9x over CAGRA-cuVS; 2.4x storage efficiency improvement over prior refinement schemes; distance-estimation MSE 0.0159 vs 0.258 for scalar quantization; accelerator costs 0.729 mm^2 in ASAP7 at 1 GHz. Speedup narrows at 95% recall as coarse traversal begins to dominate.

Limitations. The CXL Type-2 device and its accelerator are entirely simulated (Ramulator extension + ASAP7 synthesis) — no real CXL silicon or FPGA prototype, so link contention, real .mem protocol behavior, and host-device coherence costs are not validated. Single hypothetical device; no multi-device, multi-tenant, or interference experiments. Fixed assumed CXL parameters (271 ns, 22 GB/s). Authors admit speedup narrows at high (95%) recall; no other explicit limitations section.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL Type-2/Type-3 hardware (real latency ~277 ns matches their assumption, but real per-device bandwidth ceilings and co-runner interference are untested); scaling across multiple CXL devices; integration with OS tiering; index update/insertion path; multi-tenant sharing of the CXL accelerator; end-to-end RAG pipeline impact.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x CZ120 Type-3 memory-expansion testbed: FaTRQ assumes a CXL Type-2 device with a custom near-memory accelerator, which the CZ120 (Type-3, no compute) cannot provide, and its CXL behavior is entirely Ramulator-simulated. Its assumed CXL parameters (271 ns, 22 GB/s) are close to the requester's measured 277 ns / ~35.5 GiB/s, so the paper is useful as a workload motivator: ANNS/RAG residual refinement is a concrete bandwidth-bound, streaming, object-granularity workload that ObjPlace could place on CXL, and FaTRQ-SW (codes in CXL, filtering on CPU) is directly reproducible on real hardware. It says nothing about multi-device scheduling, cross-device interference, or QoS, so it does not compete with the per-device-resource direction.

Could not verify. Full paper PDF not read end-to-end; details extracted via HTML fetches answered by an intermediary model (three consistent fetches)., Exact wording of Table I beyond quoted parameters., Whether the paper states any far-memory capacity assumption or device count explicitly (fetch reported it does not).

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We extend Ramulator to simulate far-memory access patterns of a CXL Type-2 device, with DRAM parameters listed in Table I
  • A custom accelerator is deployed in a CXL Type-2 device to perform low-latency refinement locally
  • Hardware overhead is assessed by synthesizing our accelerator in Verilog at 1 GHz using the ASAP7
  • 40-thread Intel Xeon Gold 6230 CPU with 128 GB DRAM and a 1 TB SSD
  • FaTRQ-HW delivers 3.1× to 9.4× speedup over IVF-FAISS and 2.6× to 4.9× over CAGRA-cuVS
  • The speedup narrows at 95% recall, as deeper traversal and coarse filtering required for high accuracy begin to dominate runtime

https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09985 https://arxiv.org/html/2601.09985v1

CARINA: An Efficient CXL-Oriented Embedding Serving System for Recommendation Models

SIGMOD 2025 (PACMMOD Vol. 3, No. 3, paper pp. 1-29, DOI 10.1145/3725274) (2025) unknown unknown verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. Embedding-based recommendation models (ERMs) need huge memory for embedding tables and generate massive embedding-read traffic; running DRAM-designed ERM serving systems directly on CXL memory performs poorly because CXL bandwidth is much lower than DRAM and easily saturated, and NUMA effects in modern CXL servers make it worse.

Idea. Carina, an ERM serving system co-designed for DRAM+CXL heterogeneous memory: (1) balance access across memory devices by keeping hot (high-frequency) embeddings in DRAM and explicitly placing embedding tables across NUMA nodes; (2) bandwidth-aware task execution that decomposes each request batch into fine-grained tasks and schedules them to cap real-time CXL bandwidth utilization and avoid instantaneous saturation.

Testbed. Could not verify. Abstract states evaluation "under real CXL devices" on servers with NUMA architecture, but the paper is closed-access (no arXiv/author PDF); CPU model, DRAM config, CXL device vendor/model/capacity/link width, and kernel version could not be read.

Evaluation. Could not verify beyond the abstract: evaluation of ERM serving throughput and request latency on real CXL devices against a CXL-oblivious baseline. Specific workloads/datasets (e.g., which recommendation models or traces) not readable.

Baselines. A "CXL-oblivious baseline" (a DRAM-based ERM serving system run unmodified on CXL) per the abstract; full baseline list not verifiable.

Key findings. Carina outperforms a CXL-oblivious baseline by an average of 5.38x in system throughput and 4.04x in request latency on real CXL devices. Core diagnosis: CXL bandwidth (not latency) is the bottleneck for ERM serving and can be instantaneously saturated; hot/cold DRAM/CXL placement plus rate-controlled fine-grained task scheduling recovers the loss.

Limitations. Full text unreadable this session, so authors' own admissions unknown. From what is verifiable: the headline comparison is against a CXL-oblivious baseline (naively running a DRAM system on CXL), which inflates speedups relative to stronger tiering baselines; number of CXL devices, device type, and whether multi-tenant or tail-latency scenarios were tested are all unverifiable.

Open questions. Whether Carina manages multiple CXL devices independently (per-device placement/scheduling) or treats CXL as one flat pool; behavior under multi-tenant contention and CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference; generality beyond embedding serving. These cannot be confirmed without the full text.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly adjacent to ObjPlace: Carina's diagnosis (CXL bandwidth easily saturated instantaneously, so place hot objects in DRAM and rate-control CXL access) matches your measured 35.5 GiB/s per-region saturation at 4 threads and 2x co-runner slowdowns, and its hot-embedding DRAM placement plus NUMA-node table placement overlaps ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement — it must be cited and differentiated (Carina is application-specific to embedding serving; ObjPlace is a general object-granularity runtime). The critical unknown is whether Carina's 'placement on the NUMA nodes' means per-CXL-device independent placement; if so it partially preempts the per-device-scheduling direction, so obtaining the full text (e.g., via NCSU library ACM access) before positioning the 4-device work is important. Its bandwidth-aware task scheduling to avoid instantaneous saturation is also a candidate baseline/mechanism for your CXL-to-CXL interference and QoS experiments.

Could not verify. realCXL device type (ASIC vs FPGA) — abstract says 'real CXL devices' (plural) but vendor/model never readable; full text is closed-access with no arXiv preprint, no author-hosted PDF, no OA repository copy (OpenAlex: oa_status=closed), and dl.acm.org (abstract page and PDF) is Cloudflare-blocked from this session, exact number of physical CXL devices in the testbed, device treatment (pooled/interleaved vs independent NUMA nodes) — abstract's 'specifying the placement of embedding tables on the NUMA nodes' hints at multiple NUMA targets but does not establish per-CXL-device management, testbed CPU/DRAM/kernel details, workloads/datasets and full baseline list, authors' stated limitations and future work

Verifier note. Confidence: low on the three testbed fields (realCXL, numCXLDevices, deviceTreatment) — they remain couldNotVerify. Bibliographic facts (title, venue, authors, DOI, pub date 2025-06-17) are solid across Semantic Scholar, Unpaywall, DBLP id journals/pacmmod/YinZYWLLLFZYC25, and author pages. Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We evaluate Carina under real CXL devices and find that it outperforms a CXL-oblivious baseline by an average of 5.38x and 4.04x in system throughput and request latency, respectively.
  • directly running DRAM-based ERM serving systems on CXL yields poor performance because the bandwidth of CXL is notably lower than DRAM and can be easily saturated, making CXL memory the bottleneck. The non-uniform memory access (NUMA) architecture in modern CXL servers further decreased the system performance.
  • Carina balances the memory access from different memory devices by storing hot embeddings with high access frequencies on DRAM and specifying the placement of embedding tables on the NUMA nodes. Moreover, Carina adopts bandwidth-aware task execution, which decomposes each batch of ERM requests into fine-grained tasks and schedules the tasks to control the real-time utilization of CXL bandwidth to avoid instantaneous saturation.

Cosmos: A CXL-Based Full In-Memory System for Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search

IEEE Computer Architecture Letters (CAL) (2025) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 4 (simulated devices, 256GB each, 1TB total; no physical CXL hardware)

Problem. RAG demands high-throughput, low-latency ANNS over billion-scale vector databases; conventional DRAM/SSD tiers hit capacity/latency limits, and specialized accelerators or RDMA clusters lack flexibility or add network overhead. Plain CXL memory expansion suffers from host-side compute bottlenecks and PCIe traffic when the host must traverse graphs and compute distances over data in CXL memory.

Idea. Cosmos is a near-data-processing CXL memory device architecture: programmable general-purpose cores (GPCs) embedded in CXL memory controllers execute the entire ANNS pipeline (graph traversal, distance computation, candidate updates) locally, eliminating host intervention and PCIe traffic. It adds (1) rank-level parallel distance computation that partitions vector dimensions across DRAM ranks to exploit internal DRAM parallelism/bandwidth, and (2) an adjacency-aware cluster placement algorithm that assigns graph clusters to devices by proximity, balancing load and enabling parallel search across the four devices; the host dispatches queries via a CXL switch to relevant devices, which return local top-k results.

Testbed. No physical testbed — evaluation via a custom simulator integrated with Ramulator. Modeled system: 1TB CXL memory from four CXL devices, each with four DDR5-4800 channels and two ranks of 16Gb x4 DRAM chips per channel (256GB per device). Host CPU model, kernel, and CXL link width not specified.

Evaluation. Trace-driven simulation: node-visit traces extracted from 10,000 queries each on SIFT1B and DEEP1B (BigANN benchmark) injected into the Ramulator-integrated simulator, modeling graph traversal, distance calculation, and candidate-update operations on the modeled GPC-equipped CXL devices.

Baselines. Base (host-side computation over plain CXL memory expansion); DRAM-only (unlimited DRAM capacity); CXL-ANNS (prior state-of-the-art CXL-based ANNS system); round-robin (RR) cluster placement as the placement baseline.

Key findings. Cosmos achieves up to 6.72x higher throughput than the Base CXL system on SIFT1B (5.35x on DEEP1B) and 2.35x over CXL-ANNS. Adjacency-aware cluster placement yields consistently lower load-imbalance ratio (LIR) than round-robin placement across devices. Rank-level parallel distance computation exploits DRAM internal parallelism otherwise wasted behind the narrow CXL link.

Limitations. Entirely simulation-based (Ramulator); no real CXL silicon, FPGA prototype, or measured link latencies — the GPC-in-CXL-controller hardware does not exist in any shipping device. Only one application (graph-based ANNS) and two datasets; no multi-tenant or interference experiments; no energy/area cost analysis of the GPCs visible; host CPU and CXL link modeling details are thin (short 4-page CAL letter format). Authors state no explicit limitations section.

Open questions. Feasibility/cost of embedding programmable cores in production CXL memory controllers; behavior under real CXL link latencies and contention; sharing GPC-equipped devices among multiple tenants/workloads; handling index updates/inserts; generalization beyond ANNS; validation on real hardware — all unaddressed.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance for systems-software work on the requester's real 4x CZ120 testbed: Cosmos assumes hypothetical compute-in-CXL hardware and evaluates purely in Ramulator simulation, so none of its mechanisms are runnable on commodity CZ120 expanders. However, it is a useful conceptual citation for the candidate direction of treating each CXL device independently — its adjacency-aware cluster placement explicitly distributes data across four separate CXL devices for load balancing, directly analogous to per-device placement/load-balancing of ANNS or KV data across the requester's four devices. It also motivates that cross-device load imbalance matters (their LIR metric), which the requester could measure with real CXL-to-CXL interference numbers that this paper cannot provide.

Could not verify. Exact CXL link latency parameters and host-side simulator configuration (not surfaced in fetched HTML summary), Whether the CAL camera-ready differs from arXiv v1

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • To evaluate the performance of Cosmos quantitatively, we developed a simulator integrated with Ramulator.
  • Our setup models a 1TB CXL memory comprising four CXL devices, each with four DDR5-4800 channels and two ranks of 16Gb ×4 DRAM chips per channel (256GB per device).
  • Cosmos integrates programmable general-purpose cores (GPCs) in CXL memory controllers for local ANNS execution, eliminating host intervention and PCIe traffic during search.
  • We propose adjacency-aware cluster placement algorithm that assigns clusters based on proximity, balancing load and enabling parallel search.
  • The host dispatches queries via the switch to relevant CXL devices. Each device performs local ANNS using its GPC...and returns local top-k results.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.16096 https://arxiv.org/html/2505.16096v1

CXL-ANNS: Software-Hardware Collaborative Memory Disaggregation and Computation for Billion-Scale Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search

USENIX ATC (2023) mixed simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. FPGA prototype (functional validation only): 4 ANNS EPs behind 1 CXL switch; gem5 simulation (all reported performance numbers): 4 EPs default, 8 for Meta-S, more in 4B-vector and multi-host scalability tests

Problem. Billion-point graph-based ANNS needs tens of TB of working memory (e.g., Microsoft 40TB+, Alibaba TB-scale). Existing fixes either compress vectors (product quantization — fails to reach recall@k 0.9 on many workloads) or tier to SSD/PMEM (DiskANN/HM-ANN — 35.9x/77.6x lower QPS than a DRAM-only oracle). Naively placing all data in a CXL memory pool preserves accuracy and capacity but is 3.9x slower than the oracle because every access pays CXL flit-conversion/far-memory latency.

Idea. Software-hardware collaborative design over a switched CXL memory pool: (1) relationship-aware graph caching — nodes within the innermost edge hops from the entry node are kept in local DRAM, the rest in the CXL pool; (2) ANNS-behavior-aware prefetching of likely-next nodes (82.3% of next-iteration visits come from the candidate array); (3) collaborative kNN search — each CXL endpoint (EP) contains a domain-specific accelerator (DSA) that computes distances near-memory while the host RC does graph traverse and candidate update, with hardware-config-aware sharding of the embedding table across EPs; (4) dependency relaxation splitting candidate update into urgent/deferrable subtasks for fine-granular scheduling.

Testbed. Two-part methodology. Real FPGA prototype (functional validation): 16nm FPGA implementing a custom CXL CPU adapted from a RISC-V core, 4 ANNS EPs each with four memory controllers (each a DIMM channel with 256GB DRAM), connected via a CXL switch; Linux 5.15.36 kernel driver; FAISS v1.7.2. Evaluation testbed (gem5 full-system simulation, cycle-cross-validated against prototype, Table 1): CXL CPU = 40 O3 cores, ARM v8, 3.6GHz, 64KiB/2MiB L1/L2 per core; local memory 128GiB DDR4-3200; CXL memory pool = 1 CXL switch, 256GiB/device DDR4-3200; storage 4x Intel Optane 900P 480GB (for DiskANN/HM-ANN baselines); CXL-ANNS DSA = 1GHz, 10 ANNS PE/device, 2 distance-calc units/PE. No production CXL ASIC anywhere in the paper.

Evaluation. Simulation-based studies on six 1-billion-vector datasets from the BigANN benchmark: BigANN (128d), Yandex-T (200d, angular), Yandex-D (96d), Meta-S (256d, 8 devices), MS-T (100d), MS-S (100d). Target accuracy recall@k = 0.9 per BigANN guidance; k = 1/5/10 (k=10 primary). Metrics: QPS, query latency with breakdown (graph traverse / distance calc / candidate update / interface), resource utilization. Scalability tests: synthetic 4B-vector Yandex-D (3B noise-augmented vectors) with more EPs; multi-host test splitting EP HDM/PEs across up to 6 CXL hosts, with and without doubling EP count.

Baselines. Comp = product quantization (compression approach); Hr-D = DiskANN and Hr-H = HM-ANN (hierarchical approaches, both on Intel Optane); Orcl = oracle DRAM-only system with unlimited local DRAM; internal ablation variants: Base (all data in CXL pool, CPU does everything), EPAx (+EP-side distance acceleration), Cache (+relationship-aware caching/prefetch), CXLA (full system +fine-granular scheduling).

Key findings. CXL-ANNS achieves 111.1x higher QPS and 93.3% lower query latency than the tested state-of-the-art billion-scale ANNS methods, and beats even the DRAM-only oracle by 68.0% (latency) and 3.8x (throughput). Pure CXL pooling without the co-design (Base) is 3.9x slower than oracle (graph traverse 2.6x, distance calc 4.3x longer) but already 9.4x/20.3x higher QPS than DiskANN/HM-ANN. EP-side distance acceleration (EPAx) cuts distance-calculation time 119.4x and query latency 7.5x vs Base, with interface overhead only 5.4% of Base's distance-calc latency. Comp fails to reach recall@k 0.9 on 7 workload configs. At 4B vectors CXLA stays 2.7x faster than oracle; multi-host QPS scales to 4 hosts then drops at 6 hosts due to PE shortage (fixed by doubling EPs). Doorbell/notification overhead grows with EP count.

Limitations. All reported performance numbers come from gem5 simulation, not the real prototype — the authors admit "the prototype system does not offer the flexibility needed to explore various ANNS design spaces" and that there was a "lack of a publicly available, fully functional CXL system". No production CXL silicon; CXL latency characteristics are those of their own FPGA/RISC-V prototype. The design requires custom hardware (DSA inside EPs, a custom CXL CPU, CXL switch) that does not exist in commodity CXL 1.1/2.0 memory expanders, so results are not reproducible on passive devices like CZ120. Single-workload domain (graph ANNS only), single-tenant: no interference, QoS, or co-runner experiments; the multi-host test statically partitions EP resources rather than studying contention. Static/read-only datasets — no graph updates/insertions. Industry-supported work (Panmnesia), with GPU baselines discussed only qualitatively.

Open questions. How the design performs on real CXL ASIC expanders with production latency/bandwidth profiles and without in-EP compute; dynamic index updates and mutable datasets; contention and fairness when the CXL pool is shared by uncoordinated tenants (their multi-host scheme assumes cooperative static partitioning); quantitative comparison against GPU-accelerated ANNS; energy and cost analysis of DSA-equipped EPs; behavior under bandwidth saturation of individual EPs (per-device interference is never measured).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Limited direct transferability: CXL-ANNS's evaluation is gem5 simulation of custom DSA-equipped EPs behind a CXL switch, not passive expanders like the requester's 4x CZ120, and it never runs on production silicon. Still, it is one of the few papers that treats multiple CXL devices as separately commanded, separately sharded resources (per-EP doorbells, hardware-config-aware sharding, per-device PE counts), and it shows per-device interface/notification overhead growing with device count — a data point for the requester's per-device-scheduling direction. Its hop-count-based hot/cold placement (inner-hop graph nodes in local DRAM, rest in CXL) is a workload-specific instance of the sensitivity-driven placement ObjPlace already generalizes, so it serves as related work to position against rather than a method to reuse. It contains no interference, QoS, or multi-tenant measurements, leaving the requester's CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference angle untouched.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Given the lack of a publicly available, fully functional CXL system, we constructed and validated the CXL-ANNS software and hardware in an operational real system (Figure 21). This hardware prototype is based on a 16nm FPGA. To develop our CXL CPU prototype, we adapted the RISC-V CPU [55]. The prototype integrates 4 ANNS EPs, each equipped with four memory controllers, linked to the CXL CPU via a CXL switch. The system's software for the prototype, including the kernel driver, is compatible with Linux 5.15.36.
  • Unfortunately, the prototype system does not offer the flexibility needed to explore various ANNS design spaces. As a remedy, we also established a hardware-validated full-system simulator [29] that represents CXL-ANNS, which was utilized for evaluation. This model replicates all operational cycles extracted from the hardware prototype and is crossvalidated with our real system at the cycle level. We conducted simulation-based studies in this evaluation, the system details of which are outlined in Table 1.
  • Although our system by default uses 4 EPs, our system increases their count for specific workloads (e.g., Meta-S) that necessitate larger memory spaces (more than 2TB) compared to others.
  • Table 1 (Simulation setup): '40 O3 cores, ARM v8, 3.6GHz / L1/L2 $: 64KiB/2MiB per core' ... 'Local memory 128GiB, DDR4-3200' ... 'CXL memory pool: 1 CXL switch, 256GiB/device, DDR4-3200' ... 'Storage: 4x Intel Optane 900P 480 GB' ... 'CXL-ANNS: 1 GHz, 10 ANNS PE/device, 2 distance calc. unit/PE'
  • in our prototype, an EP has four memory controllers, each having a DIMM channel that has 256GB DRAM modules
  • Our empirical evaluation results show that CXL-ANNS exhibits 111.1x higher QPS with 93.3% lower query latency than state-of-the-art ANNS platforms that we tested. CXL-ANNS also outperforms an oracle ANNS system that has DRAM-only (with unlimited storage capacity) by 68.0% and 3.8x, in terms of latency and throughput, respectively.
  • it exhibits 3.9x slower search latency than the oracle, on average

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/atc23-jang.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/atc23/presentation/jang

Pooling, disaggregation & memory sharing (17 papers)

Pooling/disaggregation is the hyperscaler lane (OSDI'26 is dense with it) and requires switches/multi-host gear you don't have. Cite as the complementary topology; don't compete.

A Programming Model for Disaggregated Memory over CXL (CXL0)

ASPLOS 2026 (also arXiv:2407.16300, v3 2026-01-24; DOI 10.1145/3779212.3790121) (2026) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one FPGA configured as a CXL 1.1 Type 2 device attached to one x86 host, plus a Teledyne LeCroy T516 protocol analyzer on the link)

Problem. CXL promises cache-coherent shared/disaggregated memory pools, but there is no formal programming model for concurrent programs over CXL, so one cannot reason about correctness, data management, or crash consistency (including partial crashes where one machine fails while the pool survives) of systems built on it.

Idea. CXL0, the first formal programming model for concurrent programs over CXL: an abstract machine of compute/memory nodes each with cache+memory, a small set of memory-access primitives (Read, LStore/RStore/MStore, LFlush/RFlush, RMWs) with formal operational semantics covering partial-system crashes; a mapping (via protocol-analyzer observation) from real CXL.mem/CXL.cache transactions to CXL0 primitives; and a general FliT-based transformation that makes any linearizable concurrent object durably linearizable under CXL0 in the distributed partial-crash setting.

Testbed. One x86 CPU host (model not stated) + one FPGA device using "a proprietary CXL IP by Intel" configured as a CXL 1.1 Type 2 device, with custom logic between FPGA memory and the CXL IP; a Teledyne LeCroy T516 Protocol Analyzer (PCIe 5.0/CXL) on the link. CPU accesses FPGA memory (HDM) via CXL.mem; FPGA accesses host memory via CXL.cache; both coherent. CPU model, DRAM config, link width, FPGA board model, and kernel version are not stated in the text. Latency measured with LATTester (CPU side) and custom AXI-cycle counting (FPGA side).

Evaluation. Two-part evaluation: (1) use the protocol analyzer to observe which concrete CXL.mem/CXL.cache transactions are generated for every CXL0 primitive under all MESI state pairs (Table 1), showing a many-to-one mapping and that some primitives (host RStore, LFlush from either side) cannot be generated at all; (2) microbenchmark latency of individual CXL0 primitives in isolation for five access types (host->HM, host->HDM, device->HM, device->HDM host-bias, device->HDM device-bias), median of 1000 sequential accesses, invalid initial cache state for loads, full-cache-line stores. No application workloads, no end-to-end system, no throughput/bandwidth or multi-tenant experiments.

Baselines. No system baselines; results are cross-checked against prior latency characterizations ("consistent with previous findings [25, 51]"). The durability transformation adapts FliT [Wei et al.] and is compared conceptually (not empirically) to Izraelevitz et al., NVTraverse, Mirror, Montage.

Key findings. Local loads/stores are ~2x faster than remote for Read and MStore: 2.34x faster on the CPU, 1.94x on the FPGA device. Host and device accesses to their respective remote memory show the same latency despite different sub-protocols. For device writes to host-attached memory, MStore is 1.45x slower than RStore, which is 2.08x slower than LStore. RFlush is the only flush both sides can invoke; its latency is nearly identical to MStore. Current hardware/spec cannot generate host-side RStore or LFlush from either side, restricting programmer control, so the authors advocate extending the CXL spec with precise ISA-level primitives. Theoretically, the adapted FliT transformation gives durable linearizability under CXL0 with address-based automatic instrumentation.

Limitations. Authors admit: "given that CXL 3.0 hardware is not currently available, we cannot test our model against actual implementations. Thus, our evaluation is confined to version 1.1 of the CXL standard"; also no CPU/pool device implements CXL 3.0 back-invalidation, so cache-coherent shared pools are only modeled, not measured; CXL0 does not apply to today's non-coherent pools; the durability transformation still needs manual flush tuning; they omit modeling hardware write-back-responsibility policies. Evident from methodology: single FPGA prototype device, no production CXL ASIC, no memory-expander (Type 3) latency/bandwidth characterization, only isolated-primitive latency microbenchmarks (no applications, bandwidth, contention, or multi-device experiments), hardware model details (CPU, FPGA board, kernel) unreported, transformation never implemented/measured on hardware.

Open questions. How to compensate in software for missing hardware coherence in realistic (non-coherent or partially coherent) CXL 3.0 memory pools — explicitly called "an interesting open research question"; whether/when the CXL spec will expose the missing primitives (RStore from host, LFlush) as ISA-level controls; performance of the FliT-based durability transformation on real disaggregated hardware; behavior under real multi-host, multi-device pool configurations, which are only modeled abstractly.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x CZ120 Type 3 memory-expansion testbed: this paper's hardware is a single CXL 1.1 Type 2 FPGA (CXL.cache + CXL.mem coherent sharing), and its contribution is a formal semantics/durability model, not placement, bandwidth, interference, or QoS. It contains no multi-device, multi-tenant, or bandwidth-contention experiments, so it neither supports nor competes with per-device scheduling or CXL-to-CXL interference work. Useful mainly as related-work framing: its "partitioned disaggregated memory pool" configuration formally models exactly the requester's setting (N disjoint memory nodes per host), and its finding that primitive choice changes latency ~2x echoes the requester's 1.53x load-to-use measurement. Cite it if the paper discusses crash consistency or future coherent pools; otherwise it is orthogonal to ObjPlace-style placement/isolation.

Could not verify. Exact CPU model, FPGA board model, DRAM configuration, CXL link width, and kernel version are not stated anywhere in the paper text I read (arXiv v3); the final ASPLOS'26 camera-ready (DOI 10.1145/3779212.3790121) was not fetched from dl.acm.org and could conceivably add testbed details, though the v3 (2026-01-24) text is presumably near-final.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our setup consists of an x86 CPU (host) and an FPGA (device), both running CXL 1.1, with a protocol analyzer connected between them. The CPU accesses FPGA memory via CXL.mem, and the FPGA accesses CPU memory via CXL.cache.
  • On the FPGA side, we use a proprietary CXL IP by Intel to configure it as a CXL Type 2 device.
  • The analyzer is a Teledyne LeCroy's T516 Protocol Analyzer for PCIe 5.0 and CXL, which supports all CXL sub-protocols and device types.
  • We observe that loads and stores to local memory are twice as fast as those to remote memory for Read and MStore primitives. For the CPU, it is 2.34x faster, and for the device, it is 1.94x faster
  • When a device writes to Host-attached Memory (green bars), MStore is 1.45x slower than RStore, which is, in turn, 2.08x slower than LStore.
  • However, given that CXL 3.0 hardware is not currently available, we cannot test our model against actual implementations. Thus, our evaluation is confined to version 1.1 of the CXL standard (see §5).

https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.16300 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.16300v3 https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3779212.3790121

Aquifer: Hierarchical Memory Pooling with CXL and RDMA for MicroVM Snapshots

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2606.24079) (2026) numa-emulated n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL device; CXL pool emulated via the remote NUMA node of a dual-socket CloudLab r6525; RDMA tier uses a real ConnectX-6 Dx NIC)

Problem. Memory stranding wastes 25-35% of installed DRAM in production cloud clusters; MicroVM snapshot restore latency dominates serverless cold starts, and neither CXL (pod-limited) nor RDMA (high-latency, per-access software overhead) alone is an adequate pooling substrate.

Idea. Serve MicroVM snapshots from a two-tier hierarchical memory pool: characterization shows most snapshot pages are zero or cold, so a hotness-based snapshot format drops zero pages, places the hot working set in a pod-local CXL multi-headed-device (MHD) pool and cold pages in a cluster-wide RDMA pool. An ownership-based coherence protocol built on cross-host atomics (plus clflushopt on every "borrow") gives correctness on CXL 2.0 MHDs that lack hardware inter-host coherence. Restore pre-installs hot pages from CXL via uffd.copy/zeropage before resume and demand-pages cold pages asynchronously over RDMA; a pre-provisioned MicroVM skeleton pool removes control-plane latency.

Testbed. CloudLab Clemson r6525 node (single dual-socket machine): 2x AMD EPYC Milan 7543 @2.8 GHz, 32 cores; DRAM 2x 8x 16 GiB ECC DDR4-3200; 480 GiB Samsung PM883 SATA + 1.6 TiB Samsung PM1733 NVMe; control NIC 25 Gb/s ConnectX-5 MT27800; experiment NIC 100 Gb/s Mellanox ConnectX-6 Dx MT28924 (SR-IOV VFs via eSwitch, DOCA driver 2.9.3, FW 22.47.1088). No CXL hardware: the remote NUMA node's DRAM emulates the shared CXL MHD pool; two VMs (orchestrator + pool master, 16 pcores / 64 GiB each, pinned to one NUMA node) both map the region via virtio-pmem/DAX and use clflushopt to mimic non-coherence. Ubuntu 24.04, stock Linux 6.8.0, Firecracker v1.15 unmodified; Aquifer orchestrator + pool master in 5,953 LoC Rust.

Evaluation. 9 serverless function workloads from FunctionBench and SeBS (chameleon, compression, json, ffmpeg, image, matmul, pagerank, pyaes, recognition) restored as Firecracker MicroVMs via userfaultfd; ablation of invocation-time stages on chameleon (32 concurrent restores); scalability sweep restoring n = 1..32 concurrent MicroVMs (recognition only to 16); end-to-end invocation time median+IQR.

Baselines. All adapted to run over the same RDMA pool: (1) Firecracker demand paging (every fault -> RDMA read + uffd.copy); (2) REAP (record-and-prefetch, adapted to RDMA); (3) FaaSnap (prefetch non-zero working set, adapted to RDMA); (4) FcTiered (Firecracker + Aquifer's hotness format and two-tier serving, no prefetch).

Key findings. Geometric-mean end-to-end invocation speedup of 2.2x over Firecracker, 1.3x over FaaSnap, 1.1x over REAP across 9 workloads; per-workload speedups over FaaSnap 1.00x (pyaes) to 1.57x (json), over REAP up to 1.35x (image). Ablation (chameleon, 32 concurrent): 2.12x lower total invocation time than Firecracker, 1.19x than FaaSnap; setup 1.45x/1.67x faster than FaaSnap/REAP; demand-paging baselines pay 187x higher page-installation cost. Fastest in 8 of 9 workloads; loses to REAP only on ffmpeg (many zero pages in working set that Aquifer skips prefetching). CXL-based page pre-installation is currently serialized and slower than parallel RDMA prefetch.

Limitations. Authors' own (Sec 3.6): coherence protocol assumes cross-host CXL atomics, which prior empirical work supports but the CXL 2.0 spec does not formally guarantee; assumes CXL pool capacity always fits all hot data (eviction only sketched); offline-profiled hot sets can go stale under input shift; single pool master is a single point of failure (Raft failover only proposed); snapshot deduplication not implemented. Methodology weaknesses: entirely NUMA-emulated CXL on ONE physical machine — two VMs on one dual-socket box stand in for two hosts sharing an MHD, so real MHD latency, coherence, and link-bandwidth behavior are untested; no real CXL device of any kind; remote-NUMA "latency characteristic" emulation, no bandwidth throttling described; RDMA "cluster" is SR-IOV VFs looped through one NIC's eSwitch; concurrency capped at 32 MicroVMs by the benchmarking harness; no multi-pod or true multi-node experiments; no interference or multi-tenant isolation study.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL 2.0 MHD silicon (atomics support, actual cross-head latency/bandwidth contention); CXL pool eviction policy under capacity pressure; online hot-set refresh; automated pool-master failover; deduplication's cache-flush interference across MicroVMs sharing a host; parallelizing the currently serialized CXL page pre-install; scaling beyond 32 concurrent restores and beyond a single emulated pod.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct hardware relevance: Aquifer has zero real CXL devices (remote-NUMA emulation of a multi-headed pool on one CloudLab box), so it says nothing empirical about per-device bandwidth, cross-device interference, or real CZ120 behavior — it actually strengthens the gap your 4x-CZ120 testbed can fill, since even a 2026 pooling paper still emulates. Conceptually adjacent to ObjPlace: its hotness-based hot/cold partition across a fast CXL tier and a slow far tier is page/snapshot-granularity tiering, and its finding that serialized CXL page pre-install underperforms parallel RDMA prefetch is a placement-runtime data point. Its ownership/clflushopt coherence protocol targets multi-headed cross-host sharing, which your single-host memory-expander devices do not exhibit; the closest tie to your "per-device independent resource" direction is that Aquifer treats the whole CXL pool as one flat region and never considers per-device scheduling or CXL-to-CXL interference — an explicit non-goal you could contrast against.

Could not verify. Exact arXiv submission date (2026-06-23 per task hint; not visible in extracted PDF text), Whether any latency-injection mechanism beyond plain remote-NUMA access was used ('emulating the access latency characteristic' is not further specified)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Because CloudLab does not currently offer machines with CXL-attached memory devices, we emulate CXL memory using a remote NUMA node, following prior work [35]. We use a dual-socket r6525 node from the CloudLab Clemson cluster
  • CPU 2× AMD EPYC Milan 7543 @2.8 GHz, 32 cores / DRAM 2× 8× 16 GiB ECC DDR4-3200 / ... Experiment NIC 100 Gb/s Mellanox ConnectX-6 Dx MT28924 (Table 1: Hardware configuration of CloudLab r6525 nodes)
  • To emulate a shared CXL memory pool on a single dual-socket machine, we run two VMs on one NUMA node, one acting as the orchestrator and the other as the pool master, each pinned to 16 dedicated physical cores with 64 GiB of memory. The memory attached to the remote NUMA node serves as the CXL pool media, while emulating the access latency characteristic.
  • Although the two VMs share the same physical machine and thus have implicit cache coherence over the backing memory, we still treat the region as non-cache-coherent and require clflushopt on every borrow, emulating a CXL 2.0 MHD where no inter-host coherence is provided.
  • Overall, Aquifer achieves a geometric-mean speedup of 2.2× over Firecracker and 1.3 × over FaaSnap, with per-workload speedups over FaaSnap ranging from 1.00× under pyaes to 1.57× under json.
  • Aquifer's coherence protocol (§3.3) relies on atomic operations across hosts sharing a CXL MHD, an assumption supported by prior empirical findings [49] but not formally guaranteed by the CXL 2.0 specification [1].

https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24079 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.24079

Blowfish: Elastic Virtual Machine Memory for Disaggregated Memory

OSDI 2026 (USENIX) (2026) unknown unknown verify: corrected confidence: low

CXL devices. 0

Problem. Memory overcommitment across VMs is bottlenecked by cold-memory rebalancing: page-access tracking under Transparent Huge Pages (THP) and frequent guest/IO page-table remapping make reclamation and restoration of cold (and free) VM memory slow, causing throughput and latency problems for existing mechanisms (ballooning, virtio-mem, HyperAlloc-style approaches).

Idea. Blowfish is a paravirtualization-based memory overcommitment framework built on disaggregated memory that performs cold/free memory reclamation and restoration at microsecond scale: a lightweight guest-level THP-aware hotness tracker identifies cold pages using guest program semantics, and the hypervisor directly reclaims and reallocates host physical memory across VMs via a dedicated cross-layer path for cold memory that bypasses guest page-table and I/O page-table modifications.

Testbed. Unknown — full text not available before the conference. The abstract says the framework is "built on disaggregated memory" but does not name CXL, RDMA, or any hardware; no testbed details (CPU, DRAM, device model, kernel) could be read.

Evaluation. Unknown in detail. From the abstract, evaluation compares reclamation/restoration speed and reclamation ratios against HyperAlloc under a bounded (5%) performance-degradation budget; specific workloads and how VMs were run are not stated in the abstract.

Baselines. HyperAlloc (explicitly named as state-of-the-art baseline). Likely also virtio-balloon/virtio-mem given the topic, but that is not verified from the paper text.

Key findings. Per the abstract: Blowfish performs cold/free memory reclamation and restoration at microsecond scale; page reclamation 2.48x faster and restoration 2.14x faster than HyperAlloc; improves memory reclamation ratios by 1.6x-6.1x while staying within 5% performance degradation.

Limitations. Cannot assess methodology weaknesses — the full text (methodology/evaluation sections) is not publicly available as of 2026-07-07 (OSDI '26 proceedings PDF not yet posted, no arXiv preprint found). From the abstract alone: the mechanism requires paravirtualized guests (guest-level hotness tracker), so it does not apply to unmodified/opaque guest VMs; the abstract never specifies the disaggregated-memory substrate, so whether results hold on real CXL hardware vs RDMA vs emulation is unverifiable.

Open questions. Whether the disaggregated-memory substrate is real CXL (and how many devices), RDMA, or emulation; multi-tenant interference behavior; applicability to unmodified guests. Unresolvable until the full paper is released at OSDI '26.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Blowfish operates at the hypervisor/VM layer (cross-VM cold-memory reclamation via paravirtualization), which is orthogonal to the requester's ObjPlace object-granularity DRAM/CXL placement and to the candidate per-device scheduling direction — it manages capacity elasticity, not device-level bandwidth/interference. It is relevant as related work if the requester pursues multi-tenant CXL memory elasticity or QoS: Blowfish's us-scale reclaim/restore path is the kind of mechanism a per-device, tail-aware placement system could sit under or compete with. However, since the abstract never names CXL and the testbed is unknown, it currently provides no evidence about real multi-device CXL behavior on hardware like the 4x CZ120 setup. Watch for the full text at OSDI '26 (July 2026) to check whether its "disaggregated memory" is CXL-based.

Could not verify. realCXL: abstract never names CXL, RDMA, or any hardware; full text unavailable, numCXLDevices: no testbed information available, deviceTreatment: no testbed information available, hardwareEnv: CPU/DRAM/device/kernel details not published yet, evalSetup: workloads and run methodology not in abstract, baselines beyond HyperAlloc, authors' own stated limitations, Full paper PDF: https://www.usenix.org/system/files/osdi26-zhang-yulong.pdf returns 404 as of 2026-07-07; no arXiv preprint or author-hosted PDF found

Verifier note. CORRECTED realCXL: no (no CXL hardware; disaggregated memory is RDMA over 100Gbps InfiniBand between a CPU server and a memory server) CORRECTED deviceTreatment: N/A for CXL — far memory is a dedicated remote memory server accessed via an RDMA paging backend (Hermit-inspired) over ConnectX-5 InfiniBand; no NUMA emulation of CXL and no CXL device tiering Primary source obtained: full paper PDF from co-author Diyu Zhou's homepage (https://zhou-diyu.github.io/files/blowfish-osdi26.pdf), downloaded and text-extracted this session (usenix.org was Cloudflare-403 for both the presentation page and osdi26-zhang-yulong.pdf). The analyst's three abstract quotes match the PDF abstract verbatim (modulo the second quote being truncated mid-word). Key finding: despite 'Disaggregated Memory' in the title, this is an RDMA/InfiniBand far-memory paper — the analyst's 'unknown' on all three CXL fields is corrected to no-CXL/0-devices/RDMA-remote-server. CXL is cited only as one of several emerging fabrics in the introduction ('emerging fabrics, e.g., InfiniBand [1], RoCE NICs [43], and CXL [22, 37]') and in related work. Single testbed only: 2x Intel Xeon Gold 6342, 256GB per server, Linux 6.1 + QEMU 8.2.1, THP enabled; baselines HyperAlloc/HyperAlloc-4K variants with Hermit. Corroborating sources: https://wangchenxi7.github.io/home/, https://github.com/ICTPLSys/blowfish, https://zhou-diyu.github.io/publications/.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • In this paper, we propose Blowfish, a memory overcommitment framework built on disaggregated memory, performing cold (and free) memory reclamation and restoration at μs-scale.
  • Based on paravirtualization, Blowfish leverages a lightweight guest-level THP-aware hotness tracker to monitor page access and let the hypervisor directly reclaim and reallocate host physical memory across VMs with a dedicated cross-layer path for cold memory, bypassing the modifications of guest page table and modifications of I/O page table while benefiting from the rich program semantics of guest VM when recognizing the cold memory.
  • As a result, Blowfish significantly speeds up the page reclamation and restoration, 2.48× and 2.14× faster than the state-of-the-art solution, HyperAlloc, respectively, and improves memory reclamation ratios by 1.6×-6.1× within 5% performance degradation.
  • Yulong Zhang, SKLP, Institute of Computing Technology, CAS and University of Chinese Academy of Sciences; Yilong Luo and Diyu Zhou, SCS, Peking University, China; Quan Chen, Shanghai Jiao Tong University; Quanxi Li, SKLP, Institute of Computing Technology, CAS and University of Chinese Academy of Sciences; Mosong Zhou, Lei Zhu, Senbo Fu, and Qian Peng, Huawei Cloud
