Break On Through to the Other Side: Pooling Memory Elastically with RamRyder

OSDI '26 (20th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2026) unknown unknown verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. Cloud vendors allocate VM memory capacity in a fixed ratio to vCPUs and offer no mechanism at all for allocating memory bandwidth; when an application's capacity vs. bandwidth demands diverge or change over time, both resources are underutilized.

Idea. RamRyder is a software-defined elastic memory system for cloud VMs that decouples memory bandwidth from capacity allocation: it controls the mapping between guest-OS memory pages and the underlying physical memory channels, giving per-VM performance isolation and allowing bandwidth and capacity to be re-allocated dynamically and (mostly) independently.

Testbed. Unknown — full paper is locked on USENIX (open-access PDF not yet posted as of 2026-07-07) and no preprint exists. The abstract does not name any CPU, DRAM, or CXL hardware. Authors are UCSD + Shanghai Jiao Tong University + Samsung Semiconductor; the paper sits in the OSDI '26 'Memory Tiering and CXL' session, and the channel-level page-mapping mechanism plus the Samsung Semiconductor co-authors suggest possible CXL or memory-controller-level hardware, but this is unverified.

Evaluation. Unknown beyond abstract: cluster-scale evaluation with cloud-VM workloads measuring capacity utilization, bandwidth utilization, and performance vs. exclusive access. Workloads, VM counts, and traces not readable.

Baselines. Unknown; abstract only compares against "the best case with exclusive access" (static exclusive allocation). Named baseline systems not readable.

Key findings. RamRyder improves average capacity utilization by 28.6% and average bandwidth utilization by 43.2% across the cluster, while delivering performance comparable to the best case in which each VM has exclusive access to its memory.

Limitations. Cannot assess from full text. From the abstract alone: bandwidth and capacity are only "(mostly) independently" allocatable, i.e., the decoupling is admittedly partial. Whether the evaluation used real CXL hardware, emulation, or plain DRAM channels; how many devices; and whether multi-tenant interference was measured on real silicon are all unverifiable at this time.

Open questions. Unknown from abstract. Obvious open items given the abstract: how channel-level page mapping interacts with hardware interleaving and with CXL devices whose internal bandwidth is opaque to the host; behavior under adversarial multi-tenant bandwidth contention; hypervisor/guest modification requirements and migration cost of remapping pages across channels.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly adjacent to the requester's candidate direction: RamRyder allocates memory bandwidth and capacity independently per VM by controlling page-to-channel mapping — essentially treating individual memory channels/devices as independently schedulable bandwidth resources, which is the VM-granularity analog of the requester's per-CXL-device placement idea. If RamRyder's channels include CXL devices, it is the closest OSDI'26 competitor and must be differentiated (e.g., ObjPlace works at object granularity within one process vs. RamRyder's VM/guest-page granularity; requester's CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference data with 4x CZ120 could go beyond whatever channel model RamRyder uses). Reading the full paper the moment USENIX unlocks it (conference week) is high priority — the requester's measured 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdown is exactly the isolation problem RamRyder claims to solve. Until then its hardware basis (real CXL vs. DRAM channels) is unknown.

Could not verify. realCXL — full text locked; abstract never mentions CXL, only 'memory channels'; cannot determine if evaluation uses real CXL ASICs, emulation, or plain DRAM channels, numCXLDevices — testbed not readable, deviceTreatment — not readable, hardwareEnv (CPU/DRAM/CXL models, kernel version), evalSetup workload list, named baselines, authors' stated limitations, whether 'memory channels' means DRAM channels, CXL devices, or both

Full paper text is not publicly accessible as of 2026-07-08: USENIX PDF is prepublication-locked (direct fetch 403; osdi26-zhou-yanbo.pdf 404 via reader proxy), no arXiv preprint, no PDF on either author homepage (yanbozyb.github.io, giorgioercixu.github.io/publication/) or the NVSL lab publications page, no slides found. Cxlalloc: Safe and Efficient Memory Allocation for a CXL Pod

ASPLOS 2026 (31st ACM ASPLOS, Vol. 2, March 22-26, 2026, Pittsburgh) (2026) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. testbed A (Chameleon Ice Lake, scalability runs): 0 CXL devices (DRAM shared-memory file); testbed B: 1 (one Intel Agilex 7 CXL Type-2 device)

Problem. Memory allocation for a "CXL pod" (8-16 hosts sharing one multi-headed CXL memory device at cacheline granularity) faces three challenges existing allocators fail on: (1) CXL devices may not support inter-host hardware cache coherence (HWcc), so CPU CAS is unsafe across hosts; (2) the heap is concurrently accessed by different processes with independent virtual address spaces, breaking pointer consistency when heaps grow or huge allocations change memory mappings; (3) partial failures (one thread/process crashing mid-allocation) become more likely with more hosts and must not block or leak.

Idea. Cxlalloc, a user-space lock-free allocator that: separates metadata into a small contiguous HWcc region vs. a software-cache-coherent (SWcc) region so it works under full/limited/no HWcc; provides spatial+temporal pointer consistency via offset pointers, consistent-offset layout, signal-handler-based asynchronous mmap installation, and a hazard-pointer protocol for mapping reclamation (first allocator supporting cross-process huge, e.g. 1 GiB, allocations with heap extension); tolerates partial failure via an 8-byte atomically-updated per-operation redo state enabling idempotent non-blocking recovery; and for no-HWcc devices contributes a memory-based CAS (mCAS) primitive implemented in near-memory-processing logic on an FPGA.

Testbed. Two machines. (A) Scalability/KV-store runs: Chameleon compute_icelake_r650, Ubuntu 22.04.5, Linux 5.15, 2x Intel Xeon Platinum 8380 @2.30GHz (40 cores, 120 MiB LLC, 128 GiB DDR4-3200 per socket), no CXL device — allocators backed by a 64 GiB shared-memory file bound to NUMA node 0. (B) CXL runs: Ubuntu 24.04.2, Linux 6.8, Intel Xeon 8568 (Emerald Rapids) @2.0GHz, 48 cores, 300MB LLC, 8-channel DDR5-4800; one Intel Agilex 7 CXL Type-2 device over PCIe 5.0 x16 (ASIC memory controller + ASIC-based CXL IPs, FPGA used only for the NMP mCAS logic; device supports CXL 2.0 but CPU only CXL 1.1). Measured: CXL read latency 357ns vs 112ns DRAM; CXL bandwidth 19.9 GB/s (2 channels) vs 114 GB/s local (4 channels).

Evaluation. Ported YCSB (Load/A/D) into an in-memory KV store plus 4 production Twitter memcached cluster traces (MC-12/15/31/37); allocator microbenchmarks threadtest and xmalloc (small objects, and a 1 GiB-object huge-allocation variant, 9.6M ops); Memento recoverable queue/hashmap with 0/1/2 injected thread crashes; CAS latency microbenchmark (sw_cas vs sw_flush_cas vs hw_cas mCAS) on the CXL device up to 16 threads. Cross-process runs use 10 processes, 1-8 threads/proc; 10 trials, cores pinned, turbo/HT/NUMA-balancing/KSM disabled. All sharing is among processes on ONE host; no actual multi-host pod is run.

Baselines. mimalloc (single-process performance ceiling), Boost.Interprocess, Lightning's internal cross-process allocator, ralloc (lock-free recoverable PM allocator), cxl-shm (partial-fault-tolerant CXL memory management, SOSP'23).

Key findings. On YCSB/memcached traces cxlalloc is among the best in throughput and PSS memory (cxl-shm crashes on MC-12/MC-37 due to its 1 KiB allocation-size limit). On threadtest cxlalloc gets 47% of mimalloc's throughput; on xmalloc 81%. HWcc-metadata footprint is 0.02% of total memory on average (7.1% of ralloc's HWcc usage). Partial-failure support costs only 0.3% vs a non-recoverable variant on average (94.7% threadtest, 88.4% xmalloc); recovers from thread crashes without blocking or leaking, unlike ralloc's GC-or-leak choice. Only allocator to complete the 1 GiB huge-allocation benchmark (all baselines crash or exceed 30 min). On real CXL with mCAS (no HWcc): cxlalloc-mcas reaches 80% of cxlalloc-hwcc on threadtest and 10-99x ralloc-mcas; on xmalloc it drops to 1% of hwcc (every remote free needs an mCAS) but is 9.9x ralloc-mcas at 24 threads. FPGA hw_cas: 17.4% lower p50 / 20% lower p99 than sw_flush_cas at 16 threads, but slow at 1 thread (p50 2.3us).

Limitations. Despite targeting a multi-host "pod", every experiment runs on a single host — inter-host sharing is modeled by multiple processes on one machine; no multi-headed CXL device or second host is used. Only one CXL device (Agilex 7 FPGA card, CXL 1.1 host mode), not production memory-expander ASICs; main KV-store/scalability results are on a DRAM shared-memory file with no CXL at all. Authors admit: mCAS FPGA prototype has high uncontended latency (2.3us p50, expect ASIC to close gap); xmalloc-mcas drops to 1% of HWcc throughput; split HWcc/SWcc metadata can increase total memory usage; processes are assumed correct and trusted (no memory protection); NUMA-awareness not addressed. No bandwidth-interference, tiering, or tail-latency-under-contention evaluation.

Open questions. Actual multi-host pod deployment on multi-headed CXL 3.x hardware (they note it is "unclear if HWcc will become widely supported"); ASIC implementation of mCAS; adapting Tigon (which used an early cxlalloc) to mCAS is explicitly called "interesting future work"; extending the design with MPK-style heap protection; NUMA/device-aware placement of the heap; behavior under memory-bandwidth contention or with multiple CXL devices is untested.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Adjacent but orthogonal to the requester's direction: cxlalloc manages allocation on a single shared CXL device for multi-host/multi-process pods (coherence, pointer consistency, crash recovery), not DRAM/CXL placement, bandwidth interference, or per-device scheduling — so it does not overlap with ObjPlace's placement policy contribution, but it IS the closest published claim to "allocator-level CXL memory management," so ObjPlace's positioning should cite it and stress that placement/interference across 4 independent CZ120 devices is a different problem. Its evaluation is single-host, single FPGA device with no multi-device or interference experiments, leaving the requester's per-device independent-resource direction untouched. Useful methodological reference points: their MLC-measured 357ns/19.9 GB/s Agilex numbers (vs the requester's 277ns/35.5 GiB/s CZ120 region) and their process-on-one-host emulation of pod sharing.

Could not verify. Exact CXL device memory capacity on the Agilex 7 card (text cut off in extraction; capacity not stated in the testbed paragraph I read), Whether the CXL testbed CPU has 32 or 48 cores (paper states both in different sentences)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We evaluate on two machines: a Chameleon [39] instance that does not have a CXL device, but does have 80 cores to better compare scalability, and another machine with a CXL Type-2 device, 32 cores, and our mCAS prototype to measure the effect of HWcc.
  • Our test machine runs Ubuntu 24.04.2 LTS, Linux kernel version 6.8. It has an Intel Xeon 8568 CPU running at 2.0GHz, with 48 cores and 300MB LLC. The machine is equipped with 8-channel DDR5 4800 DRAM. We use a commercially available CXL Type-2 device, the Intel Agilex 7 [21], connected to the CPU via a PCIe 5.0 x16 link. It integrates an FPGA with ASIC-based CXL IPs. The memory controller is an ASIC, the FPGA is only for implementing near memory processing logic (NMP). While the device supports CXL 2.0, our Intel Emerald Rapids CPU only supports 1.1 [37, 58].
  • CXL read latency is 357ns, compared with 112ns for local memory. Its bandwidth is 19.9 GB/s (using two channels), compared with 114 GB/s for local memory (using four channels).
  • It has two Intel Xeon Platinum 8380 CPUs running at 2.30GHz, with 40 cores, 120 MiB LLC, and 128GiB DDR4 3200 DRAM per socket. We bind all memory to NUMA node 0
  • Using a commercial CXL device, we demonstrate that cxlalloc can achieve up to 80% of its maximum allocation throughput with no HWcc (using mCAS).
  • Each memory allocator is backed by a 64 GiB shared memory file.

https://www.cs.utexas.edu/~witchel/pubs/ni26asplos-cxlalloc.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3779212.3790149

DPC: A Distributed Page Cache over CXL

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2604.19494) (2026) numa-emulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL devices; QEMU-emulated CXL devices backed by shared host DRAM across NUMA nodes on one ARM server)

Problem. Distributed file systems use uncoordinated per-node page caches that replicate hot data locally on every node, wasting aggregate cluster DRAM through redundancy and incurring heavy coherence/consistency overhead when data is shared across nodes.

Idea. An OS-level distributed page cache built on CXL 3.0 shared-memory semantics that treats the whole cluster's DRAM as one cache budget and enforces a single-copy invariant at page granularity: each file page has exactly one owner node holding the sole resident DRAM copy; all other nodes map that page remotely over CXL (via a dpc_dax driver) instead of replicating it, eliminating redundancy and coherence traffic. Offers both a relaxed-consistency mode (DPC) and a strong-consistency mode (DPC_SC).

Testbed. Dual-socket 64-bit ARMv8.2 server (model withheld for anonymization; affiliation suggests Huawei Kunpeng-class), 2 compute dies per socket, 32 cores @2.6 GHz per die = 128 physical cores; 256 GiB DDR4-2933 spread across 4 NUMA nodes; 2 TB RAID-0 of 4x480 GB SAS SSDs. Cluster of 4 QEMU VMs (16 vCPUs, 32 GB RAM each); each VM's memory is a host memory-backend file, and other VMs' backend files are attached as emulated CXL devices behind QEMU CXL root ports, all coherent because they reside in shared host DRAM. No real CXL silicon or FPGA; kernel version not captured.

Evaluation. 4-VM emulated CXL 3.0 cluster on one ARM NUMA server. Microbenchmarks with fio (libaio and mmap engines, read/write, cache-hit/cache-miss cases). Application workloads: RocksDB (60 GB KV store), DeepSeek-V2-Lite-Chat LLM inference (30 GB mmapped weights), DiskANN (40 GB ANN index), Filebench Webserver (32 GB) and Fileserver (32 GB).

Baselines. virtiofs (unmodified), NFSv4.1, JuiceFS v1.3; plus DPC's own strong-consistency variant DPC_SC vs. relaxed DPC.

Key findings. On data-sharing workloads DPC achieves up to 12.4x speedup with a 5.6x cross-workload geomean over baselines; microbenchmarks show up to 72.8x read-IOPS improvement on cache-hit reads and 2.6x read-latency reduction vs virtiofs; Webserver/Fileserver see up to ~16.2x/15.2x. Single-copy ownership removes replica redundancy so aggregate DRAM caches more distinct data. Cost side: synchronous single-page invalidation takes 99.7 us under DPC vs 11 us for virtiofs, and DPC_SC pays per-write overhead for stronger consistency.

Limitations. Authors admit production CXL 3.0 multi-host shared-memory hardware does not exist, so the entire evaluation is QEMU + intra-server NUMA coherence emulation — remote-CXL latency/bandwidth is whatever cross-NUMA access on one ARM box gives (no evidence of injected fabric latency), likely optimistic vs a real CXL 3.0 fabric. Zero physical CXL devices; no real multi-host coherence, no fabric contention or bandwidth ceilings. Fault tolerance is weak: owner-node failure can lose dirty pages. Reclamation/invalidation coordination is ~9x slower than local. Only 4 nodes, single physical machine; no multi-tenant or interference study.

Open questions. Behavior on real CXL 3.0 fabrics with back-invalidation/HDM-DB coherence, realistic ~250-400ns+ latencies and shared-link bandwidth contention; scalability beyond 4 nodes; robust fault tolerance for dirty single-copy pages; interaction with memory tiering/placement policies on the owner node; write-heavy and strongly-consistent workloads at scale.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x CZ120 CXL 2.0 memory-expansion testbed: DPC targets hypothetical CXL 3.0 multi-host shared-memory fabrics and is evaluated entirely on QEMU/NUMA emulation with zero physical CXL devices, so its numbers say nothing about real per-device latency (277ns), ~35.5 GiB/s per-region bandwidth ceilings, or CXL-to-CXL co-runner interference the requester measured. CXL 2.0 expanders like CZ120 cannot provide the cross-host coherent sharing DPC requires. Useful mostly as related work for positioning (single-copy ownership and page-granularity remote mapping ideas could inform ObjPlace-style placement, e.g., famfs-backed shared pages), and as an example of the emulation-only evaluation gap the requester's real-hardware, per-device-scheduling direction can attack.

Could not verify. Kernel/OS and QEMU versions in the testbed, Whether any extra latency was injected to model CXL fabric latency (no such sentence found; cross-NUMA latency appears to be used as-is), Exact per-workload speedup breakdown beyond the headline numbers

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Because production CXL 3.0 hardware is not yet available, our evaluation platform cannot rely on real CXL fabrics. Instead, we emulate CXL in QEMU and use NUMA nodes with the hardware cache-coherence protocol of the underlying system to provide a realistic model of coherent, byte-addressable shared memory.
  • We evaluate DPC on a dual-socket 64-bit ARMv8.2 server (model not specified for anonymization). Each socket integrates two compute dies, with each incorporating 32 cores at 2.6 GHz, for a total of 128 physical cores. The system has 256 GiB of DDR4-2933 memory, evenly distributed across the four NUMA nodes that correspond to the compute dies.
  • For every VM, we attach the memory-backend files of the other VMs as CXL devices behind CXL root ports in QEMU, where they are handled by the dpc_dax driver. As all CXL-backed regions ultimately reside in shared host DRAM, loads and stores from different VMs observe a coherent view of these regions.
  • Across these workloads within a multi-node setup, DPC achieves speedups of up to 12.4×, with a cross-workload geomean speedup of 5.6×.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19494 https://arxiv.org/html/2604.19494v1

Duhu: Shared Disaggregated Memory for Distributed Data Processing Frameworks

OSDI '26 (USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2026) unknown multi-device-pooled verify: corrected confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown

Problem. Distributed data processing frameworks (DDFs) like Ray/Spark incur large memory and network-transfer overheads because every node must copy objects into local memory before processing; shared disaggregated memory (SDM) clusters could avoid these copies, but current SDMs provide weak coherence guarantees and coherence poses scalability/complexity challenges, so DDFs would otherwise need invasive logic changes and software coordination to adopt SDM.

Idea. Duhu is an SDM-based object store that sits between DDFs and the shared disaggregated memory, handling coherence/coordination so that DDFs can use SDM without modifying their processing logic. It is integrated with Ray as the object store, letting Ray tasks on different nodes access shared objects in a CXL-attached memory pool instead of copying them over the network.

Testbed. Not verifiable from public sources as of 2026-07-07. Abstract states only: an "SDM cluster with a prototype CXL-attached memory pool" (SK hynix co-authors suggest an SK hynix pooled-memory prototype, but this is inference, not verified). No CPU, DRAM, CXL device model/count, link width, or kernel version available.

Evaluation. From abstract only: system integrated with Ray and evaluated on an SDM cluster with a prototype CXL-attached memory pool; workloads include at least a shuffle workload where JCT is measured. Full workload list, cluster size, and configuration unverifiable pre-publication.

Baselines. Not verifiable from abstract; presumably vanilla Ray (Plasma object store with network object transfer), but the paper text naming the baselines could not be read.

Key findings. Duhu improves job completion time by up to 3.39x on a shuffle workload versus the baseline (per abstract). No other numbers publicly available yet.

Limitations. Cannot assess from full text (not yet released). From the abstract alone: evaluation is on a single "prototype" CXL-attached memory pool, i.e., pre-production pooling hardware not commercially available (unlike memory-expansion ASICs such as CZ120); the headline 3.39x number is for shuffle, a best-case copy-heavy workload; nothing in the abstract indicates multi-tenant or interference-aware evaluation, and coherence is explicitly worked around (weak-coherence SDM) rather than solved in hardware.

Open questions. Whether the approach generalizes beyond Ray to other DDFs; behavior under multi-tenant sharing/contention on the pool; scalability of the software-coordination layer as node count grows; performance on non-shuffle workloads; portability to commodity (non-prototype) CXL 2.0/3.x pooling hardware. All are inferred gaps — the paper text could not be read to check what the authors themselves punt on.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Adjacent rather than directly comparable: Duhu targets multi-host shared CXL memory pooling (SDM) with a prototype pool, while the requester's testbed is single-host memory expansion with 4x CZ120 behind one EPYC 9745 — no cross-host sharing or hardware pooling is possible there. Duhu's object-store-level placement of DDF objects into CXL overlaps conceptually with ObjPlace's object-granularity DRAM/CXL placement, so it is a must-cite for positioning and for the argument that object granularity is the right interface for CXL memory. It does not appear to address per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or tail-aware QoS, so the requester's candidate direction (treating each of the 4 devices as an independently schedulable resource) remains unclaimed by this paper as far as the abstract shows — but this must be rechecked when the full OSDI '26 PDF drops on ~2026-07-13.

Could not verify. realCXL hardware type (ASIC vs FPGA prototype vs emulation) — abstract only says 'prototype CXL-attached memory pool', numCXLDevices — never stated in publicly available material, deviceTreatment — 'multi-device-pooled' is an inference from the phrase 'memory pool'; actual device topology unverified, hardwareEnv details (CPU, DRAM, device model/capacity, kernel), evalSetup beyond Ray+shuffle, baselines, authors' own limitations section, Full paper text: OSDI '26 proceedings not released until ~2026-07-13; no arXiv preprint found; WebFetch of usenix.org returned 403 (curl succeeded for the abstract page only); osdi26-men.pdf returns 403/not available

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We have integrated Duhu with Ray, and evaluated our system on an SDM cluster with a prototype CXL-attached memory pool. We show that Duhu can improve job completion time (JCT) by up to 3.39× on a shuffle workload.
  • Emerging shared disaggregated memory (SDM) clusters enable an alternate approach because they allow nodes to access data in a shared memory. However, using SDMs for DDFs is challenging: current SDM clusters provide weak coherence guarantees, and even for emerging SDMs coherence poses a scalability and complexity challenge.
  • In this paper, we describe Duhu, an SDM-based object store that is designed to allow DDFs to use SDMs without these changes, simplifying their adoption.

Exploring Dynamic Memory Allocation of CXL Memory Pools in Enterprise In-Memory Database Management Systems

EDBT (Industrial & Applications Paper), pp. 687-695, doi 10.48786/edbt.2026.58 (2026) real-asic multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 2 Samsung CMM-D 128GB devices behind one XConn B2 CXL switch (the "CXL memory box", used in both testbeds); plus directly-attached CMM-D device(s) in the single-host testbed for comparison (count of direct-attached devices not stated)

Problem. Whether a real switch-based CXL memory pool — with its extra switch hop adding ~100ns latency and lower bandwidth than directly-attached CXL — can serve an enterprise in-memory DBMS (SAP HANA) for dynamic capacity allocation across hosts without unacceptable performance loss, and what DB use cases justify it (TCO reduction, avoiding OOM, elastic provisioning).

Idea. Deploy a real 2-host CXL 2.0 switch-based memory pool (XConn B2 switch + 2 Samsung CMM-D) and integrate it with a SAP HANA prototype (NUMA-aware far-memory allocation in the HEX engine, temp-table NUMA placement). Dynamic pooling is realized at the OS layer (Linux memory online/offline over a statically-configured 256GB HDM region per host, since fabric-manager HDM remapping was too unstable) rather than at the switch layer. Evaluate three use cases: moving main-storage table data / HEX heap to the pool (TPC-C, TPC-DS), HANA fast restart using pool persistence, and dynamic allocation of intermediate-result temp tables across two servers.

Testbed. Testbed 1 (single host, Sec 5.1/5.2): 1x Intel 5th-gen Xeon (Emerald Rapids), 48 physical / 96 HT cores, 8x 128GB DDR5-4800 = 1,024 GB local DRAM; commercial CXL memory box = 1 XConn B2 switch (up to 256 PCIe Gen5 lanes) + 2x Samsung CMM-D 128GB (DDR5-5200 DIMM, ASIC CXL-to-DDR5 controller, PCIe Gen5 x8 each); hosts on Gen5 x16 upstream ports; 256GB HDM per host; direct-attached CMM-D and pool both connected to the remote socket for latency study. Testbed 2 (two servers, Sec 5.3): Server A: 2x Xeon Platinum 8568 (Emerald Rapids, 96 logical cores/socket, 8x128GB DIMMs = 1TB DRAM/socket); Server B: 2x Emerald Rapids (112 logical cores/socket, 8x64GB = 512GB/socket); each server expanded by 128GB from the shared pool. Kernel version not stated (Linux, kernel online/offline of memory blocks; fsdax + device-mapper interleaving for the x2 bandwidth config).

Evaluation. SAP HANA prototype with CXL far-memory allocation. OLTP: TPC-C 100 warehouses, sweeping 1-384 client threads, throughput normalized to baseline. OLAP: TPC-DS SF100, 1-16 parallel streams, queries/unit-time. Placements compared for main storage (fsdax) and HEX heap (NUMA-bound): local DRAM, remote DRAM, remote direct CMM-D, local/remote M-pool, and M-pool x2 (two fsdax devices, one per downstream CMM-D, interleaved via Linux device mapper to double bandwidth). Fast restart: measure Restart/Ready/Completed timestamps with main storage in the pool. Two-server: SQL scripts from the top-5 TPC-DS queries by temp-table footprint; temp tables placed in DRAM/remote-DRAM/M-pool; interference measured with Server A on the first CMM-D's 128GB range and Server B on the other device, run concurrently vs separately; MLC used to validate dynamic capacity changes.

Baselines. All-local-DRAM baseline; remote-socket DRAM; directly-attached CXL memory (CMM-D on remote socket); CXL memory pool local vs remote socket; pool with 2x interleaved bandwidth; HANA restart with/without preload and with/without fast-restart. Related-work comparisons (Pond, PolarDB, Octopus) are discussed, not run.

Key findings. (1) TPC-C (OLTP) shows no observable degradation with main storage or HEX heap in the pool; peak CXL traffic ~13 GB/s at 288 threads, well below device bandwidth. (2) TPC-DS (OLAP) degrades noticeably with main storage in the pool; performance is more bandwidth- than latency-sensitive — device-mapper interleaving across the two CMM-Ds (x2) cuts degradation to single-digit % vs baseline. (3) HEX heap in CXL is rejected as a use case (>50% degradation in prior work due to random access). (4) Fast restart from the persistent pool saves 87% of preload time, a 5.25x restart acceleration vs preload-from-disk (but 27% slower than no-preload restart). (5) Temp tables for big TPC-DS SQL scripts in the pool: performance effectively unchanged vs local DRAM. (6) Two servers driving the same switch concurrently (each on its own CMM-D device) show no measurable interference; dynamic online/offline of pool regions incurs no measurable delay and no restarts.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: switch-layer dynamic HDM remapping via the fabric manager was NOT used "due to system stability concerns with our current hardware" — pooling is emulated at the OS layer over a static HDM config; memory sharing (overlapping regions) among hosts is deferred to future work; HANA CXL features may never ship commercially. Methodological weaknesses: only 2 CXL devices and 2 hosts; the no-interference result is weak — each server used a disjoint 128GB range on a DIFFERENT device and the paper itself concedes overhead stays negligible only "as long as there are no other resource conflicts, such as device bandwidth contention", i.e., same-device bandwidth contention was never tested; workloads chosen for the interference test are sequential/prefetch-friendly with low CXL traffic; no tail-latency analysis, only throughput; no kernel version or latency/bandwidth microbenchmark numbers for the pool are reported (the ~100ns switch penalty is cited, not measured).

Open questions. Cross-host sharing of table data / intermediate results with CXL 3.x cache coherency (explicit future work); true fabric-manager-driven dynamic HDM reassignment (skipped for stability); interference when multiple hosts contend for the SAME device's bandwidth; QoS isolation on the switch (stated as a requirement in Sec 2.1 but never evaluated); behavior at >2 hosts / >2 devices; latency-sensitive random-access workloads on pools beyond the blanket "use local DRAM" advice.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant to the "each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource" direction: their interference experiment partitions the pool per-device (Server A on CMM-D #1, Server B on CMM-D #2) and finds no cross-device interference through a shared XConn switch — but only under low, prefetch-friendly traffic, and they explicitly exempt "device bandwidth contention", which is exactly the 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown the requester already measured on a single interleaved region. This leaves same-device and cross-device contention under saturating load an open gap the requester's 4x CZ120 testbed can fill without a switch. Their x2 device-mapper interleaving result (bandwidth, not latency, dominates OLAP) matches the requester's 35.5 GiB/s-per-region, 4-thread-saturation finding and supports per-device vs interleaved placement as a real knob. Their fsdax-based main-storage placement and OS-level online/offline pooling are also mechanically similar to the requester's famfs/daxctl setup, making SAP HANA-style DB workloads a plausible additional workload class for ObjPlace-style per-device placement.

Could not verify. Exact count of directly-attached CMM-D devices used in the 'Main/HEX Remote CMM-D' comparison configs (not stated), Linux kernel version (not stated), CXL link/spec revision of the CMM-D devices (Gen5 x8 stated, CXL revision not explicit)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We use the XConn B2 CXL switch chip that supports up to 256 PCIe Gen5 lanes for CXL upstream and downstream links. We configure our memory pooling system with 2 hosts, each connected to PCIe Gen5x16 upstream ports (USPs). On downstream ports (DSPs), we install two 128GB Samsung CXL memory devices (CMM-D), each consisting of a 128GB DDR5 DIMM supporting 5200 MT/s, an internal memory controller, an ASIC logic converting CXL protocols to DDR5, and a PCIe Gen5x8 interface.
  • Specifically, we configure the HDM mapping to bind all 128GB CMM-D devices sequentially, and form a single 256GB HDM region for each host to enable dynamic pooling over the entire downstream devices.
  • First, we create two fsdax devices in our test machine, one per downstream device. Then, we create a mapped device by interleaving these two fsdax devices using the Linux device mapper.
  • Server A uses the first 128 GB range located at the first CXL memory device and Server B uses the remaining 128 GB range at the other CXL memory device. Under this setup, all CXL traffic passes through the same CXL switch within the memory box during benchmark execution.
  • At the switch layer, the HDM mapping can be dynamically modified via a fabric manager. However, this approach is not applied in our evaluation due to system stability concerns with our current hardware.
  • As shown in Fig. 10, no noticeable performance degradation is observed, indicating that shared memory access through the CXL memory pool does not introduce any interference between two servers. Thanks to the CXL switch's ability to relay traffic in parallel across devices, performance overhead remains negligible as long as there are no other resource conflicts, such as device bandwidth contention.
  • Thus, it saves 87% of data preloading time, totally resulting in a 5.25x acceleration of restart time, compared to the second configuration.
  • we set up a system based on Intel's 5th generation Xeon® processor, code-named Emerald Rapids. The system is equipped with a single processor featuring 48 physical cores, totally 96 cores with Hyper-Threading enabled. Each of the eight memory channels is populated with a 128 GB DDR5 4800 MT/s DIMM, providing a total of 1,024 GB of local DRAM.

https://openproceedings.org/2026/conf/edbt/paper-280.pdf

MEGALON: Efficient Data Sharing for Partly Coherent CXL Memory

OSDI (20th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation, OSDI '26) (2026) numa-emulated n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL devices; CXL memory emulated as one NUMA socket of a 4-socket server)

Problem. CXL 3.x lets multiple hosts share memory, but hardware cache coherence is expected to cover only a small coherent region (SCR, a few hundred MB) while total CXL memory spans TBs (the large non-coherent region, LNR). Existing software-coherence schemes (HCMeta, as in Tigon) put per-object coherence records AND the index in SCR; for large datasets the metadata does not fit, causing repeated unshare/reshare "churn" that degrades throughput by up to ~10x.

Idea. Split the sharing metadata: the big but infrequently-updated index is logically shared by replicating it in each host's local DRAM and keeping replicas consistent via a CXL shared log (Node-Replication-style, with only the log tail pointer required to be in the SCR); only tiny, heavily-updated per-object coherence records (a lock bit + counter) live in the hardware-coherent SCR, and they are allocated dynamically only for read-write-shared objects. The shared log additionally enables cheap churn, local-DRAM partitions, local read-only data copies, and shrinkable coherence records.

Testbed. 4-socket server with Intel Xeon Gold 6418H CPUs. NUMA node 0 designated as emulated CXL memory (uncore frequency throttled to approximate CXL latency, following prior work); NUMA nodes 1-3 act as 3 emulated hosts, each with 24 physical cores (hyperthreading disabled) and 64 GB local DRAM. SCR is 200 MB by default (matching Tigon). No real CXL device, no link parameters; kernel version not stated; achieved emulated latency values not reported.

Evaluation. Two applications built on MEGALON: a linearizable KV store (1KB objects, up to 24M keys) and a user-level shared file-system page cache (48GB dataset of 4KB pages). Microbenchmarks: Zipfian (0.99 and 0.7) read-only and read-write (5%/50% write) workloads while varying dataset size, SCR size (32MB-1GB+), key size (10-90B), coherence-record size (8-32 bits), and shared ratio; plus YCSB A/B/C/D/F macrobenchmark on 18M objects. Threads distributed equally across the 3 emulated hosts.

Baselines. HCMeta (authors' reimplementation of Tigon-style hardware-coherent metadata-based sharing in their KV store), HCMeta-Local (data in host-local DRAM, shared objects moved to CXL), HCMeta-Unlimited (idealized unlimited SCR); Tigon artifact measured directly for churn overhead (~55 us); MEGALON self-variants AllLog (all metadata via shared log) and Megalon-Data-Copy (local object replicas).

Key findings. With a 200MB SCR, MEGALON supports up to 12x larger datasets without churn than HCMeta (coherence records for up to 50M objects), improving throughput 4x-10x (10.11x at 5% writes / 24M keys, 4.22x at 50% writes); even when MEGALON itself churns it is 2.5x-14.9x faster (e.g., 11.16x at 64MB SCR) and each churn is ~8x cheaper. Read-only: 15x over HCMeta and even beats HCMeta-Unlimited thanks to local index replicas. YCSB speedups 3.18x-14.18x; page cache 1.88x-5.68x. Cost: only 7.6% more memory (27.71GB vs 25.76GB at 24M objects) for 15.25x higher throughput (14.19x more MOps/s per GB). MEGALON sustains high throughput down to 128MB SCR, 8x smaller than HCMeta needs.

Limitations. Entirely emulation-based: "CXL 3.0 hardware is not commercially available," so both the CXL memory and the SCR are emulated via a NUMA socket with uncore throttling on one 4-socket box — no real CXL latency/bandwidth asymmetries, no real multi-host fabric, no CXL.mem back-invalidation hardware. Only 3 emulated hosts evaluated; authors state MEGALON targets 8-16 nodes because local-memory footprint (index replicas) grows linearly with node count. Fail-stop model: failure of any host or CXL memory fails the whole system; KV store has no checkpointing. Coherence-record size is fixed at 32 bits (no dynamic sizing). Inter-host RPC path "barely optimized". No multi-tenant or interference experiments; no real bandwidth-contention study.

Open questions. Scaling beyond 8-16 hosts (partial metadata views, sharding across multiple shared logs) is explicitly left open. Dynamically choosing optimal coherence-record size per workload is unimplemented. Moving MPMC queue entries to LNR / using the shared log for RPC left as future work. Behavior on real CXL 3.x hardware with actual back-invalidate coherence, real link bandwidth limits, and cross-host contention is untested. Fault tolerance beyond fail-stop (host crash recovery via the shared log) is not addressed.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's testbed and direction. MEGALON is about multi-host CXL 3.x shared memory with a small hardware-coherent region — the requester's single-host EPYC 9745 + 4x CZ120 CXL 2.0 memory-expansion setup has no multi-host sharing, no SCR, and MEGALON's evaluation is pure NUMA emulation with zero physical CXL devices (so it offers no data on per-device behavior, cross-device interference, or bandwidth contention relevant to ObjPlace). Two indirect connections: (1) its metadata-splitting logic (put small hot state in the scarce fast/coherent tier, replicate large cold state) is a placement-policy pattern conceptually adjacent to ObjPlace's object-granularity DRAM/CXL split; (2) if the requester ever uses the famfs-hosted devices for cross-process shared state, MEGALON's shared-log/replicated-index design is the closest software-coherence prior art to cite. It is a useful related-work citation, not a competing baseline.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Setup. CXL 3.0 hardware is not commercially available, so we emulate CXL and hosts on a 4-socket server with Intel Xeon Gold 6418H processors. We designate NUMA node 0 as CXL memory and nodes 1-3 as hosts. Each emulated host has 24 physical cores with hyperthreading disabled and 64GB local DRAM. To approximate CXL latencies, we throttle the uncore frequency of node 0, as in prior work [3].
  • We compare against three baselines: HCMeta, HCMeta-local, and HCMeta-Unlimited
  • All experiments by default runs on the KV Store application with 1KB objects. Unless stated otherwise, we use 200MB for the SCR (like in Tigon [26]).
  • Currently, M EGALON is targeted to a system with 8–16 nodes as M EGALON’s local memory footprint grows linearly with the number of nodes.
  • In fact, with a 200MB SCR, M EGALON can hold coherence records for up to 50M objects, which is 12× more than what HCMeta can support without churn.

https://dassl-uiuc.github.io/pdfs/papers/megalon.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/hu-jiyu https://dassl-uiuc.github.io/

No Atomics, No Problem. Developing a RAG Pipeline for Shared CXL Memory

IEEE Transactions on Computers, vol. 75, issue 4, pp. 1291-1308 (CXL special issue); DOI 10.1109/TC.2026.3667821 (2026) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. unknown (full text paywalled; abstract confirms shared fabric-attached memory behind an H3 Falcon C5022 CXL switch and famfs page-level interleaving, which implies >=2 memory devices, but the exact device count and model are never visible in any text I could access)

Problem. How to actually build and run a commercial RAG pipeline (vector search + database) on real multi-host SHARED CXL memory today, given that hosts sharing CXL fabric-attached memory form two disjoint cache-coherency domains and current x86 CPUs cannot use atomic operations correctly on shared CXL memory — so standard shared-memory synchronization is unavailable.

Idea. Experience paper: run unmodified DuckDB and Faiss over shared CXL memory via famfs (Fabric-Attached Memory File System), using famfs producer-consumer queues to get synchronization patterns that need no atomics; use famfs page-level interleaving to scale multi-host read throughput from one shared Faiss index; orchestrate shared-memory allocations with Kubernetes in a full vertical RAG deployment.

Testbed. H3 Falcon C5022 CXL 2.0 switch chassis with fabric-attached CXL memory shared by multiple hosts (single- and multi-host experiments; a second host serves queries from the same Faiss index). NVMe SSD used as the demand-paging comparison tier. Host CPU models, DRAM config, CXL memory device model/capacity/count, link widths, and kernel version are in the paywalled full text — could not verify. Authors span Scalemem AS (Oslo), Micron (famfs author John Groves), H3 Platform, and PNNL.

Evaluation. Commercial RAG pipeline over all of English Wikipedia split into 46 million text passages embedded into a high-dimensional embedding space; Faiss vector index + DuckDB passage store placed in shared CXL memory via famfs; subjected to heavy load in single-host and multi-host experiments with both real query workloads and synthetic workloads; multi-host scaling test with a second host serving queries from the same shared Faiss index; Kubernetes-orchestrated full vertical RAG deployment.

Baselines. Same queries run with demand paging from NVMe (mmap/page-cache over NVMe SSD) as the primary baseline; presumably local-DRAM numbers also appear in the full text but I could not verify that.

Key findings. (1) Unmodified DuckDB and Faiss get up to 12x query-latency reduction on real workloads and 40x on synthetic workloads when data lives in shared CXL memory vs demand paging from NVMe. (2) famfs producer-consumer queues provide correct synchronization across the two disjoint coherency domains without any atomic operations on current x86. (3) famfs page-level interleaving gives near-linear throughput scaling when a second host serves queries from the same shared Faiss index. (4) Shared-memory allocations can be orchestrated by Kubernetes in a full vertical RAG deployment.

Limitations. Authors' own admission (abstract): high-performance atomics and locking in shared CXL memory remain open challenges — their solution sidesteps rather than solves synchronization, restricting the model to read-mostly sharing plus lock-free producer-consumer queues. Methodology weaknesses visible from accessible text: baseline is NVMe demand paging (an easy target), not DRAM, so the 12x/40x numbers say CXL beats storage, not how close it gets to memory; multi-host scaling shown only up to a second host; single switch testbed; experience-paper style with no interference/QoS/tail-latency analysis and no per-device measurements evident. Full evaluation details unverifiable (paywalled).

Open questions. Explicitly punted: high-performance atomics and locking mechanisms for shared CXL memory across coherency domains (they discuss it as an open challenge). Obviously untested from what is visible: scaling beyond two hosts, write-heavy shared workloads, multi-tenant isolation and cross-host/cross-device interference on the shared fabric, tail-latency behavior under contention, and comparison against DRAM-resident serving rather than only NVMe paging.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly relevant tooling overlap: the requester already runs famfs on 2 of their 4 CZ120 devices, and this paper is the flagship application study of famfs (co-authored by famfs creator John Groves), including famfs page-level cross-device interleaving — the switch-attached analogue of the requester's root-decoder 2-way interleaved region. However, its setting is multi-HOST sharing through an H3 Falcon C5022 switch, not single-host multi-device management: devices are fused into one shared pool, the baseline is NVMe paging rather than DRAM, and there is no per-device treatment, interference, or QoS analysis. For the candidate direction (per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL interference, tail-aware QoS), this paper is complementary motivation — it demonstrates real RAG/vector-search demand for pooled CXL and near-linear multi-consumer scaling under interleaving — while leaving cross-device contention and independent per-device scheduling completely unexamined, which supports the novelty claim of ObjPlace's next step. It also flags no-atomics synchronization as an open problem, orthogonal to the requester's single-host coherent setup.

Could not verify. Full text (methodology/evaluation sections) — paywalled on IEEE Xplore (bot-blocked) and CSDL (subscriber-only); no arXiv/TechRxiv/OSTI/author-site preprint found, Exact number, model, and capacity of CXL memory devices behind the Falcon C5022 switch (deviceTreatment=multi-device-pooled is inferred from 'famfs page-level interleaving' over shared fabric-attached memory, not from an explicit device count), Host CPU models, DRAM size, CXL link width, kernel version, Whether a local-DRAM baseline is included in addition to NVMe demand paging, Number of hosts beyond the 'second host' scaling experiment

PIPM: Partial and Incremental Page Migration for Multi-host CXL Disaggregated Shared Memory

ASPLOS 2026 (Vol. 2), Session 6D Disaggregated Memory Systems; DOI 10.1145/3779212.3790203 (2026) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 physical (fully simulated; models 1 shared CXL-DSM memory node of 128GB serving 4 hosts in ChampSim)

Problem. Existing page-migration schemes (Nomad, Memtis, HeMem), designed for single-host tiered memory, are inefficient in multi-host CXL 3.x cache-coherent disaggregated shared memory (CXL-DSM): (1) "local gain, global pain" — promoting a hot shared page into one host's DRAM makes other hosts' accesses non-cacheable inter-host accesses that are slower than CXL memory access; (2) kernel-based whole-page (4KB) migration overheads (page-table updates, TLB shootdowns, bulk copies) are amplified across hosts.

Idea. Hardware-based partial + incremental migration co-designed with the CXL multi-host coherence protocol. Partial migration: migrate only the cache blocks exclusively/majority-accessed by one host (majority-vote policy with per-page access counters), keeping the rest in CXL memory. Incremental migration: data moves via ordinary cache-fill/eviction traffic — no extra bulk transfers, no kernel involvement. Architectural support: a two-level hardware remapping table (16KB global remapping cache on the CXL device + 1MB local remapping cache per host RC) and a PIPM coherence protocol (verified with Murphi model checking on MSI) enabling coherent inter-host access to blocks resident in another host's local DRAM.

Testbed. No physical testbed — cycle-level, trace-based timing simulation using ChampSim ("the Championship simulator"), 8x scaled-down config (Table 2): 4 hosts, each 1 single-socket CPU with 4 OoO cores @4GHz (6-wide, 224-entry ROB), 32KB L1, 2MB/core LLC; per-host local DRAM 1x DDR5-4800 channel 32GB; shared CXL-DSM 2x DDR5-4800 channels 128GB; CXL link 50ns latency / 5GB/s per direction (100ns switch case in sensitivity); CXL directory 2048-set 16-way x16 slices. Traces collected with Intel Pintool on real hardware, replayed with 1B-instruction checkpoints, 10B instructions per core.

Evaluation. 13 memory-intensive workloads (Table 1): GAPBS graph kernels SSSP/BFS/PR/CC/BC/TC on Kron (48GB each), XSBench (42GB), PARSEC streamcluster/fluidanimate/canneal/bodytrack (8-18GB), Silo TPC-C (24GB) and YCSB R:W 4:1 (15GB). Heap data shared across the 4 simulated hosts and initially placed in CXL-DSM; code/page tables/stacks private local. Migration baselines assume 20us per-4KB kernel migration cost, 10ms interval, batched TLB shootdowns. Sensitivity: CXL link latency (50 vs 100ns), bandwidth (x8/x16/x32 lanes), remapping-cache sizes, migration threshold (4-16 similar).

Baselines. (1) Native CXL-DSM (no migration); (2) Nomad (recency-based, async kernel migration); (3) Memtis (frequency-based); (4) HeMem (frequency-based); ablations: (5) OS-skew (PIPM policy + kernel migration mechanism), (6) HW-static (PIPM incremental mechanism + static mapping, Intel Flat Mode-like); (7) Local-only / Ideal upper bound (single socket, all-DRAM).

Key findings. PIPM achieves 1.86x average (up to 2.54x) speedup over Native CXL-DSM and reaches 0.73x of the all-DRAM Ideal; graph analytics gain 142-151%, databases (TPC-C/YCSB) 36-53%. Single-host schemes Nomad/Memtis/HeMem give only marginal gains and degrade performance by up to 18% in five workloads. PIPM beats ablations OS-skew and HW-static by 41.7% and 61.1% avg. Local memory hit rate 56.1% avg vs Nomad 26.5%/Memtis 31.0%/HeMem 28.1%; inter-host access stalls drop to 1.5% of execution time. Memory frugal: 7.3% of footprint remapped at page level, only 5.5% actually moved at line level (vs 25% fixed for HW-static). With 100ns switch latency PIPM gains an extra 55.7% avg. Hardware cost: 16KB global + 1MB per-host remapping caches; coherence protocol verified deadlock-free/SC with Murphi.

Limitations. Entirely simulation-based (ChampSim trace-driven, 8x scaled-down: 4 cores/host, 5GB/s CXL links) — no real CXL silicon, no FPGA prototype, and requires new hardware in both the CXL device and each host's root complex plus a modified coherence protocol, so it is not deployable on any existing platform. Only 4 hosts simulated; scalability to more hosts is argued qualitatively in the Discussion (majority-vote "continues to suppress performance-degrading migrations") but not measured. Single shared CXL memory node modeled — no multi-device CXL pool, no device-level interference or QoS. Assumes CXL 3.x multi-host hardware coherence, which has no shipping hardware. No tail-latency or multi-tenant isolation evaluation; migration baselines' kernel costs are assumed constants (20us/4KB) rather than measured on the simulated system.

Open questions. Behavior at larger host counts and with CXL switches/fabrics beyond a latency knob; interaction with multiple CXL devices or pooled/interleaved regions; software interfaces for application-directed line-level migration (mentioned as future possibility in Related Work); how PIPM composes with capacity pressure in local DRAM (eviction policy under contention is lightly treated); real-hardware validation once CXL 3.x coherent multi-host devices exist; multi-tenant/QoS effects of hardware migration.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Useful to cite as the migration-granularity counterpoint to page-level placement: PIPM's core evidence that whole-4KB migration is wasteful and that cache-block/incremental movement plus a placement policy aware of cross-host sharing wins (1.86x avg) supports ObjPlace's argument that placement granularity and access-pattern awareness matter more than hotness alone. However, it is pure ChampSim simulation of a hypothetical CXL 3.x coherent multi-host system with new hardware in the device and root complex — nothing runs on real silicon like the requester's CZ120 testbed, and it models one shared CXL memory node, not multiple independently schedulable devices. For the per-device direction, PIPM is orthogonal prior art (multi-host sharing vs single-host multi-device); its 'local gain, global pain' framing has a loose analog in cross-device interference but the paper offers no data on device-level bandwidth contention, QoS, or tail latency. Cite it in related work on migration granularity and hardware tiering (alongside Intel Flat Mode, Nomad, Memtis), not as a competing evaluation.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We model the multi-host CXL-DSM architecture using a cycle-level, trace-based timing simulator [1, 24]. The simulator configuration is detailed in Table 2.
  • using the Championship simulator [1, 24] configured as a four-host CXL-DSM
  • Table 2. Scaled-down System Configuration. Architecture: 4 hosts, 1 single-socket CPU each host ... DRAM: 2x DDR5-4800 channels 128GB CXL-DSM; 1x DDR5-4800 channel 32GB DRAM per host ... CXL link latency: 50ns, bandwidth: 5GB/s (per direction)
  • We use an 8x scaled-down setting as the default configuration (32 cores => 4 cores per host, 64 GB/s (40 GB/s effective [15]) => 8 GB/s (5 GB/s) over x16 CXL lanes).
  • (1) We first execute the target multi-threaded workloads on real hardware and use Intel Pintool [8] to collect instruction and memory traces for each thread.
  • PIPM outperforms the other schemes across all workloads, achieving an average performance of 1.86x and 0.73x (up to 2.54x and 0.94x) compared to Native CXL-DSM and Ideal, respectively
  • we implement an Intel Flat Mode-like baseline (referred to as HW-static), utilizing parts of our design

https://ghuang14.github.io/files/pipm2026asplos.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3779212.3790203

Beluga: A CXL-Based Memory Architecture for Scalable and Efficient LLM KVCache Management

SIGMOD 2026 (arXiv:2511.20172; Alibaba) (2025) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: corrected confidence: high

CXL devices. 32 CXL memory devices (32 × 256 GB DDR5-4800, 8 TB total) in a switched pool behind 2 × XConn XC50256 CXL 2.0 switch chips; the paper explicitly states interleaving across '32 memory devices', contradicting the analyst's 'never states the number' — though it does not break down controllers per device

Problem. LLM serving needs huge KVCache capacity beyond GPU HBM; existing offload/pooling paths use RDMA, which "was fundamentally designed as a networking protocol, not a memory bus," causing extra data movement through host memory, high control-path overhead (request prep, completion polling, cross-component synchronization), and high TTFT for cache reuse.

Idea. Beluga: a rack-scale CXL 2.0 switched memory pool (XConn XC50256, up to 16 servers sharing 8 TB) exposed to each host as DAX-mapped contiguous physical address space, with both CPUs and GPUs directly load/store or P2P-DMA accessing pool memory (cudaMemcpy P2P + custom CUDA kernels for fine-grained non-contiguous access), software interleaving at 2 MB granularity across CXL devices for bandwidth, a lightweight CXL-based RPC (shared-memory producer-consumer) replacing RDMA for metadata/control, and a KVCache management layer (Beluga-KV) integrated with vLLM. Claimed first system letting GPUs directly access large switched CXL memory pools.

Testbed. Per server: 2 × Intel Xeon Platinum 8575C, 2 TB DRAM (32 × DDR5-4800 64 GB), 8 × NVIDIA H20 (96 GB), 2 × PCIe 5.0 x16 CXL adapters; Ubuntu 22.04, kernel 6.2.0-1015. Pool: XConn XC50256 dual-chip CXL 2.0 switch (256 PCIe 5.0 lanes, 2 TB/s forwarding per chip), memory box with 8 TB (32 × DDR5-4800 256 GB), up to 16 servers, 1 TB/s aggregate. Measured: CPU read from CXL 46.2 GB/s per adapter; GPU access 26 GB/s (Root Complex bottleneck vs 55.4 GB/s theoretical); CXL-RPC roundtrip 2.11 us vs RDMA-RC 8.39 us.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks (CPU/GPU latency and bandwidth to CXL pool vs DRAM/RDMA, 16 KB ops); end-to-end LLM serving with vLLM (V1, v0.8.5) + LMCache v0.3.1 on Qwen-32B (default, unquantized) and Llama-3.1-8B using LV-Eval long-context QA traces (>15K tokens; LV-Eval-2K/4K/8K variants); KVCache transfer/read comparisons against RDMA-based KV pools.

Baselines. MoonCake (v3.2), NVIDIA Dynamo (v0.4.1), vLLM V1 v0.8.5 with LMCache v0.3.1, local-DRAM and RDMA-based memory pool configurations.

Key findings. GPUs/CPUs can use a switched CXL 2.0 pool at near-local efficiency: CXL-RPC roundtrip 2.11 us vs 8.39 us RDMA-RC; GPU H2D copy from CXL 11.73 us vs 10.32 us from CPU DRAM (16 KB). End-to-end: 89.6% TTFT reduction and 7.35x throughput improvement in vLLM vs RDMA-based cache-hit path; 95.9% KVCache read latency reduction for sparse access; 4.79x improvement over MoonCake. GPU bandwidth to pool capped at 26 GB/s per adapter by Root Complex (vs 46.2 GB/s CPU reads), mitigated via multiple adapters and 2 MB software interleaving.

Limitations. Authors admit CXL-RPC "provides lower reliability guarantees compared to RDMA transport protocols" (reliability pushed to upper layers) and that GPU access is Root-Complex-bottlenecked (26 GB/s vs 55.4 GB/s theoretical). Methodological: exotic testbed (XConn switch + custom 8 TB memory box) that few labs can reproduce; number and model of the actual CXL memory controllers never disclosed; pool treated as one flat interleaved address space — no per-device measurement, no study of inter-host or inter-device interference/contention on the shared pool; no multi-tenant QoS/isolation evaluation; hardware interleaving deferred to future Granite Rapids CPUs; evaluation centered on one workload family (LV-Eval) and cache-hit scenarios.

Open questions. Contention and tail latency when many of the 16 servers hammer the shared pool concurrently; per-device bandwidth management and placement inside the pool; fault tolerance/memory failure blast radius of a shared 8 TB pool; CXL 3.x coherent sharing; behavior beyond long-context QA traces; RAS/reliability of the load/store fabric they explicitly punt to upper layers.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Beluga is the strongest recent datapoint that industry treats multi-device CXL as one flat pooled address space (2 MB software interleave, unified space control) — exactly the design point the requester's candidate direction argues against. It never measures per-device behavior, cross-device or cross-host interference, or tail latency under contention on the shared pool, so a per-device-scheduling / CXL-to-CXL interference / multi-tenant QoS paper on the requester's 4x CZ120 direct-attach testbed is complementary rather than scooped. Its hardware class differs (switched XConn 2.0 pool shared by GPUs across servers vs direct-attached expanders on one EPYC host), so its latency/bandwidth numbers (46.2 GB/s CPU, 26 GB/s GPU per adapter) are useful comparison points for motivating that even pooled deployments are bandwidth-constrained and interference-prone. Cite it as motivation ('production systems fuse devices into one pool and ignore device-level scheduling') and for the LLM KVCache-on-CXL use case.

Could not verify. Exact number and vendor/model of CXL memory controller devices inside the 8 TB memory box (paper gives only DIMM count: 32 x 256 GB), Number of servers actually used in end-to-end evaluation (switch supports up to 16), Official SIGMOD 2026 acceptance status (arXiv page indicates SIGMOD'26; not cross-checked against proceedings)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • At its core, the switch is equipped with two chips (XConn XC50256). Each chip provides 2 TB/s of forwarding capacity over 256 PCIe 5.0 lanes.
  • The underlying CXL switch connects up to 16 servers to an 8 TB memory pool with 1 TB/s of total bandwidth
  • the CXL memory pool itself comprises a switch node and a separate memory box
  • CXL MemPool: 8 TB (32 × DDR5, 4800 MT/s, 256 GB); CPU: 2 × Intel Xeon Platinum 8575C; GPU: 8 × H20 (96 GB); OS: Ubuntu 22.04, kernel 6.2.0-1015 (testbed Table 2)
  • Beluga then leverages this foundation by having each host manage the CXL memory pool in Direct Access (DAX) mode.
  • Beluga provides a unified address space with simplified space control
  • Beluga interleaves data across multiple CXL memory devices
  • software interleaving at a 2MB granularity
  • 89.6% reduction in Time-To-First-Token (TTFT) and 7.35x throughput improvement
  • CXL-RPC...provides lower reliability guarantees compared to RDMA transport protocols...Our implementation relies on upper-layer mechanisms for reliability assurance.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.20172 https://arxiv.org/html/2511.20172v1

Oasis: Pooling PCIe Devices Over CXL to Boost Utilization

SOSP 2025 (2025) real-asic single-device verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one multi-headed CXL 2.0 memory pool device, 256 GB DDR5-4800, connected to two hosts via x8 lanes each; vendor/model undisclosed for confidentiality)

Problem. PCIe devices (NICs, SSDs) are heavily underutilized in cloud datacenters — Azure data shows P99.99 rack NIC-bandwidth utilization of allocated bandwidth is only 20%, 33% of SSD capacity and 27% of NIC bandwidth are stranded, and redundant NICs for failover lower utilization further. Existing pooling options (PCIe switches, RDMA disaggregation) are expensive or inflexible.

Idea. Since CXL memory pools are already being deployed for memory TCO reasons, reuse them as a data path to pool PCIe devices entirely in software: a shared (non-cache-coherent) CXL memory region carries I/O buffers (DMA'd by devices, load/store by CPUs) and a novel message channel optimized for non-coherent CXL memory (careful cache-line invalidations to "unblock" prefetching, 29x message throughput vs cache-bypass baselines). A common datapath plus per-device-class engines (network engine implemented; storage engine designed only) and a pod-wide allocator handle mapping, load balancing, and NIC failover across host boundaries.

Testbed. Main testbed: two AMD 5th-gen EPYC hosts (EPYC 9825 144-core and EPYC 9015 8-core), each 768 GB DDR5-5600 local memory + 100 Gbit Mellanox ConnectX-5 NIC; both connected via x8 CXL 2.0 lanes to ONE shared multi-headed CXL 2.0 memory device with 256 GB DDR5-4800 (vendor confidential; raw latency undisclosed, reported as 2.29x local DDR5 on EPYC). Ubuntu 24.04, Linux 6.8.0, CXL exposed as DAX via hmem (CXL driver patched out because it cannot parse the device's HDM decoder). Microbenchmarks used a dual-socket Intel Xeon 8592 with each socket connected to the same CXL pooling device via x8 lanes (non-coherent ranges). Network: 100 Gbit Arista 7060X switch; Xeon 8592 + CX5 as load driver.

Evaluation. Message-channel microbenchmarks (16 B messages, throughput/latency across coherence strategies) on the dual-socket Xeon; end-to-end NIC pooling on the two-AMD-host + one-CXL-device pod: web applications (Python HTTP server, Rust Rocket, nginx, Apache Tomcat), memcached (75 B and 1500 B packets, P50/P90/P99 latency vs throughput), replay of a production Azure network packet trace for utilization multiplexing, and NIC-failure injection for failover timing.

Baselines. Junction (state-of-the-art kernel-bypass container networking) using a host-local NIC; baseline non-coherent message channels using cache-bypass (CLFLUSHOPT/MFENCE + CLWB, non-temporal ops) from prior work (e.g., HydraRPC-style designs); implicit comparison to PCIe-switch and RDMA-based pooling on cost/flexibility grounds.

Key findings. Oasis datapath adds only 4-7 us per packet (single-digit us) vs typical 50-110 us cloud network latency; its non-coherent CXL message channel achieves 29x higher throughput than cache-bypassing designs (baseline reached only 3.0 MOp/s vs the 14.0 MOp/s needed for 7.0 MOp/s I/O); replaying a production Azure trace, Oasis improves aggregated NIC bandwidth utilization by 2x with negligible overhead; transparent NIC failover completes in 38 ms. Also reports CXL idle load-to-use latency 2.29x local DDR5 on AMD 5th-gen EPYC, and that MWAIT/MONITOR-based polling optimizations from HydraRPC do not work on a real non-coherent CXL 2.0 pool.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions (Section 6): single-threaded datapath per frontend/backend driver underutilizes high-bandwidth devices; evaluation matches only a single 100 Gbps NIC to a x8 CXL link ("production systems use eight times as many CXL lanes... Unfortunately, these systems are not available to us"); single Arista switch "vastly oversimplifies production networks" (no VIP/DIP load-balancer integration); load rebalancing only at instance start/failure; CXL link/cable failures not tolerated (redundant pod topologies left to future work); storage (SSD) engine is designed but NOT implemented — only NIC pooling is evaluated; raw CXL device latency undisclosed. Methodological limits: only 2 hosts and 1 CXL pool device (no scale-out pod), one-way message-channel measurements done on a dual-socket single host rather than two hosts, no multi-tenant QoS experiments (QoS via Intel RDT only discussed).

Open questions. Sharded multi-channel datapath scaling beyond one I/O core; SSD/storage-engine implementation and other device classes (accelerators); resilient CXL pod topologies for CXL link failures; QoS/bandwidth partitioning when bandwidth-hungry CXL uses (e.g., OLAP) share links with Oasis; advanced burstiness-exploiting load-balancing policies; integration with cloud load balancers (VIP-to-DIP remapping); behavior with CXL 3.0 back-invalidation coherence (which vendors doubt will ship); larger pods with multiple MHDs / more hosts.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Orthogonal to the requester's tiering/placement work: Oasis uses one multi-headed CXL pool device as a cross-host I/O datapath, not as tiered memory, and never manages multiple CXL devices independently — so it neither anticipates nor conflicts with a per-device placement / CXL-to-CXL interference direction on the 4x CZ120 box. Useful pieces: their finding that non-coherent CXL access patterns (invalidation-aware prefetching) dominate performance, their 2.29x DDR5 latency number on 5th-gen EPYC (vs the requester's 1.53x on Turin), and their explicit punt on "QoS control for CXL bandwidth" when co-located uses saturate links — which directly names the interference/QoS gap the requester's candidate direction targets. It is also evidence that SOSP-level CXL papers can succeed with a single device, meaning per-device multi-CXL management remains open territory.

Could not verify. Exact vendor/model of the multi-headed CXL 2.0 pooling device (paper withholds it for confidentiality; classified real-asic based on the paper's 'off-the-shelf CXL 2.0 devices' / 'commodity CXL memory pools' wording, but ASIC-vs-FPGA is not explicitly stated), Raw CXL access latency of the device (deliberately undisclosed; only normalized 2.29x vs DDR5 given)

Verifier note. Primary source read directly: downloaded https://drkp.net/papers/oasis-sosp25.pdf and extracted full text with pdftotext this session. All three analyst quotes match the paper verbatim (modulo the '×8' unicode glyph). Attempted refutations: (1) searched for emulation/NUMA-emulation — none found ('emulat' has zero occurrences); (2) hunted for a second testbed — found footnote 5's dual-socket Xeon 8592 microbenchmark host, but it attaches to the same single CXL pooling device, so it does not change the device count; (3) checked for channel/DIMM-as-device miscounting — the paper consistently says "one CXL 2.0 memory device"/"one CXL device". One small imprecision in the analyst's paraphrase: the paper's confidentiality statement withholds the raw access latency, not explicitly the vendor/model (those are just never named) — this does not affect any verdict.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Our evaluation setup consists of two AMD hosts and one CXL 2.0 memory device. The first host uses an AMD EPYC 9825 144-core CPU, while the second uses an AMD EPYC 9015 8-core CPU.
  • Each host has 768 GB of local DDR5-5600 memory and a 100 Gbit Mellanox ConnectX-5 (CX5) NIC. The two hosts are connected to one CXL device, each with ×8 lanes. The CXL device has 256 GB DDR5-4800 memory, which is exposed to both hosts. Due to confidentiality requirements, we do not disclose the raw access latency of the CXL device.
  • Oasis is the first end-to-end prototype that empirically supports this argument with off-the-shelf CXL 2.0 devices, delivering immediate benefits in total cost of ownership (TCO), near-native performance, and tens-of-milliseconds failover time.
  • We run Ubuntu 24.04 with Linux 6.8.0 on both hosts and configure the kernel to expose CXL memory as DAX devices [33]. Because the Linux CXL driver cannot recognize the HDM decoder of our CXL device, we apply a small patch to disable the CXL driver and instead use hmem [15] to expose the CXL memory as DAX devices.
  • The system is an Intel Xeon 8592 dual-socket server, with each socket connected to the CXL pooling device via ×8 lanes. The CXL memory ranges exposed to the two sockets are not cache coherent.
  • Our evaluation shows that Oasis improves the NIC utilization by 2× and handles NIC failover with only a 38 ms interruption.
  • The cost of Oasis is an added 4–7 µs of overhead per network packet, a modest overhead compared to typical cloud network latencies of 50–110 µs.
  • our own measurements on AMD 5th Gen EPYC show 2.29× higher latency.

https://sigops.org/s/conferences/sosp/2025/accepted.html https://drkp.net/papers/oasis-sosp25.pdf

Octopus: Enhancing CXL Memory Pods via Sparse Topology

arXiv preprint (cs.AR), arXiv:2501.09020; authors from Columbia, Microsoft Azure, UW-Madison, UW (incl. D. Berger, M. Hill) (2025 (v1 2025-01-15; v3 2026-04-03)) real-asic multi-device-independent verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 3 (three pre-production multi-ported pooling devices/MPDs, each with two x8 CXL 2.0 ports, shared across three servers); scaled-up results with 25/64/96 servers and 4-port MPDs are simulation only

Problem. Existing CXL memory-pooling pod designs either fully connect a small number of servers to multi-port devices (limiting pod size and pooling benefit) or route through CXL switches (adding ~220 ns per hop and enough CapEx that switch cost can outweigh pooling savings). Pods must also serve two conflicting goals: memory pooling (wants broad reachability) and low-latency pairwise communication (wants overlap on the same device).

Idea. Octopus builds switchless, sparsely connected CXL pods from low-port-count (e.g., 4-port) multi-ported devices (MPDs): each MPD connects a carefully chosen subset of servers. Servers are grouped into "islands" with high overlap for low-latency intra-island shared-memory communication/RPC, while cross-island links are laid out to maximize pooling reachability. Firmware interleaving across MPDs is disabled and each CXL port is exposed as a distinct NUMA node so software can target specific MPDs for capacity balancing and sharing.

Testbed. Prototype: three servers, each AMD EPYC 9825 (Turin), local DDR5-5600, 100 Gbit Mellanox CX5 NIC, Arista 7060X 100G switch, Ubuntu 24.04. Three pre-production MPDs each with two x8 CXL 2.0 ports (N=2); custom soldered risers route CXL control signals; only 16 of 64 CXL lanes per CPU used (two x8), forming a simplified island X=N=2 where each server pair shares one MPD. Measured MPD: 267 ns read latency (vs 233 ns CXL expansion device, ~115 ns local DRAM), 24.7 GiB/s read / 22.5 GiB/s write per x8 link (mixed R/W only 28.8 GiB/s due to MPD firmware issue). MPD vendor and capacity not stated; ASIC vs FPGA not explicitly stated (pre-production silicon implied; commercial 2-port MPDs cited from AsteraLabs, Marvell, xFusion).

Evaluation. Four parts: (1) three-server hardware prototype (§6.2): RPC latency/bandwidth over shared CXL memory, all-gather bandwidth, and application slowdown on cloud workloads — Ruby YJIT (web), YCSB on Redis and Memcached, TPC-C on Silo, TPC-H on PostgreSQL; (2) scaled-up simulations (§6.3) of 25/64/96-server pods with 2-8-port MPDs (focus N=4), replaying Azure VM memory-demand traces collected over two weeks in Dec 2024 from thousands of cloud VMs; (3) physical layout/cabling validation under 1.5 m copper-cable constraints (§6.4); (4) cost modeling (§6.5).

Baselines. In-rack RDMA (100G, ToR), CXL switch topologies (e.g., XConn XC50256 class), fully-connected MPD pods (256B firmware interleaving), other sparse MPD/expander topologies (Jellyfish-style), and CXL expansion devices as latency reference.

Key findings. On real hardware, Octopus RPCs are 3.2x faster than in-rack RDMA and 2.4x faster than CXL switches (median ~1.2 us), and 4.5x lower latency than other sparse MPD topologies with good pooling savings. MPD adds only 267 ns vs 233 ns for an expansion device, so application slowdowns closely match plain CXL expansion, whereas a CXL switch would increase the slowdown of 25% of applications by 3x. Assuming 10% tolerable slowdown, 65% of memory can be pooled via MPDs vs 35% via switches. Per-server bandwidth saturates at 22.1 GiB/s with both sharers active. In simulation (up to 96 servers, 4-port MPDs), pooling savings pay for device costs: 5.4% overall server cost reduction with existing CXL expansion, 3.0% without; CXL switches always net a cost increase. Open source at github.com/yuhong-zhong/Octopus-CXL-Pod.

Limitations. Authors' own (§7): cross-island communication traverses two MPD hops (higher latency/lower bandwidth); broadcast-style collectives favor full connectivity and need multi-hop reads or replication in Octopus; suboptimal pooling under extremely skewed demand (per-server reachability bounded by MPDs it connects to); irregular cabling harder to manage; topologies are specific to X/N and re-optimization for other port counts is left to future work. Methodological: hardware prototype is only 3 servers with N=2 MPDs (the target N=4, X=8 design is never built); all pooling-savings and cost numbers come from trace-driven simulation and a cost model with assumed device prices; MPD vendor/capacity undisclosed and pre-production firmware limits bidirectional bandwidth; mixed R/W bandwidth artifact attributed to firmware bug; no multi-tenant isolation or QoS experiments.

Open questions. Re-optimizing island/cross-island port splits for other X,N configurations (explicitly left to future work); combining MPD islands with a small switch fabric for global reachability; optical CXL links; security of shared MPDs; memory-allocation policy on distinct per-port NUMA nodes; multi-socket servers; behavior at production scale (8 ports, 400 Gbit NICs) beyond the 4x-scaled-down prototype; contention/interference between the two servers sharing an MPD is only touched via bandwidth saturation, not tail latency or QoS.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant precedent for the requester's candidate direction: Octopus explicitly disables cross-device interleaving and exposes each CXL port as a distinct NUMA node so software can target individual devices — the same per-device-independence stance the requester is considering for their 4x CZ120 box (where 2 devices are currently interleaved into one node). Octopus is a topology/cost paper, not a placement/QoS paper: it does not study CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, tail latency under co-runners, or multi-tenant isolation on a device, leaving exactly the gap the requester's interference measurements (2.0-2.13x victim slowdown) and ObjPlace could fill at the single-host level. Its measured MPD numbers (267 ns, ~24.7 GiB/s per x8 port saturating quickly) corroborate the requester's CZ120 observations that per-device bandwidth is the scarce, contended resource. Also useful as motivation/citation that industry pods will present many small independently addressable CXL devices per host, making per-device scheduling a forward-looking abstraction.

Could not verify. Whether the pre-production MPDs are ASIC or FPGA-based is not explicitly stated (classified real-asic based on 'pre-production' silicon description and 267 ns latency; vendor undisclosed), MPD memory capacity per device is not stated, Exact kernel version (only 'an updated Ubuntu 24.04' given)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We have access to three pre-production MPDs with two ×8 CXL 2.0 ports (i.e., N=2). To connect them, we soldered custom risers to route CXL control signals to all ports. We use three servers each with an AMD EPYC 9825 Processor, local DDR5-5600 memory, and a 100 Gbit Mellanox CX5 NIC.
  • Each server has 64 CXL lanes, but we connect only 16 lanes (two ×8 lanes) per CPU to form a simplified Octopus island (X=N=2): each pair of servers shares one MPD.
  • Octopus disables this interleaving in firmware and exposes each CXL port as a distinct NUMA node (Figure 9(b)). This lets software target a specific MPD for capacity balancing and for sharing with peer servers that connect to the same MPD.
  • On our lab system, our N=2 MPD offers 267 ns read latency and 28 GiB/s read bandwidth per port.
  • Given that MPDs have higher latency than expansion devices (267 ns vs. 233 ns, measured with our hardware), we evaluate slowdown across a broad set of cloud workloads: web (Ruby YJIT), key-value stores (YCSB on Redis and Memcached), and databases (TPC-C on Silo, and TPC-H ...
  • On hardware, Octopus RPCs are 3.2× faster than in-rack RDMA and 2.4× faster than CXL switches. In simulation, Octopus achieves net server cost savings of 3–5.4% whereas CXL switches result in a net cost increase.
  • We simulate scaled-up Octopus topologies of 25, 64, and 96 servers based on 4-port MPDs.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.09020 https://arxiv.org/html/2501.09020v3

Unlocking the Potential of CXL for Disaggregated Memory in Cloud-Native Databases

SIGMOD-Companion '25 (industry track), Berlin, DOI 10.1145/3722212.3724460 (2025) real-asic multi-device-pooled verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 8 CXL-attached DDR5 memory modules (2TB total) behind 1 XConn XC50256 CXL 2.0 switch ASIC; the deployment topology figure shows a "CXL memory box" scaling to "Up to 64 memory devices (16TB)" with two switches, but the evaluated setup is 8 modules / 2TB on one switch

Problem. RDMA-based disaggregated memory in commercially deployed cloud-native databases (PolarDB) suffers page-granular read/write amplification (up to ~4x measured), RDMA NIC bandwidth saturation under multi-instance consolidation, slow ARIES-style crash recovery plus buffer-pool warm-up, and requires database-managed cache coherency for multi-primary data sharing.

Idea. PolarCXLMem: the first CXL-switch-based disaggregated memory system, built on the XConn CXL 2.0 switch, that places the ENTIRE database buffer pool (data + metadata) directly in switch-attached shared CXL memory — eliminating the tiered local-buffer structure and its amplification. On top of it: PolarRecv, an instant-recovery scheme exploiting the switch/memory's independent power supply (buffer pool survives host crash, restored without full redo-log replay), and a cache-line-granularity (64B) software cache-coherency protocol for multi-primary PolarDB-MP data sharing (CXL 3.0 hardware coherency not yet available).

Testbed.
