XConn XC50256 CXL 2.0 switch (256 lanes, 2TB/s switching capacity) connecting 8 DDR5 memory modules totaling 2TB in a CXL memory box with independent PSU; CXL memory exposed to each host as devdax via daxctl and accessed via mmap. Hosts: dual Intel Xeon Platinum 8575C (2.8GHz), 192 vCPUs per machine, CentOS-7 (kernel version not stated). RDMA baseline network: 100Gbps Mellanox ConnectX-6. Measured latency (MLC): DRAM 146ns local / 231ns remote; CXL without switch 265.2/345.9ns; CXL with switch 549/651ns (3.76x local DRAM).

Evaluation. PolarCXLMem integrated into production PolarDB (single-primary pooling) and PolarDB-MP (multi-primary sharing). Workloads: Sysbench (point-select, range-select, read-write, point-update), TPC-C, TATP, default configs, deliberately memory-bound (whole dataset fits in disaggregated memory; storage-I/O-bound scenarios excluded). Pooling: 1-12 database instances (16 vCPUs each) on one 192-vCPU host sharing the pool; RDMA baseline uses 30% local buffer pool. Sharing: 8/12/15-node clusters with 0-100% shared-data fraction. Recovery: random process kills under read-only/read-write/write-only Sysbench.

Baselines. RDMA-based disaggregated memory in PolarDB / PolarDB Serverless (tiered local-buffer + remote memory, LBP 10-100% sweeps); RDMA-based PolarDB-MP for sharing; "vanilla" storage-based ARIES recovery for the recovery study; local-DRAM PolarDB as the upper-bound reference.

Key findings. (1) Pooling: up to 2.1x throughput vs RDMA-based; point-select scales linearly to 3.6M QPS at 12 instances while RDMA saturates at 3 instances / 1.1M QPS (RDMA NIC caps at ~11 GB/s); at 1 instance RDMA bandwidth is 4x CXL bandwidth, i.e. 4x read amplification; PolarCXLMem also cuts memory cost 30% by dropping the local buffer. (2) Latency characterization: switch-attached CXL is 549ns (3.76x local DRAM, ~2.1x direct-attached CXL), yet DB throughput on CXL is near local-DRAM parity; CXL data transfer beats RDMA 5.74x (write) / 6.07x (read) at 64B. (3) PolarRecv: recovery in 2s (read-only) / 8s (read-write) / 15s (write-only) vs 33-110s (RDMA) and 173s (vanilla, write-only); 4.13-13.75x recovery and 5-15x warm-up speedups. (4) Sharing: up to 1.55x throughput vs RDMA-based PolarDB-MP; 33% gain even at 0% sharing; RDMA-MP memory overhead 1.53x at 8 nodes. TPC-C/TATP: 1.3x vs RDMA-10%LBP, 1.1x vs RDMA-30%LBP.

Limitations. Authors acknowledge: CXL switch adds substantial latency (549ns local, 3.76x DRAM — they argue it is negligible only for cloud-DB workloads); CXL 3.0 hardware cache coherency unavailable, so coherency is a software protocol; redo-log buffer still in local DRAM so unflushed logs are lost on crash (handled by tailored policy); evaluation restricted to memory-bound scenarios. Methodological weaknesses: the 8 CXL memory modules are fused into one flat devdax pool — no per-device measurement, placement, or interference analysis; only RDMA-based variants of their own PolarDB stack as baselines (no tiered-CXL or other CXL systems compared); no tail-latency (only average latency) and no QoS/isolation mechanism for the 12 co-located instances contending on the shared pool; single CPU/host generation; kernel and CXL memory-controller vendor unspecified; industry-track paper on proprietary hardware/software, not reproducible.

Open questions. Bandwidth contention and fairness among co-located instances sharing the CXL pool (they show RDMA saturates but never characterize CXL-side interference or per-instance QoS); per-device management inside the memory box; behavior when CXL bandwidth does become the bottleneck at larger scale; hybrid DRAM+CXL placement (they go all-in on CXL, no tiering — whether selective placement beats "everything on CXL" for latency-sensitive structures is untested); CXL 3.0 hardware coherency migration; storage-I/O-bound and mixed scenarios; tail latency under sharing.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Different regime from the requester's box: switch-attached, multi-host shared pool (549ns, 3.76x DRAM) versus their direct-attached CZ120s (277ns, 1.53x DRAM) — useful as the canonical data point that a CXL 2.0 switch roughly doubles load-to-use latency, and that a production OLTP database still runs near DRAM parity at that latency, which pressures latency-centric placement arguments. Crucially, the paper fuses all 8 CXL memory modules into one flat devdax pool and runs up to 12 co-located instances on it WITHOUT any per-device placement, interference characterization, tail-latency analysis, or QoS/isolation — exactly the gap the requester's per-device-scheduling / CXL-to-CXL interference / multi-tenant-isolation direction targets, and their own 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown result is the phenomenon this paper never measures. It is also a strong citation for real multi-device pooled deployments being managed as a unit today, motivating device-granularity resource management as the next step.

Could not verify. CXL memory controller/expander vendor and model inside the CXL memory box (not named in the paper), Linux kernel version (only CentOS-7 stated), CXL link width between hosts and switch (x16 stated only for switch-to-memory-box links)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Test platform. For the CXL devices, we use the XConn XC50256, the world's first CXL 2.0 switch, as introduced in Section 2.3. In the experiment setup, the switch connects 8 DDR5 memory modules, totaling 2TB.
  • To enable shared memory access for all hosts connected to the CXL 2.0 switch, we configure the CXL-attached memory on each host as a dax device in device direct access (devdax) mode using daxctl, allowing straightforward memory access through mmap. Each physical machine is equipped with two Intel Xeon Platinum 8575C CPUs (2.8GHz) and runs CentOS-7.
  • This paper employs the XConn CXL switch [50] for the evaluation, the world's first CXL 2.0 switch, supporting up to 256 lanes with a total switching capacity of 2TB/s.
  • In contrast, our work leverages a state-of-the-art, commercial-grade CXL 2.0 switch, which supports terabyte-scale memory and allows us to explore the full potential of memory disaggregation (e.g., pooling and sharing) in a real-world environment.
  • For local NUMA node access, CXL latency is 3.76× that of DRAM; for remote NUMA access, it is 2.82×.
  • Our evaluation shows that PolarCXLMem can improve throughput by up to 2.1× in pooling scenarios and 1.55× in sharing scenarios compared to RDMA-based systems.
  • The switches are connected via CXL x16 lanes to memory devices organized in a CXL memory box, which supports up to 16TB of memory, forming a pool of the same capacity.

http://cighao.com/papers/PolarDB_CXL.pdf

Pond: CXL-Based Memory Pooling Systems for Cloud Platforms

ASPLOS 2023 (28th ACM International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, Volume 2; DOI 10.1145/3575693.3578835) (2023) numa-emulated simulated-pool verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no physical CXL devices; pool emulated via remote NUMA socket of 2-socket servers; multi-host pool savings from trace-driven simulation; the EMC pool ASIC is a proposed design, not built)

Problem. DRAM is ~50% of Azure server cost and heavily wasted: 3-27% of memory is stranded at the 95th percentile (outliers 36%) because VM cores exhaust before memory, plus much VM memory is never touched. Cross-host memory pooling could cut DRAM provisioning, but must satisfy cloud constraints: opaque VMs (no guest cooperation, no page-fault-based tiering), NUMA-local-like performance, and low host overhead.

Idea. Pond: small CXL memory pools spanning 8-16 sockets (analysis of 100-cluster production traces shows this captures most pooling benefit while an estimated 70-90ns is added over NUMA-local DRAM, vs >180ns for rack-scale). A proposed multi-headed External Memory Controller (EMC) ASIC exposes pool DRAM to multiple hosts via x8 CXL ports; memory is explicitly moved between hosts at 1GB-slice granularity. VMs see pool memory as a zero-core virtual NUMA (zNUMA) node so guest OSes naturally bias allocations to local DRAM. Two ML models drive the local/pool split per VM: a GBM predicting untouched memory from VM metadata/history, and a RandomForest predicting latency sensitivity from core PMU counters; a QoS monitor detects mispredictions and migrates VMs back to all-local memory.

Testbed. Azure production servers plus similarly configured lab servers: either 2x Intel Skylake 8157M sockets with 384GB DDR4 each (measured 78ns NUMA-local / 142ns remote latency = 182%; 80GB/s local / 30GB/s remote bandwidth, "3/4 of a CXL x8 link"), or 2x AMD EPYC 7452 sockets with 512GB DDR4 each (115ns local / 255ns remote = 222%). CXL pool emulated by disabling all cores in one socket and using its memory cross-socket; hyper-threading, turbo-boost, C-states disabled in BIOS. Modified Azure hypervisor instantiates zNUMA topologies; no kernel version stated. No physical CXL device or EMC hardware; CXL port latency (25ns round-trip) cited from Intel measurements, not measured by the authors.

Evaluation. 158 workloads: Redis, VoltDB, TPC-H on MySQL, Spark, GAPBS, SPLASH2x, SPEC CPU, PARSEC, plus 13 proprietary Azure internal workloads; each run fully on emulated pool memory under 182% and 222% latency increases and compared to NUMA-local placement. zNUMA behavior validated with four internal workloads on production nodes (access-bit scans, PMU accesses to zNUMA). ML models trained/evaluated on production resource logs (100-fold validation). End-to-end DRAM savings from a second-granularity simulator replaying VM traces from 100 randomly selected production clusters across 34 datacenters over 75 days.

Baselines. Performance baseline: VMs entirely backed by same-NUMA-node (NUMA-local) DRAM. DRAM-savings baselines: a "Static" policy allocating a fixed pool fraction per VM (3% savings vs Pond's 7-9%) and Pond's individual models vs its combined model. Prediction baselines: threshold heuristics on "Memory bound" and "DRAM bound" PMU metrics vs the RandomForest. Latency/architecture comparison: CXL-switch-based pool designs vs the multi-headed EMC.

Key findings. (1) Pooling across 8-16 sockets captures most benefit; estimated CXL latency adder 70-90ns for that scale, >180ns rack-scale. (2) Under 182% latency, 26% of 158 workloads see <1% slowdown, +17% see <5%, but 21% suffer >25%; under 222%: 23% <1%, 14% <5%, >37% suffer >25%. (3) zNUMA works: the video workload sends <0.25% of memory accesses to the zNUMA node. (4) GBM untouched-memory model is 5x more accurate than a static policy; identifies 25% untouched memory while overpredicting only 4% of VMs; RandomForest can place ~30% of workloads with controlled false positives. (5) End-to-end (Finding #9): at a 16-socket pool, Pond cuts overall DRAM requirements by 9% (182% latency) and 7% (222%), vs 3% for Static, with performance within the 1-5% degradation margin; headline claim: 7% DRAM cost reduction with performance within 1-5% of same-NUMA-node allocations. (6) First public characterization of stranding: 3-27% at p95, outliers 36%.

Limitations. No CXL hardware anywhere in the evaluation: latency is emulated with a remote NUMA socket (142ns/255ns), which the authors note differs from real CXL (FPGA prototypes report higher latency; 70ns ASIC overhead is an industry expectation, cited from Intel, not measured). The EMC pool ASIC is a paper design; no multi-host pooling was ever run on hardware — all cross-host DRAM savings come from trace-driven simulation. Cross-socket emulation gives 30GB/s remote bandwidth and inherits coherence-fabric behavior, so CXL bandwidth contention/interference is not studied at all; no multi-tenant co-runner interference experiments. Authors' own admissions: CXL was "still a year from broad deployment"; ATS/PRI devices for page-fault-based approaches "not yet broadly available"; ~20% of VMs lack history for predictions; opaque VMs mean production performance cannot be directly observed; mispredictions require QoS-monitor mitigation (memory migration costs). Emulated latencies (182%/222%) exceed their own 70-90ns estimate, conservatively conflating pool distance effects.

Open questions. Validation on real CXL silicon of the 25ns-port/70-90ns end-to-end estimates. Bandwidth contention and interference among VMs/hosts sharing EMC ports and DDR5 channels — entirely untested (no QoS/tail-latency-under-contention experiments). Management of multiple discrete CXL devices per host (Pond assumes one logical pool device per host). Dynamic tiering/hot-page migration between local and pool memory (explicitly out of scope; static allocation at VM start only). Behavior at 32-64 socket switched pools. Robustness of ML predictions to adversarial/shifting customer behavior. Memory offlining/reassignment speed at scale (only simulated: <1-10GB/s for 99.99-99.999% of VM starts).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Pond is the canonical cloud-pooling motivation paper but contains zero real CXL: everything is remote-NUMA emulation plus trace simulation, so the requester's real 4x CZ120 testbed directly tests Pond-era assumptions — e.g., measured 277ns load-to-use (1.53x DRAM) sits between Pond's 182% and 222% emulation points, but Pond's 30GB/s cross-socket "pool" bandwidth and lack of any co-runner experiments miss exactly the 2.0-2.13x CXL bandwidth-contention slowdown the requester measured. Pond treats the pool as one flat logical device per host with a VM-granularity static local/pool split; per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and tail-aware multi-tenant QoS are precisely what it leaves open, making it the natural motivating citation and contrast point for the candidate per-device direction. Pond's PMU-based latency-sensitivity RandomForest is also a useful VM-granularity analog (and baseline contrast) to ObjPlace's object-granularity bandwidth-sensitivity signals.

Could not verify. Exact kernel/hypervisor version (paper says modified Azure hypervisor; no kernel version stated), Whether any EMC silicon was ever fabricated (paper describes it as a design; no fabrication claim found)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We run experiments on production servers at Azure and similarly-configured lab servers. The production servers use either two Intel Skylake 8157M sockets with each 384GB of DDR4, or two AMD EPYC 7452 sockets with each 512GB of DDR4.
  • we emulate a single-socket system with a CXL pool on a two-socket server by disabling all cores in one socket, while keeping its memory accessible from the other socket. This memory mimics the pool.
  • On Intel, we measure 78ns NUMA-local latency and 80GB/s bandwidth and 142ns remote latency and 30GB/s bandwidth (3/4 of a CXL ×8 link).
  • To characterize the performance impact of CXL latency for typical workloads in Azure’s datacenters, we evaluate 158 workloads under two scenarios of emulated CXL access latencies: 182% and 222% increase in memory latency, respectively.
  • A new external memory controller (EMC) ASIC implements the pool using multiple DDR5 channels accessed through a collection of CXL ports running at PCIe 5 speeds.
  • We evaluate the effectiveness of pooling using simulations based on VM traces from 100 production clusters.
  • Intel measures round-trip CXL port traversals at 25ns [68] which, when combined with expected controller-side latencies, leads to an end-to-end overhead of 70ns for CXL reads in a basic topology, compared to NUMA-local DRAM reads.
  • Finding #9: At a pool size of 16 sockets, Pond reduces overall DRAM requirements by 9% and 7% under 182% and 222% latency increases, respectively. Static reduces DRAM by 3%.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.00241 https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2203.00241 https://pages.cs.wisc.edu/~markhill/papers/asplos2023_pond.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3575693.3578835

Direct Access, High-Performance Memory Disaggregation with DirectCXL

USENIX ATC (2022 USENIX Annual Technical Conference), pp. 287-294 (short paper) (2022) real-fpga multi-device-independent verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 4 (custom FPGA CXL memory add-in-cards, each with 8 DDR4 DIMMs; 32 DRAM modules total), connected to 4 FPGA RISC-V hosts via a custom CXL 2.0 switch

Problem. Pre-CXL memory disaggregation (page-based swap and object-based KVS systems) requires RDMA-style networks with redundant data copies between host and remote memory plus software-fabric intervention (page-fault handling, kswapd, KV hashing), making remote memory latency orders of magnitude worse than local DRAM. No real CXL product, platform, or OS support existed to integrate CXL memory into a disaggregation/pooling network.

Idea. Build the first real end-to-end CXL 2.0 memory disaggregation system, DirectCXL: passive FPGA-based CXL memory devices (CXL controller + DRAM controllers, no remote compute), in-house RISC-V host processors whose LLC implements a CXL root port, a custom CXL switch with a fabric manager that creates 1:1 virtual hierarchies from each host to a CXL device, and a Linux "CXL software runtime" that splits HDM into cxl-namespaces exposed to applications via mmap — so applications access disaggregated memory with sheer load/store instructions and zero data copies.

Testbed. Fully custom FPGA testbed (no commercial CXL silicon or commercial CPU): 4 in-house RISC-V host processors, each 4 out-of-order cores at 100 MHz implemented on a high-performance datacenter accelerator card, LLC implements CXL RP; 4 custom add-in-card CXL memory blades, each a 16nm FPGA with a from-scratch CXL controller + 8 DRAM controllers and 8 DDR4 DIMMs (64GB) — 32 DRAM modules total; custom CXL 2.0 switch with fabric manager on another accelerator card; PCIe backplane (FlexBus); CXL/PCIe IPs (RP, EP, Switch) at 250 MHz; hosts run Linux 5.13 (RISC-V Linux 5.13.19). RDMA baseline hardware: Mellanox ConnectX-3 VPI 56Gbps InfiniBand RNIC with OFED v4.9.

Evaluation. Host and memory node configured 1:1 for the RDMA-vs-CXL analysis. In-house microbenchmark issuing groups of random RISC-V load/store instructions with varying payload (64B-4KB) and working-set sizes (1K-1G), measured via register-level cycle instrumentation; latency breakdowns (Library/Copy/Network/Memory for RDMA; CPU cache/PCIe/Memory for CXL), latency CDFs, and a speed-scaling estimation to a 1.2GHz CPU / 1GHz CXL-IP clock domain. Real workloads: Facebook DLRM (68GB embedding tables), MemDB in-memory KV database from HERD (16GB, 2M KV pairs, 8M random Gets), and 4 Ligra graph workloads (MIS, BFS, CC, BC; 28GB), with tables/data in remote memory and code/static data in host-local memory.

Baselines. Local (host-local DRAM only, CXL nodes disabled); RDMA raw one-sided transfers (ConnectX-3 56Gbps InfiniBand); Swap = FastSwap (page-based disaggregation over RDMA); KVS = HERD (object-based disaggregation over RDMA) — all ported to the same RISC-V Linux 5.13.19 FPGA testbed.

Key findings. First work to bring CXL 2.0 into a real (FPGA-prototyped) system. 64B load: DirectCXL 328 cycles vs RDMA 2705 cycles (8.3x faster); vs RDMA's best-case 2027 cycles, DirectCXL is 6.2x faster on average; InfiniBand network alone is 78.7% of RDMA latency. DirectCXL best-case latency equals Local (4-24 cycles) when served from CPU caches; its tail is 2.8x worse than Local but the CDF shape tracks Local, while RDMA is 36.8x worse than Local on average. PCIe + CXL IPs account for 77.8% of DirectCXL latency (pure FlexBus 28%), so faster IP clocks help: scaled estimate is 426 ns for a 64B load at 1.2GHz CPU / 1GHz CXL IPs (Table 2: L1/L2 25ns, CXL IPs 239ns, FlexBus 57ns, DRAM controller 105ns). Real workloads: DirectCXL 3x better than Swap and 2.2x better than KVS on average; graph workloads 2.2x better than Swap (Swap moves 4KB pages to read 8B pointers); Swap spends 51.8% of execution in kswapd+FastSwap driver.

Limitations. Authors' own: cycle counts come from a 100 MHz softcore CPU with 250 MHz CXL/PCIe IPs, so absolute latency on production silicon is only estimated (426 ns projection); future work explicitly defers efficient kernel CXL memory management and an SoC silicon implementation. Methodology-evident: everything is in-house FPGA IP (no commercial CXL device, CPU, or switch), and the host is a 4-core RISC-V softcore, so cache hierarchy/MSHR behavior (16 concurrent misses) is unrepresentative of server CPUs; all performance experiments use a 1:1 host-to-device virtual hierarchy, so despite having 4 devices and 4 hosts there are no multi-host or cross-device contention/interference measurements; virtual hierarchies deliberately prohibit HDM sharing between hosts; no NUMA integration, tiering, or placement policy (HDM exposed only via mmap'd cxl-namespaces); no multi-tenant runs, no bandwidth-saturation study, no tail-latency QoS mechanisms; small workload footprints relative to modern servers.

Open questions. Contention and QoS when multiple hosts share the CXL switch or when a device's logical devices serve multiple hosts (the architecture supports it, but it is never evaluated); performance of the design at production clock speeds and on real CXL silicon; kernel-level CXL memory management (they punt to future work) and integration with virtual memory/NUMA tiering rather than mmap namespaces; coherent memory sharing across hosts (explicitly disallowed in their virtual hierarchy design); scaling beyond n=m=4 with cascaded switches; bandwidth behavior (the paper is almost entirely latency-focused).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. DirectCXL is the canonical "real CXL beats RDMA" citation but is architecturally distant from the requester's box: a 100 MHz RISC-V softcore with FPGA CXL devices behind a switch, versus a production EPYC 9745 with 4 direct-attached CZ120 ASICs, so its latency numbers (328 cycles / est. 426 ns) are not comparable to the requester's 277 ns load-to-use measurement. Its most relevant aspect for the per-device-scheduling direction is that its switch fabric manager treats each CXL device as an independently assignable, whole-device resource (1:1 virtual hierarchies, no HDM sharing) — device-granularity partitioning with zero evaluation of cross-device interference, multi-tenant contention, bandwidth saturation, or tail QoS. That gap is exactly what the requester's ObjPlace follow-on could fill with measured CXL-to-CXL co-runner interference on real silicon; DirectCXL serves as positioning/related work (pooling feasibility), not as a competing baseline.

Could not verify. Exact per-DIMM capacity is ambiguous in the text: '8 different DDR4 DRAM modules (64GB)' most plausibly means 64GB per add-in-card, but per-module size is not stated., Figure-only details (exact CDF tail values, some breakdown percentages in Figures 7/9/10) were read from surrounding prose, not from the figures themselves, since figure text extracted imperfectly from the PDF.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • In this work, we prototype DIRECTCXL using many customized memory add-in-cards, 16nm FPGA-based processor nodes, a switch, and a PCIe backplane.
  • Specifically, each CXL device prototype is built on our customized add-in-card (AIC) CXL memory blade that employs 16nm FPGA and 8 different DDR4 DRAM modules (64GB). In the FPGA, we fabricate a CXL controller and eight DRAM controllers, each managing the CXL endpoint and internal DRAM channels.
  • As yet there is no processor architecture supporting CXL, we also build our own in-house host processor using RISC-V ISAs, which employs four out-of-order cores whose last-level cache (LLC) implements CXL RP.
  • We expose four CXL devices (32 DRAM modules) to the four hosts through our PCIe backplane.
  • There are n numbers of compute hosts connected to m number of CXL devices through a CXL switch; in our prototype, n and m are four, but those numbers can scale by having more CXL switches.
  • Note that each CXL virtual hierarchy only offers the path from one to another to ensure that no host is sharing an HDM.
  • Our in-house softcore processors work at 100MHz while CXL and PCIe IPs (RP, EP, and Switch) operate at 250MHz.
  • To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work that brings CXL 2.0 into a real system and analyzes the performance characteristics of CXL-enabled disaggregated memory design.
  • In contrast, DirectCXL only takes 328 cycles for memory load request, which is 8.3× faster than RDMA.
  • For real-world applications, D IRECT CXL exhibits 3× better performance than RDMA-based memory disaggregation, on average.
  • In this subsection, we compare the performance of RDMA and CXL technologies when the host and memory nodes are configured through a 1:1 connection.
  • The authors are extending the kernel for efficient CXL memory management and consider having an SoC silicon as future work of DirectCXL.

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/atc22-gouk.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/atc22/presentation/gouk

Near-data processing architectures (8 papers)

Hardware architectures and observability infrastructure: near-data processing silicon plus NEMO's memory observability and CXL-Bench's shared-memory benchmarking.

Accelerating Retrieval-Augmented Generation (IKS — Intelligent Knowledge Store)

ASPLOS 2025 (30th ACM Int'l Conf. on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems, Vol. 1) (2025) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 physical CXL devices; 1 proposed IKS device modeled via a cycle-approximate simulator (RTL-synthesis timing) and emulated end-to-end as a remote CPU socket (NUMA emulation) on a two-socket Sapphire Rapids server

Problem. Retrieval (nearest-neighbor search over large vector databases) is a growing bottleneck in RAG inference: exact nearest-neighbor search (ENNS) on CPUs over 100s-of-GB corpora is memory-bandwidth-bound and slow, while ANNS (e.g., HNSW) trades accuracy and forces sending more, lower-quality documents to the generative model, inflating end-to-end inference time. GPUs have too little HBM capacity per dollar to hold large vector DBs.

Idea. IKS: a type-2 CXL memory-expander/accelerator card with 512 GB of LPDDR5X (8 packages) and 8 near-memory accelerators (64 PEs each, 1 GHz) that offloads exact vector search. A novel host-accelerator interface built on CXL.cache uses cache-coherent shared-memory offload contexts and doorbell registers (umwait-based notification) instead of MMIO/interrupts; the device's DRAM is simultaneously disaggregated as ordinary memory expansion via CXL.mem so capacity is not stranded.

Testbed. Host testbed: two servers with Intel 4th-gen Xeon (Xeon 4416+, 16 cores @ 2.0 GHz, 37.5 MB L3, 2x AVX-512 FMA), 512 GB DDR5-4000 across 8 channels (256 GB/s), Ubuntu 22.04.3, Linux 5.15.0-88-generic; NVIDIA H100 SXM for GPU baselines; Intel AMX baseline on same CPU. IKS itself is NOT built: NMA RTL synthesized with Synopsys DC at TSMC 16nm; IKS (emulated) modeled at 1.1 TB/s, 69.9 TFlop/s; end-to-end runs emulate IKS as a CXL device on a remote CPU socket of a two-socket Sapphire Rapids machine.

Evaluation. ENNS traces fed into a cycle-approximate simulator (RTL timing + LPDDR5X access timing + PCIe/CXL timing + measured software-stack overheads for top-K aggregation and umwait). End-to-end RAG pipelines: FiDT5, Llama-8B, Llama-70B over Wikipedia-based vector databases (50 GB and 512 GB), Google Natural Questions queries; retrieval quality vs. count studied for exact vs. approximate search. CXL.cache interface benefits measured on a real two-socket Sapphire Rapids machine (coherent shared memory vs. non-temporal writes mimicking CXL.io). Artifact: iks_simulator on GitHub + modified FAISS.

Baselines. CPU ENNS on Intel Sapphire Rapids (FAISS, AVX-512), Intel AMX (BFloat16), NVIDIA H100 GPUs (1-8x), ANNS (HNSW) variants.

Key findings. IKS achieves 13.4-27.9x faster exact nearest-neighbor search over a 512 GB vector database vs. Sapphire Rapids CPU, giving 1.7-26.3x lower end-to-end RAG inference time (up to 2.0-49x vs. Sapphire Rapids accelerators per conclusion). Outperforms 1 H100 GPU by 2.6x and 4.6x at batch sizes 1 and 16 for retrieval. Cache-coherent CXL.cache offload-context communication gives 1.6x higher throughput than non-temporal writes mimicking the PCIe MMIO (CXL.io) datapath. Exact retrieval lets the generator consume fewer, higher-quality documents, so paying for ENNS can reduce total inference time at equal accuracy.

Limitations. No real CXL hardware anywhere in the loop: the device exists only as synthesized RTL + a cycle-approximate simulator, and end-to-end numbers splice simulated retrieval latency into runs where IKS is emulated as a remote NUMA socket — real CXL link behavior (contention, controller queuing, coherence traffic under load) is untested. Single proposed device; no multi-device, multi-tenant, or interference experiments; the memory-disaggregation claim (CXL.mem expansion for co-running apps) is asserted but not evaluated with co-runners. Authors admit: if a dataset is amenable to clustering, the ENNS-ANNS accuracy gap shrinks (weakening the case for exact search), and NMA chip utilization is low for batch sizes under 64. CXL.cache (type-2) devices are essentially unavailable commercially, limiting near-term realizability.

Open questions. Behavior when the same IKS DRAM is concurrently used as CXL.mem expansion by other tenants while NMAs scan it (bandwidth interference between search and expansion traffic) is unaddressed. Scaling across multiple IKS cards per host, cross-device coordination, and QoS are not studied. Real-silicon validation of the CXL.cache doorbell/shared-context interface (measured only via socket-to-socket coherence as a proxy) remains open, as does ANNS-on-IKS and dynamic exact/approximate selection.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to your 4x CZ120 testbed: IKS is a proposed custom type-2 (CXL.cache) near-memory accelerator evaluated purely in simulation/NUMA-emulation, not a study of real CXL.mem expanders, and it never exercises multiple devices or cross-device interference. It is useful as related work in two ways: (1) it quantifies that large-vector-DB search is bandwidth-bound on host memory, which supports your bandwidth-sensitivity-driven ObjPlace placement story for RAG/vector workloads on CZ120s; (2) its "disaggregate the device DRAM so it isn't stranded" claim is asserted without co-runner experiments — exactly the search-traffic-vs-expansion-traffic interference question your victim/co-runner measurements can answer on real hardware. Cite it for RAG-retrieval motivation and for the contrast between simulated NDP proposals and real multi-device CXL characterization; it does not compete with a per-device placement/QoS direction.

Could not verify. Exact number of IKS cards assumed in the 512GB end-to-end configuration is described as one device with 8 LPDDR5X packages/8 NMAs; scaling to multiple cards is discussed conceptually but no explicit multi-card testbed row exists.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • To evaluate the performance of the IKS, we developed a simulator (see appendix A) and fed ENNS traces into it to obtain the retrieval time of IKS. The simulator is a cycle-approximate performance model that utilizes timing parameters from the RTL synthesis, LPDDR5X access timing, PCIe/CXL timing [43; 81], along with calculations of real software stack overhead (top-K aggregation and umwait() overhead). It emulates an IKS as a CXL device running on a remote CPU socket.
  • We implemented the RTL design of the Near-Memory Accelerator (NMA) used in IKS and synthesized it using Synopsys Design Compiler targeting TSMC's 16nm technology node.
  • We ran the experiments on two servers equipped with Intel Xeon 4th generation CPUs and one NVIDIA H100 GPU
  • Table 2: CPU model: Intel Xeon 4416+ 16 cores @ 2.00 GHz ... Memory: 512 GB DDR5-4000 across 8 channels (256 GB/s) ... IKS (emulated) 1.1 TB/s, 69.9 TFlop/s ... GPU Model NVIDIA H100 SXM: 3.35 TB/s, 1979 TFlop/s
  • Our experimental results on a two-socket Sapphire Rapids CPU show that communicating the offload context through cache-coherent shared memory provides 1.6x higher throughput compared with using non-temporal writes that mimic the PCIe MMIO datapath (i.e., CXL.io).
  • We designed IKS, a type-2 CXL memory expander/accelerator
  • OS: Ubuntu 22.04.3, Kernel: Linux 5.15.0-88-generic

https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.15246 https://arxiv.org/html/2412.15246v1 https://arg.csl.cornell.edu/papers/iks-asplos2025.pdf https://doi.org/10.1145/3669940.3707264

Context-Aware Mixture-of-Experts Inference on CXL-Enabled GPU-NDP Systems

arXiv preprint (cs.LG, cs.AR) (2025) simulated single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (simulated: 1x DDR-based CXL-NDP device, 512 GB, alongside 1x H100 GPU)

Problem. MoE LLMs (Mixtral-8x7B/8x22B) have expert weights far exceeding GPU HBM capacity; conventional offloading moves cold expert parameters over PCIe on every activation, making decoding latency dominated by parameter transfer, while CXL-NDP devices have too little compute to run experts at full precision.

Idea. Execute cold experts in place on a CXL-attached near-data-processing (NDP) memory device — moving small activations instead of large parameters — and use prefill-stage expert-activation statistics (which correlate strongly with decoding-stage routing, cosine similarity ~0.89 for Mixtral-8x7B) to drive per-sequence context-aware expert placement (hot experts on GPU HBM, cold on NDP) plus per-expert mixed-precision quantization (1-4 bit) so the compute-limited NDP can keep up.

Testbed. Fully simulated GPU-NDP system, no physical CXL hardware. NDP side simulated on Ramulator following MoNDE's methodology; GPU modeled as NVIDIA H100 (80 GB HBM3, 132 SMs, 989.4 TFLOP/s peak). System composition: 1x H100 GPU + 1x DDR-based NDP (512 GB capacity, 512 GB/s internal bandwidth, 64x 4x4 systolic arrays at 1 GHz), interconnect PCIe Gen4 x16. No real CXL device model, no CPU/kernel details (not applicable — simulation).

Evaluation. Simulation of MoE inference with Mixtral-8x7B (45.1B expert params) and Mixtral-8x22B (135.5B expert params). Accuracy evaluated on MMLU (5-shot), MathQA, HellaSwag, ARC-Easy/Challenge, BoolQ, WinoGrande, PIQA (zero-shot). Performance (decoding throughput, end-to-end speedup) measured in the Ramulator-based GPU-NDP simulator using the same configuration as MoNDE for fair comparison.

Baselines. MoNDE (prior GPU-NDP MoE offloading system, primary baseline), HOBBIT (GPU-only mixed-precision expert offloading), and original full-precision models as accuracy reference.

Key findings. Executing cold experts in place on the CXL-NDP with context-aware placement and mixed-precision quantization yields 6.6-8.7x end-to-end speedup over MoNDE (Mixtral-8x7B, 3-bit), 8.7x decoding throughput at 3-bit (11.2x at 2-bit), up to 18x over GPU-only offloading, with only ~0.13% average accuracy drop at 3-bit. Prefill-stage routing distributions strongly predict decoding-stage expert usage (cosine similarity 0.89), enabling per-sequence expert placement decided at prefill time.

Limitations. Entirely simulation-based (Ramulator NDP simulator, no real CXL silicon or FPGA); the NDP device with 64 systolic arrays is hypothetical hardware that does not exist as a product. Single GPU + single NDP device only — no multi-device, multi-GPU, or multi-tenant scenarios. Interconnect is modeled as PCIe Gen4 x16 rather than an actual measured CXL link, so real CXL latency/contention effects are not captured. Authors state no explicit limitations or future-work section in the conclusion. Batch/serving-level dynamics (continuous batching, real inference server) untested.

Open questions. How the approach behaves on real CXL hardware (production CXL memory has no compute; CXL-NDP devices are not commercially available); scaling to multiple NDP devices and inter-device expert placement; contention when NDP memory is shared with other tenants or with the host; robustness of prefill-to-decode routing correlation for very long generations or shifting contexts; integration with real serving stacks and batching.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's testbed: this paper simulates a hypothetical compute-capable CXL-NDP device in Ramulator, whereas the requester's CZ120s are passive memory expanders with no near-data compute — the core mechanism (executing experts in place) cannot be reproduced on their hardware. It uses a single simulated NDP device, so it says nothing about per-device scheduling, cross-device interference, or multi-device pooling. It is useful mainly as related work for the LLM-on-CXL positioning section (motivating capacity offload of cold model state) and as evidence that the NDP branch of CXL work remains simulation-only, which strengthens a real-hardware characterization/placement paper's novelty claim.

Could not verify. Whether the paper text itself ever labels the interconnect as a CXL link with specific CXL bandwidth (fetch indicated only 'PCIe Gen4 x16' in Table 2), Exact wording of any limitations discussion (fetches found none)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • To enable a fair and intuitive comparison, we adopt the same GPU–NDP system configuration used in MoNDE (kim2024monde) and build our NDP system simulator on Ramulator (kim2015ramulator) following the same methodology.
  • System Composition: 1×H100 GPU + 1×DDR-based NDP
  • Interconnect PCIe Gen4 ×16
  • CXL-attached NDP devices provide large-capacity DDR-class memory and high internal bandwidth...execute computations adjacent to offloaded parameters.
  • Expert activation probability distribution during the prefill stage is often very similar to the distribution observed during decoding

https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04476 https://arxiv.org/html/2512.04476v1

PIM Is All You Need: A CXL-Enabled GPU-Free System for Large Language Model Inference (CENT)

ASPLOS 2025 (ACM ASPLOS'25, also arXiv:2502.07578) (2025) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 physical (simulated system models 8/20/32 custom CXL-PIM devices depending on model size)

Problem. LLM inference (especially decoding with KV caches) has much lower operational intensity than the compute-centric workloads GPUs/TPUs are designed for; it is memory-bandwidth- and capacity-bound, making GPU-based serving expensive in energy and dollars.

Idea. CENT: a GPU-free LLM inference system built entirely from custom CXL memory-expansion devices with near-bank processing-in-memory. Each CXL device integrates a CXL controller, PNM units (exponent/reduction/accumulation, RISC-V cores), and 16 memory chips with two GDDR6-PIM channels each; weights and KV caches live in the CXL-PIM devices and computation happens near the banks, with devices connected via a CXL switch to a host CPU.

Testbed. No physical testbed — fully simulated. Modified Ramulator2 models a CXL device with 32 GDDR6-PIM memory channels; CXL/PCIe interconnect modeled analytically ("analytical model based on the CXL latency and PCIe 6.0 bandwidth"); correctness checked with a functional simulator on generated CENT instruction traces. GPU baseline: 4x NVIDIA A100 80GB with NVLink 3.0 running vLLM. No production CXL silicon (no CZ120 etc.), no kernel version applicable.

Evaluation. Simulation of Llama2 7B/13B/70B inference; queries with 512 prefill tokens + 3584 decode tokens, up to 32K context; configurations of 8, 20, and 32 CXL-PIM devices per model size; throughput, energy, and TCO analyses vs a 4x A100 vLLM baseline at batch size 128.

Baselines. 4x NVIDIA A100 80GB (NVLink 3.0) running vLLM at batch size 128 (throughput-saturating); TCO comparison against GPU rental/ownership cost.

Key findings. CENT achieves geomean 2.3x higher decoding throughput than the 4x A100 vLLM baseline across Llama2 7B/13B/70B, ~2.3-2.9x less energy (2.3x more tokens/Joule per HTML text; abstract claims 2.9x less energy), 5.2x more tokens per dollar, and 2.5x cheaper 3-year owned cost ($0.73/hr vs $1.76/hr). Prefill is 2.5x slower than GPU due to lower peak FLOPS.

Limitations. Entirely simulation (Ramulator2 + analytical CXL/PCIe model) — no real CXL device, no FPGA prototype; the CXL devices are hypothetical custom GDDR6-PIM parts, not commodity expanders. Authors admit: lower memory density of PIM vs standard DRAM, prefill 2.5x slower than GPU, scaling capped at 64 devices per CXL switch (lanes/ports) and by server power supply. No multi-tenant runs, no OS/software placement questions, no measured CXL link contention on real silicon.

Open questions. How a real CXL controller/switch fabric behaves under this traffic (all interconnect behavior is analytical); prefill acceleration (GPU-PIM hybrid); manufacturability/cost of GDDR6-PIM CXL devices; multi-tenancy and QoS across devices; software stack/driver integration on real hosts.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's real 4x CZ120 testbed: CENT is a clean-slate simulated architecture with custom GDDR6-PIM CXL devices, not commodity memory expanders, so none of its numbers transfer to CZ120 latency/bandwidth or interference behavior. It is useful mainly as motivation/related work: it quantifies that LLM decoding is bandwidth-bound and treats a pool of CXL devices as parallel, per-device compute+memory resources (8/20/32 devices, work partitioned across them), which loosely echoes the requester's "each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource" direction. Cite it for the LLM-on-CXL trend and per-device parallelism argument, not for placement, interference, or QoS evidence on real hardware.

Could not verify. Exact per-configuration device counts vs model size mapping (which of 8/20/32 devices maps to 7B/13B/70B), Discrepancy between abstract's 2.9x energy claim and body's 2.3x tokens/Joule figure not resolved from fetched text, Kernel/software stack details (n/a for simulation)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We modify Ramulator2 to model a CXL device containing 32 GDDR6-PIM memory channels
  • We use an analytical model based on the CXL latency and PCIe 6.0 bandwidth
  • 32 CXL devices, driven by a host CPU
  • Each CXL device integrates a CXL controller, PNM units, and 16 memory chips, each equipped with two GDDR6-PIM channels
  • The GPU system contains 4 NVIDIA A100 80GB GPUs equipped with the NVLink 3.0 interconnect

https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.07578 https://arxiv.org/html/2502.07578v2

Scalable Processing-Near-Memory for 1M-Token LLM Inference: CXL-Enabled KV-Cache Management Beyond GPU Limits

arXiv preprint (cs.AR, cs.AI), arXiv:2511.00321 (2025) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 0 physical (simulated: up to 8 CXL-PNM devices per DGX node, and 1-4 standalone PNM nodes in rack-scale configs)

Problem. Multi-million-token LLM context windows blow past GPU HBM capacity; existing CXL non-eviction KV-cache offloading frameworks still pay costly recall transfers moving non-resident KV tokens back over CXL/PCIe into limited GPU memory, throttling long-context decode throughput.

Idea. Put a processing-near-memory (PNM) accelerator inside the CXL memory device so KV-cache token-page selection and attention over offloaded KV pages execute near the LPDDR5X CXL memory instead of recalling data to the GPU. A custom CXL-PNM ASIC (7nm, 1.0 GHz, 32 VPU tiles with FP16 multiplier/comparator arrays and adder/comparator trees) attaches via a CXL 3.0 switch; a hybrid "PnG" steady mode co-executes attention on GPU+PNM, and the design scales from GPU-server-attached PNM to standalone rack-scale PNM nodes.

Testbed. Fully simulated: cycle-level simulator parameterized by synthesized 7nm timing/power models. Modeled system: NVIDIA DGX with up to 8x A100-80GB GPUs (2 TB/s HBM2E), Intel 4th-gen Xeon host; CXL-PNM devices with LPDDR5X-based CXL memory, 512 GB and 1.1 TB/s per module, connected through a CXL 3.0 switch (PCIe 6.0 PHY), x8 lanes per device, x16 host-to-switch. PNM ASIC: 32 VPU tiles, 128-wide multiplier/comparator arrays, 64-length adder/comparator trees, FP16, 1.0 GHz. No kernel version (no real system).

Evaluation. Cycle-level simulation of long-context LLM inference (128K-1M tokens) with Llama3.1-8B/70B/405B; accuracy evaluated on InfiniteBench En.Sum (longbook summarization) comparing eviction (StreamingLLM) vs non-eviction (ArkVale-style) KV management. Configurations sweep GPU count x PNM device count (e.g., 1GPU+8PNM, 2GPU+8PNM) and rack-scale 2x GPU nodes with 1-4 PNM nodes.

Baselines. (i) CXL-memory-expanded DGX baseline (no PNM), (ii) PNM-KV (CXL-PNM-attached DGX offloading KV-cache), (iii) PnG-KV (PNM-KV plus hybrid GPU+PNM steady mode); plus pure GPU scaling and eviction-based StreamingLLM vs non-eviction ArkVale for accuracy.

Key findings. Up to 21.9x throughput improvement and up to 60x lower energy per token vs the CXL-expanded DGX baseline (Llama3.1-70B, 2GPU+8PNM PnG-KV); average 2.7x throughput and 4.9x lower energy for that config; up to 7.3x higher cost efficiency (1GPU+8PNM vs 8-GPU baseline, Llama3.1-8B). Also shows PNM count must exceed GPU count: with equal PNM and GPU counts throughput can fall below baseline because CXL-PNM bandwidth is lower than GPU HBM.

Limitations. Entirely simulation-based — no fabricated silicon, no real CXL device, no real end-to-end system run; timing/power come from synthesis of a hypothetical 7nm ASIC with speculative LPDDR5X CXL modules (1.1 TB/s per module) and CXL 3.0/PCIe 6.0 switches that do not exist as products. Authors admit attention on PNM can be slower than GPU due to lower memory bandwidth, and that matching PNM count to GPU count can underperform baseline; hybrid mode raises instantaneous power. No multi-tenant or interference evaluation; no OS/software-stack coexistence (device is a compute accelerator, not system-ram); accuracy study limited to one InfiniteBench task.

Open questions. How the design behaves on real CXL 2.0/3.0 silicon with realistic (~30-60 GB/s) per-device bandwidth rather than 1.1 TB/s LPDDR5X modules; contention when PNM devices are shared across tenants or co-resident with general-purpose CXL memory expansion; scheduling/placement policy across heterogeneous PNM node counts; host software stack and coherence handling; behavior for prefill vs decode beyond the modeled steady mode.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's testbed: this is a simulated 7nm PNM ASIC with hypothetical 1.1 TB/s LPDDR5X CXL 3.0 devices — nothing runnable on CZ120s, and its per-device bandwidth assumptions are ~30x the requester's measured ~35.5 GiB/s per region. Its useful angle is conceptual: it treats multiple CXL(-PNM) devices as independently countable, schedulable resources (throughput scales with PNM:GPU ratio), which loosely supports the requester's per-device-scheduling direction. It also confirms that recall traffic over CXL is the dominant cost in KV-cache offloading — a motivation ObjPlace-style placement on real hardware can address without custom silicon. Cite as related work on near-memory alternatives, not as a comparable system or baseline.

Could not verify. Author affiliations (Hanyang Univ./Samsung) not shown on the fetched abstract page, Exact simulator name/implementation details beyond 'cycle-level simulator', Whether the modeled multiple PNM devices share one address space or are separately addressed — the text indicates independent devices behind one CXL 3.0 switch but I did not see an explicit interleaving statement, Quotes are as returned by the fetch tool's extraction of the HTML full text; I could not double-check them character-by-character against a PDF

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • For performance evaluation, we use a cycle-level simulator parameterized by synthesized timing and power models of the 7 nm design.
  • Each CXL device is equipped with LPDDR5X-based CXL memory modules, offering a 512 GB capacity and 1.1 TB/s bandwidth per module, interconnected via a CXL 3.0 switch that supports PCIe 6.0 physical layers and device links, each configured with x8 lanes per device. The host connects to the switch with x16 lanes.
  • Each bar group corresponds to a fixed number of GPU cards, varying the number of PNM devices. From left to right, both the number of GPU cards and context length (starting from 128K tokens) increase.
  • NVIDIA DGX appliance populated with up to eight A100-80GB GPUs (2 TB/s HBM2E bandwidth)
  • When the number of PNM devices matches the number of GPUs, throughput can fall below the baseline because the batch-size gain in the FC layers is smaller than the attention throughput loss caused by the lower bandwidth of CXL–PNM.

Enabling Efficient Large Recommendation Model Training with Near CXL Memory Processing (ReCXL)

ISCA 2024 (51st ACM/IEEE Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture), pp. 382-395 (2024) mixed simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 physical CXL devices. Naive-CXL baseline: remote NUMA socket of a real dual-socket Xeon server emulating one CXL memory pool (1 DDR5 channel on socket 1). Proposed ReCXL: simulated only — default config is one ReCXL device with 4 channels x 2 DIMMs (1 TB, 16 ranks); scalability study sweeps up to 128 ranks / multiple simulated devices.

Problem. DLRM training embedding layers need 100s of GB-TBs of capacity and huge irregular memory bandwidth; placing them in CXL memory scales capacity but the CXL link (PCIe 5.0 x8, 32 GB/s — slower than one DDR5-4800 channel at 38.4 GB/s) causes 1.8-3.1x per-iteration slowdown vs local DRAM, making naive CXL memory impractical for training.

Idea. ReCXL: a CXL Type-2 near-memory-processing memory-pool device that executes the ENTIRE embedding training (forward + backward + optimizer update) inside CXL memory using rank-level NMP PUs in the DIMM data buffers plus an 8-core device processor, so only indices/gradients cross the bandwidth-limited CXL link. Software-hardware co-optimizations: dependency-free prefetching (resolves RAW dependencies between gradient updates and next-batch lookups) and fine-grained update scheduling, plus embedding-table placement that splits long vectors across ranks.

Testbed. No real CXL silicon. (1) Real dual-socket server for the naive-CXL baseline: 2x Intel Xeon Gold 6430 32-core @2.1 GHz, 4 ranks x 4x 128GB DDR5-4800 DIMMs; socket 0 = 4 channels local memory, socket 1 = 1 channel "emulated as CXL memory". (2) ReCXL itself: cycle-accurate simulator built from modified Ramulator ("Romulator") + ZSim; simulated device = 4 channels x 2 DIMMs DDR5-4800, 1 TB total, 1x 128-bit SIMD PE per rank-level PU, 8-core @2.4 GHz device processor; CXL latency modeled at 70 ns; DNN-layer times taken from a real A100 GPU and replayed. Synopsys DC 28nm for PU area/power, Micron DDR4 power calculator + CACTI-IO for energy. No kernel version relevant.

Evaluation. DLRM open-source model with 4 configurations RM1-RM4 (RM1-3 from Gupta et al., RM4 an extreme case: 80 tables, 1M rows, dim 128, 160 lookups), training batch sizes 1024-8192 (sensitivity up to 64K), embedding dims 16-256, lookups 10-160. End-to-end training time simulated by offloading embedding ops from ZSim to Ramulator and replaying A100 cycles for MLP layers. Scalability swept 16-128 ranks.

Baselines. (1) Hybrid CPU-GPU baseline (embeddings in host DRAM, CPU processes them; GPU does MLPs); (2) NaiveCXL (embeddings in CXL memory, CPU processing); (3) RecNMP (inference-only rank-level NMP, forward pass only); (4) ReCXL-D (device processor only, no rank-level NMP); (5) ReCXL-I (idealized infinite CXL bandwidth, zero latency).

Key findings. ReCXL outperforms the CPU-GPU baseline by 7.1-10.6x (9.4x avg) and naive CXL memory by 12.7-31.3x (22.6x avg); beats RecNMP by 6.4-8.1x and ReCXL-D by 4.3-7.9x (6.5x avg). NaiveCXL is 1.8-2.9x SLOWER than the CPU-GPU baseline, quantifying the CXL-bandwidth penalty for bandwidth-bound workloads. ReCXL-I (infinite CXL bandwidth) gains only marginally over ReCXL, showing NMP fully hides the link bottleneck. Performance scales with rank count until internal bandwidth exceeds ~20-80x the CXL link bandwidth (r<1 saturation); RM4 still scales at 128 ranks, RM3 saturates at 32. Also reports energy reductions vs all baselines (Fig 16).

Limitations. Entirely simulation-based for the proposed system (custom Ramulator+ZSim integration, not silicon-validated); the "CXL memory" in all real-machine measurements is a remote NUMA socket, and CXL link latency is an optimistic fixed 70 ns model. No real CXL device (no ASIC/FPGA) anywhere in the evaluation. Requires custom NMP hardware in DIMM data buffers plus a device processor and PyTorch/driver stack changes — nothing deployable on commodity CXL expanders. Single-workload class (DLRM embedding training); no multi-tenant runs, no interference or QoS study, no tail-latency analysis. Multiple ReCXL devices are treated as one flat pooled address space; cross-device contention is never measured. Energy uses a DDR4 power calculator despite modeling DDR5.

Open questions. How ReCXL behaves with real CXL controller latencies/variability rather than a fixed 70 ns; sharing the ReCXL pool among multiple training jobs or tenants (isolation, scheduling across devices); CXL 2.0/3.0 switch-fabric effects when "up to 4K nodes" are claimed but never simulated at scale beyond 128 ranks; coherence cost of the Type-2 design; applicability to inference serving or other sparse workloads; interaction with host-side tiering when embedding tables are mixed across host DRAM and ReCXL (mentioned as possible but not evaluated in depth).

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct methodological relevance: ReCXL is a simulated custom NMP hardware proposal with zero real CXL devices, so it offers no techniques deployable on the requester's 4x CZ120 testbed. Its main value is as motivation and related-work positioning: it independently confirms (via NUMA emulation) that bandwidth-bound ML workloads degrade 1.8-3.1x on CXL because link bandwidth (~32 GB/s PCIe5 x8) trails even one DDR5 channel — closely matching the requester's measured ~35.5 GiB/s per-region saturation and 2.0-2.13x co-runner slowdown. Notably, ReCXL treats multiple devices as one flat pooled address space and never studies per-device scheduling, cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS, so it leaves open exactly the per-device independent-resource direction the requester is considering; cite it as the NDP/accelerator end of the design space that ObjPlace-style software placement contrasts with.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • To thoroughly evaluate the performance of our ReCXL architecture, we employ both real machines and simulators. As prior research indicates [10], [45], [61], the performance metrics (bandwidth and latency) of CXL memory closely resemble those of a remote NUMA node in a dual-socket system. Hence, we simulate the naïve CXL memory using a real dual-socket system by allocating memory in the remote NUMA node, with detailed configurations provided in Table I.
  • While for ReCXL, we developed a cycle-accurate simulator by integrating the DRAM simulator Romulator [34] and the host simulator ZSim [57] to evaluate its performance and effectiveness. Romulator is modified to simulate the NMP-featured ReCXL memory pool by adding the rank-level PU and the ReCXL device processor
  • Table I (System Configurations): Host CPU: 2×Intel Xeon Gold 6430 32-core @2.1GHz; DIMM: 4 Ranks ×4 128GB DDR5-4800 DIMM; Memory: socket 0: 4 channels (local memory), socket 1: 1 channel (emulated as CXL memory) — Configuration of ReCXL: NMP-DIMM 4 Ranks ×4 128GB DDR5-4800 DIMM; Device Memory: 4 channels, 2 DIMMs per channel, 1TB in total; NMP PU: 1×128 bit-width SIMD PE per PU, 1 PU per Rank; Device Processor: 8-core @2.4GHz
  • The CXL memory access latency we modeled aligns with a latency of 70 ns as per [60], which adheres to the recommended latency targets in the CXL Specification [5].
  • the ReCXL device can connect to the CXL switch via its PCIe slot. Multiple ReCXL devices can compose a highly scalable CXL memory pool where embedding tables are stored.
  • Evaluation results show that ReCXL outperforms the CPU-GPU baseline and the naïve CXL memory by 7.1× ∼ 10.6× (9.4× on average) and 12.7× ∼ 31.3× (22.6× on average), respectively.
  • the transmission bandwidth of CXL, particularly over PCIe 5.0 ×8, is only 32GB/s, slower than even a single channel of DDR5-4800 memory, which offers 38.4GB/s

https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10609725 https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/stampPDF/getPDF.jsp?tp=&arnumber=10609725 https://dl.acm.org/doi/abs/10.1109/ISCA59077.2024.00036 https://arxiv.org/html/2412.20249v2

PIFS-Rec: Process-In-Fabric-Switch for Large-Scale Recommendation System Inferences

MICRO 2024 (per ACM DL/IEEE listing; arXiv page does not state venue) (2024) mixed simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. characterization testbed: unknown device count ("four channels of DDR4 memory" via CXL, 256GB total; device model/vendor never stated); proposed PIFS system: simulated only (Ramulator 2.0)

Problem. DLRM inference at datacenter scale is bottlenecked by huge, sparsely-accessed embedding tables; placing them in CXL-attached memory pools (Pond-style) incurs high latency (up to ~270 ns per access, ~37% from CXL I/O port transfers and retimer delays) and limited bandwidth, and prior near-data schemes (BEACON, RecNMP) still move data across the fabric or are DIMM-bound.

Idea. PIFS: place lightweight processing cores at the downstream ports of a CXL fabric switch so embedding gather/pooling operations execute near the memory endpoints inside the fabric, with an FM-endpoint extension, on-switch buffering/caching (HTR), and page-management support, so only reduced results traverse upstream to the host.

Testbed. Characterization: dual-socket AMD Genoa, 96 physical cores/socket, 12 DDR5 channels/socket (768GB); CXL memory "enabled through four channels of DDR4 memory resulting in memory capacity of 256GB" — CXL device model, link width, and kernel version not stated. Evaluation of PIFS-Rec itself: cycle-level simulation in Ramulator 2.0 with custom cycle-accurate models (1 ns/clock) of the Process Core, FM Endpoint Extension and switch logic; no physical PIFS switch exists.

Evaluation. Characterization of DLRM embedding-lookup phase on the real Genoa+CXL box with representative traces; then Ramulator 2.0 cycle-level simulation of PIFS-Rec on four DLRM configurations (RMC1–RMC4, e.g., RMC1 16K embeddings x 64B up to RMC4 1M embeddings x 128B) using Meta open-source DLRM traces.

Baselines. Pond (CXL pooling), Pond+PM (with page management), BEACON (in-switch/near-memory compute for recommendation), RecNMP (processing-near-memory DIMM).

Key findings. PIFS-Rec achieves 3.89x lower latency than Pond and 2.03x lower than BEACON; on-switch buffer with HTR gives 14.8% speedup on RMC4 with a 512KB cache. Characterization shows CXL pool fetch latency up to 270 ns with ~37% from CXL I/O port transfers/retimer delays, motivating in-fabric processing.

Limitations. The core contribution (processing in the fabric switch) is evaluated purely in simulation (Ramulator 2.0 + custom models) — no real CXL 2.0/3.x fabric switch hardware. The real-hardware part is only motivational characterization on one host whose CXL memory device(s) are never identified (model, count, link width unknown). No multi-tenant or interference experiments; no end-to-end DLRM serving with latency SLOs on real hardware; per-device management is not studied since devices sit behind an idealized simulated switch.

Open questions. How PIFS behaves with real switch silicon (no CXL 3.x fabric switches with compute exist commercially); coherence and CXL.mem protocol overheads for in-switch compute; sharing the in-switch processors across tenants/workloads; applicability beyond embedding gather (other sparse workloads); interaction with host-side tiering/page placement beyond the simple PM scheme.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Moderate relevance. The characterization half mirrors the requester's setup (AMD EPYC host + real CXL DDR4-backed expansion, ~270 ns CXL latency vs their 277 ns measurement — a useful corroborating data point), but the paper's contribution assumes a hypothetical compute-capable CXL fabric switch evaluated only in simulation, so it does not inform per-device placement or CXL-to-CXL interference on real CZ120s. It is best used as related work motivating that CXL fabric/port overheads dominate far-memory latency, and as a contrast point: PIFS pushes compute into the fabric, whereas the requester's candidate direction manages real, independently addressable devices from the host (ObjPlace-style), a niche PIFS leaves untouched (no multi-tenant, no per-device scheduling, no interference study).

Could not verify. Exact CXL device model/vendor, number of physical CXL expansion cards, and link width in the characterization testbed (paper only says 'four channels of DDR4 memory' via CXL, 256GB), Kernel version, MICRO 2024 venue attribution (taken from task prompt/ACM-IEEE listings, not confirmed on arXiv page), Whether the characterization CXL memory is production ASIC vs FPGA prototype

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • we run the embedding table lookup phase of the DLRM inference process on a dual-socket AMD Genoa system (each socket having 96 physical CPU cores and 12 channels of DDR5 memory that populates a capacity of 768GB)... Here, CXL memory is enabled through four channels of DDR4 memory resulting in memory capacity of 256GB.
  • we conducted cycle-level memory simulations using Ramulator 2.0 [57] for a detailed evaluation of PIFS-Rec.
  • Fetching a single address from memory pools can take up to 270 ns, with approximately 37% attributed to frequent CXL I/O port transfers and retimer delays.
  • The HA manages the CXL.mem protocol, presenting memory to the CPU as if it were on a remote NUMA node, allowing direct access via standard load/store commands.
  • These limitations include the risk of flex bus congestion under heavy memory traffic, increased data access latency (by over 4x compared to local DRAM), and limited bandwidth expansion capabilities.

Smart-Infinity: Fast Large Language Model Training using Near-Storage Processing on a Real System

HPCA 2024 (30th IEEE International Symposium on High-Performance Computer Architecture; Best Paper Award Honorable Mention) (2024) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL devices; up to 10 Samsung SmartSSD computational storage devices)

Problem. Storage-offloaded LLM training (ZeRO-Infinity style, offloading optimizer state/parameters to NVMe SSDs) is bottlenecked by storage bandwidth on the host PCIe/storage path, making training of tens-of-billion-parameter models on a single node slow.

Idea. Use near-storage processing: Samsung SmartSSD computational storage devices (NVMe SSD + Kintex UltraScale+ KU15P FPGA on the same card) execute the optimizer parameter update near the storage (SmartUpdate), removing most parameter/optimizer traffic from the host storage path; plus a transfer-handler overlap optimization and accelerator-assisted gradient compression (CSD-side decompression) to reduce write traffic.

Testbed. CPU: Intel Xeon Gold 6342, 2 sockets (2x48C/96T as listed); Memory: 32x32GB DDR4-3200 (1 TB); GPUs: NVIDIA A5000 (also A100 40GB and A4000 for sensitivity); Storage: up to 10 Samsung SmartSSD devices, each a 4TB NVMe SSD paired with a Xilinx Kintex UltraScale+ KU15P FPGA (522K LUTs, 984 BRAMs, 1968 DSPs, 4GB DDR4) over PCIe Gen3 x4. No CXL hardware. Kernel version not captured.

Evaluation. Storage-offloaded training of GPT-2 variants (4.0B, 8.4B, 16.6B, 33.0B), BERT-345M, BLOOM, ViT with batch size 4, mixed precision, on a single server with 1-10 SmartSSDs; fine-tuning accuracy case studies on GLUE tasks (MNLI, QQP, SST-2, QNLI); scaling studies over number of CSDs and GPU type.

Baselines. DeepSpeed ZeRO-Infinity with software RAID0 across the same SSDs (state-of-the-art storage-offloaded training framework).

Key findings. SmartUpdate alone gives 1.18x-1.24x speedup with 6 SSDs and 1.54x-1.60x with 10 SSDs over ZeRO-Infinity+RAID0; adding the transfer optimization reaches 1.60x-1.66x; the full system (SmartUpdate + optimization + gradient compression) reaches 1.85x-1.98x, and up to 2.11x with an A100 GPU. Fine-tuning accuracy is preserved on GLUE despite gradient compression. Speedups grow with the number of CSDs because host storage-bandwidth pressure is removed.

Limitations. Authors admit a slight slowdown with a single CSD (benefits need >=4 CSDs), SmartSSD cost (~6x a plain SSD) hurting cost-efficiency at low device counts, and reduced speedup in multi-GPU settings due to PCIe congestion; extending compression to quantization/pruning/low-rank is left as future work. Methodologically: single-node only, FPGA-based CSDs (product-grade but niche hardware), no CXL or memory-tiering component at all, no multi-tenant or interference study.

Open questions. Integration of quantization/pruning/low-rank compression into CSD-side update (explicitly deferred); scaling to multi-node distributed training; interaction with faster interconnects (PCIe Gen4/5 or CXL-attached memory) which could change where the bottleneck sits.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Essentially irrelevant to the requester's 4x CZ120 CXL testbed: Smart-Infinity operates at the NVMe-storage tier with FPGA computational storage, not load/store CXL memory, and studies no placement, interference, or per-device scheduling questions applicable to CXL NUMA nodes. Its only transferable ideas are architectural analogies — treating each of N expansion devices as an independent bandwidth resource and offloading work to the device to relieve host-link pressure — which loosely echo the candidate per-device-scheduling direction but on read/write block storage, not cacheable memory. It is not a baseline, competitor, or design source for ObjPlace-style DRAM/CXL placement.

Could not verify. Kernel/OS version of the testbed (not captured from the paper text)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • "We use at most 10 SAMSUNG SmartSSD devices. Each SmartSSD has a 4TB NVMe SSD which directly communicates with Kintex UltraScale+ KU15P FPGA through a PCIe Gen3.0 x4 bus. The attached FPGA has approximately 522K LUTs, 984 BRAMs, 1968 DSPs, and DDR4 4GB DRAM."
  • Testbed table (Table II): "GPU: NVIDIA A5000, A100 (40GB), A4000; CPU: Xeon(R) Gold 6342, 2x48C 96T; Memory: 32x32GB DDR4-3200; SSD: SAMSUNG SmartSSD, 4TB"
  • CXL absence verified mechanically: grep -ci 'cxl' over the full arXiv HTML (https://arxiv.org/html/2403.06664v1) returns 0 matches — the paper never mentions CXL; it is a near-storage (computational storage) system, hence realCXL=other-hw, numCXLDevices=0, deviceTreatment=n/a.

The Breakthrough Memory Solutions for Improved Performance on LLM Inference

IEEE Micro 44(3):40-48 (2024) unknown unknown verify: corrected confidence: low

CXL devices. 8 (Figure 6 compares 1 device and 8 devices; the headline 4.4x result is at 8 CXL-PNM devices vs an 8-GPU appliance)

Problem. LLM inference is memory-bandwidth-bound: logic (compute) scaling outpaced memory-process scaling, so transformer LLM performance is hindered by main-memory bandwidth and capacity limits (e.g., GPT-3.5-class models needing >300 GB do not fit in GPU memory).

Idea. Samsung industry overview of three in/near-memory compute products for LLM inference: (1) HBM-PIM integrated into a GPU-cluster system, (2) an LPDDR5-PIM-based system, and (3) a CXL-based PNM solution that implements the acceleration logic inside the CXL memory controller (LPDDR-backed CXL memory expander with near-memory compute), offloading bandwidth-bound GEMV/attention work from the host/GPU.

Testbed. Could not verify from the paper itself (paywalled). Abstract-level: HBM-PIM in a GPU-cluster system; an LPDDR5-PIM system; a CXL-PNM device with logic in the CXL memory controller. The companion HPCA'24 paper (Park et al., "An LPDDR-based CXL-PNM Platform for TCO-efficient Inference of Transformer-based LLMs", DOI 10.1109/HPCA57654.2024.00078) describes an LPDDR5X-based CXL-PNM device with 512 GB capacity and 1.1 TB/s internal bandwidth, evaluated as an 8-device appliance vs an 8-GPU appliance — but this is from that paper's abstract, not the IEEE Micro text.

Evaluation. Could not verify. Abstract indicates transformer-based LLM inference workloads evaluated on each of the three memory solutions; specific models, batch sizes, and measurement methodology (real silicon vs FPGA vs simulation) unverified.

Baselines. Could not verify from full text. Implied baselines are the corresponding non-PIM/PNM systems (standard HBM GPU cluster, standard LPDDR5 system, and for CXL-PNM presumably a GPU or non-PNM CXL baseline); exact baseline systems unverified.

Key findings. HBM-PIM-based GPU-cluster system: up to 1.9x LLM inference performance improvement. LPDDR5-PIM-based system: up to 2.7x improvement. CXL-based PNM solution: performance gain of over 4.4x with energy reduction of about 53%. (All from the verified abstract.) Companion HPCA'24 CXL-PNM result (context, not this article's text): 8-device CXL-PNM appliance gives 23% lower latency, 31% higher throughput, 2.8x higher energy efficiency at 30% lower HW cost than an 8-GPU appliance.

Limitations. Industry magazine article (IEEE Micro) summarizing Samsung product prototypes rather than a peer-reviewed system evaluation; methodology depth is limited by format. Full text was inaccessible, but from the abstract: results are vendor-reported speedups on Samsung's own hardware; no indication of multi-tenant runs, interference studies, or OS/placement concerns. The CXL device here is a compute device (PNM), not a passive memory expander, so results do not characterize CXL memory-expansion behavior (latency/bandwidth/contention) at all.

Open questions. How CXL-PNM composes with commodity CXL memory expansion and host-side tiering; programming-model and software-stack generality beyond transformer GEMV kernels; multi-device scheduling, sharing, and QoS across PNM devices; behavior under multi-tenant load. None of these are addressed at the abstract level.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x Micron CZ120 memory-expansion testbed: this is a near-data-processing accelerator embedded in the CXL controller, not passive CXL memory whose placement/interference an OS runtime like ObjPlace manages. It offers no data on load-to-use latency, per-device bandwidth contention, or DRAM/CXL tiering on commodity expanders. Its value is as related-work motivation: it shows industry treating CXL endpoints as bandwidth-rich compute resources, and the companion HPCA'24 work's 8-device appliance is a datapoint for the "each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource" framing — though there the devices run dedicated inference jobs rather than shared tiered memory. Cite for positioning, not for methodology.

Full paper text (IEEE Xplore 418, ACM DL 403, ResearchGate 403, CSDL empty shell, no arXiv version), Whether the CXL-PNM evaluation in this article used fabricated silicon, an FPGA prototype, or simulation, Exact number of CXL-PNM devices in the article's testbed, CPU model, DRAM config, CXL link width, kernel version, Workload models/sizes, baselines, and energy-measurement methodology, Whether the >4.4x / 53% numbers are measured or projected
