Simulators, emulation & tooling (6 papers)

Simulators now come silicon-validated (CXL-DMSim) but none reproduces the microsecond tails Melody measured on real devices - the core argument for real-hardware work.

Cohet: A CXL-Driven Coherent Heterogeneous Computing Framework with Hardware-Calibrated Full-System Simulation (SimCXL)

HPCA 2026 (arXiv 2511.23011, "Accepted by HPCA 2026") (2026) mixed multi-device-mixed verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. real calibration testbed: 2 (1x Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA dev kit as CXL type-1/2 device + 1x Samsung CMM-D 512 GB memory expander as type-3); main application evaluations (RAO, RPC) run entirely in the SimCXL simulator

Problem. Conventional heterogeneous (CPU+accelerator) computing built on PCIe suffers from inefficient fine-grained host-device interactions (DMA at cacheline granularity is slow) and complex programming models; there is also no full-system, cycle-level simulation infrastructure covering all three CXL protocols and all device types to explore CXL-coherent heterogeneous architectures.

Idea. Cohet: a CXL-driven coherent heterogeneous computing framework that "decouples the compute and memory resources to form unbiased CPU and XPU pools which share a single unified and coherent memory pool," programmable via standard malloc/mmap. To study it, the authors build SimCXL, a cycle-level full-system simulator (gem5-based, X86O3CPU, modified Linux v6.12) whose modular CXL controller models CXL.io/.cache/.mem and type-1/2/3 devices, calibrated against a real FPGA+expander testbed to ~3% mean absolute percentage error.

Testbed. Real calibration testbed: Intel 4th-gen Xeon Platinum 8468V (48 cores, 97.5 MB LLC), 1 TB DDR5-4800; CXL devices: Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA Development Kit at 400 MHz on PCIe5.0 x16 (type-1/2, 128 KB 4-way host-managed cache) and Samsung CMM-D 512 GB expander on PCIe5.0 x8 to Socket1, exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node; Linux v6.5.0. SimCXL config: 48-core X86O3CPU, 32 GB DDR5-4400, modified kernel v6.12.

Evaluation. Calibration: custom load/store-unit microbenchmarks measuring CXL.cache and DMA latency/bandwidth on the real FPGA, using CLDEMOTE/CLFLUSH to control cacheline placement (HMC hit / LLC hit / memory hit). Application evaluation runs in SimCXL: six remote-atomic-operation (RAO) access patterns from the CircusTent benchmark (CENTRAL, STRIDE1, SCATTER, GATHER, SG, RAND) and six RPC (de)serialization benches from HyperProtoBench (Google Protobuf).

Baselines. PCIe-NIC (conventional DMA-based design), CXL-NIC (coherent design, with and without prefetching), and an RpcNIC reference design for RPC offloading.

Key findings. CXL.cache reduces latency by 68% vs DMA for a memory hit at 64B granularity and delivers 14.4x DMA bandwidth for fine-grained transfers. SimCXL achieves a mean absolute percentage error of 3% vs the real FPGA testbed. In simulation, CXL-NIC RAO speedups over PCIe-NIC range from 5.5x (RAND) to 40.2x (CENTRAL), 22.4x for STRIDE1; RPC deserialization 1.33-2.05x, serialization with CXL.mem 2.0-4.06x, average 1.86x for (de)serialization.

Limitations. Authors admit ATS-based address translation overhead "remains unexplored due to the lack of ATS support on current CXL FPGA platforms," and CXL.mem H2D access measurement is deferred ("will be presented in another paper"). Methodological: only one type-3 memory expander (Samsung CMM-D) and one FPGA on the real testbed despite the abstract's "various memory expanders" phrasing; all killer-app results (RAO, RPC) come from simulation, not hardware; no multi-expander, multi-tenant, or interference experiments; simulated memory config (32 GB) is far smaller than the real testbed.

Open questions. ATS translation overhead; CXL.mem host-to-device performance characterization (explicitly deferred); behavior with multiple type-3 devices / pooled memory and CXL 3.x fabrics; contention/QoS between coherent accelerators and memory-expansion traffic; validation of simulator accuracy for type-3-heavy (memory-tiering) workloads rather than type-1/2 accelerator traffic.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x Micron CZ120 type-3 testbed: Cohet is about CXL.cache-coherent accelerators (type-1/2 FPGA NICs) and its real testbed has only ONE type-3 expander (Samsung CMM-D, exposed as one CPU-less NUMA node), with all application results in simulation — it offers no data on per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, or multi-tenant QoS. Its main value to the requester is SimCXL itself (open source, TianheMICALab/SimCXL): a hardware-calibrated cycle-level simulator that models multiple type-3 devices could be used to explore per-device scheduling / interference scenarios beyond what the 4-device box allows, or to sanity-check ObjPlace policies at scale. Also useful as a citation that fine-grained coherent access beats DMA, but that is orthogonal to memory-expansion tiering.

Could not verify. Whether the calibration table includes additional memory expander models beyond the Samsung CMM-D (abstract says 'various memory expanders' but only Samsung 512 GB + Agilex FPGA are named in the testbed section), Exact CXL.mem latency numbers for the Samsung expander (paper defers H2D CXL.mem measurement to another paper)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • SimCXL has been rigorously calibrated against a real CXL-supported testbed with Intel FPGA accelerators and various memory expanders, achieving an average simulation error of 3%.
  • The CXL devices on the testbed include an Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA Development Kit and a Samsung 512 GB memory expander.
  • The Samsung expander, connected to Socket1 via PCIe5.0 ×8, is exposed to the system as a CPU-less NUMA node.
  • We evaluated six RAO access patterns from the CircusTent benchmark on both PCIe-NIC and CXL-NIC in SimCXL.
  • The modular CXL controller in SimCXL can model CXL type-1/2/3 devices.
  • After all calibration tests, our simulator achieves a mean absolute percentage error of 3%.
  • Cohet decouples the compute and memory resources to form unbiased CPU and XPU pools which share a single unified and coherent memory pool.
  • The overhead of ATS-based address translation remains unexplored due to the lack of ATS support on current CXL FPGA platforms.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.23011 https://arxiv.org/html/2511.23011v2

Cylon: Fast and Accurate Full-System Emulation of CXL-SSDs

USENIX FAST '26 (24th USENIX Conference on File and Storage Technologies) (2026) mixed single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. validation testbed: 1 (Samsung CMM-H FPGA prototype, 1TB); emulation testbed: 0 real CXL devices (Cylon emulates one CXL-SSD in software on a dual-socket Xeon)

Problem. CXL-SSD research lacks a usable evaluation platform: commercial prototypes like Samsung CMM-H are black boxes; trace-driven and gem5-based simulators are too slow and cannot run full software stacks; vanilla QEMU CXL emulation inflates every access to ~15us via MMIO/VM-exits.

Idea. Cylon is a FEMU-based full-system CXL-SSD emulator with a hybrid access path: Dynamic EPT Remapping (DER) lets DRAM-cache hits complete as direct cacheline-granular load/stores with no VM-exit (~remote-NUMA latency), while cache misses trap into FEMU's validated NAND timing engine (channel/die/plane parallelism, GC interference); Shared EPT Memory between KVM and userspace QEMU/FEMU cuts miss-path syscall overhead (23.04us -> 16.27us). On top it provides a configurable caching framework (FIFO/LIFO/CLOCK/S3FIFO eviction, next-N prefetching) and an application-level hint interface for HW-SW co-design. Upstreamed to FEMU (github.com/MoatLab/FEMU); ~implementation spans QEMU, KVM module, on Linux v6.4.6.

Testbed. Two testbeds. (1) Emulation host: dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6242, 384GB DDR4 (192GB/socket), local/remote DRAM latency 90ns/150ns (MLC), Ubuntu 20.04, modified Linux kernel v6.4.6; guest VM Ubuntu 22.04, vanilla kernel v6.4.6, 8 vCPUs, 96GB DRAM; emulated CXL-SSD = 96GB DAX device with 4.8GB DRAM cache, NAND timing 40us read / 200us write / 2000us erase; guest vCPUs pinned to node 0, backend memory reserved on NUMA node 1 so emulated hits incur remote-NUMA latency. (2) Hardware validation: dual-socket Intel Xeon 6710E, 512GB DDR5 (256GB/socket), one Samsung CMM-H CXL-SSD prototype (48GB DRAM cache with 4KB cachelines + 1TB NVMe SSD backend, Intel Agilex FPGA controller, PCIe Gen5) exposed as a CPU-less NUMA node with 1TB capacity, Ubuntu 24.04, kernel v6.13.0. CloudLab infrastructure acknowledged.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: Intel MLC (latency/bandwidth) and MIO pointer chasing (sequential/random/stride patterns), sweeping working-set size (WSS) normalized to each device's DRAM-cache capacity for apples-to-apples Cylon-vs-CMM-H comparison. Macrobenchmarks: Redis with YCSB workload C (100% reads, 1KB records, Zipfian, 8 threads) at 0.33x/1.06x/2.10x normalized WSS; GAPBS Betweenness Centrality on kron graphs (10.2/20.4/40.8GB footprints, Cylon cache matched to CMM-H's). Policy studies: 4 eviction policies (FIFO/LIFO/CLOCK/S3FIFO) and next-N prefetching (N=0..8) across access patterns.

Baselines. Real Samsung CMM-H hardware (ground truth for validation) and QEMU-CXL (upstream QEMU CXL Type-3 emulation) as software baseline; internal variants Cylon-I (ioctl-based EPT remap) vs Cylon-S (shared EPT memory). Other platforms (MQSim-CXL, ESF, CXL-SSD-Sim, CXL-DMSim, OpenCXD) discussed but not directly compared (trace-driven, gem5-speed, or FPGA-gated).

Key findings. (1) At 100% hit rate Cylon matches remote-NUMA latency (0.16us) vs QEMU-CXL's 14.74us (>2 orders of magnitude inflation); miss-path emulation overhead is 16.27us (Cylon-S) vs 23.04us (Cylon-I). (2) Cylon reproduces the bimodal latency distribution of real CXL-SSDs: avg 977ns hits + tens-of-us misses, while QEMU collapses everything to ~14.6us. (3) Bandwidth: Cylon sustains remote-NUMA bandwidth (32GB/s) until its 4.8GB cache saturates, then converges to NAND-bound throughput like CMM-H; CMM-H degrades earlier due to opaque controller overheads and has higher hit latency (~800ns from FPGA controller). (4) Redis and GAPBS trends track CMM-H closely across cache-resident to NAND-dominated regimes. (5) Policy exploration: S3FIFO improves hit rates over FIFO/CLOCK on sequential/stride patterns; LIFO surprisingly best for Zipfian Redis; next-8 prefetching lifts Stride-4096 hit rate 18%->86%; random access collapses all policies to ~24% hits.

Limitations. Authors' own: CMM-H is a first-generation FPGA prototype with evolving firmware, so Cylon models "intended architectural behavior" rather than reproducing prototype-specific artifacts — absolute latencies/bandwidth diverge (Cylon faster on hits, slower on some misses); the earlier CMM-H bandwidth drop cannot be explained because the device is a black box; direct capacity comparison required WSS normalization since Cylon's emulated cache (4.8GB) is 10x smaller than CMM-H's (48GB). Methodological: only ONE CXL device (one CMM-H) ever used — no multi-device, no interleaving, no CXL memory expanders; emulation-only for all policy studies; no multi-tenant or interference experiments; miss-path still carries 16us software overhead (hidden only when NAND latency dominates); validation and emulation hosts are different machines (Xeon 6710E vs Gold 6242), so hit-latency comparisons conflate platform differences.

Open questions. How severe end-to-end stalls from tens-of-us misses are across real full stacks, and which cache/prefetch policies best mitigate them — Cylon is the vehicle, but the paper only scratches these with FIFO-family policies and next-N prefetching. Untested: multiple CXL-SSDs or mixed CXL memory + CXL-SSD tiers, multi-tenant sharing/QoS of the device DRAM cache, CXL 3.x pooling/sharing semantics, write-heavy workloads and GC-induced tails at application level, and emulation of next-gen designs beyond CMM-H (NVMe-oC, SkyByte-style) which they claim Cylon supports but do not deeply evaluate.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's 4x CZ120 memory-expander testbed: Cylon targets CXL-SSDs (DRAM cache + NAND behind CXL.mem, tens-of-us misses), a very different latency regime from the requester's 277ns CZ120 loads, and its evaluation is single-device with no interference or multi-tenant study. It is useful as (a) a tool if the requester ever wants to prototype per-device placement or QoS policies against a hypothetical CXL device with configurable internals without hardware access, and (b) methodological precedent that FAST accepts emulator/validation papers — reinforcing that the requester's real-4-device cross-device-interference angle is largely unoccupied. Notably, this is the same VT group (Liu/Noh/Li, authors of the ASPLOS'25 Melody/MIO CXL characterization and Pond), so they are active adjacent competitors in CXL systems.

Could not verify. Exact CMM-H CXL link width (paper says PCIe Gen5 but not xN lanes), Whether the emulation host has any CXL hardware at all (none mentioned; NUMA node 1 is the second socket's DRAM)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Validation Testbed. For hardware validation, we use a dual-socket Intel Xeon 6710E server with 512GB DDR5 DRAM (256GB per socket). A commercial CXL-SSD prototype, Samsung's CMM-H, is attached and recognized by the host as a CPU-less NUMA node with 1TB capacity [1]. The system runs Ubuntu 24.04 with Linux kernel v6.13.0.
  • Host Platform. Experiments run on a dual-socket Intel Xeon Gold 6242 server with 384GB DDR4 DRAM (192GB per socket). Local and remote DRAM latencies, measured with Intel MLC [38], are 90ns and 150ns, respectively. The host runs Ubuntu 20.04 with a modified Linux kernel v6.4.6.
  • CMM-H is a hybrid CXL memory prototype that integrates a 48GB DRAM cache (4KB cacheline) with a 1TB NVMe SSD backend, coordinated by an Intel Agilex FPGA controller over PCIe Gen5 [8].
  • Cylon exposes a 96GB CXL DAX device to the guest, emulating a CXL-SSD with a 4.8GB DRAM cache and 96GB NAND flash. NAND timing parameters are fixed to realistic values (40 µs read, 200 µs write, and 2,000 µs erase)
  • With a 100% cache-hit rate, Cylon matches remote-NUMA latency (0.16µs), because cached pages are served via direct load/store instructions with no VM-exit. By contrast, QEMU's MMIO-based design inflates access latency to 14.74µs
  • CMM-H is a first-generation FPGA prototype with evolving firmware and opaque controller behavior. Internal overheads (e.g., metadata management, controller-level prefetching) lead to higher cache-hit latency (∼800ns) and lower bandwidth even before cache saturation on our platform

https://huaicheng.github.io/p/fast26-cylon.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/fast26/presentation/yoon

Finding NEMO: Nimble and Expressive Memory Observability

OSDI (USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2026) real-fpga unknown verify: corrected confidence: low

CXL devices. 1

Problem. OSes managing heterogeneous multi-tier memory hierarchies need memory-usage measurements for placement/control decisions, but existing HW/SW tracking mechanisms (e.g., PEBS/PMU sampling, page-table scanning) force difficult tradeoffs between coverage, timeliness, granularity, flexibility, and CPU overhead.

Idea. NEMO: a programmable hardware memory-telemetry engine placed in server memory controllers that gives OS subsystems policy-specific views of memory behavior via flexible telemetry rules that filter memory operations, map accesses to counters, and apply simple per-counter state updates; prototyped in an FPGA-based CXL-attached memory expander and plugged into HeMem, MEMTIS, and Linux noisy-neighbor detection.

Testbed. Not verifiable: full text embargoed. Only known from abstract: prototype implemented on an FPGA-based CXL-attached memory expander. CPU model, DRAM config, FPGA board, CXL link width, capacity, and kernel version unknown.

Evaluation. From abstract only: three use cases evaluated against state-of-the-art memory-management systems — (1) hot-set change reaction in HeMem, (2) THP splitting in MEMTIS, (3) noisy-neighbor detection in Linux — with end-to-end workloads including key-value stores and databases. Detailed workload/run methodology unverified (embargoed).

Baselines. HeMem (PEBS-sampling-based tiering), MEMTIS, and stock Linux telemetry/noisy-neighbor detection mechanisms (per abstract); exact baseline configurations unverified.

Key findings. Per abstract: NEMO speeds up HeMem's reaction to hot-set changes by 5x, accelerates THP splitting in MEMTIS by 10.4x, and detects noisy neighbors in Linux with 350x lower overhead; these telemetry improvements yield up to 1.7x higher throughput and 23% lower latency across key-value stores and databases.

Limitations. Could not read methodology/evaluation sections (paper embargoed until OSDI '26 opens proceedings), so author-admitted limitations are unknown. Evident from the abstract: the prototype is an FPGA CXL expander, not production ASIC silicon, so absolute latency/bandwidth are not representative of devices like CZ120; NEMO requires new memory-controller hardware, so it is not deployable on existing servers; telemetry appears device-side (CXL expander MC), so DRAM-side accesses presumably still need other mechanisms unless host MCs also adopt NEMO.

Open questions. Unknown from available text. Obvious untested/unstated questions: behavior with multiple CXL devices (per-device telemetry aggregation, cross-device interference attribution); adoption path into production host/CXL memory controllers; multi-tenant isolation enforcement (abstract covers detection of noisy neighbors, not mitigation); scalability of rule/counter state at TB-scale capacity.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Highly relevant conceptually: NEMO argues the device/MC side is the right place for hotness and interference telemetry, exactly the observability layer ObjPlace currently approximates from the host (bandwidth-sensitivity/skew/phase signals). Its noisy-neighbor detection at 350x lower overhead speaks directly to the requester's measured 2.0-2.13x CXL co-runner slowdown and the candidate per-device QoS/interference direction — NEMO provides detection, leaving per-device placement, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference management, and mitigation policy open. However, NEMO needs a programmable FPGA memory expander; the requester's Micron CZ120 ASICs expose no such telemetry (at best CXL 2.0 CPMU-style counters), so on their testbed NEMO is a positioning/related-work anchor and a motivation for host-side or multi-device software techniques rather than a reusable mechanism. Caveat: this judgment rests on the abstract only; the full paper is embargoed as of 2026-07-07.

Could not verify. Full paper text (methodology and evaluation sections) — USENIX page is embargoed (no PDF link, sign-in gated) as of 2026-07-07 and no arXiv/author preprint exists, Exact number of physical CXL devices in the testbed (numCXLDevices), Device treatment (single vs multi-device, NUMA exposure), Testbed details: CPU model, DRAM config, FPGA board model, CXL link width/speed, expander capacity, kernel version, Exact workloads, benchmark clients, and baseline configurations, Authors' own stated limitations and future work

Verifier note. CORRECTED deviceTreatment: devdax slow tier managed by NEMO's userspace memory manager (CXL memory is enumerated as a CPU-less NUMA node by default, then reserved via memmap= and reconfigured with ndctl into a devdax device, /dev/dax1.0 = CXL SLOWMEM, alongside /dev/dax0.0 = DRAM FASTMEM) Paper PDF is not yet public (USENIX page has no PDF; osdi26-li-shihang.pdf probes 404; no arXiv preprint found). All testbed conclusions come from the authors' official artifact-evaluation branch (github.com/vic-lsh/nemo, branch osdi26_ae), which I cloned and read directly — this is authors' primary-source material for the same OSDI'26 paper, but not the paper body itself. Key facts: Intel Agilex 7 (AGIB027R29A1E2VB) FPGA with R-tile CXL Type-3 hard IP + DDR4 backing, one FPGA device at phys 0x4080000000 (~16 GiB in docs example, 32 GiB in AE config), exposed by default as one CPU-less NUMA node and reconfigured to devdax; DRAM fast tier is also a devdax (dax0.0), so anyone counting dax devices as CXL devices would double-count. Analyst's evidence quotes match the abstract verbatim.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We prototype NEMO on an FPGA-based CXL-attached memory expander.
  • We present NEMO, a nimble and expressive hardware memory telemetry engine for server memory controllers (MCs) that gives OS subsystems policy-specific views of memory behavior. NEMO enables flexible telemetry rules that filter memory operations, map accesses to counters, and apply simple updates to per-counter state.
  • it speeds up HeMem’s reaction to hot-set changes by 5×, accelerates THP splitting in MEMTIS by 10.4×, and detects noisy neighbors in Linux with 350× lower overhead. These telemetry improvements yield up to 1.7× higher throughput and 23% lower latency across key-value stores and databases.
  • Shihang Li and Matthew Giordano, University of Washington; Tushar Garg, Meta; Rohan Kadekodi, University of Washington; Daniel S. Berger, University of Washington and Microsoft Azure; Baris Kasikci, Thomas Anderson, and Simon Peter, University of Washington

https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/presentation/li-shihang https://vic-li.me/ https://homes.cs.washington.edu/~rohankad/ https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi26/technical-sessions

Xerxes: Extensive Exploration of Scalable Hardware Systems with CXL-Based Simulation Framework

FAST (USENIX Conference on File and Storage Technologies), 24th (2026) mixed simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. validation testbed: 1 physical CXL memory expander (Montage MXC M88MX5891 controller, 4x DDR5-4800 DIMMs, 128 GB, CXL 2.0 / PCIe 5.0 x16); all design-space experiments are simulated (1 to 64 simulated devices/endpoints)

Problem. No hardware exists for the newest CXL 3.x features (port-based routing/PBR for non-tree fabrics, device-managed coherence/DMC via back-invalidate, PCIe 6.0 full-duplex), and existing tools cannot model them: NUMA-based emulation is physically limited to socket counts and tree topologies; gem5-style computation-centric simulators lack PCIe/CXL fabric detail; behavioral simulators (MESS, CXLMemSim) need ground-truth latency-bandwidth curves and miss cross-layer effects.

Idea. Xerxes, a from-scratch simulation framework with two collaborating layers: a graph-based interconnect layer (topology graphs + shortest-path routing enabling arbitrary non-tree fabrics and peer-to-peer paths) and a device layer (requesters, buses with full-duplex bandwidth allocation, PBR switches with routing tables, device-side snoop filters implementing DMC with pluggable victim policies). Integrates with gem5 (full-system), DRAMsim3, and SimpleSSD; open-sourced at github.com/ChaseLab-PKU/Xerxes.

Testbed. Validation platform: dual-socket server, each socket an Intel Xeon Gold 6416H with 8x DDR5-4800 DIMMs (76.8 GB/s peak, 512 GB total main memory). One socket attaches one CXL memory expander using a Montage Technology MXC controller (M88MX5891), CXL 2.0 / PCIe 5.0 x16, containing 4x DDR5-4800 DIMMs = 128 GB CXL HDM-H memory; expander splits lanes (MCIO cable to remote socket) so each socket effectively gets PCIe 5.0 x8 (~24 GB/s practical). Kernel version not stated. All other experiments run inside the Xerxes simulator (DRAMsim3 endpoints; gem5 for full-system, cache hierarchy matched to hardware: 1.7 MB L1D, 72 MB L2, 96 MB L3).

Evaluation. Three-stage validation: (1) MLC-driven idle latency, peak bandwidth at varying R:W ratios, and loaded latency measured on local DRAM / NUMA remote / real CXL expander vs. Xerxes synthetic requesters; (2) CXL 3.1 features (PBR, DMC) validated against theoretical performance models since no CXL 3.1 hardware exists; (3) end-to-end workload validation (execution-time overhead of CXL memory, SPEC-style workloads via gem5-Xerxes and trace-driven Xerxes-standalone) plus application traces (BTree, liblinear, redis, silo, XSBench). Design-space case studies simulate topologies (chain/tree/ring/spine-leaf/fully-connected) up to system scale 64, DMC snoop-filter policies (FIFO/LRU/LFI/LIFO/MRU), InvBlk lengths, and full-duplex PCIe with varying R:W mix and header overheads.

Baselines. Real CXL hardware (Montage MXC expander), NUMA-based emulation (remote socket, DIMM count matched to 4), behavioral simulators MESS and CXLMemSim (fed ground-truth hardware curves), and gem5-garnet as detailed architectural simulator.

Key findings. Accuracy vs. real CXL hardware: bandwidth errors 0.1-10%; loaded-latency curves average error 4.3%; end-to-end CXL-overhead errors as low as 0.7%, while MESS and CXLMemSim (even with ground-truth inputs) err up to 28.3% and 16.5% (avg 9.3%/16.6% on loaded latency), NUMA emulation up to 9.2%, gem5-garnet up to 9.0%. Speed: only ~2% simulation-time overhead over vanilla gem5 vs. 22.5% for garnet; large-scale simulations stay under 90 s up to scale 64. Case studies: tree/chain topologies bottleneck at 1x port bandwidth while spine-leaf/fully-connected scale (fully-connected gives up to 3.63x throughput and 0.28x latency vs. chain on real traces); scaling 1 to 16 endpoints behind switches raises average latency ~9x from port contention; LIFO snoop-filter victim policy improves bandwidth ~5% and latency ~15% over FIFO, LFI cuts invalidations ~15%; InvBlk length 2 helps but longer lengths do not; each +0.1 in read-write mix degree yields ~9% bandwidth gain on full-duplex links, but high header-to-payload ratios can negate the full-duplex benefit.

Limitations. Authors' own: no CXL 3.1 hardware exists, so PBR/DMC are validated only against theoretical models, not silicon ("Since hardware is unavailable for validation of CXL 3.1 features..."); end-to-end validation measures only CXL-induced execution-time overhead because exact CPU micro-architecture cannot be simulated. Methodological: the real testbed has just one CXL 2.0 device on an Intel (not AMD) platform, so all multi-device, multi-switch, and coherence results are purely simulated; latency parameters partly calibrated from prior-work empirical numbers; no real multi-tenant or OS-level (tiering, kernel) experiments; largest simulated scale is 64 devices, and DRAM-endpoint-only workload traces (no accelerators exercised in validation).

Open questions. Extending Xerxes to other coherent interconnects (Huawei UB, UB-Mesh topologies) is explicitly left as future work. Untested: validation of PBR/DMC against any real CXL 3.x silicon or FPGA once available; interaction with OS memory management / tiering policies; multi-tenant contention and QoS on shared fabrics; energy/power modeling; behavior at true rack scale beyond 64 devices.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Xerxes is a simulator, not a systems mechanism, so it does not compete with ObjPlace; its real testbed (1 Montage CXL 2.0 expander on Intel) is smaller than the requester's 4x CZ120 box. Its value here is as a what-if tool for the candidate per-device direction: it can simulate multiple independent CXL endpoints, switch port contention, and topologies, and its finding that latency rises ~9x when scaling from 1 to 16 endpoints behind switches (port contention) supports the thesis that cross-device interference and per-device scheduling matter. Its validation data (MLC loaded-latency curves on real hardware, ~24 GB/s practical per-socket CXL bandwidth) is a useful comparison point to the requester's 35.5 GiB/s 2-way-interleaved region. Conversely, Xerxes contains no data on real multi-device interference on one host, which leaves the requester's 4-device, CXL-to-CXL co-runner measurements unoccupied territory.

Could not verify. Kernel/OS version of the validation platform (not stated in the paper)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Each socket is equipped with an Intel Xeon Gold 6416H CPU [11] and eight DDR5-4800 DRAM DIMMs, offering a peak bandwidth of 76.8 GB/s and providing 512 GB of main memory. One of the sockets is attached by a CXL memory expander with a CXL memory expander controller (MXC) from Montage Technology [9], supporting up to CXL 2.0 and PCIe 5.0 × 16 interconnections.
  • The memory expander consists of four DDR5-4800 DRAM DIMMs, providing 128 GB of CXL HDM-H memory.
  • Since hardware is unavailable for validation of CXL 3.1 features (e.g., PBR and DMC), we verify Xerxes’ correctness by comparing its simulation results against established theoretical performance models.
  • To simulate the CXL system, we construct an example system in Xerxes, which includes a requester, an interconnect bus, and four memory endpoints.
  • The validation experiment proves the accuracy of Xerxes with errors ranging from 0.1% to 10%.
  • The codes of Xerxes are open-sourced and available at https://github.com/ChaseLab-PKU/Xerxes.

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/fast26-an.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/fast26/presentation/an https://arxiv.org/html/2411.08312v1

CXL-Bench: Benchmarking Shared CXL Memory Access

ADMS workshop @ VLDB 2025 (16th Int'l Workshop on Accelerating Analytics and Data Management Systems) (2025) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (one Seagate Composable Memory Appliance blade with 8x 128 GiB DDR4 DIMMs, two CXL 1.1 ports, one port per server)

Problem. Database developers lack a way to quantify access performance across increasingly heterogeneous memory paths (local/remote socket, multi-die, CXL device memory), and in particular do not know how multiple servers simultaneously accessing one shared CXL memory device affect each other's latency/bandwidth, or how expensive (un)mapping shared CXL regions is.

Idea. CXL-Bench: a highly configurable open-source microbenchmark framework (extending PerMA-Bench) covering scalar/vector/streaming loads and stores, cache instructions (clwb/clflush/clflushopt), CAS/FAA atomics, mmap/unmap latency, NUMA-node vs devdax character-device abstractions, thread pinning, interleaved/partitioned placement, and a distributed mode that time-synchronizes workloads on multiple servers attached to one shared CXL device. Demonstrated by characterizing two servers sharing one CXL 1.1 device in devdax mode.

Testbed. Server EMR: Supermicro SYS-741GE-TNRT, 2x Intel Xeon Gold 6542Y (24 cores/CPU, Emerald Rapids), 8x 32 GB DDR5-4800 (1 DPC), Ubuntu 24.04, kernel 6.13.0. Server GNR: Avenue City Platform, 1x Intel Xeon 6 engineering sample (96 cores, Granite Rapids), 12x 16 GB DDR5-5600, same OS/kernel. CXL device: FPGA-based Seagate Composable Memory Appliance (CMA) Blade prototype, 4 DDR4 channels x 2 DPC = 8x 128 GiB DIMMs at 1866 MT/s, data interleaved across all 8 DIMMs, PCIe Gen5 x16 CXL 1.1 (64 GB/s per port), two ports each connected to one server; configured as character device in devdax mode (/dev/dax2.0), not as a NUMA node.

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks only, via CXL-Bench distributed mode: a "main" workload on EMR measures latency (10 M ops, sample every 1000th -> 10 000 samples) or throughput (10 s runs, 1-48 threads) of 64 B vector/streaming vector loads and stores plus CAS/FAA atomics against 16 GiB (also 64 GiB) devdax regions, with and without a "background" workload on GNR doing continuous sequential vector reads to the same device (same-offset and disjoint 512 GiB-offset variants). Separate experiment: mmap/unmap latency for 16-128 GiB devdax regions vs anonymous shared/private regions (MAP_POPULATE, 5 repetitions averaged).

Baselines. No system baselines (it is itself a benchmark tool). Comparisons are internal: with vs without cross-server background load; vector vs streaming-vector instructions; devdax mmap vs anonymous shared/private mmap. Framework is compared qualitatively to PerMA-Bench in related work.

Key findings. (1) Cross-HOST interference on one shared device: with GNR background reads, EMR median random load latency rises ~70% (480/490 ns -> 820/830 ns; p99 860 -> 1270 ns), and load throughput drops ~50%; store latencies are unaffected but store throughput drops significantly. Interference persists even when the two hosts use disjoint regions (offset 512 GiB), because the device interleaves data across all 8 DIMMs. (2) Streaming vector stores reach ~40 GB/s, 2.4x higher than regular vector stores (16.5 GB/s max), by avoiding RFO reads. (3) Uncached CAS/FAA latency is dominated by memory access latency (500-550 ns unloaded, 800-850 ns loaded; <50 ns p90 if cache line pre-loaded); atomics on CXL 1.x/2.0 shared memory trigger no cross-host coherence. (4) devdax mmap of 128 GiB takes ~1 s vs 40 s/26 s for anonymous shared/private; unmap <0.1 s — frequent CXL region re-allocation is feasible.

Limitations. Single CXL device only — no multi-device experiments at all, and CXL 1.1 only (no coherent sharing, no pooling/DCD). Device is an FPGA prototype (Seagate CMA) with DDR4 at 1866 MT/s, so absolute numbers (480 ns loads, ~40 GB/s) may not transfer to production ASICs like CZ120. Only one interference pattern tested (background = sequential vector reads from one server); no write-heavy or mixed background loads, no >2 hosts despite the discussion of hundreds of cores. Microbenchmarks only — no application/database workloads in this paper. Fixed 64 B access size in the load/store experiments; asymmetric servers (2-socket EMR vs 1-socket GNR ES) with main workload always on EMR. No QoS/isolation mechanism proposed, only measurement.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on how to distribute data across memory tiers to avoid oversubscribing CXL/memory controllers ("one needs to carefully distribute data") — no placement policy given. Untested: interference among multiple CXL devices; more than two hosts; per-region hardware QoS or bandwidth partitioning on the device; behavior of CXL 2.0/3.x pooling, DCD, and hardware coherent sharing; whether findings hold on ASIC devices; application-level (DBMS) impact of the measured interference; manual erasure cost of reused devdax pages.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Adjacent but orthogonal axis of interference: this paper shows contention between two HOSTS on ONE shared CXL device, whereas the requester's candidate direction is contention/scheduling ACROSS 4 devices under one host — so it does not occupy that niche and can be cited as evidence that device-internal controllers/DIMM interleaving are the contention point (interference persisted even for disjoint regions because the device interleaves across all DIMMs; analogous to the requester's 2-way root-decoder interleave coupling their two online CZ120s). The ~70% load-latency and ~50% throughput degradation numbers on a shared device complement the requester's 2.0-2.13x victim slowdown and support the argument that per-device (non-interleaved) management is needed for isolation. The devdax fast-mmap result (1 s for 128 GiB) is directly relevant to per-device dynamic re-allocation in an ObjPlace extension, and note two of the requester's CZ120s already host famfs on devdax. Caveats for citation: FPGA prototype with DDR4, CXL 1.1, Intel hosts — absolute numbers differ from the requester's ASIC CZ120/EPYC Turin setup.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • The CXL memory device is an FPGA-based Seagate Composable Memory Appliance (CMA) Blade prototype [14, 30] with four DDR4 memory channels and two DIMMs per channel (DPC). Data stored on the device is interleaved across all eight DIMMs. The CMA supports the PCIe Gen5 x16 CXL 1.1 specification connectivity. One CMA has two ports. Each port can be connected to a separate server for a shared memory setup. We refer to a single blade as CXL device in the remainder of this work.
  • We demonstrate the utility of CXL-Bench by quantifying memory access characteristics of two servers accessing a shared CXL 1.1 memory device. Our results show that memory accesses of one server to the device affect the access performance of another server accessing the same device.
  • The CXL device is configured as character device in devdax mode. Its physical memory is mapped into the virtual memory address space using the mmap system call, as shown in Listing 1. In this example code, the CXL device path is /dev/dax2.0.
  • Without background load, the median latencies for vector loads and streaming vector loads are 480 ns and 490 ns. ... With background load, the median latencies increase to 820 ns and 830 ns.
  • With sequential read load on the CXL device generated by the GNR server, the achieved throughput is reduced by approximately 50%.
  • Changing the start offset of the background workload's memory region (to 512 GiB) so that the regions of the main and background workload are disjoint yields the same latencies.
  • Vector stores achieve a maximum of 16.5 GB/s. The throughput of streaming stores is by 2.4× higher, reaching a plateau of approximately 40 GB/s.
  • For 128 GiB, mapping the character device takes only one second while allocating anonymous memory regions takes 40 seconds and 26 seconds in shared and private mode.

CXL-DMSim: A Full-System CXL Disaggregated Memory Simulator With Comprehensive Silicon Validation

IEEE TCAD (per arXiv comments); arXiv 2411.02282 (v7, 2026-01-11) (2025) mixed multi-device-independent verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 2 physical devices on the validation testbed: 1x Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA CXL 1.1 device (16GB DDR4) + 1x in-house CXL ASIC device with two CXL memory controllers (128GB DDR5, exposed as two CPU-less NUMA nodes). The simulator itself models arbitrary configurations including switch-attached pools.

Problem. Researchers lack accessible, accurate tools to study CXL disaggregated memory: real CXL silicon is scarce, NUMA-based emulation misrepresents CXL latency/bandwidth behavior, and existing simulators lack full-system CXL protocol support (drivers, kernel-managed vs app-managed modes) and silicon validation.

Idea. Build a gem5-based open-source full-system simulator (CXL-DMSim) with CXL.mem/CXL.io protocol modeling, CXL device drivers, both app-managed (devdax-style) and kernel-managed (NUMA) modes, and switch-attached topologies; then comprehensively validate it against a real dual-Xeon testbed hosting both an FPGA CXL prototype and an in-house CXL ASIC device, achieving ~3.4% average simulation error.

Testbed. Validation testbed: dual-socket Intel Xeon Platinum 8468V (4 iMCs / 8 channels each; one 32GB DDR5-4800 DIMM populated per NUMA node), Linux kernel v6.5.0. CXL devices (both on Socket1): (a) Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA Dev Kit with hard CXL 1.1 IP, 16GB DDR4-3200, exposed as CPU-less NUMA4; (b) in-house CXL ASIC with two CXL memory controllers each driving two DDR5 DIMMs, 128GB total, exposed as two CPU-less NUMA nodes (NUMA2, NUMA3). Remote-NUMA DDR used as an emulation baseline. Simulator: gem5-based; also models switch-attached ASIC (CXL-DMSimS).

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: LMbench lat_mem_rd (2048 MB array, 64 B stride) for latency; STREAM Copy/Scale/Add/Triad (1024 MB) for bandwidth — run on real local DDR, remote NUMA DDR, CXL-FPGA, CXL-ASIC, and in CXL-DMSim for cross-validation. Applications: Redis 5.0.5 with YCSB A/B (800 MB working set, single thread), Viper hybrid PMEM-DRAM KV store, Meta DLRM inference (bandwidth-sensitive); memory-constrained scenarios with CXL as capacity expansion, and 50:50 DDR:CXL interleaving for bandwidth expansion.

Baselines. Real hardware baselines: local DDR, remote NUMA DDR (emulation), CXL-FPGA, CXL-ASIC (direct and switch-attached). Simulator baselines: vanilla gem5 (speed overhead comparison); simulated variants CXL-DMSimA (app-managed) / kernel-managed / CXL-DMSimS (switched) validated against corresponding real configurations.

Key findings. (1) Simulator fidelity: average simulation error 3.4% vs real silicon (STREAM ~4.3% avg); simulation speed comparable to gem5 (CXL-DMSimA ~16% slower). (2) Characterization: CXL-ASIC load latency 284 ns = 2.18x local DDR (130 ns); CXL-FPGA 375 ns = 2.88x; switch-attached ASIC 487 ns = 3.75x. Bandwidth: CXL-FPGA 45-69% of local DDR, CXL-ASIC 82-83%, remote NUMA DDR 68-74% — NUMA emulation mimics neither latency nor bandwidth of real CXL. (3) Apps: Viper up to 25x/23x faster with CXL capacity expansion in memory-constrained settings; DLRM +39%/+60% with 50:50 DDR-CXL interleaving.

Limitations. Authors admit: gem5 cache model causes high latency error in the LLC-transition region (96-384 MB working sets); simulation ~16% slower than gem5 with inherently slow speed, high modeling complexity for new features, and limited system scale. Methodology weaknesses: real-device characterization treats each CXL device independently — no measurement of cross-device interference or multi-tenant contention on real silicon; switch-attached topology exists only in simulation; app validation is single-thread/small working sets (Redis 800 MB); the ASIC is an in-house device, not a commercial product like CZ120, limiting comparability.

Open questions. Cross-device CXL-to-CXL interference and QoS on real multi-device systems (their two devices are characterized separately, never contended); validation of switch/pooling models against real switch silicon; CXL 2.0/3.x features (hot-plug, sharing, back-invalidation); large-scale multi-tenant workloads; more accurate LLC modeling in gem5.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Directly useful as a validated simulation companion to the requester's real 4x CZ120 testbed: CXL-DMSim could model per-device and switch-attached topologies (including CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference scenarios) that the 4-device box cannot easily reconfigure, and its silicon-validated error (~3.4%) lends credibility to simulated extensions of ObjPlace experiments. Its characterization corroborates the requester's numbers (ASIC latency 2.18x DRAM vs their 1.53x; NUMA emulation shown inadequate — a citation supporting the value of real-hardware work). Notably, the paper never contends two real CXL devices against each other, so the requester's per-device interference/QoS direction remains unclaimed; the simulator could serve as a scaling/sensitivity tool for that direction (e.g., >4 devices, switches).

Could not verify. Verbatim quotes were extracted from the arXiv v7 HTML via the fetch pipeline's summarizer; exact wording could differ slightly from the PDF., Whether the two real CXL devices were ever interleaved together on the testbed (evidence indicates they were characterized independently)., Final IEEE TCAD publication details (volume/pages) — venue taken from arXiv comments field.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Intel Agilex I-Series FPGA Development Kit with an integrated hard CXL IP ... 16GB 3200MT/s DDR4 memory, connected to the CPU via a CXL 1.1 interface ... recognized as a separate CPU-less NUMA node (denoted as NUMA4)
  • In-house CXL-ASIC device has two CXL memory controllers, each of which is composed of a CXL protocol controller plus a DDR5 controller driving two DIMMs ... 128GB ... appearing in the OS as two separate NUMA nodes without CPUs (denoted as NUMA2 and NUMA3 respectively)
  • Dual-socket Intel Xeon Platinum 8468V processors, each of which has 4 integrated memory controllers with 8 memory channels ... a single memory channel (i.e., a 32GB DDR5 4800MT/s DIMM) for each NUMA node ... Linux kernel v6.5.0
  • average simulation error of 3.4%
  • CXL-ASIC: 2.18 times higher than local DDR (284 ns vs. 130 ns) ... CXL-FPGA: 2.88 times (375 ns)
  • Remote NUMA memory lacks realism and accuracy in mimicking CXL memory

Reliability, security & power (3 papers)

ReScue: Reliable and Secure CXL Memory

HPCA 2026 (IEEE International Symposium on High-Performance Computer Architecture) (2026) real-fpga single-device verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 1 (one Intel Agilex 7 I-series FPGA acting as a CXL Type-3 device)

Problem. Hyperscalers want to recycle past-generation (e.g., DDR4) DRAM modules behind CXL memory expanders, but such recycled modules (i) contain many uncorrectable/unrepairable faulty words after years of strenuous hyperscale use, and (ii) are more vulnerable to modern attacks such as Row Hammer (and to timing side channels that reverse-engineer physical-to-DRAM address mappings) because their defenses were designed for older attack models. Additionally, host/controller AXI interface limitations under delayed CXL responses can cause full system hangs.

Idea. A suite of in-CXL-controller hardware mechanisms that exploit two CXL-unique properties: variable-latency out-of-order responses and near-memory processing. ReScue-R stores the remap pointer to a fault-free block inside the faulty 64B block itself; on access to a faulty block it follows the pointer (with a Bloom filter to identify faulty blocks cheaply) and returns data without blocking subsequent accesses. ReScue-S shapes (equalizes/randomizes) memory-access latency at the device to defeat timing side channels used to reconstruct DRAM address mappings for Row Hammer. They also identify an AXI in-flight-command limitation that hangs the system under delayed CXL memory and validate a makeshift fix that caps in-flight commands. All implemented in RTL on a real Intel Agilex CXL platform.

Testbed. Host: "Intel Xeon scalable server with CXL support" (exact CPU model not stated in accessible sources). CXL device: one Intel Agilex 7 I-series FPGA (revision RBES) configured with Intel Agilex FPGA Hard IP for CXL as a CXL Type-3 memory device; link width/speed and attached DRAM module details not stated in accessible sources. OS: Linux kernel 6.5.5. FPGA tooling: Intel Quartus Prime Programmer.

Evaluation. Four artifact experiment sets on the FPGA testbed: (1) system-hang reproduction with a delayed-CXL bitstream and a custom multithreaded microbenchmark sweeping RD:WR ratios and thread counts, plus validation of the in-flight-command-limiting makeshift (Fig. 4); (2) latency-distribution measurements of baseline CXL vs ReScue-S equalizer vs ReScue-S randomizer using a DRAMA-based latency harness (Figs. 11-12); (3) ReScue-S execution-time overhead on SPEC CPU2017 (Fig. 13); (4) ReScue-R overhead with/without a Bloom filter (e.g., 512Kb) (Fig. 10). GAPBS graph benchmarks also listed as performance workloads. Each configuration is a different FPGA bitstream programmed via Quartus followed by a host power cycle.

Baselines. Standard SECDED ECC (fault-tolerance comparison for ReScue-R); baseline (unmodified) CXL Type-3 FPGA design with no repair / no latency shaping; ReScue-R without Bloom filter; ReScue-S pure latency equalizer vs latency randomizer variants.

Key findings. ReScue-R achieves fault tolerance 9.6x10^4 times higher than standard SECDED ECC with only 0.2% performance degradation. ReScue-S prevents timing-based side-channel attacks (e.g., reconstructing physical-address-to-DRAM-address mapping functions for Row Hammer) at 1.1% performance degradation, merging the two CXL latency groups into one; the randomizer gives slightly lower average latency than pure equalization. They also demonstrate that high write intensity + high thread count triggers system hangs on an AXI-based CXL memory controller under delayed responses, and that limiting in-flight commands removes the hangs.

Limitations. Single FPGA CXL device only — no multi-device, no interleaving, no pooling, no multi-tenant experiments visible in the artifact. FPGA prototype latencies/bandwidth differ from production ASIC expanders (e.g., CZ120), so the 0.2%/1.1% overhead numbers may not transfer directly. The recycled-DDR4 fault model is central to the motivation, but the artifact documentation does not show evaluation on actually degraded recycled modules (fault injection/Bloom-filter configs suggest modeled faults). Full paper text is paywalled, so authors' own stated limitations could not be read; workload coverage in the artifact is limited to SPEC CPU2017, GAPBS, DRAMA, and custom microbenchmarks — no datacenter/LLM/multi-tenant workloads.

Open questions. Behavior with multiple CXL devices or interleaved regions (does per-device latency shaping interact badly with host interleaving?); interaction of ReScue-S latency randomization with host-side tiering/placement policies that assume stable CXL latency; scalability of the block-remap scheme as fault density grows over module lifetime; whether the AXI in-flight-command hang and makeshift generalize to production ASIC controllers; security analysis against adaptive attackers who average over randomized latencies.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's direction: ReScue is a device-controller (RTL) paper on a single FPGA CXL Type-3 prototype, not host-side placement or multi-device scheduling on ASIC expanders like the CZ120s. Two useful touchpoints: (1) it documents that CXL devices legitimately return variable-latency out-of-order responses and can even hang AXI-based controllers under high write intensity — a device-side source of tail-latency variance the requester's per-device QoS/interference story could cite; (2) ReScue-S deliberately reshapes/randomizes per-device latency, which would confound any host-side placement runtime (like ObjPlace) that profiles per-device latency — an interesting adversarial consideration but not a baseline. Nothing here addresses multi-device interleaving, cross-device interference, or multi-tenant isolation.

Could not verify. Full paper text (IEEE paywalled; no arXiv or open PDF found) — methodology/evaluation sections read only via official artifact repo and program-page abstract, Exact host CPU model, CXL link width/speed, and DRAM module type/capacity behind the FPGA, Authors' own stated limitations and future-work section, Whether actual recycled/degraded DDR4 modules were used vs modeled faults, Exact per-benchmark overhead numbers beyond the abstract's 0.2% / 1.1% aggregates

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • All experiments are evaluated on an Intel Agilex FPGA–based CXL Type-3 device connected to an Intel Xeon system.
  • Device: **Intel Agilex 7 I-series FPGA (revision version RBES)** (CXL Type-3 device) configured with Intel Agilex FPGA Hard IP for CXL.
  • Host: Intel Xeon scalable server with **CXL support**.
  • OS: Linux with **kernel 6.5.5** (or compatible) configured and selected as default.
  • ReScue-R stores the remapping addresses to fault-free blocks in faulty 64-byte blocks... accomplishing fault tolerance 9.6 × 10^4 times higher than standard SECDED ECC with only 0.2% performance degradation. (HPCA program page abstract)
  • ReScue-S shapes the latency of memory accesses to prevent timing-based side-channel attacks, such as reconstructing physical-address-to-DRAM-address mapping functions for Row Hammer attacks, with 1.1% performance degradation. (HPCA program page abstract)
  • Compare latency distributions of Baseline CXL design, eScue-S latency equalizer and ReScue-S latency randomizer.
  • Evaluate performance impact of ReScue-R under: Baseline (no repair) and ReScue-R with Bloom filter (e.g., 512Kb)

Space-Control: Process-Level Isolation for Sharing CXL-based Disaggregated Memory

arXiv preprint (arXiv:2603.06951) (2026) simulated simulated-pool verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 1 (simulated shared disaggregated-memory device modeled in SST, shared by 8 simulated gem5 hosts)

Problem. CXL 3.0 shared/pooled memory only enforces host-level access control: once a host is authorized for a shared region, every process on that host can access it, violating least privilege. Process-level isolation is left to each host's OS, so a compromised or malicious OS/kernel on any sharing host can read/corrupt other tenants' data in shared disaggregated memory (SDM). The paper asks: "How can we ensure isolation in shared disaggregated memory that enforces the principle of least privilege and operates independently of the OS with minimal overhead?"

Idea. Space-Control, a HW/SW co-design that adds a cross-host process identity primitive and moves authorization out of the OS. Three components: (1) SPACE (Secure Process Attribute Context Engine), a hardware-rooted validation engine that registers/authenticates a process's address space and mints an immutable ContextID; (2) a Permission Table mapping PA ranges of shared memory to authorized ContextIDs across hosts, with keys/permissions managed via the CXL fabric manager; (3) a Permission Checker, an on-chip enforcer after the LLC / before the CXL downstream port that validates every LD/ST to shared memory, with a small permission cache to amortize lookups. Supports up to 127 concurrent processes across 255 hosts.

Testbed. No real hardware. Simulation: CXL-ClusterSim = gem5 (host fidelity) + SST (shared disaggregated memory, parallelized simulation). Simulated config (Table 2): 8-core TimingSimpleCPU @ 4 GHz, 32 KiB L1 I+D/core, 1 MiB L2/core, 16 MiB shared L3; local memory 16 GB DDR4-2400, 2 channels (38.4 GiB/s); remote (SDM) memory 16 GiB, 4 channels, 76.8 GiB/s; Ubuntu 22.04.4, kernel 6.9.9. Eight simulated hosts; fabric-manager communication modeled over shared CXL.mem instead of CXL.io.

Evaluation. GAPBS graph kernels (pr, bfs, bc, tc; ordered regular-to-random access), modified so a graph is shared across hosts in the SDM region; number of hosts accessing the same SDM region scaled 1 to 8 in powers of two; first 4 billion cycles of the region of interest simulated per workload per host. Sensitivity studies on permission-cache size and permission-table structure/fragmentation.

Baselines. Baseline CXL 3.0 system with no per-access permission checks ("cxl"); an ideal flat permission-table structure similar to Border-Control (Olson et al. 2015); "deact-like" (Kommareddy et al. 2021); "mondrian-ext" (Mondrian memory protection, Witchel et al. 2002).

Key findings. With a modest 16 KiB permission cache, Space-Control incurs ~3.3% average performance overhead vs. the unchecked CXL 3.0 baseline. Single-entry best case is 7.3% average overhead at 1 host, growing to 12.1% at 8 hosts; worst-case fragmentation (tc with relabeling skipped) reaches 3.8x vs cxl. A 2 KiB permission cache already reaches 99.9% hit rate and improves CPI overhead 2.3x over uncached. Storage overhead of permission metadata is 1.56% for 255 hosts sharing 4 KiB pages across 127 processes.

Limitations. Authors' own: revocation cannot stop in-flight instructions from committing — a tiny window of at most two BISnp exists where an attacker can violate isolation; they trust CXL hardware to function correctly and provide availability; physical attacks and side channels are out of scope. Methodological: entirely simulated (gem5+SST CXL-ClusterSim) — no real CXL silicon, no FPGA prototype; only one shared memory device modeled; simplified fabric-manager modeling (CXL.mem instead of CXL.io); small simulated system (8-core TimingSimpleCPU hosts, 16 GiB SDM, 4B-cycle windows); only GAPBS graph kernels — no databases, LLM, or general multi-tenant cloud workloads; the SPACE engine and Permission Checker require new hardware in CPUs/devices that does not exist in any shipping product.

Open questions. How the design performs on real CXL 3.x hardware or at datacenter scale; interaction with CXL TSP/IDE and confidential-computing (TEE) stacks; multi-device pooled memory and cross-device permission management; performance under bandwidth-saturating or latency-sensitive (tail) workloads rather than graph kernels; revocation-window closure; key-management scalability beyond 127 processes / 255 hosts; integration with dynamic capacity (DCD) and memory tiering/placement runtimes.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance to the requester's testbed work: Space-Control targets CXL 3.0 multi-host shared/pooled memory security and is evaluated purely in gem5+SST simulation, whereas the requester has a single-host CXL 2.0 memory-expansion box (4x CZ120) with no cross-host sharing and no hardware hooks for SPACE/Permission Checker. It offers no data on per-device bandwidth, cross-device interference, or tail latency. It is useful mainly as related work for the multi-tenant-isolation framing: it shows the security/permission side of CXL multi-tenancy is being addressed architecturally, letting the requester position per-device performance isolation and QoS (which Space-Control explicitly does not address) as a complementary, hardware-measurable gap.

Could not verify. Quotes were extracted from the arXiv HTML full text via the fetch tool's summarization; the exact CXL link width/latency parameters of the simulated device beyond the Table 2 values reported were not captured., Whether v2 (2026-05-28) changed evaluation details relative to v1 was not checked version-by-version.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We have used CXL-ClusterSim (Goswami et al., [2026]) for evaluating Space-Control. CXL-Clustersim uses gem5 (Lowe-Power et al., [2020]) to model the hosts for fidelity and SST (Rodrigues et al., [2011]) to model the shared disaggregated memory and parallelize the simulation.
  • Number of devices: 1
  • There are eight hosts. For simplicity, instead of modeling CXL.io for FM, we use shared CXL.mem for host and FM communication.
  • We run GAPBS kernels while scaling the number of hosts accessing the same SDM region from 1 to 8 in powers of two. We use four representative kernels: pr, bfs, bc, tc; ordered from more regular to more random access patterns.
  • Space-Control incurs a minimal 3.3% performance penalty with a modest 16 KiB cache
  • Current CXL-based sharing mechanisms provide coarse-grained, host-level access control: once a host is authorized, all processes on that host can access the shared region. This violates the principle of least privilege.
  • One limitation of permission revocation is that it fails to prevent on-flight instructions getting committed. There exists a tiny window of at most two BISnp where an attacker can violate isolation.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06951 https://arxiv.org/html/2603.06951

Where are the Joules? Energy Demand Analysis of Heterogeneous Memory Technologies

HCDS 2025 (4th Workshop on Heterogeneous Composable and Disaggregated Systems, co-located with EuroSys/ASPLOS, Rotterdam) — lightning talk, ACM, DOI 10.1145/3723851.3723857 (2025) other-hw n/a verify: confirmed confidence: low

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL device in the presented measurements; CXL-equipped systems are explicitly listed as future work in the author slides)

Problem. System provisioners lack conclusive, comparable energy-consumption data for emerging heterogeneous memory technologies (DRAM, NVRAM/persistent memory, CXL): existing data is "too minimal or too specific" to guide energy-optimized memory selection.

Idea. meBench, a memory-agnostic configurable workload generator that sweeps read/write ratio, access pattern (sequential/random), access granularity, parallelism, NUMA placement, and cache optimizations (normal / non-temporal / clflush), and measures energy per data volume (J/GiB) using Intel RAPL and an external power meter, to systematically characterize the energy demand of different memory technologies.

Testbed. Not fully verifiable from the slides. Measured technologies: DRAM vs NVRAM (persistent memory; vendor/model not stated in slides — keywords per search results mention Optane), on a multi-socket NUMA machine (near vs far memory experiments). Slide-stated run configs: "2 cores, 32 threads, ... 80 s duration". CPU model, DRAM config, kernel version not given in the slides; no CXL device present in any measurement.

Evaluation. Synthetic microbenchmarks generated by meBench: read-only / mixed / write-only workloads, sequential vs random access, with cache-optimization variants (normal stores, non-temporal stores, clflush), and NUMA near vs far placement; energy measured via Intel RAPL plus an external power meter; results reported as J/GiB (2 cores, 32 threads, 80 s per run).

Baselines. No system baselines — it is a characterization/benchmark-tool paper comparing memory technologies (DRAM vs NVRAM) against each other; authors position it as improving on prior "theoretic carbon analysis" work.

Key findings. From the slide charts (J/GiB): reads are cheap on both (DRAM ~0.1, NVRAM ~0.6-1.9); writes are where NVRAM's energy cost explodes: sequential/random NVRAM writes ~20.1-20.6 J/GiB vs DRAM ~0.7-1.6 J/GiB (~13-25x). Cache-optimization choice dominates NVRAM write energy: non-temporal stores ~14.9-21.6 J/GiB, clflush pushes NVRAM to 36.5-43.0 J/GiB vs DRAM 1.6-3.4. NUMA distance barely matters for energy-per-GiB: DRAM near 0.8 vs far 0.8; NVRAM near 20.6 vs far 21.2 J/GiB.

Limitations. CXL is in the title framing/keywords but is NOT measured — the slides explicitly defer "more in-depth measurements ... of CXL-equipped systems" to future work; only DRAM and NVRAM are characterized. Synthetic microbenchmarks only (no application workloads); short workshop/lightning-talk scope; energy attribution relies on RAPL plus one external meter (whole-system attribution deferred as future work); testbed details (CPU, DIMM models) not visible in the material I could access.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on: extending meBench to more access patterns and architectures; in-depth measurements of the entire system's energy (not just memory-domain RAPL); and, most relevantly, energy measurements of CXL-equipped systems. Untested: any CXL device energy, multi-device or multi-tenant scenarios, real application workloads, energy-aware placement policies built on the data.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Low direct relevance: despite CXL in its keywords, the paper measures only DRAM vs NVRAM energy and defers CXL to future work, so it offers no data on CZ120-class devices, multi-device setups, or interference. Its indirect value is methodological: the meBench J/GiB methodology (RAPL + external meter, per-technology and near/far NUMA sweeps) could be replicated on the requester's 4x CZ120 box to add an energy dimension (per-device energy under placement/interference) to ObjPlace or a per-device-scheduling paper — an angle this workshop paper shows is still open for CXL. It provides no guidance on treating CXL devices as independently schedulable resources.

Could not verify. Full paper text (ACM PDF is CC-BY gold OA but dl.acm.org Cloudflare-blocked all fetch routes: direct, fullHtml, pdf, unpaywall redirect, Wayback, Jina proxy; no arXiv/repository/author-page copy exists per OpenAlex/Unpaywall), Exact testbed: CPU model, DRAM DIMM config, NVRAM vendor/model (Optane inferred from search-result keywords only), kernel version, Whether the paper body contains any CXL measurement beyond what the slides show (slides show none; paper may discuss CXL only qualitatively), Exact numeric values in the paper's tables (numbers above are read from slide charts)

Measurements cover DRAM vs NVRAM (workloads, cache optimizations non-temporal/clflush, NUMA near/far, J/GiB). CXL appears exactly once, as future work: "More in-depth measurements ... of CXL-equipped systems". No second testbed, no NUMA-emulated "CXL". Analyst claims fully hold.
