Position papers & outlook (3 papers)

Position papers framing the debate, including the contrarian 'CXL Is Dead In The AI Era'.

A CXL-Powered Database System: Opportunities and Challenges

ICDE (author-hosted PDF; IEEE Xplore record 10597964) (2024) unknown n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 — pure vision/position paper, no evaluation testbed of any kind

Problem. Distributed DBMSs are constrained by limited interconnect capacity, communication speed, and bandwidth; static CPU-memory coupling causes resource stranding, slow disk-log-based fault recovery, and index designs ill-suited to far memory. The paper asks how CXL (1.1/2.0/3.0/3.1) should reshape next-generation DBMS design.

Idea. A vision paper identifying four opportunity areas for CXL in DBMS — (1) hybrid buffer pools spanning near/far/pooled/shared memory, (2) elastic memory scaling via switch-managed dynamic allocation, (3) fast recovery via far-memory checkpoints/dirty-page lists/force-commit with PMEM, (4) CXL-aware index design (B+ tree node placement, dynamic allocation, concurrency via switch coherence) — and enumerating 12 concrete research challenges across them (e.g., dynamic page allocation modeled as graph partitioning, unified near/far latency cost models, dual checkpoints, hot/cold tiering agents).

Testbed. None. No testbed, no measurements. The paper describes CXL product timelines (Intel Sapphire Rapids, Agilex 7 FPGAs, Samsung CXL Memory Expander for CXL 1.1; XConn CXL 2.0 switch + Samsung expanders + MemVerge/H3 2TB pooled demo) purely as industry context, citing others' work.

Evaluation. No evaluation section exists. The paper contains only architecture figures (Fig. 1-12 are conceptual diagrams: rack memory hierarchy, hybrid buffer pool, page directories, dual checkpoint, dirty-page sync, force commit) and cites prior empirical studies (DaMoN'22 Ahn et al., DaMoN'23 Lee et al., Pond, TPP, DirectCXL) for performance claims.

Baselines. None — no experiments, hence no baselines.

Key findings. No measured results. Contributions are taxonomic: 4 opportunities (buffer pool scaling, elastic memory allocation, fast recovery from far memory, efficient index design) and 12 named challenges (hybrid buffer pool construction; dynamic data page allocation; fine-grained memory sharing for multi-write consistency; elastic hybrid buffer pool; near-far memory allocation; automatic hot-cold tiering; dual checkpoint mechanisms; dirty-page synchronization in shared memory; force commit with CXL+PMEM; B+ tree node memory allocation; dynamic memory allocation for data modifications; index concurrency for structure updates). Quantitative claims are all secondhand (e.g., sub-microsecond latency for KB-scale shared-memory page updates, cited to a CXL tutorial).

Limitations. Entirely speculative: no hardware, no prototype, no measurements — all performance assumptions (sub-us shared-memory updates, switch-mediated coherence) come from CXL Consortium white papers and a tutorial, and much of the vision (CXL 2.0 switches, 3.0 fabric/shared memory, dynamic capacity reallocation) depends on hardware that at writing was not in mass production ("expected to launch from Q2 2025 through end of 2026 or later"). Authors themselves defer type-2 devices, GPUs, and CXL-TSP security to future work. No discussion of multi-device direct-attach expansion topologies, per-device bandwidth contention, or interference — the paper treats far memory as a monolithic layer.

Open questions. All 12 challenges are explicitly open: how to allocate pages across near/far/shared buffer pools; unified latency cost model for near/far memory; predicting memory reallocation latency; hot/cold tiering agents (ML-based); dual checkpoint consistency; whether force-commit over CXL+PMEM keeps transaction latency acceptable; CXL-aware B+ tree placement and OCC-based concurrent index updates. Also punted: type-2/GPU devices, TEE/CXL-TSP security, and any empirical validation.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Useful only as motivation/citation fodder, not as a technical baseline: it argues DBMS buffer pools, tiering, and indexes should become CXL-aware, which supports the requester's ObjPlace framing, but it offers zero measurements and treats CXL memory as one homogeneous "far buffer pool" behind (mostly hypothetical) switches. It never considers direct-attached multi-device topologies like the 4x CZ120 box, per-device bandwidth saturation (~35.5 GiB/s/region), or CXL-to-CXL interference, so the candidate direction of per-device scheduling/isolation is untouched here — the paper's tiering/hot-cold challenges (#2, #6) could even be cited as validation that device-granular placement is an open problem. Its DB-workload angle (buffer pools, B+ trees) suggests database workloads as an evaluation domain where per-device QoS claims would resonate.

Could not verify. Exact ICDE 2024 page numbers / official IEEE metadata (Xplore not fetched; venue attribution based on author-hosted PDF filename 'CXL_ICDE.pdf' and the task's note of IEEE record 10597964)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • This paper explores the significant potential of employing CXL in constructing next-generation DBMS. Through a thorough analysis of CXL's key characteristics, this paper identifies emerging opportunities, particularly in buffer pool expansion, memory elasticity, swift data recovery, and index optimization.
  • Contribution. This paper presents a list of challenges when leveraging CXL protocols in DBMS design from four aspects.
  • We argue that the next wave in DBMS innovation should consider CXL. Solving the challenges proposed in this paper will move DBMS design to the next frontier.
  • Products based on this version are expected to launch from the second quarter of 2025 through the end of 2026 or later.
  • Coupled with CXL memory expanders from Samsung, software from MemVerge, and H3 Platform, a 2TB pooled CXL memory system for 8 hosts is built [20]. (cited industry demo, not the authors' testbed)
  • The first study [21] evaluated CXL memory with OLTP and OLAP workloads, finding that reasonable data allocation can mitigate the throughput impact. (performance evidence is secondhand citation; the paper runs no experiments of its own)

https://dbgroup.cs.tsinghua.edu.cn/ligl//papers/CXL_ICDE.pdf

CXL Is Dead In The AI Era

SemiAnalysis (industry newsletter blog post) — NOT HotInfra 2024; the HotInfra'24 program (verified) contains no paper by this title (2024) unknown n/a verify: not-checked confidence: medium

CXL devices. 0 (no evaluation testbed of any kind; pure market/architecture analysis from published specs)

Problem. Whether CXL will become the dominant interconnect/memory fabric of the AI datacenter era, as CXL proponents claimed circa 2023-2024.

Idea. Position/analysis piece arguing CXL is a dead end for AI systems: (1) GPU vendors don't support it (Nvidia GPUs lack CXL; AMD MI300X doesn't expose it properly); (2) PCIe-based SerDes are ~3x less bandwidth-dense per mm2 of chip shoreline/beachfront than Ethernet-style 112G/224G SerDes, and that scarce die-edge area goes to NVLink/HBM instead; (3) PCIe x16 (~64 GB/s/direction) is dwarfed by NVLink (450 GB/s). Hence hyperscalers quietly shelved CXL projects; CXL is at best relegated to legacy memory-pooling/expansion niches on CPUs.

Testbed. None. No testbed, no CPU/DRAM/CXL device specified. Analysis is based on architecture comparisons and published specifications (PCIe 5/6, NVLink, 112G/224G Ethernet SerDes, BER specs).

Evaluation. No experiments. Qualitative + back-of-envelope quantitative analysis of SerDes bandwidth density, BER requirements (PCIe 6 < 1e-12 vs Ethernet 1e-4), and GPU product support status. Substantial portion (memory pooling/expansion use-cases, hyperscaler custom silicon) is behind a paywall and was not readable.

Baselines. None (no systems compared experimentally). Rhetorical comparisons: NVLink, Infinity Fabric, Ethernet-style SerDes vs PCIe/CXL.

Key findings. Claims (not measurements): PCIe x16 = 64 GB/s per direction vs NVLink 450 GB/s; Ethernet-style SerDes have ~3x more bandwidth per unit of chip shoreline area than PCIe-style SerDes (persisting at 224G vs PCIe 6.0); PCIe 6 requires BER < 1e-12 vs 1e-4 for Ethernet, making PCIe SerDes heavier per bit; Nvidia GPUs don't support CXL at all, so CXL cannot participate in the GPU-centric AI memory hierarchy; many hyperscaler CXL projects were reportedly quietly shelved by 2023-2024.

Limitations. Not peer-reviewed; no measurements or testbed whatsoever; key sections (memory pooling/expansion verdict) paywalled; argument is scoped to CXL as a GPU-attached AI interconnect and does not (in the free portion) refute CPU-side memory expansion/tiering, which is exactly what the requester's testbed does; product-support claims are a 2024 snapshot and partially contested (e.g., subsequent Micron/MemVerge demos of CXL improving LLM GPU utilization, and later work like CXLAimPod arXiv:2508.15980 arguing the opposite).

Open questions. Whether CXL memory expansion/pooling on CPU hosts remains economically viable for AI serving (paywalled/unresolved); whether future GPUs ever expose CXL; quantitative evaluation of CXL-attached memory for LLM inference (KV-cache offload) is entirely absent — the piece never tests any CXL device.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. This is an industry op-ed, not an academic paper, and it contains zero CXL measurements — cite it (if at all) only as motivation/counterpoint framing, e.g., "despite industry skepticism about CXL as a GPU interconnect, host-side CXL memory expansion for LLM serving is real and measurable." Its argument targets CXL as a GPU-to-GPU/scale-up fabric (shoreline area, NVLink comparison), which is orthogonal to the requester's setup: 4x CZ120 CPU-attached expanders behind an EPYC 9745 used for capacity/tiering. It says nothing about multi-device scheduling, cross-device interference, or QoS, so it neither anticipates nor conflicts with the per-device-independent direction; ObjPlace-style results (1.53x latency, 35.5 GiB/s/region, 2x co-runner slowdown) are exactly the evidence-based counterweight to this piece's evidence-free framing.

Could not verify. Paywalled subscriber section (memory pooling / memory expansion verdict, hyperscaler custom-silicon discussion) could not be read, Authorship note: search metadata lists Dylan Patel and Jeremie Eliahou Ontiveros; some 2024 coverage attributes it to Dylan Patel and Myron Xie - byline on the current page reflects the former, Confirmed absence from HotInfra 2024 program via the official site; did not additionally check HotInfra 2023/2025 programs for a similarly titled academic response paper

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • The simple answer is no - the people pushing CXL for AI are dead wrong.
  • Currently, CXL availability is the main issue, as Nvidia GPUs don't support it
  • we estimate that per mm² across a large variety of SOCs, Ethernet-style SerDes...have 3x more bandwidth per unit of shoreline area.
  • PCIe 6 requires a BER of < 1e-12 while Ethernet requires 1e-4
  • PCIe 16-lane provides "64GB/s of bandwidth in each direction" versus Nvidia NVLink's "450 GB/s bandwidth" (paraphrase confirmed against page; no testbed or measurement of any CXL device appears anywhere in the readable text)

https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/cxl-is-dead-in-the-ai-era https://hotinfra24.github.io/ https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=39729509

CXL and the Return of Scale-Up Database Engines

PVLDB (Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment), Vol. 17, No. 10, pp. 2568-2575 (2024) unknown n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (vision paper; no evaluation testbed of its own — all numbers cited from prior work by Intel [Sun et al. MICRO'23], Meta [TPP], and Microsoft [Pond])

Problem. Scale-out database architectures dominated the last two decades because cloud servers could only grow by adding whole machines over slow, non-coherent networks (PCIe/RDMA); PCIe's bandwidth/latency and lack of coherence erode accelerator benefits and force batching, while per-server memory limits cause stranded memory and inelastic, hard-to-migrate database engines.

Idea. Vision: CXL's coherent cxl.mem/cxl.cache sub-protocols and CXL 2.0/3.x switches enable three architectures — (a) local memory expansion managed by the database buffer pool (not the OS), (b) far-memory pooling where a buffer pool lives in switched, shared CXL memory so engines spawn/migrate without warm-up, and (c) full rack-scale disaggregation turning the rack into one shared-memory machine — plus coherent near-data processing (active memory regions in the CXL controller) and heterogeneous device pooling; together these revive scale-up database engines and turn distributed-systems problems back into single-machine ones.

Testbed. None. No testbed of any kind; the paper cites third-party characterizations (Intel micro-benchmarks: CXL load ~35% slower than remote NUMA; Meta: ~64 GB/s CXL bandwidth; Microsoft Pond: 26% of 158 workloads <1% penalty).

Evaluation. No evaluation. Section 2.4 'CXL Performance Characterization' is a literature summary of Intel (Sun et al., MICRO'23), Meta (TPP, ASPLOS'23), and Microsoft (Pond, ASPLOS'23) results; the rest of the paper is architectural argument plus enumerated research questions.

Baselines. None run. Rhetorical comparisons only: CXL vs RDMA (latency ~200-400 ns vs a few microseconds, 'a difference of at least 2.5x'; NICs waste >20% of PCIe lane bandwidth), CXL vs PCIe copies, CXL NDP vs non-coherent NDP (Oracle SPARC S7 DAX, Farview), scale-up vs scale-out/replication+sharding databases.

Key findings. All secondhand: (1) CXL load latency ~200-400 ns, ~35% over remote NUMA (Intel); NUMA load bandwidth 70% efficient vs 46% over CXL. (2) Meta: CXL as cold-page tier gives ~64 GB/s with latency only slightly above NUMA. (3) Microsoft: 26% of 158 workloads <1% penalty, +17% <5%; TPC-H overheads mostly <20%, query-dependent. (4) CXL coherence domain scales to 4096 devices (rack scale). Positions: database engines, not the OS, should place pages across tiers; OLTP on DRAM + OLAP structures on CXL avoids interference; disaggregated buffer pools enable elastic/serverless and easy migration; coherent NDP lets host and accelerator share the lock table and work in parallel.

Limitations. It is explicitly a position paper: zero implementation, zero measurements, no testbed, no multi-device or interference data. Key enabling features it relies on (CXL 3.0/3.1 memory sharing, GIM/GFAM, peer-to-peer, multi-rack fabrics, switch silicon at scale) were not commercially available in 2024. Authors note modeling CXL as NUMA 'can be inaccurate' and that bandwidth 'highly depends on the expander's characteristics', but do not measure this. They acknowledge cost/complexity critiques (Levis et al. 'A Case Against CXL Memory Pooling') only argumentatively. Nothing on per-device bandwidth contention, tail latency, QoS, or multi-tenant isolation — the interference problem is only implied ('OLTP and OLAP data structures would not interfere with each other', asserted, not shown).

Open questions. Explicit research questions: are expanders fast enough for OLTP or only OLAP; do data structures need redesign for CXL non-uniformity and should they span DRAM+CXL with adaptive placement; byte-addressable allocation vs paging-block-device use; how do hashing/sorting/indexing behave at rack scale where coherence invalidation traffic may dominate; scheduling rack-scale resources across competing queries; engine- vs thread-level elasticity granularity; what to offload to coherent NDP; how to compose/balance pooled heterogeneous devices. Untouched entirely: multi-device management, interference/QoS, fault handling beyond citing CXL RAS.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Useful as motivation/related-work framing, not as a technical baseline: it argues application-level (engine-level) placement across DRAM/CXL tiers beats OS paging, which directly supports the ObjPlace thesis, and its admission that 'modeling CXL memory as NUMA can be inaccurate' because per-expander controller/bandwidth characteristics vary is a citable hook for treating each CZ120 (or region) as a distinct resource. It contains no measurements of multi-device contention, per-device scheduling, or QoS, so the requester's per-device interference and tail-aware isolation direction fills a gap this vision paper only gestures at (its scheduling-across-competing-queries question). Good citation for positioning a systems paper on multi-tenant CXL placement at a DB or systems venue.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Alberto Lerner and Gustavo Alonso. CXL and the Return of Scale-Up Database Engines. PVLDB, 17(10): 2568 - 2575, 2024. doi:10.14778/3675034.3675047
  • Preliminary benchmarks by Microsoft, Meta, and Intel about CXL memory performance are already available (resp., [31, 34, 52]). The perceived latency for CXL memory access is slightly higher than that for remote NUMA access, roughly in the 200–400 nanoseconds range.
  • The micro-benchmarks from Intel investigate both latency and bandwidth [52]. Regarding latency, executing a load instruction against a given type of CXL-attached memory can take just 35% longer than the equivalent NUMA memory access.
  • In this paper, we describe why such architectural transformations are now possible, how they benefit emerging heterogeneous hardware platforms for data-intensive systems, and the associated research challenges.
  • We note, however, that simply modeling CXL memory as NUMA can be inaccurate. The number of memory controllers in an expander and, thus, available bandwidth varies from product to product.

https://www.vldb.org/pvldb/vol17/p2568-lerner.pdf

Foundational pre-CXL systems (Optane/RDMA era) (5 papers)

The pre-CXL toolbox: Optane tiering (HeMem, Memtis-lineage via TMTS) and RDMA/disaggregation (LegoOS, AIFM, Clio). APIs and page-classification machinery transfer; microsecond-era overhead assumptions do not.

Clio: A Hardware-Software Co-Designed Disaggregated Memory System

ASPLOS 2022 (arXiv:2108.03492) (2022) other-hw n/a verify: confirmed confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL devices; testbed has 4 Xilinx ZCU106 FPGA-based memory nodes attached over Ethernet)

Problem. Memory disaggregation research had taken two extremes: server-based memory nodes (high monetary cost, poor tail latency, poor scalability) or raw passive memory devices with no processing power (PDM model — performance, security, and management problems, requires memory pinning). Neither meets the combined goals of low cost, 100 Gbps throughput, microsecond-level average AND tail latency, TBs of memory, and thousands of concurrent clients.

Idea. A hardware-software co-designed "middle ground": a hardware-based memory node (CBoard, prototyped on FPGA, anticipated as ASIC) that virtualizes, protects, and manages disaggregated memory in hardware. Key elements: a hardware virtual memory system with bounded-time page-fault handling on the data path (asymmetric design keeping most state at compute nodes), a connectionless co-designed network stack terminating at the memory node (no RDMA QPs/MRs), and a framework for offloading computation to memory nodes. Compute-node library (CLib) is kernel-bypass userspace issuing raw Ethernet.

Testbed. Local cluster: 4 compute nodes (Dell PowerEdge R740, Intel "Xeon Gold 5128" CPU as printed in the paper, 40 Gbps Nvidia ConnectX-3 NIC; two CNs also have Nvidia BlueField SmartNICs) + 4 memory nodes = Xilinx ZCU106 Zynq UltraScale+ MPSoC FPGA boards ($2495 each: 504K LUTs, 4.75 MB BRAM, quad-core ARM Cortex-A53, two 10 Gbps SFP+ ports, 2 GB on-board DRAM), all connected via an Nvidia 40 Gbps VPI switch. FPGA modules run at 250 MHz, 512-bit datapath. Additional RDMA results from CloudLab with ConnectX-5. No CXL hardware anywhere; CXL appears only as a related-work citation ("coherent interconnect proposals" not yet built). Kernel version not stated. End-to-end line rate limited to 9.4 Gbps by the board's 10 Gbps port; 100+ Gbps numbers measured via on-board traffic generation; ASIC latencies are projections (scaled to 2 GHz).

Evaluation. Microbenchmarks: read/write latency CDFs (16 B), throughput vs client threads, scalability vs number of processes (up to ~1000, connectionless vs RDMA QPs), PTE/MR scalability mapping up to 4 TB of virtual address space onto 2 GB physical memory, TLB hit/miss/page-fault latency breakdown, allocation/free latency and retry rate. Applications: image compression/decompression (1000 256x256-pixel images per client, scaling clients), radix tree search (pointer-chasing offload, 10K-1M entries), Clio-KV key-value store under YCSB workloads A/B/C (100K 1 KB entries, Zipf theta=0.99, 2 CNs x 8 threads, also scaled across 1-4 MNs), Clio-MV multi-version object store (1-4 CNs, 50/50 read-write, uniform and Zipf), and a select-aggregate data analytics offload. Plus energy measurement (CPU/ARM/FPGA cycles x Watts, omitting DRAM and NICs), CapEx estimation, and FPGA resource utilization vs StRoM-RoCEv2 and Tonic-SACK.

Baselines. Native one-sided RDMA (ConnectX-3 local, ConnectX-5 on CloudLab); Clover (adapted as passive disaggregated memory); HERD on CPU and on BlueField (HERD-BF); LegoOS; FPGA-resource comparison vs StRoM-RoCEv2 and Tonic-SACK.

Key findings. FPGA prototype achieves ~100 Gbps per memory node (110+ Gbps on-board for large requests, 128 Gbps max datapath at II=1) and end-to-end latency of 2.5 us median / 3.2 us 99th percentile with far smaller tail than RDMA. Clio is connectionless and scales flat to ~1000 processes and 4 TB VA, while RDMA degrades beyond 2^8 MRs (local) / 2^12 (CloudLab) and fails beyond 2^18 MRs; RDMA remote page fault costs 16.8 ms (ConnectX-4) vs Clio's small TLB-miss cost only. LegoOS peaks at 77 Gbps vs Clio's 110 Gbps and has ~2x Clio's latency at small sizes. Energy: 1.1-3.4x savings vs CPU-based and SmartNIC-based systems (HERD 1.6-3x more energy; HERD-BF worst); 2.7x faster than software SmartNIC solutions. CapEx: 1 TB server-based MN costs 1.1-1.5x and consumes 1.9-2.7x power vs CBoard. Clio's whole design uses 31% LUT / 31% BRAM of a small FPGA (VirtMem only 5.5%/3%), less than StRoM's or Tonic's network stacks alone.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: prototype board has only 2 GB memory and 10 Gbps ports (end-to-end tests capped at 9.4 Gbps line rate; 100 Gbps results come from on-board traffic generation, ASIC latency is a projection); no built-in failure handling (replication APIs left to future work); TLB shared across processes and no per-connection bandwidth limits, so side-channel/DoS isolation is unaddressed; security depends on trusted CN attaching authentic PIDs; CN-side software stack became the bottleneck; energy accounting omits DRAM and NICs; analytics offload is slower than RDMA at high select ratios (FPGA compute slower than CPU). Evident from methodology: 4 TB scalability is emulated via VA mapping onto 2 GB physical memory; microsecond-scale network-attached latency regime, not nanosecond load/store; no cross-tenant interference or QoS experiments despite multi-client scaling; pre-CXL (CXL cited only as an unbuilt "coherent interconnect proposal").

Open questions. How to extend RDMA-style security defenses (forged-request attacks) to disaggregated memory; isolation vs cost/performance tradeoffs for shared TLB and per-connection bandwidth limits (explicit future work); failure-handling primitives (replicated writes) as API extensions; real ASIC CBoard implementation; offloading the CN-side software stack to a custom NIC; better on-board memory controller. Untested: multi-tenant performance isolation/QoS between clients sharing one CBoard, coherent load/store access semantics (CXL-style), and behavior with real TB-scale physical memory.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Clio is pre-CXL, Ethernet-attached far memory at 2.5 us median latency — an order of magnitude slower regime than the requester's 277 ns CXL load-to-use, so its mechanisms (RPC-style rread/rwrite, hardware page tables on the memory node) do not transfer directly. Its main value to the 4x CZ120 work is as related-work lineage and as precedent for two candidate-direction ideas: (1) Clio-KV explicitly scales throughput across 1-4 independent memory nodes (Fig. 15), the network-attached analog of treating each CXL device as an independently schedulable resource; (2) it argues that per-memory-node processing is where protection/management belongs, yet openly leaves multi-client performance isolation (shared TLB, no bandwidth limits) to future work — exactly the CXL-to-CXL interference / tail-aware QoS gap the requester's measurements (2.0-2.13x victim slowdown) expose on real passive CXL devices that have no such processing. Cite it to position passive CXL expanders as the "no processing power" extreme Clio critiques, with the host-side runtime (ObjPlace and its per-device successor) supplying the management Clio put in hardware.

Could not verify. Kernel/OS version of the testbed (not stated in the paper), Whether the ACM ASPLOS'22 camera-ready differs from the arXiv version I read (arXiv PDF was the source; the fetch metadata confirms ASPLOS'22 acceptance 2022-01-20), CPU model 'Xeon Gold 5128' is quoted exactly as printed in the paper; no such SKU exists in Intel's lineup (likely a typo in the paper for 5118/5218) — could not verify the true SKU

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Environment. We evaluated Clio in our local cluster of four CNs and four MNs (Xilinx ZCU106 boards), all connected to an Nvidia 40 Gbps VPI switch. Each CN is a Dell PowerEdge R740 server
  • equipped with a Xeon Gold 5128 CPU and a 40 Gbps Nvidia ConnectX-3 NIC, with two of them also having an Nvidia BlueField SmartNIC [48]. We also include results from CloudLab [14] with the Nvidia ConnectX-5 NIC.
  • We prototyped CBoard with a low-cost ($2495 retail price) Xilinx MPSoC board [82] and build the hardware fast path (which is anticipated to be built in ASIC) with FPGA.
  • Our prototyping board consists of a small FPGA with 504K logic cells (LUTs) and 4.75 MB FPGA memory (BRAM), a quad-core ARM Cortex-A53 processor, two 10 Gbps SFP+ ports connected to the FPGA, and 2 GB of off-chip on-board memory.
  • The PDM model has been adopted by a set of coherent interconnect proposals [15, 24], HPE’s Memory-Driven Computing project [20, 29, 31, 77]. ... In addition to the above problems, none of the coherent interconnects or HPE’s Memory-Driven Computing have been fully built. (ref [15] = CXL Consortium; CXL appears nowhere else in the paper)
  • Our FPGA prototype of Clio demonstrates that each memory node can achieve 100 Gbps throughput and an end-to-end latency of 2.5 μs at median and 3.2 μs at the 99th percentile.
  • Clio’s default asynchronous APIs quickly reach the line rate of our testbed (9.4 Gbps maximum throughput).
  • For Clio, we map a large range of VAs (up to 4 TB) to a small physical memory space, as our testbed only has 2 GB physical memory.

https://arxiv.org/abs/2108.03492 https://arxiv.org/pdf/2108.03492

HeMem: Scalable Tiered Memory Management for Big Data Applications and Real NVM

SOSP (ACM SIGOPS 28th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles), SOSP '21, October 26-29 2021, Virtual Event, Germany (2021) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL hardware; slow tier is Intel Optane DC persistent memory: 6 NVM DIMMs = 768 GB on the single evaluated socket, alongside 6 DRAM DIMMs = 192 GB)

Problem. Commercially available DRAM+NVM (Intel Optane DC) tiered servers raise capacity up to 8x but, if managed badly, cut bandwidth up to 7x and inflate latency up to 63%. Existing tiered-memory managers fail on real NVM: hardware Memory Mode (direct-mapped DRAM cache) suffers conflict misses and has no app visibility or policy flexibility; OS/kernel approaches (Nimble, HeteroOS) were designed/evaluated on emulated NVM, rely on page-table access/dirty-bit scanning + TLB shootdowns that do not scale to terabytes (seconds per scan), ignore Optane's asymmetric read/write bandwidth (write BW saturates at 4 threads, DRAM random write is 10.7x higher), and do sequential scan+migration on one kernel thread.

Idea. HeMem, a user-level library tiered-memory manager designed from scratch for real NVM: (1) asynchronous memory-access sampling via Intel PEBS CPU counters (NVM loads, DRAM loads, all stores; ~1 sample per 5,000 accesses) instead of page-table scans, processed by a dedicated PEBS thread into hot/cold FIFO lists at huge-page granularity with periodic "cooling"; (2) asynchronous batched migration on a 10ms policy thread, offloaded to an I/OAT DMA engine, with reads allowed during migration via userfaultfd write-protection; (3) NVM-aware policies: write-heavy pages prioritized for DRAM (asymmetric NVM bandwidth), small/ephemeral allocations left in DRAM (only ranges >1 GB managed), 1 GB DRAM kept free for new allocations; (4) flexibility: per-application user-level policy (e.g., pinning a priority tenant's data in DRAM), works with unmodified apps via LD_PRELOAD, mmap interception, and DAX-file-backed DRAM/NVM.

Testbed. Single socket of a dual-socket Intel Cascade Lake-SP system @ 2.2 GHz, 24 cores per socket, 100 GbE Mellanox ConnectX-5 NIC. Per socket: 192 GB DDR4 DRAM (6 DIMMs) + 768 GB Intel Optane DC NVM (6 DIMMs), populating all 6 memory channels. Debian 10.9, Linux kernel 5.1.0rc4 with two userfaultfd patches plus 1,337 lines of custom kernel code (userfaultfd DAX fault forwarding, ioatdma copy API). NVM in App-Direct mode exposed as a DAX file; DRAM also carved out as a DAX file via memmap. Measured tiers (Table 1): DDR4 82 ns R/W latency, 107/80 GB/s R/W; Optane DC 175/94 ns, 32/11.2 GB/s R/W. All benchmarks pinned to one NUMA node; no CXL hardware anywhere.

Evaluation. (a) GUPS microbenchmark (16 threads, 1B updates each, 8-byte objects): uniform random up to 256 GB working sets; 90/10 skewed hot-set variant with 512 GB working set and 1-256 GB hot sets; dynamic hot-set shift (4 GB of hot set swapped at t=150s); thread-scalability to 24 threads; asymmetric read/write skew (128 GB write-only region); overhead breakdown (Opt/PEBS/PT-Scan variants) and sensitivity studies of PEBS sampling period, hot threshold, cooling threshold. (b) Silo in-memory database running TPC-C, 16 threads, 16-1,728 warehouses (864 fit in DRAM). (c) FlexKVS (Memcached-compatible) key-value store, 8 server threads + 48-thread client, 4 KB values, 90% GET/10% SET, 20% hot keys getting 90% of accesses, working sets 16 GB-700 GB; latency at 30% load on the TAS network stack; a 2-instance priority experiment (prioritized 16 GB instance pinned to DRAM vs 500 GB regular instance). (d) GAP benchmark betweenness centrality on Kronecker graphs of 2^28 (fits DRAM) and 2^29 vertices (exceeds DRAM), 15 iterations, plus NVM write (wear) counting.

Baselines. Intel Optane DC Memory Mode (MM, hardware tiering with DRAM as direct-mapped cache); Linux Nimble page management (ASPLOS'19 kernel tiering, 4 migration threads); X-Mem (EuroSys'16 language-level tiering, emulated by mmap-ing large heap structures into NVM); NVM-only and DRAM-only placements; HeMem ablations (HeMem-PT-Async page-table-scanning variant, copy-threads vs DMA).

Key findings. Silo TPC-C: up to 13%, 82%, 400% higher throughput than MM, Nimble, X-Mem (MM beats HeMem by 17% once warehouses exceed DRAM). FlexKVS at 700 GB working set: 14%/15%/17% higher throughput than MM/Nimble/X-Mem; 75% lower median and 28% lower 90p latency than MM (up to 69% lower tail); priority isolation gives the prioritized instance 47% lower median and 16% lower 99p latency than MM with ~no harm to the regular instance. GAP BC: 93% faster than MM on average when the graph fits DRAM; 58% (vs MM) and 36% (vs Nimble) faster at 2^29 vertices; NVM-only is 16-17x worse. GUPS: 3.2x MM at 128 GB uniform working set; up to 2x MM for hot sets >8 GB; Nimble reaches only 25% of MM's GUPS; page-table scanning (M.Sync) achieves only 18% of optimal and overestimates the hot set at up to 300 GB. NVM wear: 10x fewer NVM writes than MM. Sampling 0.02% of accesses (1/5,000) suffices; PEBS overhead is within 5.9% of hand-optimal placement.

Limitations. No CXL hardware at all - the slow tier is Optane DC NVM, whose properties (asymmetric read/write bandwidth, 256 B media granularity, wear) drive several design choices that do not transfer directly to CXL DRAM. Authors explicitly scope out multi-node effects: "NUMA effects of tiered memory are beyond the scope of this paper" - everything is pinned to one socket, so there is no notion of multiple slow-tier devices, cross-device placement, or inter-device interference; the 6 NVM DIMMs are one flat interleaved DAX-backed pool. X-Mem baseline is emulated, not run. HeMem admits it only helps workloads with a hot set ("Applications that access memory uniformly see little benefit... when their working set exceeds DRAM capacity") and only manages large (>1 GB) long-lived anonymous ranges - kernel objects, program text, and page cache are unmanaged. Isolation evaluation is a single 2-instance FlexKVS priority experiment, not a real multi-tenant study; no bandwidth-contention/interference characterization between co-runners. Requires a patched kernel (userfaultfd write-protect + DAX + ioatdma) and statically preallocated DAX carve-outs of both DRAM and NVM; PEBS is Intel-specific. Tracking is at huge-page (2 MB) granularity only.

Open questions. Authors explicitly punt on: dynamic tiered memory allocation shared with kernel anonymous memory ("It is yet unclear how Linux will integrate NVM into anonymous memory management"); global cross-process tiered memory management (sketched as a userspace daemon coordinating per-process HeMem instances, not built); kernel-object and page-cache tiering; shared tiered memory across processes (only cooperative sketch); NUMA/multi-socket tiering. Obviously untested from today's perspective: any CXL-attached tier, more than one slow-tier device managed independently, bandwidth interference between tenants sharing the slow tier, and tail-latency QoS under contention beyond the single 2-instance priority demo.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. HeMem is a canonical pre-CXL tiering baseline (PEBS sampling instead of page-table scans, async DMA migration, write-asymmetry-aware placement, priority tenant pinned to DRAM) that any ObjPlace extension or per-device placement system on the c28 box will be compared against; its 2 MB-page, access-frequency-driven policy is the "hotness" strawman that ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity/skew/phase signals go beyond. Critically for the 4x CZ120 direction, HeMem treats the slow tier as one flat interleaved pool on one socket and declares NUMA effects out of scope, so per-device scheduling, CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference, and multi-tenant bandwidth QoS are completely unaddressed - a citable gap. Some Optane-specific machinery (write-heavy page prioritization due to 11.2 GB/s NVM write bandwidth, wear reduction) does not map to CZ120 CXL DRAM, but the low per-device bandwidth concern (~35.5 GiB/s per region on c28) makes HeMem's "spare bandwidth for background migration" and migration-rate-cap (10 GB/s) ideas directly reusable. Its FlexKVS priority experiment (47% median / 16% tail-latency win by reserving DRAM for one tenant) is the primitive 2-tenant precedent that a tail-aware, multi-device CXL QoS system would generalize.

Could not verify. The ACM DL published version (dl.acm.org) returned HTTP 403; analysis is based on the CC-BY accepted manuscript retrieved from NSF PAR (par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10337601), which carries the SOSP '21 copyright block, ACM artifact badges, and page numbers 392-407, so content should be identical to the published version.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We run our evaluation on a single socket of a dual-socket Intel Cascade Lake-SP system running at 2.2GHz with 24 cores per socket and a 100 GbE ConnectX-5 Mellanox NIC. Each socket has 192 GB of DDR4 DRAM and 768 GB of Intel Optane DC NVM. To leverage all 6 memory channels, there are 6 DIMMs of DRAM and NVM per socket. The machine runs Debian 10.9 with Linux kernel version 5.1.0rc4.
  • NUMA effects of tiered memory are beyond the scope of this paper. Hence, we run each benchmark pinned to a single NUMA node. All evaluated tiered memory management systems support this mode of operation.
  • We use NVM in App-Direct mode and expose it via a DAX file to user-space. Our prototype also manages DRAM via a DAX file, which we reserve via the kernel memmap command-line argument.
  • We study existing hardware and software tiered memory management systems on the recently available Intel Optane DC NVM with big data applications and find that no existing system maximizes application performance on real NVM.
  • A deployment of the Silo [36] in-memory database running TPC-C [1], attains up to 13%, 82%, and 400% higher throughput with with HeMem than with Intel Optane DC memory mode (MM), Linux nimble memory management (Nimble), and X-Mem, respectively.
  • Table 1: Main memory technology comparison. | DDR4 DRAM 82 ns 107 / 80 1x | Optane DC [3] 175 / 94 ns 32 / 11.2 8x
  • HeMem provides up to 16% better tail-latency for a high-priority task and reduces NVM wear by making up to 10x fewer writes to NVM than MM.

https://par.nsf.gov/servlets/purl/10337601 https://nvmw.ucsd.edu/nvmw2022-program/nvmw2022-data/nvmw2022-paper4-f https://www.bsc.es/sites/default/files/public/hemem-hmem-workshop.pdf https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3477132.3483550

AIFM: High-Performance, Application-Integrated Far Memory

USENIX OSDI (14th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation) (2020) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0

Problem. Datacenter memory is inelastic and stranded: servers run at ~60% memory utilization while jobs elsewhere are killed under memory pressure. Existing far-memory/swap systems (e.g., Fastswap) are kernel-integrated and page-granular, so they suffer up to 128x read/write amplification for small objects, page-granular (not object-granular) hotness tracking, kernel-crossing/page-fault overheads, and cores that spin uselessly waiting for remote data.

Idea. Application-integrated far memory: expose application-level semantics to a high-performance userspace runtime. Developers use remoteable smart pointers and remoteable data structures (array, hashtable, vector, etc.); the runtime (built on Shenango green threads + DPDK kernel-bypass TCP) handles object-granularity swap-in/out, semantic prefetching, non-temporal access hints, a pauseless memory evacuator, and switches to other green threads while waiting on remote fetches. It also supports offloading light computation to the remote memory server ("active remote components"). Hot-path dereference costs are close to a plain C++ unique_ptr.

Testbed. Two CloudLab xl170 nodes (one compute node, one remote memory node): 10-core Intel Xeon E5-2640 v4 @ 2.40 GHz, 64 GB RAM, 25 Gbit/s Mellanox ConnectX-4 Lx MT27710 NIC each. Hyper-threads enabled; C-states, DVFS, transparent huge pages, and speculation mitigations disabled. Ubuntu 18.04.3, kernel v5.0.0, DPDK 18.11.0 (Fastswap experiments use kernel v4.11, the latest it supports). Far memory is a TCP/DPDK remote memory server; a secondary SPDK NVMe SSD backend exists but evaluation focuses on the remote-memory backend. No CXL hardware of any kind — the word CXL does not appear in the paper (2020, pre-CXL-hardware).

Evaluation. Four workloads plus microbenchmarks, all on the 2-node compute/memory-server pair: (1) synthetic web-service frontend combining a remoteable hashtable (128M KV pairs, 10 GB) and an 8KB-object array (16 GB), 26 GB working set, Zipf-distributed closed-loop requests with AES-CBC encryption + Snappy compression; (2) C++ DataFrame library (24.3k LOC, 1,192 lines modified + 233 lines of remote-device code) running the Kaggle NYC taxi trip analysis (16 GB dataset, 31 GB working set), sweeping local memory 1-31 GB; (3) memcached-style hashtable GETs with Facebook USR key/value sizes, Zipf skew s=0 to 1.35, 5 GB local cap vs 10 GB data; (4) Snappy compression/decompression of a 16 GB array (30 x 0.5 GB files), streaming access; plus hot-path (pointer dereference cycles) and cold-path (64B/4KB miss latency in cycles) microbenchmarks.

Baselines. Fastswap (state-of-the-art kernel-integrated, page-granular far memory over RDMA); an idealized all-in-local-memory baseline; plain C++ unique_ptr for hot-path dereference microbenchmarks.

Key findings. AIFM outperforms Fastswap by up to 61x (hashtable at Zipf s=0.8); 6.8x higher throughput for 64B objects and 1.81x for 4KB objects. Web frontend: 20x Fastswap max throughput (370k vs 19k req/s) and 84% of all-local performance with 5x less local memory; non-temporal hints are decisive (temporal variant stalls at 140k req/s). DataFrame/NYC-taxi: 78% of in-memory throughput with only 3.2% (1 GB) local memory, >95% from ~20%; Fastswap gets only 20% at 1 GB. Snappy: near-local performance independent of local memory size due to userspace prefetching. Cold path 90th-pct latency: AIFM 18,582 cycles for 64B reads vs Fastswap 23,712 (AIFM overheads 888 vs Fastswap 7,191 cycles, ~10x lower); for 4KB reads AIFM is ~10% slower since its TCP transfer is slower than Fastswap's RDMA. Hot path: 4x latency / 2x throughput overhead vs raw pointer for L1-resident objects.

Limitations. Authors' own admissions: no TCP offloading or RDMA support (higher CPU overhead); a compute server connects to exactly one remote memory server; remote memory cannot be shared by different clients; local/remote memory sizes are fixed at runtime; prototype limited to unshared far-memory objects on a single memory server. Evident from methodology: no CXL or load/store-addressable far memory at all — far memory is microsecond-scale TCP to another server, so its cost model (kernel-crossing and amplification avoidance, green-thread context switching to hide ~18k-cycle misses) does not transfer directly to ~277 ns CXL loads; requires nontrivial application porting (5 person-days, 1,192 LOC for DataFrame); single-application runs only, no multi-tenant or interference experiments; small 10-core/64 GB testbed; bandwidth contention on the memory path is never studied.

Open questions. Explicitly punted: multi-server far memory, dynamic sizing of local/remote memory, sharing remote memory among clients, RDMA/offloaded-TCP backends. Untested and open: how AIFM-style object-granular, semantics-driven placement behaves when far memory is directly load/store-accessible (CXL) rather than runtime-mediated; object placement across multiple independent far-memory devices; QoS/isolation when several tenants share the far-memory tier; interaction of its evacuator/prefetcher with bandwidth saturation rather than latency.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. AIFM is the canonical object-granularity far-memory runtime and thus the closest published ancestor to ObjPlace's object-level DRAM/CXL placement — its remoteable-pointer API, per-data-structure memory partitioning, object-granular CLOCK hotness tracking, non-temporal/semantic hints, and pauseless evacuator are directly reusable design vocabulary, and a paper on per-device CXL placement must cite and differentiate from it. However, its far tier is a single TCP-connected remote server at microsecond latency: all of its headline wins come from avoiding page-fault amplification and hiding ~18k-cycle misses via green threads, mechanisms that are moot for 277 ns load/store CXL. It offers no evidence on bandwidth contention, per-device scheduling, or multi-tenant isolation — exactly the gaps the 4x CZ120 direction targets — and its explicit single-memory-server limitation means "multiple independently managed far-memory devices" is literally its stated future work.

Could not verify. USENIX abstract page returned HTTP 403; venue/page metadata (pp. 315-332) taken from search-result snippets rather than usenix.org directly. Full paper text itself was read from the author-hosted PDF at cs.brown.edu.

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We run experiments on two xl170 nodes on CloudLab [25] with 10-core Intel Xeon E5-2640 v4 CPUs (2.40 GHz), 64GB RAM, and a 25 Gbits/s Mellanox ConnectX-4 Lx MT27710 NIC.
  • We use Ubuntu 18.04.3 (kernel v5.0.0) and DPDK 18.11.0, except for experiments with Fastswap, which use Linux kernel v4.11, the latest version Fastswap supports.
  • We integrated two far memory backends into AIFM: a remote memory server based on a DPDK-based TCP stack, and an NVMe SSD using an SPDK-based storage stack.
  • TCP/IP is the dominant protocol for accessing data remotely, and AIFM currently uses TCP/IP.
  • Our prototype is limited to unshared far memory objects on a single memory server. Future work may add multi-server support, devise strategies for dynamic sizing of remote memory, or investigate sharing.
  • The current implementation has some limitations. First, we do not support TCP offloading or RDMA, which would reduce CPU overhead of our runtime. Second, a local compute server connects to a single remote memory server, and the remote memory cannot be shared by different clients. Finally, the local and remote memory size cannot be changed at runtime.
  • AIFM outperforms Fastswap, a state-of-the-art kernel-integrated, pagingbased far memory system [6] by up to 61×.
  • (grep verification: the string 'CXL' occurs zero times in the full extracted paper text)

https://cs.brown.edu/people/malte/pub/papers/2020-osdi-aifm.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi20/presentation/ruan

LegoOS: A Disseminated, Distributed OS for Hardware Resource Disaggregation

OSDI (13th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation), Carlsbad CA; Best Paper award (2018) other-hw n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL hardware at all; disaggregation is emulated on 10 commodity x86 servers connected by 40 Gbps Mellanox ConnectX-3 InfiniBand RDMA)

Problem. The monolithic-server model makes datacenter resource utilization, elasticity, hardware heterogeneity, and failure isolation poor (Google/Alibaba traces show only ~50% CPU/memory utilization; motherboard/memory/CPU/power failures kill whole servers). Hardware resource disaggregation (network-attached CPU/memory/storage components) promises to fix this, but no existing OS (monolithic, microkernel, multi-kernel, or distributed OS) can manage a set of non-coherent, network-attached, independently failing hardware components.

Idea. The "splitkernel" OS architecture: disseminate OS functionality into loosely-coupled monitors, each running on and managing one hardware component (pComponent=CPU, mComponent=DRAM, sComponent=storage), communicating only via network messaging (customized RDMA-based RPC), with no cross-component coherence and only occasional global resource/failure management. LegoOS is the first splitkernel: it exposes vNodes (VM-like abstraction) with Linux ABI compatibility; moves ALL memory hardware/software (page tables, TLBs, virtual memory) to mComponents, leaving pComponents with only virtually-indexed caches plus a small software-managed DRAM "ExCache" (extended cache below LLC); uses two-level distributed virtual memory management (coarse 1GB vRegions owned by mComponents, home-mComponent + GMM/GPM/GSM global managers, delayed physical allocation until first write); and provides best-effort in-memory replication (primary mComponent + append-only log on a secondary + backup file on sComponent) for memory failure.

Testbed. Cluster of 10 machines, each with 2x Intel Xeon E5-2620 2.40GHz, 128 GB DRAM, and one 40 Gbps Mellanox ConnectX-3 InfiniBand adapter, connected by a Mellanox 40 Gbps IB switch; comparison Linux is v4.9.47. NO real disaggregated hardware and NO CXL: pComponents emulated by limiting a server's usable DRAM and configuring it as software-managed ExCache (paged, 4KB lines); mComponents/sComponents emulated by limiting a server to 2 (up to 2-5) usable cores. Storage microbenchmark uses a Samsung PM1725s NVMe SSD. LegoOS itself is 206K SLOC C on x86-64 (56K for drivers), supporting 113 Linux syscalls. Fetching an ExCache line from an mComponent takes ~6.5 us.

Evaluation. Micro- and macro-benchmarks plus two unmodified applications. Microbenchmarks: network latency (LegoOS-RPC-over-IB vs LegoOS-socket-over-IB vs Linux IPoIB, 128B-4KB messages); memory throughput (per-thread 1M sequential 4KB loads with a huge empty 32GB ExCache so every access is a cold miss to the mComponent, varying 1-8 worker threads and 1-2 mComponents); storage throughput (single-thread 4KB seq/random read/write over a 25GB file on the NVMe SSD, fsync after each write). PARSEC: BlackScholes, Freqmine, StreamCluster (1 pComponent w/ 128MB ExCache, 1 mComponent, 1 sComponent; 1 and 4 workload threads). Applications: unmodified TensorFlow (Cifar-10, 256MB 64-way ExCache, 0.9GB working set) and Phoenix MapReduce (Wikipedia dataset, 512MB ExCache, 1.7GB working set), 4 threads, ExCache set to ~25% of working set; sweeps of ExCache size, eviction policy (FIFO vs LRU, bitmap vs free-list, piggyback flush+fetch), 1-4 mComponents, replication on/off; a multi-application run of TensorFlow+Phoenix in 2P1M and 1P2M configurations; and an analytical failure study computing MTTF from published per-device replacement statistics.

Baselines. Monolithic Linux server v4.9.47 with all working set in memory; Linux with swap to local high-end NVMe SSD; Linux with swap to local ramdisk; InfiniSwap (RDMA remote-memory swapping); Linux IPoIB for network microbenchmarks; single-node Linux for memory/storage microbenchmarks.

Key findings. (1) With ExCache = ~25% of working set, LegoOS is only 1.68x (TensorFlow) and 1.34x (Phoenix) slower than a monolithic Linux server holding the entire working set (abstract states 1.3x-1.7x across workloads). (2) Against Linux with the same memory size swapping to SSD/ramdisk or InfiniSwap network swap, LegoOS is 0.8x-3.2x (mostly faster, even beating ramdisk swap). (3) Analytical MTTF: LegoOS 6.8-8.7 years vs 5.8 years for an equivalent monolithic cluster, a 17%-49% improvement, while improving resource packing (on-demand allocation from separate CPU/memory pools). (4) LegoOS RPC-over-IB latency far below Linux IPoIB; network stack + RPC optimizations gave up to 50% improvement; piggybacking dirty-flush with miss-fetch and p-local zero-fill lines add up to 5%. (5) Going from 1 to 4 mComponents LOWERS performance by up to 6% (piggyback optimization only works when flush and fetch target the same mComponent); memory replication costs 2%-23%. (6) FIFO beats LRU for ExCache eviction (LRU suffers lock contention); associativity benefit plateaus beyond 512-way. (7) In the 2P1M multi-app run, both applications suffer because their requests saturate the single mComponent's worker threads.

Limitations. Authors' own: no real disaggregation hardware exists, so everything is emulated on commodity servers over InfiniBand ("Since there is no real resource disaggregation hardware, we emulate..."); memory monitor is purely software and its worker-thread count bottlenecks throughput (they suggest future FPGA/many-core mComponents); no writable shared memory across processes (MAP_SHARED apps must be adapted); only mComponent failure is handled — pComponent failure and storage replication are left to future work; secondary-mComponent failure just restarts replication; no memory data migration, so their load-balancing mechanism shows little benefit; large-scale resource management declared out of scope; MTTF analysis excludes transient failures and is analytical, not fault-injection. Evident from methodology: microsecond-scale RDMA emulation (6.5 us ExCache miss) does not model real memory-bus-attached disaggregated memory; small scale (10 machines, max 4 mComponents); only two real applications (TensorFlow on Cifar-10, Phoenix), 4 threads each; only one two-application co-run experiment, no multi-tenant isolation/QoS or tail-latency evaluation; ExCache line fixed at 4KB pages due to x86 paging constraints.

Open questions. Handling pComponent failure; storage (sComponent) reliability inside LegoOS; memory migration between mComponents and real load-balancing policies; hardware implementations of memory monitors (ASIC/FPGA/cheap cores) and how much parallelism real mComponents need; scaling global resource management to the envisioned hundreds-to-thousands of components; supporting other hardware (GPU, NVM, DPU) as components; multi-tenant sharing of components (each component can host multiple vNodes, but isolation/interference between them is never evaluated); performance under real (non-emulated) disaggregated memory latencies.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. Foundational positioning/related-work material, not a source of transferable numbers: LegoOS's "memory components" are RDMA-emulated servers with ~6.5 us access, two orders of magnitude slower than the requester's 277 ns CZ120 loads, and there is no CXL, no bandwidth-interference, and no tail-latency study. Its conceptual contribution IS directly aligned with the candidate direction: each mComponent is an independently managed, independently failing device with its own monitor, per-device load-aware placement (GMM picks the least-loaded mComponent), per-device replication, and per-vRegion ownership — a 2018 precedent for treating each of the 4 CZ120s as an independently schedulable resource with per-device failure domains. Two evaluation findings are worth citing when arguing per-device management matters: spreading across more mComponents can HURT (up to 6% from lost locality/piggybacking), and co-running two apps on one mComponent saturates it — an early, coarse observation of per-memory-device contention that the requester's CXL-to-CXL interference work can sharpen with real hardware. Use it to frame the lineage (splitkernel -> CXL pooling) and to contrast message-passing disaggregation against load/store CXL, where OS-level per-device scheduling remains unexplored.

Could not verify. Best Paper award status comes from the authors' GitHub repository description ('USENIX OSDI 2018 Best Paper'), not from the paper PDF itself

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • Since there is no real resource disaggregation hardware, we emulate disaggregated hardware components using commodity servers by limiting their internal hardware usages. For example, to emulate controllers for mComponents and sComponents, we limit the usable cores of a server to two. To emulate pComponents, we limit the amount of usable main memory of a server and configure it as LegoOS software-managed ExCache.
  • We ran all experiments on a cluster of 10 machines, each with two Intel Xeon CPU E5-2620 2.40GHz processors, 128 GB DRAM, and one 40 Gbps Mellanox ConnectX-3 InfiniBand network adapter; a Mellanox 40 Gbps InfiniBand switch connects all of the machines. The Linux version we used for comparison is v4.9.47.
  • We use regular machines to emulate mComponents by limiting usable cores to a small number (2 to 5 depending on configuration). We dedicate one core to busy poll network requests and the rest for performing memory functionalities.
  • We implemented LegoOS on x86-64 and evaluated it by emulating hardware components using commodity servers. Our evaluation results show that LegoOS' performance is comparable to monolithic Linux servers, while largely improving resource packing and reducing failure rate over monolithic clusters.
  • With around 25% working set, LegoOS only has a slowdown of 1.68x and 1.34x for TensorFlow and Phoenix compared to a monolithic Linux server that can fit all working sets in its main memory.
  • Surprisingly, using more mComponents lowers application performance by up to 6%. This performance drop is due to the effect of ExCache piggyback optimization.
  • With better resource utilization and simplified hardware components (e.g., no motherboard), LegoOS improves MTTF by 17% to 49% compared to an equivalent monolithic server cluster.

https://www.usenix.org/system/files/osdi18-shan.pdf https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi18/presentation/shan https://github.com/WukLab/LegoOS

Data Tiering in Heterogeneous Memory Systems (X-Mem)

EuroSys (2016) numa-emulated n/a verify: not-checked confidence: high

CXL devices. 0 (no CXL; NVM emulated via Intel HMEP — separate physical memory range with custom BIOS + CPU-microcode latency/bandwidth throttling)

Problem. Whether replacing most DRAM with cheaper, higher-capacity but slower NVM (PCM/RRAM, 2-4x latency, 1/8-1/4 bandwidth) causes unacceptable application slowdown, and how to place application data across DRAM/NVM tiers to avoid it. Naive page-cache/paging approaches fail because objects with different access patterns share pages and paging overheads are unacceptable for directly-attached memory.

Idea. Object/data-structure-granularity tiering: an extended allocator API (xmalloc with a per-data-structure tag) that segregates objects of each tagged data structure into disjoint memory regions (built on per-tag jemalloc instances, mapped to tiers via mbind, entirely in user space); an offline Pin-based profiler that classifies each data structure's access pattern (sequential/streaming vs. random vs. pointer-chasing) — because effective latency on out-of-order CPUs depends on pattern, not just access frequency ("hotness" alone is insufficient); and a greedy placement optimizer that uses the resulting tag-to-priority map to decide which structures go in scarce DRAM vs. NVM.

Testbed. Intel Hybrid Memory Emulation Platform (HMEP): 2-socket Intel Xeon E5-4620, 8 cores per socket @ 2.6 GHz, hyperthreading disabled, 4 DDR3 channels per CPU, memory interleaved across CPUs; measured DRAM latency 150 ns, bandwidth 37-40 GB/s. NVM emulated via custom BIOS (separate physical range) + custom CPU microcode (configurable latency 300-600 ns and bandwidth down to 5 GB/s). Network apps driven by a dedicated client machine over direct 10 GigE links. No CXL hardware; no kernel version stated.

Evaluation. Three applications modified with 10-50 LOC each to tag large data structures via the X-Mem API: MemC3 key-value store (Facebook-like trace), VoltDB in-memory database (TPC-C), GraphMat graph analytics (PageRank on Twitter dataset; plus GraphLab, X-Stream, Galois in the motivating study). NVM emulator swept over latency 300/450/600 ns and bandwidth 40/20/10/5 GB/s; DRAM fraction varied (e.g., 1/2 down to 1/16 of footprint). Offline profile run (Pin tracing) produces tag-to-priority map used in production runs.

Baselines. DRAM-only (all data in DRAM, best case); NVM-only (all data in emulated NVM, worst case); frequency-based/manual placements ("based on the access frequencies alone and/or our understanding of the applications"); all possible placement permutations exhaustively for GraphMat; discussion vs. AutoNUMA-style co-location and DRAM-as-cache paging.

Key findings. With DRAM at only 6% of footprint (GraphMat) or 25% (VoltDB, MemC3) and NVM at 4x latency / 1/8 bandwidth of DRAM, X-Mem-guided placement yields only 13-40% slowdown vs. DRAM-only; unguided worst-case placement suffers 1.5-5.9x slowdown on the same configuration. Assuming NVM is 5x cheaper per bit, hybrid gives 2-2.8x better performance/$ than DRAM-only (1/2-DRAM config: 1.5-1.7x better perf/$ within 9% of DRAM-only performance). Access pattern matters, not just frequency: NVM-only slowdown up to 6x for bandwidth-sensitive streaming apps. For GraphMat, the automatic placement beat all exhaustively-enumerated alternatives (one instance 1.0-5.8x better). Pin profiling overhead up to 40x (offline only).

Limitations. Authors admit: static offline classification assumes homogeneous behavior within a data structure and must be redone if workload behavior changes (they suggest shadow-infrastructure re-profiling); Pin tracing is up to 40x slowdown, unusable online (they punt to hoped-for x86 ISA/hardware profiling extensions); manual tagging of data structures required (compiler automation left as future work). Methodological: NVM is emulated (custom BIOS/microcode), not real device silicon; single-node, single-application runs — no multi-tenant or interference experiments; placement is static at classification time, no phase-adaptive migration; pre-CXL, so no coherent-device link effects (no port/link contention, no per-device topology).

Open questions. Online/low-overhead profiling and real-time placement (explicitly deferred to hardware extensions); per-object heterogeneity within a tagged data structure (skew inside a structure); dynamic workload adaptation/re-classification; automation of tagging; behavior on real NVM or CXL devices; multi-tenant contention on the slow tier; write-endurance-aware placement beyond their brief device-wear discussion.

Relevance to the 4x CZ120 testbed. This is the foundational ancestor of ObjPlace: allocator-tag object-granularity DRAM/slow-tier placement driven by access-pattern classification (streaming vs. pointer-chasing vs. random), directly anticipating ObjPlace's bandwidth-sensitivity-driven placement — cite it as the origin of the object-tiering paradigm and differentiate on real CXL silicon, online profiling, and phase/pressure adaptivity. Its key insight that effective latency depends on access pattern (bandwidth-bound streaming suffers most on the slow tier) matches the requester's own 2.0-2.13x bandwidth-victim result on CZ120s. It offers nothing on the candidate next direction: single emulated slow tier, zero devices, no per-device scheduling, no cross-device interference, no multi-tenancy — so treating 4 CZ120s as independently schedulable resources is untouched territory relative to this paper.

Could not verify. Kernel version (not stated in paper), Exact DRAM capacity of the HMEP testbed (only 'a sufficient number of' DIMMs implied; capacity not stated in extracted text)

Evidence quotes (verbatim)
  • We perform all experiments on a hybrid memory emulator (§5.1) that runs at full speed.
  • The hybrid memory emulation platform (HMEP) [39] was built by Intel to support the exploration of software stacks for hybrid memory systems. HMEP enables the study of hybrid memory with real-world applications by implementing – (i) separate physical memory ranges for DRAM and emulated NVM (using a custom BIOS), and (ii) fine-grained emulation of configurable latency and bandwidth for NVM (using custom CPU microcode).
  • HMEP is restricted to Intel proprietary Xeon E5-4620 platforms, with each of its two processors containing eight 2.6 GHz cores. Hyperthreading is disabled. Each CPU supports four DDR3 channels and memory is interleaved across the CPUs. The measured DRAM latency and bandwidth on the system are 150ns and 37GB/s, respectively.
  • With such guided placement and with DRAM constituting only 6% of the total memory footprint for GraphMat and 25% for VoltDB and MemC3 (remaining memory is NVM with 4× higher latency and 8× lower bandwidth than DRAM), we show that our target applications demonstrate only a 13% to 40% slowdown.
  • these applications see, in the worst case, 1.5× to 5.9× slowdown on the same configuration. Based on a realistic assumption that NVM will be 5× cheaper (per bit) than DRAM, this hybrid solution also results in 2× to 2.8× better performance/$ than a DRAM-only system.
  • Memory tracing (with Pin) during the profiling step can significantly slow down the application (up to 40× in our tests), and is therefore suited only for offline use and not for production.

Part II — From Gaps to Research Directions (added 2026-07-08)

Generated from the Part-I verified map (97 papers). Facts cite papers from the map; judgments are marked as inference. Companion decision context: WORKLOG sessions 9–10 (2026-06-12), docs/RESULTS.md (own measurements: CXL latency 1.53× DRAM, ~35.5 GiB/s/region saturating at 4 threads, victim 2.0–2.13× under CXL co-runner).

1. Executive summary

Three load-bearing facts from Part I: (i) no published system manages ≥2 direct-attached commodity-ASIC CXL devices independently on one host — the four near-misses are CSFQ-transport (NSDI'26, switched-pool transport layer), Weisgut (VLDB'25, static DB placement on FPGA blades), CXL-CCL (arXiv'26, GPU collectives behind a switch), and Liaw & Chen (arXiv'25, single-tenant, 2 nodes); (ii) CXL-to-CXL cross-device interference on one host has never been measured on real hardware (CXL-Interplay DAC'25 says so verbatim; Melody ASPLOS'25 measures devices on separate hosts and punts mitigation); (iii) every multi-tenant CXL QoS system regulates above one fused pool (Memstrata OSDI'24, MTTM EuroSys'26, Equilibria arXiv'26, Mercury arXiv'24).

This yields one umbrella direction — device-granular CXL memory management — decomposed into three paper candidates: a placement/runtime paper (Idea 1), a multi-tenant isolation paper (Idea 2), and an adaptive-interleaving paper (Idea 3). All share the same Week-1–2 characterization and the same hardware gate: de-interleaving dax2.0 into per-device NUMA nodes (root decoder0.2 is at interleave_ways=2; needs admin). Recommendation: run the shared characterization first and let the interference/tail data pick between Idea 1 (default) and Idea 2 (if isolation effects dominate); Idea 3 is the merge-in mechanism; the famfs/FAST angle is the pivot if the gate fails.

2. Ranked research ideas
